  4. Record the recommendation and the client's response.

The recurring theme of this lesson. These are technical subjects the licensee does not practice in. What he is responsible for is noticing that the transaction has walked into one, and the noticing is done with a checklist of questions rather than with knowledge.

5.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to list the water questions you would ask on a rural listing and explain what a severed mineral interest means for a buyer.

6. Fixture disputes in a sale

6.1 The professional answer

Everything in Sections 1 through 4 describes how a dispute is resolved. This section is about not having one.

The professional practice is to make the fixture tests unnecessary by naming the disputable items in the agreement.

Agreement overrides the tests. A contract that says the chandelier stays and the mounted television goes has answered the question, and no analysis of intention or adaptation is required.

6.2 The naming list

In most residential transactions, these are worth addressing expressly:

  • Appliances — refrigerator, washer, dryer, freestanding range
  • Window coverings — rods and panels separately
  • Mounted televisions and their brackets
  • Chandeliers and distinctive light fixtures
  • Above-ground spas and portable water features
  • Storage sheds and outbuildings
  • Security cameras, doorbells, thermostats, and other smart-home devices
  • Water treatment and filtration systems
  • Mounted shelving and closet systems
  • Potted and container plantings
  • Anything the seller has mentioned intending to take
  • Anything the buyer has admired aloud

The last two are the practical ones. A seller who says "I'll be taking the dining room fixture, it was my grandmother's" has just told you what to name in the contract. A licensee who nods and writes nothing has created the dispute.

6.3 The intake conversation

The right time is the listing appointment, and the right question is direct:

"Before we photograph, walk me through anything you're planning to take with you — light fixtures, appliances, anything mounted, anything with sentimental value. I'd rather name it now than have it come up two days before closing."

This costs five minutes and prevents the most common non-financial dispute in residential practice.

6.4 Marketing consistency

A second source of disputes is the gap between marketing and contract. If the photographs show the chandelier, the description mentions the built-in wine refrigerator, and the seller intends to take both, the buyer has been shown something he is not getting.

Photographs are advertising, and they are held to the truthfulness standard. Where staging includes items that are not conveying — or where the seller intends to remove a photographed fixture — say so in the marketing rather than leaving it for the walkthrough.

6.5 When it surfaces at the walkthrough

The final verification confirms that included items are present. Where a named item is missing:

  1. Document it — photographs and dated notes.
  2. Address it through the contract before closing, because the ordinary leverage disappears at closing.
  3. Do not instruct escrow to hold funds — escrow acts only on mutual written instructions.
  4. Do not close and pursue afterward, which converts a solvable issue into a claim.

6.6 The licensee's limits

Where the parties genuinely dispute whether an item was a fixture and the contract is silent, the licensee's role is to state what the contract says, document the positions, and escalate. Whether a particular item was a fixture is a legal conclusion the licensee should not deliver, however clear it seems.

The honest position is: "The contract doesn't name it, which means this comes down to how the law classifies it. That's a question for counsel, and here's what I can do in the meantime."

6.7 The habits that eliminate the category

Fixture disputes are among the most preventable problems in a transaction, and three habits eliminate nearly all of them.

One: the seller names what he is taking, before listing. Section 6.3's intake conversation, asked directly — "is there anything here you intend to take with you?" — and written down. The chandelier, the appliance, the light fixture, the plant, the mounted equipment. The best answer is that the seller removes and replaces it before any photograph is taken, so no buyer ever saw it.

Two: the marketing shows what conveys. Section 6.4's consistency requirement. A photograph is a representation, and a buyer who saw an item in the listing and does not find it at the walkthrough has a grievance whatever the fixture analysis says. Where an item appears in a photograph and is excluded, the exclusion is stated where the photograph is, not buried in a document delivered later.

Three: the uncertain items are named in the agreement. Not the obvious ones — the uncertain ones. Section 2.7's shed, television bracket, and wine refrigerator are the items to name, because the items whose classification is clear do not produce disputes.

A fourth habit for the buyer's side, less often stated: walk the property asking what the buyer assumes is included. Lesson 08 of the practice course makes the same point about the agreement generally — "if it matters to you and it isn't written down, it isn't part of the deal" — and fixtures are where that sentence earns its keep. A buyer who assumed the play structure, the workbench, or the water softener conveys should learn now rather than at the walkthrough.

Why this is worth a licensee's time. The amounts in dispute are usually small and the damage is not. A transaction that reaches the walkthrough and turns into an argument about a chandelier has lost the goodwill that was carrying every other unresolved item, and the closing that was on schedule is now a negotiation between people who are angry.

And the limit, restated. Section 6.6 applies to all of it. When a dispute arises anyway, the licensee identifies the issue and declines to declare who is right. The classification of a contested item is a legal question, and a licensee who announces the answer at the walkthrough has given legal advice under pressure to a party who will rely on it.

6.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to run the intake conversation, produce the naming list for a specific property, and state the correct sequence when an item is missing at the walkthrough.

Key terms

TermDefinition
Real propertyLand, permanent attachments, and the bundle of legal rights. Transfers by deed, is recorded, and conveys unless excluded.
Personal propertyMovable things not permanently attached. Transfers by bill of sale or delivery, and does not convey unless included.
AnnexationPersonal property becoming real property by attachment.
SeveranceReal property becoming personal property by detachment — physically, or on paper by contract or reservation.
FixtureAn item that was personal property and became real property through attachment. Conveys unless excluded.
MARIAMethod of attachment, Adaptability, Relationship of the parties, Intention, Agreement.
IntentionGenerally the most important test. The other tests are largely evidence of it, since intention itself is invisible.
AgreementThe test that overrides the others. Where the parties named the item, the analysis is never reached.
AdaptabilitySpecial adaptation to this property — custom storm windows, a key to this lock — pointing toward fixture status even where removal is easy.
Relationship of the partiesWhy "is it a fixture?" is incomplete. Doubt tends to favor the buyer against a seller, the tenant against a landlord, and the lender against a borrower.
Trade fixtureAn item a tenant attaches to leased premises for the tenant's trade or business, generally removable at or before the end of the tenancy, with repair of removal damage.
AccessionThe rule by which trade fixtures not removed in time may become the landlord's property.
Building improvementA change to the building itself rather than trade equipment — a new roof, upgraded service. Not a trade fixture.
Natural attachmentThings growing on the land.
Artificial attachmentThings people placed — buildings, fences, walls, paving.
Perennial growthTrees, shrubs, and growth not requiring annual cultivation. Generally real property, conveying with the land.
Annual cropGrowth requiring annual planting and cultivation. Generally personal property, even while growing, because it represents the season's labor rather than a permanent characteristic of the land.
Riparian rightsRights arising from ownership of land bordering a watercourse, to reasonable use.
Appropriative rightsRights arising from diversion to beneficial use, under a system of priority.
Littoral rightsRights of an owner adjoining a lake or sea rather than a flowing watercourse.
Accretion and avulsionGradual deposit, which generally benefits the owner; and sudden change, which generally does not shift boundaries.
Severed mineral rightsSubsurface rights sold or reserved separately. Once severed they stay severed, appear as a title exception, and may carry surface access rights.
Lateral and subjacent supportSupport a parcel receives from adjoining land, and from beneath.
Naming the itemThe professional practice that makes the fixture tests unnecessary.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A buyer and seller dispute the refrigerator. The buyer says it was in the house when he saw it and nobody said it was going. The seller says appliances are obviously hers and she never offered it. The contract is silent.

Explain why both are reasoning correctly, and state the professional failure.

Solution. They are applying opposite defaults, and each default is correct for its own category. Real property conveys unless excluded; personal property does not convey unless included. The buyer looked at the house and applied one; the seller looked at her appliance and applied the other.

A freestanding refrigerator is usually personal property — which makes it the single most disputed item in residential practice, because it is also the item buyers most reliably assume conveys.

The professional failure is the silent contract. Neither party did anything unreasonable. The licensee had one job in this dispute — to name the item at the listing appointment — and did not do it.

Problem 2

Apply the tests to a wall-mounted television and its bracket, a hardwired chandelier, and a custom storm window cut to fit one opening.

State which test does the work in each.

Solution. The television and bracket: method of attachment separates them. The bracket is bolted to studs and cannot come off without damage; the set lifts off. They are frequently treated differently, which is exactly why both are named in contracts rather than analyzed.

The chandelier: attachment plus intention. Hardwired into a ceiling box, it is ordinarily a fixture — and it is among the most commonly removed by sellers who assumed otherwise, usually for sentimental reasons.

The storm window: adaptability. It is easy to remove and worth very little anywhere else, and that special adaptation is evidence the annexor meant it to stay. The same reasoning covers a key to this lock and shelving built to these alcove dimensions.

What transfers: the five tests are not a checklist of equal weight. Intention is generally the most important, and the others are largely evidence of it — because intention itself is invisible and must be inferred.

Problem 3

The same above-ground spa is argued over in three settings: between a buyer and a seller at closing; between a landlord and a tenant at the end of a lease; and between a lender and a borrower after default.

Does the analysis change?

Solution. The item is identical and the outcome may differ, because "is it a fixture?" is an incomplete question. A fixture as between whom?

Buyer and seller: doubt is generally resolved in favor of the buyer — the item stays.

Landlord and tenant: doubt tends to favor the tenant, particularly where the item is a trade fixture.

Lender and borrower: doubt tends to favor the lender, whose security was measured by the property as it stood when the loan was made.

The spa itself is genuinely contested on the other tests — adaptation and wiring pull toward fixture, portability pulls away — which is why the relationship test carries more weight here than it would for a hardwired chandelier.

Problem 4

A restaurant tenant installed walk-in refrigeration, a commercial hood, a built bar, and a new roof. The lease is silent on removal. The tenant vacates on the last day of the term, returns three weeks later for the equipment, and finds the landlord has changed the locks.

Classify each item and assess the timing.

Solution. Trade fixtures: the refrigeration, the hood, and the bar — equipment the tenant attached for the conduct of its business, removable at or before the end of the tenancy with repair of the damage removal causes. The hood's removal damage may be substantial, and repairing it is the tenant's obligation, not a reason the fixture is not removable.

Not a trade fixture: the roof. That is a building improvement rather than trade equipment, and the distinction runs exactly there.

The timing is fatal. Removal happens during the tenancy or within the period the lease or law allows. A tenant who vacates and returns three weeks later has usually lost the right, and under accession the unremoved trade fixtures may become the landlord's property.

What transfers: this rule exists because businesses could not operate in leased space if their equipment became the landlord's on installation — and inattention converts that protection into a gift. A well-drafted lease addressing installation, removal, condition, and restoration makes the whole analysis unnecessary.

Problem 5

A licensee lists a 40-acre parcel with a mature walnut orchard, forty acres of tomatoes planted this spring, mature landscaping around the house, and solar panels on the roof. The seller mentions he plans to dig up several ornamental trees before closing.

Classify each item and identify what must be addressed.

Solution. The orchard is real property — perennial growth not requiring annual cultivation, conveying with the land.

The tomatoes are generally personal property, even while growing and attached, because an annual crop represents the cultivator's labor and investment for the season rather than a permanent characteristic of the land. This does not pass automatically the way the orchard does, and the agreement must address who owns the crop in the ground, who harvests it, and who bears the cost.

The mature landscaping is real property. A seller who removes it before closing has removed real property — a contract problem, not a matter of taste. Say so now, and name any specific plants he is keeping.

The solar panels require a question, not a classification. They are frequently subject to a lease or financing arrangement that does not convey automatically. A licensee who assumes they convey has skipped a question that changes both what the buyer receives and what the buyer owes.

Problem 6

A buyer is purchasing rural acreage intending to plant an orchard and build a house. The preliminary report shows a reservation of mineral rights to a third party. The listing agent tells him mineral reservations are standard in the area and nothing to worry about, and that the well "has always been fine."

Assess both statements and list the questions that should have been asked.

Solution. Both statements are outside the license, and the mineral one is a legal opinion on a document neither of them has read.

A severed mineral interest stays severed through subsequent transfers, and the mineral estate may carry surface access rights that affect the surface owner's use — which matters a great deal to a buyer planning an orchard and a house. Whether and how is a legal question. The licensee's role is to ensure the buyer sees the exception, obtains the underlying document, and gets it reviewed before his contingency expires.

The water questions belong at the listing appointment: what is the source — municipal, well, shared well, mutual water company, district? If a well, what is its documented production and quality, and when was it tested? Is any water right appurtenant to the parcel and documented? Is the property within a groundwater management area? Are there shared-facility agreements — a shared well, a pipeline, an access easement? And is the supply sufficient for the buyer's intended use, which here includes irrigating an orchard.

What transfers: for every topic in this area the response is the same three steps — recognize, ask and obtain, refer. "It has always been fine" is not a documented answer to any of the six questions.

Problem 7

At the listing appointment a seller mentions that the dining room chandelier was her grandmother's and she will be taking it. The licensee nods, photographs the room with the chandelier in it, and lists the property. Two days before closing the buyer's agent asks about it.

Trace the failures.

Solution. Three, and they compound.

The seller told the licensee exactly what to name in the contract, and nothing was written. "Anything the seller has mentioned intending to take" is at the top of the naming list precisely because sellers volunteer it.

The photograph made it worse. Photographs are advertising and are held to the truthfulness standard. The buyer has been shown something he is not getting — and where staging or photography includes items that are not conveying, the marketing should say so rather than leaving it for the walkthrough.

The timing is now the worst possible. With two days to closing the parties have maximum pressure and minimum flexibility, over an item that five minutes at the listing appointment would have settled.

The conversation that prevents it: "Before we photograph, walk me through anything you're planning to take — light fixtures, appliances, anything mounted, anything with sentimental value. I'd rather name it now than have it come up two days before closing."

Problem 8

At the final verification the buyer finds a named built-in wine refrigerator has been removed. His agent proposes instructing escrow to hold $4,000 and closing on schedule; the listing agent says the item was "obviously personal property anyway."

State the correct sequence and the licensee's limit.

Solution. The item was named, which ends the fixture argument entirely — agreement overrides the tests, and the listing agent's classification opinion is both irrelevant and outside the license.

The sequence: document the finding with photographs and dated notes; address it through the contract before closing, because the ordinary leverage to resolve it disappears once the transaction closes; do not instruct escrow to hold funds, since escrow acts only on mutual written instructions; and do not close intending to pursue the seller afterward, which converts a solvable issue into a claim.

Where an item is genuinely disputed and the contract is silent, the licensee states what the contract says, documents both positions, and escalates. Whether a particular item was a fixture is a legal conclusion, however clear it seems. The honest position: "The contract doesn't name it, which means this comes down to how the law classifies it — that's a question for counsel, and here's what I can do in the meantime."

Chapter summary

The classification decides transfer, recording, description, security interests, taxation, and what conveys. Real property transfers by deed and conveys unless excluded; personal property transfers by bill of sale and does not convey unless included — and those opposite defaults are why ordinary disputes arise between reasonable people.

Items move between categories by annexation and severance, and severance can occur on paper without anything physically moving.

Classification is made at a moment in time — when the deed was delivered, when the lien attached, when the contract was signed.

MARIA lists the tests; intention generally decides them, with the others serving largely as evidence of an invisible fact. Agreement overrides everything.

Relationship matters: doubt tends to favor the buyer against a seller, the tenant against a landlord, and the lender against a borrower. "Is it a fixture?" is incomplete without "as between whom?"

Trade fixtures are the tenant's business equipment, removable at or before the end of the tenancy with repair of damage — and lost to accession if left. Building improvements are not trade fixtures, and a well-drafted lease makes the analysis unnecessary.

Perennial growth is real property; annual crops are generally personal property, which must be addressed expressly in agricultural transactions. Removing mature landscaping before closing removes real property.

Solar panels require a question rather than an assumption, because a lease or financing arrangement may not convey with the property.

Water and mineral interests are specialist areas where the licensee's job is to recognize, ask and obtain, and refer. Ask the six water questions on rural property, and never characterize a severed mineral interest as routine.

Do not resolve fixture disputes — prevent them. Run the intake conversation, name the disputable items, keep marketing consistent with what conveys, and at the walkthrough document, address it before closing, and leave the legal conclusion to counsel.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Classify each item in dispute and state which test drives your analysis, identify how the relationship of the parties affects the outcome, state what the agreement should have said and draft that language, identify anything you would refer to counsel or to a specialist, and state the evidence you would create and retain. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 03

Estates, ownership, and common interests

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Distinguish freehold from leasehold estates and identify the leasehold types by how each terminates.
  • Explain fee simple and life estates, and identify who holds what interest during and after a life estate.
  • Distinguish joint tenancy from tenancy in common by their operative differences, and explain what severance does.
  • Describe California community property at the level a licensee must recognize, and identify what belongs to counsel.
  • Explain how a common interest development is organized and what a buyer must review before the contingency expires.
  • Verify authority to convey where title is held in a trust or by an entity.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: estates and their duration55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: concurrent ownership and community property50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: common interest developments, trusts, and entities45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Freehold and leasehold estates

1.1 What an estate is

An estate is the nature and extent of a person's interest in land. It answers two questions: what rights does the holder have, and for how long?

The primary division is by duration:

DurationNature
Freehold estateIndefinite — potentially forever, or for a lifeAn interest in the land itself
Leasehold estateA fixed or terminable periodA right of possession, with the owner retaining the reversion

The practical difference: a freehold holder owns; a leasehold holder possesses. Both are real property interests, and both can be bought, sold, and encumbered.

1.2 The four leasehold estates

Leaseholds are classified by how they end, which is the useful way to hold them.

EstateDurationHow it ends
Estate for yearsA definite period stated in the leaseAutomatically, at the stated end. No notice required.
Periodic estateRenews period to period — month to monthBy notice from either party, per the required period
Estate at willIndefinite, with the consent of bothWhen either party ends it, subject to statutory notice
Estate at sufferanceThe tenant holds over after the right endsBy the landlord's action; the tenant is a holdover, not a trespasser

The name misleads on the first one. An "estate for years" is defined by having a definite ending date, not by lasting years. A six-month lease and a ten-day lease are both estates for years.

1.3 The distinctions that matter in practice

Estate for years versus periodic estate: notice. An estate for years expires on its stated date without anyone giving notice. A periodic tenancy continues until someone gives the required notice. A licensee who advises a landlord that a fixed-term lease "needs a 30-day notice to end" has described the wrong estate.

Estate at sufferance is not trespass. The holdover tenant entered lawfully. That is why the landlord's remedy is a legal process rather than self-help, and why changing the locks is unlawful no matter how clear the tenant's obligation to leave.

A leasehold survives a sale. The buyer of a leased property takes subject to the lease on its terms. This is why existing leases and their terms are an intake item, and why "the tenant will be out by closing" needs to be verified against the lease rather than accepted.

1.4 The reversion

When a leasehold ends, possession returns to the owner. That future interest is the reversion, and it is what the landlord holds during the tenancy.

The landlord's estate is not suspended by the lease. It is a fee simple subject to a leasehold, which is why the landlord can sell, mortgage, and devise it while the tenant is in possession.

1.5 Why the estate concept controls everything downstream

A principles course opens with estates because the estate held determines what can be done with the property, and every later topic assumes the answer.

What the estate determinesWhere it surfaces later
What can be conveyedA seller cannot convey more than he holds. A life tenant conveying "the property" conveys a life estate measured by his life, and the buyer finds out later.
What can be encumberedA lender's security is limited by the estate pledged, which is why a lender's title requirements probe the estate rather than accepting a description.
Who must signConcurrent owners, spouses, trustees, and entity signatories — Sections 3, 4, and 6 of this lesson are all versions of this one question.
What happens at deathSurvivorship, probate, or the terms of an instrument. The difference between two forms of concurrent ownership is invisible on the property and total in effect.
What a creditor can reachWhich affects who is willing to lend and on what terms.
What the occupant may doAlter, encumber, lease, or let deteriorate — the life tenant's limits in Section 2.4 are the clearest case.
How long it lastsPerpetual, for a life, for a term, or until a condition occurs.

Two conclusions.

The most consequential fact about a property is frequently invisible. Two identical houses on the same street can be held under estates with completely different consequences for conveyance, financing, and inheritance, and nothing about the property reveals it. The estate is established from documents, which is why the intake practice in Section 6.5 asks for them rather than for a description.

"Who owns it?" is the wrong question. The professional question is "what interest is held, by whom, and who has authority to convey it?" — three questions, frequently with three different answers, and a transaction that assumes they collapse into one is the transaction that fails at title clearance.

The failure this prevents. A listing taken from a person who describes himself as the owner, marketed, and put under contract — after which title clearance reveals a co-owner, a life estate, a trust with a successor trustee requirement, or an entity whose authority document says something other than what the signer said. Every one of those is discoverable at intake with a question and a document, and every one of them is expensive at day nineteen.

1.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to name a leasehold estate from a description of how it terminates, and state what happens to a lease when the property is sold.

2. Fee simple and life estates

2.1 Fee simple absolute

Fee simple absolute is the greatest estate the law recognizes: ownership of indefinite duration, freely transferable during life and at death, with the full bundle of rights.

It is the default. A deed conveying property without limiting language conveys a fee simple absolute, and most residential transactions transfer exactly this.

Fee simple is still subject to the four government powers taken up in Lesson 06, and to private restrictions taken up in Lesson 05. "Absolute" describes the estate's duration and transferability, not freedom from regulation.

2.2 Defeasible fees

A fee can be granted subject to a condition, so that it may end if the condition is broken. These are called defeasible fees, and California practice encounters them mainly in older chains of title and in conveyances to public or charitable uses.

The practical point for a licensee is narrow and important: a deed containing conditional language is a title matter, not a curiosity. It goes to title and to counsel, and the buyer needs to understand it before the contingency expires. Whether a condition is enforceable, and what happens if it is broken, is a legal question.

2.3 Life estates

A life estate is ownership measured by a lifetime rather than by a fixed period.

PartyInterest
Life tenantOwns and possesses for the measuring life
RemaindermanTakes when the measuring life ends, where the grantor named a third party
Reversion holderTakes when the measuring life ends, where the interest returns to the grantor

A variant worth naming: a life estate pur autre vie is measured by the life of someone other than the holder. The holder may die first, in which case the estate passes to the holder's successors until the measuring life ends.

2.4 What a life tenant may and may not do

May: possess and use the property, receive its income, and transfer whatever interest they hold — which is the life estate, and no more.

May not: commit waste — conduct that permanently impairs the property to the detriment of the remainderman. Neglecting maintenance, removing valuable timber, or allowing the property to deteriorate can all be waste.

Must generally: maintain the property, pay taxes and ordinary carrying charges, and preserve the remainderman's interest.

2.5 The transaction problem

A life tenant can only sell what they own, and what they own ends when the measuring life ends. A buyer purchasing from a life tenant alone receives an interest that terminates on someone else's death.

The professional response: where title shows a life estate, a conveyance in fee requires the life tenant and the remainderman or reversion holder to join. This is an authority question of exactly the kind Lesson 01 described, and it is verified against the instrument, not assumed.

A licensee who takes a listing from a life tenant without identifying the remainderman has taken a listing that cannot deliver a fee.

2.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to say who must sign to convey a fee where a life estate exists, and explain what waste is.

3. Joint tenancy and tenancy in common

3.1 Concurrent ownership

Where two or more people own the same property at the same time, they hold it concurrently. California's principal forms are tenancy in common, joint tenancy, and the community property forms taken up in Section 4.

3.2 The operative comparison

Tenancy in commonJoint tenancy
SharesMay be unequalEqual
Right of survivorshipNoYes
On deathThe share passes by will or intestate successionThe interest passes to the surviving joint tenants
Transferable during lifeYes, freelyYes — but transfer severs the joint tenancy as to that interest
CreationThe default where two or more take without specifyingRequires express language creating it
Number of ownersAny numberTwo or more

The survivorship difference is the whole point, and it is what makes vesting an estate planning question rather than a form selection.

3.3 The four unities

Joint tenancy traditionally requires four unities, conventionally remembered as PITT:

  • Possession — each holds an undivided right to possess the whole.
  • Interest — equal interests.
  • Time — the interests vested at the same time.
  • Title — the interests arose from the same instrument.

Tenancy in common requires only unity of possession. That single requirement is why tenants in common may hold unequal shares acquired at different times from different sources.

3.4 Severance

A joint tenant who transfers their interest during life severs the joint tenancy as to that interest, which becomes a tenancy in common.

Three joint tenants each hold a one-third interest. One sells to an outsider.

The buyer holds a one-third interest as a tenant in common. The remaining two continue as joint tenants between themselves as to their two-thirds, with survivorship operating between them and not as to the outsider's third.

Severance is a recurring examination topic and a recurring practical surprise, because the transferring joint tenant has changed the survivorship expectations of people who did not agree to the change.

3.5 Partition

Where concurrent owners cannot agree, any of them may generally seek partition — a court-supervised division of the property, either physically or by sale with division of the proceeds.

This is a legal remedy, and a licensee's role is to recognize it as the answer to "we can't agree what to do with the house" and refer it. Licensees are frequently asked to take a listing where the co-owners disagree; the authority question must be answered before the listing is signed, not during escrow.

3.6 What the licensee actually does

Vesting has ownership, survivorship, tax, and estate consequences, and advising which to choose is outside the license. The licensee's obligations are:

  1. Explain that the choice matters and that changing it later is neither free nor simple.
  2. Refer to legal and tax counsel before escrow prepares the deed.
  3. At intake on a listing, confirm how title is held and who must sign — against the record, not from the seller's description.

Two unmarried siblings say they want the survivor to take the whole property automatically, and each to be able to leave her share to her own children.

Those goals point in different directions. Survivorship and the ability to devise a share are not both satisfied by a single form. Resolving that tension is estate planning, and it belongs to counsel — not to the licensee, and not to escrow's deadline for the deed instruction.

3.7 A worked ownership intake

Six listing appointments in one week. At each, the licensee asks the Section 6.5 questions: how is title currently held, may I see the document, and who must sign to convey?

1. "My wife and I own it." Two people, married, and the form of ownership is a question with several possible answers — Section 4.2's concepts are engaged, and the answer affects survivorship, creditors, and taxation. The licensee does not advise on which form is better and does not assume which one applies. He obtains the vesting from the record.

2. "It's in my trust." Section 6.2 governs. The trustee conveys, and the question is who currently holds that office — which the trust instrument answers and the seller's description does not. A successor trustee acting after a death or incapacity is a different situation with its own documentary requirements. The licensee obtains the documentation the title company and counsel will require, and routes the legal questions.

3. "My brother and I inherited it." Two owners, and the form of their concurrent ownership determines what happens if one dies before closing and whether one can act alone. Both sign. How the property passed to them — by will, by trust, by operation of law — may also determine whether an administration is required, which is a legal question with a timeline attached to it.

4. "It's owned by our LLC." Section 6.3. The entity conveys, through whoever its governing documents authorize, and the authority is established from the documents rather than from the person's title on a business card. A member is not automatically authorized.

5. "I have power of attorney for my father." Section 6.4. The instrument's scope is what matters — whether it reaches real property, whether it is currently effective, and whether any conditions on it have been satisfied. This is a legal question and it goes to counsel and the title company early, because a defect discovered at closing cannot be repaired at closing. And where the principal's capacity is in question, Lesson 15 of the practice course applies: speak with the principal directly, document what you observed, and escalate.

6. "I bought it with my partner but it's really mine — she just helped with the loan." Two different claims: what the record says and what the seller believes the arrangement was. The record controls who must sign. What the parties intended between themselves is a legal question the licensee does not resolve, and a listing taken on the seller's characterization is a listing that will fail.

What the week produced. Six appointments, six documents requested, two matters routed to counsel before listing. None of the six sellers described their situation inaccurately on purpose. Each described it the way people describe ownership in conversation, which is by relationship rather than by interest — and Section 1.5's point is that those are not the same thing.

3.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to state the operative differences between the two forms, explain what severance does to a three-party joint tenancy, and say what you would do when a client asks which to choose.

4. Community property orientation

4.1 What the system is

California is a community property state. In outline, property acquired by a married person during marriage is generally community property, and property acquired before marriage or by gift or inheritance during marriage is generally that spouse's separate property.

This section is an orientation, not a guide. The rules governing characterization, transmutation, commingled assets, and dissolution are complex, fact-dependent, and squarely legal. What a licensee needs is enough to recognize the issue at intake and to route it correctly.

4.2 The three concepts to hold

Community property. Generally, property acquired during marriage other than by gift or inheritance. Each spouse generally holds an equal interest.

Separate property. Generally, property owned before marriage, or acquired during marriage by gift, devise, or inheritance — together with the rents, issues, and profits of separate property.

Community property with right of survivorship. A form California recognizes that combines community property characterization with survivorship. It has both tax and estate consequences, and whether it fits a particular couple is a question for their advisors.

4.3 The intake question that matters

The licensee's operative concern is authority to convey.

Where community property is involved, both spouses generally must join in a conveyance or encumbrance of real property.

That single proposition drives the intake practice:

  1. Ask marital status as part of confirming authority, in the same neutral way as every other intake question.
  2. Check how title is held against the record.
  3. Where the record and the answer do not reconcile, stop and escalate rather than proceeding.
  4. Where a spouse is not on title but may have an interest, that is a title and legal question — route it to title and to counsel before the listing is signed.

A listing signed by one spouse where the other has an interest is the same category of failure as a listing signed by a beneficiary rather than a trustee: it cannot deliver what it promises, and it surfaces in escrow where the cost is highest.

4.4 Dissolution and separation

Where a marriage is being dissolved, authority to convey may be affected by court orders, and the timing of a sale may be constrained. A licensee working on a sale during a dissolution should expect that counsel is already involved and should coordinate rather than improvise.

What the licensee must not do is advise either spouse on their rights, characterize property as community or separate, or predict how a court will divide it. Each of those is a legal conclusion.

4.5 A note on neutrality

Questions about marital status at intake exist to establish authority to convey. They are asked of every client in the same way, recorded, and used for that purpose. A licensee who asks selectively, or who uses the answer for anything other than the authority analysis, has created a fair housing problem alongside the compliance one.

4.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to state the general rule about joining in a conveyance, and describe what you do when the record and the client's description of title do not match.

5. Condominiums and common interest developments

5.1 What a common interest development is

A common interest development combines individually owned interests with a shared interest in common areas, governed by a set of recorded documents and administered by an association.

The recurring forms:

FormWhat the owner owns
CondominiumAn individual unit — often defined by interior surfaces — plus an undivided interest in the common areas
Planned developmentA separate lot, plus membership in an association owning or managing common areas
Stock cooperativeShares in a corporation owning the building, plus a right of occupancy
Community apartmentAn undivided interest in the whole, plus an exclusive right to occupy a unit

The differences matter for financing, for what is being conveyed, and for how the owner's obligations arise.

5.2 The governing documents

Ownership in a common interest development comes with a body of private law the buyer is agreeing to be bound by:

  • The declaration — recorded covenants, conditions, and restrictions establishing the scheme and the use restrictions.
  • Bylaws — how the association operates and is governed.
  • Rules and regulations — adopted by the association, and easier to change than the declaration.
  • The budget and reserve study — what the association plans to spend and what it has set aside.
  • Meeting minutes — where problems appear before they appear anywhere else.
  • Financial statements and assessment history.
  • Pending or threatened litigation, including construction defect claims.

5.3 Why this belongs in a principles course

Because the documents impose obligations that bind the buyer regardless of zoning and regardless of what the seller told him:

Use restrictions. Whether he may rent the unit, and on what terms. Whether he may keep animals. What he may do to the exterior, the patio, or the windows. Whether he may run a business from it.

Assessments. The regular assessment, and the association's power to levy special assessments — which can be substantial and which a buyer inherits.

Reserves. An association with inadequate reserves against known future work has a special assessment in its future, whether or not one has been declared.

Litigation. Construction defect litigation affecting the building is a material fact, and it can also affect the availability of financing.

5.4 The timing point

The buyer must have the documents in time to review them within the contingency period — which means requesting them promptly rather than late.

This is one of the slow items. An association that takes three weeks to produce documents will destroy a fourteen-day review contingency that was ordered on day ten. A licensee who orders the documents at the start has given the buyer a real review; one who orders them late has given him a formality.

5.5 What a licensee does and does not do

Does: identify that the property is in a common interest development; order or ensure the documents are ordered immediately; confirm the buyer received them; recommend he read the declaration, the budget and reserves, the minutes, and any litigation disclosure; and document the recommendation.

Does not: summarize the declaration for the buyer, characterize the reserves as adequate, predict whether a special assessment will be levied, or advise whether litigation is serious. Each of those exceeds the license and invites reliance.

A buyer asks: "Are the reserves okay?"

Correct: "That's outside what I can assess, and it's exactly what to have reviewed before your contingency expires — the reserve study and the budget are in the packet, and a CPA or your attorney can tell you what they show. I'll note that we discussed it."

Incorrect: "They look fine to me — this association seems well run."

5.6 What a buyer in a common interest development is actually buying

Section 5.3 explains why this belongs in a principles course. This section makes the point concrete, because the gap between what a buyer thinks he is buying and what he is buying is wider here than anywhere else in residential real estate.

The buyer is acquiring four things at once:

  1. An interest in a unit or a lot, defined by an instrument rather than by the walls he can see. What is his, what is common, and what is exclusive-use common area is a documentary question, and it determines who repairs what.
  2. An undivided interest in common area, with its condition, its deferred maintenance, and its future costs.
  3. Membership in an association, with governing documents that bind him and a board that can act.
  4. A share of an ongoing financial obligation — regular assessments, and special assessments that have not happened yet.

What the documents answer, and what each one is for:

The documentThe question it answers
Declaration and restrictionsWhat may and may not be done — rentals, pets, alterations, business use, parking, occupancy
Bylaws and rulesHow the association operates and what it may enforce
Budget and reserve studyWhether the association can pay for what is coming — the single most predictive document in the package
Financial statementsPresent condition, delinquency rates, and whether assessments are being collected
MinutesWhat the board has been discussing, which is frequently where a future special assessment first appears
Insurance informationWhat the association covers and, more importantly, what it does not
Litigation disclosuresPending matters, which can affect both value and financing availability

Three points a licensee should make to every buyer, in writing.

Order the documents on day one. Section 5.4's timing point. The buyer's review period is what remains after delivery, and a package ordered on day six against a seven-day delivery obligation leaves a buyer reading hundreds of pages in a weekend.

Read the reserve study and the minutes. Buyers read the pet rules and skip these two. A thinly funded reserve is a special assessment that has not been announced yet, and the minutes are where it is visible first.

Confirm what financing requires. Some programs impose conditions on the development itself — owner-occupancy ratios, litigation status, budget requirements — and a buyer can be approved while the development is not.

Section 5.5's limit governs all of it. The licensee delivers the documents, recommends they be reviewed with counsel, and does not interpret the restrictions. Whether a provision permits a particular use is a legal question about a recorded instrument, and it is asked of licensees constantly.

5.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to name what a buyer must review, explain why the documents must be ordered early, and answer a reserves question correctly.

6. Trust and entity ownership basics

6.1 Why this section exists

Because the most common and most expensive intake failure in residential practice is unverified authority to convey, and trusts and entities are where it happens.

The failure has a consistent shape: someone with an obvious connection to the property — the occupant, the person paying the taxes, a beneficiary, an officer — signs a listing they have no authority to sign. It surfaces in escrow, where unwinding costs the most.

6.2 Trust ownership

Where title is held in a trust, the trustee holds legal title and may convey only within the authority the trust document grants.

PartyRole
Trustor / settlorCreated the trust and transferred property into it
TrusteeHolds legal title and administers the trust; signs
BeneficiaryHolds the beneficial interest; does not sign

What must be verified at intake:

  1. Who the current trustee is — trustees change, and the person who was trustee three years ago may not be.
  2. That the trust authorizes sale of real property.
  3. What the trust requires of the trustee in doing so — some require consents or notices.
  4. That title is actually held in the trust, against the record.

Occupancy, payment of taxes, and beneficial interest are each evidence of connection to the property, and none establishes authority to sell it.

6.3 Entity ownership

Where title is held by a corporation, limited liability company, partnership, or other entity, authority comes from the entity's governing documents and from whatever authorization those documents require.

The verification questions:

  • Does the entity exist and is it in good standing?
  • Who is authorized to act for it — and does the governing document require a resolution, a manager's consent, or the signature of more than one person?
  • Does the authorization cover this transaction, including its terms?

A signature block reading "President" is not verification. It is a claim to be verified against the entity's documents.

6.4 Other authority situations

Probate and decedents' estates. The personal representative acts within what the appointment permits, and the process may require court involvement. Verify the appointment.

Conservatorship and guardianship. Authority comes from the court order, which may limit what may be done and may require approval.

Power of attorney. The instrument must currently be valid and must expressly authorize conveyance of real property. A general power of attorney signed years ago is not self-evidently sufficient, and whether it is, is a legal question.

Co-owners. All of them, unless the record or an instrument establishes otherwise.

6.5 The intake practice

Verify authority against a source at intake, and stop and escalate where it is unclear rather than postponing the question.

Ten minutes with the trust document, the operating agreement, or the letters of appointment at the listing appointment prevents a failure that would otherwise appear in escrow with an accepted offer, a buyer with a loan approval, and no way to deliver title.

And where the answer is genuinely unclear, the licensee's role is not to resolve it. It is to route it to title and to counsel before the listing is signed.

6.6 Trust ownership and entity ownership, compared

Sections 6.2 and 6.3 of Lesson 03 introduce these. The comparison is worth setting out directly, because a licensee will meet both constantly and the questions they raise are structurally similar and practically different.

Trust ownershipEntity ownership
Who holds titleThe trustee, in that capacityThe entity itself
Who signsThe currently serving trustee — which may not be the person who created the trustWhoever the entity's governing documents authorize
What establishes authorityThe trust instrument and, where required, the documentation the title company acceptsThe formation documents, the operating or governing agreement, and any resolution required
The common failureAssuming the person describing it as "my trust" is the acting trustee — after a death or incapacity the office may have passedAssuming a member, officer, or partner is authorized because of the title he uses
What changes on deathDepends on the instrument; a successor may take office, with its own documentary requirementsDepends on the governing documents and the interest held
Where it goes wrong in a transactionAt title clearance, typically lateAt title clearance, typically late
Whose question it isCounsel and the title companyCounsel and the title company

The two rows that matter most to a licensee are the last two, and they are identical.

Both fail at the same point — when the title company asks for evidence of authority and the documents do not say what the seller said they say. By then the property has been marketed, an offer accepted, and a buyer's deadlines are running.

Both are resolved by the same intake practice. Section 6.5 of Lesson 03: ask how title is held, ask to see the document, and ask who must sign. Three questions, asked before the listing agreement, and routed to counsel and the title company when the answer is not immediately clear from the document.

What a licensee must never do with either. Advise which structure to use, advise on whether to move property into or out of one, interpret an instrument's provisions, or conclude from a person's description that he has authority. Each is a legal determination, and the last one is the one that feels like ordinary listening rather than like a legal conclusion.

A useful framing for the client conversation. "The title company is going to need to see the document that shows who can sign. Let's get it now — if there's anything in it that needs attention, today is a much better day to find out than three weeks into escrow." Clients accept this immediately, because it is obviously true and it is offered as service rather than as suspicion.

6.7 The intake questions, and why each one exists

Section 6.5 supplies the intake practice. Each question earns its place by preventing a specific, recurring failure.

The questionThe failure it prevents
"How is title currently held?"A listing taken from someone whose interest is not what he described — a life tenant, a co-owner, a beneficiary rather than a trustee
"May I see the document?"The gap between what a person says an instrument provides and what it provides. The single most valuable question in this lesson
"Who must sign to convey?"A transaction that reaches title clearance and discovers a required signature nobody obtained
"Is anyone else on title, or claiming an interest?"Unrecorded interests, family arrangements, and the person who "helped with the loan"
"Has anyone died, been divorced, or lost capacity since acquisition?"Every event that changes who may act, and none of which is visible in the property
"Is anyone in possession other than you?"Tenancies, family occupancy, and the rights that come with them
"Is there any pending matter affecting the property?"Litigation, liens, association proceedings, and code matters

Two properties of this list are worth noticing.

None of the questions requires legal knowledge to ask, and none of the answers is evaluated by the licensee. The entire value is in surfacing the issue and routing it, which Sections 6.2 through 6.4 establish for each ownership form.

Every question is cheap now and expensive later. Asked at the listing appointment, each produces at most a document request and occasionally a referral. Discovered at day nineteen of an escrow, each is a transaction in trouble — with a buyer's deadlines running, a lender's timeline committed, and a seller who has already made plans.

What the licensee records: what was asked, what was answered, what document was produced, and what was routed to whom. Two minutes at the appointment, and it is the record that shows the question was asked — which matters, because a seller who gave an incomplete answer and a licensee who never asked look identical in a file with no note.

The framing that makes clients cooperative rather than defensive. The questions are not about them. They are about what the title company will require, which is true, verifiable, and obviously in the seller's interest — and a seller told "let's get this now so it isn't a problem in escrow" produces the document without being asked twice.

6.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to list what you would verify at a listing appointment where title is held in a trust, and explain why a beneficiary's signature is insufficient.

Key terms

TermDefinition
EstateThe nature and extent of a person's interest in land — what rights, and for how long.
Freehold estateAn interest of indefinite duration in the land itself.
Leasehold estateA right of possession for a fixed or terminable period, with the owner retaining the reversion.
Estate for yearsA leasehold with a definite ending date, ending automatically without notice. Not defined by lasting years.
Periodic estateA leasehold renewing period to period, ending only on the required notice.
Estate at willA leasehold of indefinite duration held with both parties' consent, subject to statutory notice.
Estate at sufferanceA tenant holding over after the right ends. A holdover, not a trespasser — which is why self-help is unlawful.
ReversionThe owner's future right to possession when a lesser estate ends.
Fee simple absoluteThe greatest estate: indefinite duration, freely transferable, full bundle of rights. The default a deed conveys without limiting language.
Defeasible feeA fee subject to a condition that may end it. Conditional language in a deed is a title matter for counsel, not a curiosity.
Life estateOwnership measured by a lifetime. The life tenant may transfer only what they hold.
Pur autre vieA life estate measured by the life of someone other than the holder.
RemaindermanThe party taking when the measuring life ends, where a third party was named.
WasteConduct by a life tenant permanently impairing the property to the remainderman's detriment.
Tenancy in commonConcurrent ownership with possibly unequal shares, no survivorship, and shares passing by will or intestate succession. The default where two or more take without specifying.
Joint tenancyConcurrent ownership with equal shares and a right of survivorship, requiring express language to create.
PITTThe four unities of joint tenancy: Possession, Interest, Time, Title. Tenancy in common requires possession alone.
SeveranceA joint tenant's lifetime transfer, converting that interest to a tenancy in common while survivorship continues among the others.
PartitionA court-supervised division of concurrently owned property, physically or by sale. A legal remedy.
Community propertyGenerally, property acquired by a married person during marriage other than by gift or inheritance. Both spouses generally must join in a conveyance or encumbrance.
Separate propertyGenerally, property owned before marriage or acquired during it by gift, devise, or inheritance, with its rents, issues, and profits.
Common interest developmentIndividually owned interests plus a shared interest in common areas, governed by recorded documents and administered by an association.
Declaration (CC&Rs)The recorded covenants, conditions, and restrictions establishing the scheme and its use restrictions. Binds the buyer regardless of zoning.
Special assessmentAn assessment beyond the regular one, which the buyer inherits and which inadequate reserves foreshadow.
TrusteeThe holder of legal title under a trust, who signs — and only within the authority the trust document grants.
BeneficiaryThe holder of the beneficial interest, who does not sign.
Verified authorityConfirmation against a source — the trust document, the operating agreement, the letters of appointment, the record — rather than inference from occupancy, tax payment, or a title in a signature block.

Practice problems

Problem 1

Classify each and state how it ends: (a) a twelve-month lease expiring March 31; (b) a month-to-month tenancy; (c) a tenant who stayed past March 31 without a new agreement; (d) an occupancy both parties may end at any time. A landlord asks whether he must give thirty days' notice to end (a).

Answer.

Solution. (a) An estate for years — defined by a definite ending date, not by lasting years; a six-month lease and a ten-day lease are both estates for years. It ends automatically on March 31 without notice. So the landlord's question is answered no, and a licensee who tells him a 30-day notice is required has described a different estate.

(b) A periodic estate, which continues until someone gives the required notice.

(c) An estate at sufferance. The tenant entered lawfully and is a holdover, not a trespasser — which is exactly why the remedy is a legal process and why changing the locks is unlawful however clear the obligation to leave.

(d) An estate at will, subject to statutory notice.

Problem 2

A buyer is purchasing a fourplex. Three units are on twelve-month leases with eight months remaining; the seller says the tenants "will be out by closing."

What does the buyer take, and what should the licensee do?

Solution. A leasehold survives a sale. The buyer takes subject to the leases on their terms — the leases do not end because the property changed hands, and the buyer steps into the landlord's position with a reversion eight months out.

The seller's statement is a representation to be verified against the leases, not accepted. Ask for the leases themselves, the deposits held and their amounts, any notices already given, and any written agreement to vacate. "They'll be out" is not a lease term.

What transfers: existing leases and their terms are an intake item on any occupied property, because they determine what is actually being conveyed.

Problem 3

A licensee takes a listing from a man who has lived in and maintained a house for eleven years. Title shows a life estate in him, with a remainder to his niece.

What can he convey, and who must sign?

Solution. He can convey only what he owns, which is an interest that terminates when he dies. A buyer purchasing from him alone receives an estate that ends on someone else's death — which is not what any residential buyer thinks he is buying.

To convey a fee, the life tenant and the remainderman must both join. The niece is not a formality; she holds a present interest in the property's future.

What transfers: this is the Lesson 01 authority question in another costume. Verify against the instrument, and a licensee who takes this listing without identifying the remainderman has taken a listing that cannot deliver a fee.

Also worth knowing while he holds it: the life tenant must not commit waste — neglecting maintenance, removing valuable timber, or allowing deterioration to the remainderman's detriment.

Problem 4

Three siblings hold a property as joint tenants, each with a one-third interest. One sells her interest to an outside investor.

Describe the resulting ownership.

Solution. The sale severs the joint tenancy as to that interest.

The investor holds a one-third interest as a tenant in common. He has no survivorship rights, and none run against him.

The two remaining siblings continue as joint tenants between themselves as to their combined two-thirds, with survivorship operating between them only.

Why this is a recurring surprise: the transferring joint tenant unilaterally changed the survivorship expectations of two people who never agreed to the change. Nothing required their consent, and nothing notified them.

If the three later cannot agree what to do with the property, any of them may generally seek partition — a court-supervised division physically or by sale. That is a legal remedy to recognize and refer, and the authority question must be settled before a listing is signed rather than during escrow.

Problem 5

Two unmarried siblings buying together tell the licensee they want the survivor to take the whole property automatically, and each to be able to leave her share to her own children. Escrow needs the vesting instruction tomorrow.

Respond.

Solution. Name the tension and refer it — do not choose.

Those two goals point in different directions. Survivorship means the surviving owner takes; the ability to devise a share means each can direct her interest elsewhere. Joint tenancy delivers the first and defeats the second; tenancy in common delivers the second and defeats the first. No single form satisfies both, and resolving it is estate planning, not form selection.

The licensee may explain that the choice matters and carries ownership, survivorship, tax, and estate consequences, and that changing it later is neither free nor simple. The licensee may not advise which to choose.

The referral goes to legal and tax counsel before escrow prepares the deed — and escrow's deadline is a scheduling pressure, not a reason to answer a question outside the license.

Problem 6

A married man signs a listing alone. He says the house was bought during the marriage but title is in his name only, that his wife "has nothing to do with it," and that they are in the middle of a dissolution. He asks the licensee whether the house is community property and what his wife is entitled to.

What does the licensee do?

Solution. Stop, verify, and escalate — and answer neither question.

The operative concern is authority to convey. Where community property is involved, both spouses generally must join in a conveyance or encumbrance of real property, and title in one name does not resolve whether the other has an interest. Property acquired during marriage other than by gift or inheritance is generally community property — but characterization is fact-dependent and squarely legal.

What the licensee does: confirm how title is held against the record; where the record and the client's description do not reconcile, stop rather than proceeding; and route the interest question to title and to counsel before the listing is signed. In a dissolution, expect that authority may be affected by court orders and that counsel is already involved — coordinate rather than improvise.

What the licensee must not do: characterize property as community or separate, advise either spouse on their rights, or predict how a court will divide it. All three are legal conclusions.

And a note on how the question is asked: marital status is an intake question about authority, asked of every client the same way and used only for that purpose. Asking selectively creates a fair housing problem alongside the compliance one.

What transfers: a listing signed by one spouse where the other has an interest is the same failure as a listing signed by a beneficiary rather than a trustee — it cannot deliver what it promises, and it surfaces in escrow where the cost is highest.

Problem 7

A buyer is fourteen days into a review contingency on a condominium. The association documents were ordered on day ten and have not arrived. When they do, the buyer asks his agent whether the reserves are adequate and whether the construction defect litigation mentioned in the minutes is serious.

Assess the timing and answer the questions.

Solution. The timing failure came first. Association documents are a slow item and belong at the front of the queue. An association taking three weeks to produce a packet destroys a fourteen-day contingency ordered on day ten. Ordered at the start, the buyer gets a real review; ordered late, he gets a formality.

Neither question may be answered. "That's outside what I can assess, and it's exactly what to have reviewed before your contingency expires — the reserve study and budget are in the packet, and a CPA or your attorney can tell you what they show. The litigation disclosure is the same: it's a legal question, and it can also affect financing availability. I'll note that we discussed both."

Saying "they look fine to me, this association seems well run" characterizes reserves the licensee cannot evaluate, on which the buyer will rely.

What he must review: the declaration and its use restrictions — rental limits, animals, exteriors, business use — which bind him regardless of zoning; the budget and reserves, since inadequate reserves against known work mean a special assessment in the future whether or not one has been declared; the minutes, where problems appear first; the assessment history; and any pending or threatened litigation.

Problem 8

A woman calls to list her late mother's house. She has lived there twelve years, pays the taxes, and is one of three beneficiaries of the trust that holds title. She produces a trust document from 2011 naming her mother as trustee. Separately, an LLC-owned property is offered by a man whose signature block reads "President."

State what must be verified in each.

Solution. Neither person has yet shown authority to convey.

On the trust: the trustee signs, and only within the authority the trust document grants. A beneficiary does not sign, however many there are and however clearly they benefit. Occupancy, payment of taxes, and beneficial interest are each evidence of connection to the property and none establishes authority to sell it. Verify: who the current trustee is — the 2011 document names someone now deceased, so successor trustee provisions and any acceptance must be examined; that the trust authorizes sale of real property; what the trust requires of the trustee in doing so, since some require consents or notices; and that title is actually held in the trust, against the record.

On the LLC: a signature block reading "President" is a claim to be verified, not verification — and an LLC does not ordinarily have a president at all, which is itself a signal. Verify: that the entity exists and is in good standing; who is authorized to act for it under the operating agreement, and whether a resolution, a manager's consent, or more than one signature is required; and that the authorization covers this transaction including its terms.

What transfers: verify authority against a source at intake, and stop and escalate where it is unclear rather than postponing. Ten minutes at the listing appointment prevents a failure that otherwise appears in escrow with an accepted offer, a buyer with loan approval, and no way to deliver title.

Chapter summary

An estate answers what rights and for how long. Freeholds are of indefinite duration in the land itself; leaseholds are possession for a period, with the owner holding the reversion.

Leaseholds are best held by how they end. An estate for years expires on its date without notice; a periodic estate requires notice; an estate at will ends at either party's election subject to statutory notice; and an estate at sufferance is a holdover who entered lawfully — which is why self-help is unlawful.

A leasehold survives a sale. Verify existing leases rather than accepting assurances about them.

Fee simple absolute is the default and remains subject to government powers and private restrictions. Conditional language in a deed is a title matter for counsel.

A life tenant owns for a lifetime, may not commit waste, and can convey only what they hold. A fee requires the life tenant and the remainderman to join.

Joint tenancy has equal shares and survivorship and requires express language; tenancy in common allows unequal shares, has no survivorship, and is the default. A lifetime transfer by a joint tenant severs as to that interest while survivorship continues among the others.

Vesting is an estate planning question. Explain that it matters, refer it to counsel before the deed is prepared, and confirm at intake how title is actually held.

California is a community property state, and both spouses generally must join in a conveyance. Ask marital status neutrally as an authority question, check the record, and stop where they do not reconcile.

A common interest development brings a body of private law with it. Use restrictions, assessments, reserves, minutes, and litigation all bind or affect the buyer — and the documents are a slow item that must be ordered early enough for the review to be real.

Verify authority to convey against a source. Trustee, not beneficiary; the entity's governing documents, not a signature block; the appointment, the court order, the power of attorney's actual terms, and all co-owners. Where it is unclear, stop and escalate before the listing is signed.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Identify the estate or ownership form involved, state who must sign to convey and what you would verify against which source, identify the questions you would ask at intake, state what you would refer to legal or tax counsel and when, identify anything a buyer must review and by what deadline, and state the evidence you would create and retain. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 04

Land description, measurement, and boundaries

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Explain why a street address is not a legal description and identify the three systems California uses.
  • Read a metes and bounds description and identify the elements that make it close.
  • Locate a parcel within the government survey system and compute acreage from a sectional description.
  • Distinguish a recorded map description from an assessor's parcel number and explain why the two are not interchangeable.
  • Distinguish record boundaries from occupied boundaries, and identify what a survey does that a title policy does not.
  • Perform the area, linear, and volume calculations a licensee actually needs, and state the sources a measurement should carry.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: legal descriptions and metes and bounds55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: the survey system, recorded maps, and parcel numbers50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: boundary evidence and measurement45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Metes and bounds

1.1 Why a legal description is required

A deed must describe the land well enough to identify it. A street address does not.

Addresses are assigned administratively, change, are duplicated across jurisdictions, and describe a building rather than a parcel. A parcel with no building has no address. A parcel split into two has one address and two parcels. None of that identifies land in a way a court or a title insurer can rely on.

A legal description identifies the parcel itself, permanently, in a way that can be located on the ground. California uses three systems, and a single description may combine them.

SystemTypical use
Metes and boundsIrregular parcels, rural land, older descriptions, boundary calls
Government survey (rectangular)Rural and undeveloped land, large parcels
Lot, block, and tract (recorded map)Subdivided land — most residential property

1.2 What metes and bounds does

Metes and bounds describes a parcel by tracing its perimeter. Metes are distances; bounds are directions and boundaries.

The description starts at a defined point, runs a series of courses, and returns to where it began. If it does not return, it does not describe a closed parcel, and the description is defective.

1.3 The elements

Point of beginning. A defined, locatable starting point. Historically tied to a physical monument; modern practice ties it to a surveyed reference such as a section corner or a point on a recorded map.

Courses. Each leg of the perimeter, stated as a direction and a distance — "North 45 degrees East, 150 feet."

Monuments. Physical or recorded reference points a call may run to — an iron pipe, a section corner, the centerline of a road, a watercourse.

Closure. The requirement that the described perimeter return to the point of beginning.

1.4 Reading a bearing

Bearings are stated as a deviation from north or south, toward east or west.

North 45 degrees East means: start facing north, rotate 45 degrees toward the east.

South 30 degrees West means: start facing south, rotate 30 degrees toward the west.

A bearing always begins with N or S and ends with E or W, with an angle between 0 and 90 degrees in between. A description reading "East 45 degrees North" is not a bearing, and a licensee who encounters one is looking at a transcription error worth flagging.

Angles are subdivided into minutes (60 to a degree) and seconds (60 to a minute). "N 12°30'15" E" is a bearing of 12 degrees, 30 minutes, 15 seconds east of north.

1.5 A worked description

Beginning at the northeast corner of Section 14, Township 3 North, Range 2 West, San Bernardino Base and Meridian; thence South 00°00'00" East, 660.00 feet; thence North 90°00'00" West, 660.00 feet; thence North 00°00'00" West, 660.00 feet; thence North 90°00'00" East, 660.00 feet to the point of beginning.

Read it as a walk. From the section corner, go 660 feet due south, 660 feet due west, 660 feet due north, and 660 feet due east — arriving back at the start. The parcel is 660 by 660 feet.

Area: 660 × 660 = 435,600 square feet ÷ 43,560 = 10 acres.

Note what made it readable: the point of beginning was tied to the survey system, every course had a direction and a distance, and it closed.

1.6 Monuments and the order of priority

Where the elements of a description conflict — the call says 150 feet but the monument is at 148 — the law applies an order of priority. In general terms, natural monuments are given the greatest weight, then artificial monuments, then references to recorded maps, then courses and distances, then area.

The reasoning is that a physical or recorded reference is less likely to be mistranscribed than a number, and more likely to reflect what the parties actually intended to convey.
