For a licensee this is recognition, not application. A conflict between a call and a monument is a boundary question for a surveyor and, if it is contested, for counsel.

1.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to read a bearing correctly, identify the elements of a metes and bounds description, and say what closure means.

2. Government survey system

2.1 What it is

The rectangular survey system — also called the government survey or the Public Land Survey System — divides land into a grid measured from established reference lines.

California uses three base and meridian systems: Humboldt, Mount Diablo, and San Bernardino. A description must state which, because the grid coordinates repeat under each.

2.2 The framework

LineWhat it does
Base lineRuns east–west; the reference for measuring north and south
Principal meridianRuns north–south; the reference for measuring east and west
Township linesRun east–west, parallel to the base line, every 6 miles
Range linesRun north–south, parallel to the meridian, every 6 miles

Townships are numbered north or south of the base line. Ranges are numbered east or west of the meridian. Their intersection identifies a six-mile square, also called a township.

T3N, R2W, SBB&M means: the township three tiers north of the San Bernardino base line, in the range two tiers west of the San Bernardino meridian.

2.3 The numbers to know

UnitSize
Township6 miles × 6 miles = 36 square miles
Sections per township36
Section1 mile × 1 mile = 640 acres
Half section320 acres
Quarter section160 acres
Quarter of a quarter40 acres
Acre43,560 square feet
Mile5,280 feet

Section numbering. Sections are numbered 1 through 36 beginning in the northeast corner, running west across the top row, then dropping down and reversing direction — a serpentine or boustrophedon pattern. Section 1 is the northeast corner; Section 6 is the northwest; Section 31 is the southwest; Section 36 is the southeast.

2.4 Reading a sectional description

Sectional descriptions are read backwards — from the right, working left. Each division cuts the previous one.

The NE¼ of the SW¼ of Section 14

Start with Section 14 (640 acres). Take the southwest quarter: 160 acres. Take the northeast quarter of that: 40 acres.

The shortcut: divide 640 by the denominators.

NE¼ of SW¼ → 640 ÷ 4 ÷ 4 = 40 acres.

S½ of NW¼ of SE¼ → 640 ÷ 2 ÷ 4 ÷ 4 = 20 acres.

N½ of NE¼ → 640 ÷ 2 ÷ 4 = 80 acres.

Where a description joins two parcels with "and," compute each and add:

The NE¼ of the SW¼ and the N½ of the SE¼ of Section 14 = 40 acres + (640 ÷ 2 ÷ 4 = 80 acres) = 120 acres.

2.5 Why sections are not exactly 640 acres

Range lines converge as they run north, because the earth is curved. To keep townships approximately square, the system introduces correction lines and guide meridians at intervals, and the resulting adjustments fall on particular sections.

The practical consequences: sections along the north and west edges of a township frequently contain government lots — irregular parcels of odd acreage described by lot number rather than by fractional call — and a section is a nominal 640 acres rather than a guaranteed one.

A licensee encountering "Government Lot 3" in a description is looking at a real parcel with a real acreage that must be read from the survey, not computed from fractions.

2.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to compute acreage from a sectional description, state how sections are numbered, and explain why a government lot exists.

3. Lot and block descriptions

3.1 The system most residential property uses

Where land has been subdivided, a subdivision map is recorded showing lots, blocks, streets, and easements. Once recorded, each parcel can be described by reference to it:

Lot 14, Block 3, of Tract No. 28471, in the City of Burbank, County of Los Angeles, State of California, as per map recorded in Book 812, Pages 44 through 47, of Maps, in the Office of the County Recorder of said County.

The description is short because the map carries the detail. Every dimension, bearing, easement, and dedication shown on the recorded map is incorporated by the reference.

3.2 Why the recording reference matters

The book and page citation is not decoration. It identifies which map, in a county that has recorded thousands, and it is what makes the description locatable.

A description reading "Lot 14, Block 3, Tract 28471" without the recording reference is incomplete, and a licensee copying a legal description from one document to another must copy it exactly and completely — including the recording reference, the county, and any "except" or "together with" language.

3.3 "Except" and "together with"

Legal descriptions frequently carry additions and subtractions:

"Except the westerly 20 feet thereof" — the parcel is the lot minus that strip. Someone else owns or has taken it.

"Together with an easement for ingress and egress over…" — the parcel comes with a right over other land, which may be essential to its use.

A licensee who drops either phrase while copying has described a different parcel. On a landlocked lot, dropping the "together with" removes the access.

3.4 The Subdivision Map Act and the Subdivided Lands Law, in outline

Two bodies of California law govern subdivided land, and they do different things.

The Subdivision Map Act is the local land use mechanism. It governs the creation of parcels — the map process, the conditions a local agency may impose, dedications, and improvements. It answers whether a parcel may lawfully be created and sold.

The Subdivided Lands Law is a consumer protection mechanism administered by the Department of Real Estate. It addresses the sale of subdivided interests, requiring a public report before offering certain subdivided property for sale.

The practical point for a licensee: an unlawfully created parcel is a real problem, and "the seller says it was split years ago" is not evidence that it was lawfully split. Where a parcel's creation is in question, that is a matter for the local agency, title, and counsel.

3.5 Checkpoint

You should be able to identify the elements of a lot and block description and explain what "together with" adds.

4. Recorded maps and assessor parcels

4.1 The distinction that causes real errors

An assessor's parcel number is an administrative identifier a county assigns for taxation. A legal description identifies the land in law.

An APN is not a legal description and must not be used as one in a conveyance.

The reasons are practical:

  • APNs are assigned for the assessor's convenience and are changed, split, and combined as assessment practice requires.
  • One APN may cover more than one legal parcel, and one legal parcel may carry more than one APN.
  • An APN is not a promise that a lawful parcel exists. The assessor taxes what is there; the assessor does not adjudicate whether it was lawfully created.

That last point is the expensive one. A seller pointing to a separate tax bill as proof that a parcel is separately saleable has produced evidence of an assessment practice, not of a lawful split.

4.2 What the assessor's records are good for

They are genuinely useful within their limits: identifying the parcel for research, locating it on a parcel map, finding the assessed value and tax history, and finding characteristics such as year built and recorded square footage.

With the source stated. A licensee using assessor square footage in marketing states it as assessor data — "1,840 sq ft (per assessor; buyer to verify)" — rather than presenting it as measured fact. The assessor, the listing service, an appraisal, and an actual measurement routinely produce four different figures for one house.

4.3 Where descriptions are actually found

SourceWhat it gives
The recorded deedThe operative legal description as conveyed
The preliminary reportThe description title would insure, plus exceptions
The recorded subdivision or parcel mapDimensions, bearings, easements, dedications
A surveyThe boundaries located on the ground
The assessor's recordsAdministrative identification and characteristics

The deed and the preliminary report are the operative sources. The others are research and support.

4.4 The licensee's practice

  1. Obtain the legal description from a recorded document, not from a prior listing.
  2. Copy it exactly and completely, including recording references and any except or together-with language.
  3. Reconcile the description, the APN, and the map — if they do not agree, stop and route it to title.
  4. Never substitute an address or an APN for a legal description in a document intended to convey.
  5. State sources for every measurement used in marketing.

4.5 A worked description problem

A licensee is preparing a listing for a rural parcel. He has three documents.

  • The deed, describing the property by metes and bounds, running from a monument and closing back to it.
  • The assessor's parcel map, showing a parcel with an assessor's parcel number and an area of "4.7 AC."
  • The seller's statement that "it's about five acres, and the fence line is the property line."

The listing service form asks for lot size, and it asks for the legal description.

What each document is and is not.

The deed's description is the legal description. It is what conveys, and it is what belongs on the listing and in the agreement. Section 4.1's distinction governs.

The assessor's parcel number is not a legal description. Section 4.2 explains what the assessor's records are for — taxation — and their boundaries and areas are maintained for that purpose. An APN identifies a tax parcel and does not describe the land conveyed. Using it as the legal description is the error Section 4.1 names, and it is common because it is convenient.

"4.7 AC" on the assessor's map is an assessor's figure. It may be accurate; it is not authoritative for what the deed conveys.

The seller's "about five acres" is a recollection. Lesson 03 of the practice course establishes what happens when a licensee repeats a number from a client's memory: it becomes the licensee's representation.

"The fence line is the property line" is Section 5.1's problem exactly — the difference between the boundary of record and the boundary in use, and it is the sentence that precedes most boundary disputes.

What the licensee does.

The legal description comes from the deed, transcribed exactly, not paraphrased and not replaced with the APN.

The area is stated with its source. "Assessor's records show 4.7 acres" is defensible. "5 acres" is not, and "approximately 5 acres" is the same representation with a hedge in front of it.

The fence statement goes into the disclosure conversation, and the licensee recommends a survey — Section 5.6 — in writing, because a seller who volunteers that the fence and the line differ has disclosed a known condition affecting what the buyer is acquiring.

The licensee does not locate the boundary, interpret the metes and bounds description, opine on whether the fence has legal significance, or reconcile the deed's area against the assessor's. Section 5.5 forbids each of those, and the last one is the most tempting because it looks like arithmetic.

The result. A listing whose description is the deed's, whose area is attributed, and whose file contains a written survey recommendation with a date. Three sentences of work, and it removes the two representations most likely to produce a claim on a rural parcel.

4.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain why a separate tax bill does not establish a separately saleable parcel, and list the operative sources of a legal description.

5. Survey and boundary evidence

5.1 Two different boundaries

Almost every boundary dispute reduces to a single distinction:

The record boundary is where the documents say the line is. The occupied boundary is where people have been behaving as though it is.

They frequently differ, and the difference is invisible until someone builds, sells, or measures.

5.2 What a survey does

A survey locates the record boundaries on the ground, using the description, the recorded maps, and physical evidence, and shows where improvements sit relative to them.

A survey is what answers: is the fence on the line? Does the neighbor's driveway cross? Is the addition inside the setback? Where exactly does the easement run?

5.3 What a title policy does not do

This is among the most useful things a licensee can explain to a buyer.

A preliminary report and a title policy address record title — what the records show about ownership and encumbrances. They are not surveys. A standard policy typically excepts matters that a survey would disclose, which means precisely that the policy does not insure against them.

A buyer asks whether the fence is on the property line, and the agent says the title report "came back clean."

That answer is wrong. The report addresses record title, and boundary location is exactly the category the standard policy excepts. Nothing in the report speaks to where the fence is.

The professional answer: the report does not address that, a survey does, and here is how to get one before the contingency expires.

5.4 Common boundary problems

ProblemWhat it is
EncroachmentAn improvement extending onto another's land — a fence, eave, driveway, wall, or building
Overlapping descriptionsTwo descriptions covering the same ground, usually from historic conveyances
GapsA strip described in neither adjoining parcel
Fence-line discrepancyThe fence is not on the record line, sometimes by decades of custom
Ambiguous or defective descriptionA description that does not close, or whose calls conflict

5.5 What the licensee must not do

Boundary questions are legal questions, and the doctrines that resolve them — adverse possession, agreed boundaries, prescriptive rights, acquiescence — carry requirements and consequences a licensee cannot assess.

Must not: state where the boundary is; characterize an encroachment as minor or as no problem; predict whether a neighbor has a claim; advise a seller to move a fence to resolve it; or tell a buyer that long-standing use "means it's theirs now."

Must: disclose what is observed; recommend a survey where the facts call for one; recommend counsel where a claim may exist; document the recommendation and the client's response; and ensure it happens before the contingency expires.

5.6 When to recommend a survey

The recurring triggers:

  • The buyer intends to build, fence, or improve near a boundary.
  • A fence, driveway, or structure appears close to or across a line.
  • The description is metes and bounds, old, or complex.
  • The property is rural, unimproved, or irregular.
  • The preliminary report shows easements whose location matters to the buyer's plans.
  • The buyer or seller says anything about a disagreement with a neighbor.
  • Acreage matters to the price.

That last one deserves emphasis. Where a buyer is paying by the acre, the acreage in the listing is a factual claim requiring a source — and the source is a survey or a recorded map, not the assessor and not the prior listing.

5.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain the difference between record and occupied boundaries, state what a title policy does not cover, and list the triggers for recommending a survey.

6. Area and linear measurement

6.1 The constants

ConstantValue
Acre43,560 square feet
Mile5,280 feet
Section1 mile square = 640 acres
Square yard9 square feet
Cubic yard27 cubic feet
Front footOne foot of frontage, measured along the street

6.2 Area formulas

ShapeFormula
Rectanglelength × width
Triangle½ × base × height
Trapezoid½ × (base₁ + base₂) × height
Circleπ × radius²

Irregular parcels are handled by decomposition: divide the shape into rectangles and triangles, compute each, and add.

6.3 Worked problems

A lot measures 120 feet by 200 feet. How many acres?

120 × 200 = 24,000 sq ft. 24,000 ÷ 43,560 = 0.551 acres.

A parcel is a rectangle 300 × 400 feet with a triangular extension whose base is 300 feet and height 100 feet.

Rectangle: 300 × 400 = 120,000 sq ft. Triangle: ½ × 300 × 100 = 15,000 sq ft. Total: 135,000 sq ft ÷ 43,560 = 3.10 acres.

A 5-acre rectangular parcel has 400 feet of road frontage. How deep is it?

5 × 43,560 = 217,800 sq ft. 217,800 ÷ 400 = 544.5 feet.

Land sells for $12,000 per acre. What is a 130 × 250 foot lot worth?

130 × 250 = 32,500 sq ft ÷ 43,560 = 0.746 acres × $12,000 = $8,957.

A driveway is 60 feet long, 12 feet wide, and 4 inches thick. How many cubic yards of concrete?

4 inches = 0.333 feet. 60 × 12 × 0.333 = 240 cubic feet ÷ 27 = 8.9 cubic yards.

6.4 The unit errors that produce wrong answers

Most measurement errors are not arithmetic. They are unit errors, and three account for most of them:

  1. Mixing feet and inches without converting. Four inches is 0.333 feet, not 4.
  2. Confusing linear and square measure. A 100-foot lot line is not 100 square feet.
  3. Converting to acres in the wrong direction. Square feet ÷ 43,560 = acres; acres × 43,560 = square feet.

The reasonableness check catches all three. A residential lot of 0.55 acres is plausible; one of 550 acres is not. Before reporting a number, ask whether it is the right order of magnitude for what is being measured.

6.5 Measuring improvements, and the square footage problem

Square footage is the most litigated number in residential practice, because buyers price on it and sources disagree.

Sound practice:

  • State the figure with its source — "1,840 sq ft (per assessor)."
  • Where sources conflict, say so and invite the buyer to verify.
  • Do not include areas the local convention excludes — garages, unfinished basements, and unpermitted conversions are commonly excluded, and including them inflates the figure.
  • Never present an unpermitted conversion as living area without disclosing its status.

A licensee who writes "1,840 sq ft (per assessor; buyer to verify)" has given the buyer better information than one who writes "1,840 sq ft" — and is in a materially better position if the figure turns out to be wrong.

6.6 What a measurement must carry

Every measurement a licensee reports should carry three things: the number, the unit, and the source. A number without a source is an assertion; a number with a source is information the buyer can evaluate and verify.

6.7 Where measurement errors reach the client

Section 6.4 covers unit errors and Section 6.5 covers the square footage problem. This section connects the arithmetic to the consequence, because a principles course can make measurement feel like an exam topic when it is a liability topic.

The measurementHow the error reaches the client
Living areaStated in the listing, relied on by buyers, used in price-per-square-foot comparisons, and relied on by the appraiser's grid. An overstatement inflates the comparison in both directions — this property and every property later compared to it.
Lot sizeDetermines what can be built, whether a division is conceivable, and what setbacks permit. A buyer purchasing for a specific use is buying the dimension, not the house.
FrontageAccess, and in some settings value directly.
AcreageOn rural parcels, frequently the price basis itself. A five percent error is five percent of the purchase price.
Room dimensionsWhether furniture fits, which sounds trivial and is the complaint that arrives in week one.
Ceiling height and volumeWhere a use, a conversion, or a code requirement depends on it.

The three rules that prevent all of it:

  1. Give the source with every number. "Per assessor," "per builder's plans," "per the prior listing," "measured by [name] on [date]." A number without a source is the licensee's own statement, whatever its origin.
  2. Do not convert between sources. Taking an assessor's acreage and restating it in square feet, or a builder's plan area and adjusting it for what was added, produces a number no source stands behind.
  3. Distinguish what is included. Section 6.5's point. Whether a converted garage, an enclosed patio, a basement, or an accessory structure counts as living area is a definitional question with a real answer, and different sources answer it differently — which is why two accurate figures can disagree by hundreds of square feet.

The arithmetic in Section 6.3 is worth practicing for a reason beyond the examination. A licensee who can compute area, convert units, and check a figure against its dimensions will notice when a source is wrong — and a listing whose stated area does not reconcile with its own stated dimensions is a listing with a defect that nobody caught because nobody multiplied.

The professional standard here is unusually simple: report, attribute, and refuse to improve. The number you were given, from the source you were given it by, with the source named. Every measurement claim that has ever produced a complaint was an improvement on one of those three.

6.8 Why the measurement topics belong in a licensing course

A principles course teaches area formulas and section arithmetic, and students reasonably ask why a licensee needs them when a listing service supplies the numbers.

Four reasons, in ascending order of importance.

One: the numbers supplied are frequently wrong. A listing service field is populated by a person, from a source, sometimes by carrying forward what the last listing said. A licensee who can check a stated area against its own stated dimensions will catch errors that otherwise propagate — into the next listing, into a comparable grid, and into an appraisal.

Two: units are where errors hide. Section 6.4's unit errors are not exam artifacts. Acres and square feet, front feet and lineal feet, dimensions in feet and inches — a conversion performed casually produces an answer wrong by a factor, and a factor-of-ten error looks obviously wrong on paper and entirely plausible on a form.

Three: the arithmetic underlies the analysis. Price per square foot, adjustment amounts, lot coverage, buildable area, and yield all rest on measurement. A licensee who cannot compute them cannot check them, which means he cannot tell whether an analysis handed to him is sound.

Four, and most important: a stated measurement is a representation. Section 6.6 establishes what a measurement must carry. The number goes into a listing, a buyer relies on it, and it is the licensee's statement unless it was attributed to a source. This is the reason the topic sits in a licensing course rather than in a technical one — the arithmetic is the small part, and the discipline of sourcing, attributing, and refusing to improve is the large part.

The three-part rule, restated as a working practice:

  1. Report the number from a source, and name the source.
  2. Check it against itself where you can — dimensions against area, area against acreage, unit against unit. An internal inconsistency is a signal that one of the inputs is wrong.
  3. Do not reconcile sources. Where the assessor says one thing and the plans say another, report both with their sources rather than producing a third number that no source stands behind.

The failure this prevents is not an arithmetic failure. It is a licensee who repeated a number, was believed, and turned out to be the only person who had ever asserted it. Section 5.5's prohibition and this section's arithmetic are the same subject: the licensee's job is to convey what is known, from where it is known, and to be visibly absent from the parts he is not qualified to determine.

6.9 Checkpoint

You should be able to compute acreage and value from lot dimensions, convert between units correctly, apply a reasonableness check, and state a square footage figure the way a professional states it.

Key terms

TermDefinition
Legal descriptionA description identifying the parcel itself, permanently and locatably. A street address is not one.
Metes and boundsDescription by tracing the perimeter — metes are distances, bounds are directions and boundaries.
Point of beginningThe defined, locatable starting point of a metes and bounds description, to which the description must return.
CourseOne leg of a perimeter, stated as a direction and a distance.
MonumentA physical or recorded reference point a call may run to.
ClosureThe requirement that the perimeter return to the point of beginning. A description that does not close is defective.
BearingA direction stated as a deviation from north or south toward east or west — N 45° E, S 30° W. Always N or S first, E or W last, 0–90° between.
Order of priorityWhere description elements conflict: natural monuments, then artificial monuments, then recorded maps, then courses and distances, then area. Recognition for a licensee; application for a surveyor.
Base line and principal meridianThe east–west and north–south reference lines of the rectangular survey. California uses Humboldt, Mount Diablo, and San Bernardino.
TownshipA 6-mile square containing 36 sections, identified by tiers north or south of a base line and ranges east or west of a meridian.
SectionOne mile square, a nominal 640 acres. Numbered 1–36 beginning in the northeast corner and running serpentine.
Reading backwardsHow a sectional description is computed — from the right, each division cutting the previous one. The shortcut is 640 divided by the denominators.
Correction lines and guide meridiansAdjustments introduced because range lines converge northward, producing irregular sections.
Government lotAn irregular parcel of odd acreage described by lot number rather than fractional call, read from the survey rather than computed.
Lot, block, and tractDescription by reference to a recorded subdivision map, which carries the dimensions, easements, and dedications.
Recording referenceThe book and page identifying which recorded map. Not decoration — it is what makes the description locatable.
Except / together withLanguage subtracting from or adding to a described parcel. Dropping either while copying describes a different parcel — and on a landlocked lot, dropping the "together with" removes the access.
Subdivision Map ActThe local land use mechanism governing the lawful creation of parcels.
Subdivided Lands LawThe DRE-administered consumer protection mechanism governing the sale of subdivided interests, including the public report requirement.
Assessor's parcel numberAn administrative identifier for taxation. Not a legal description, not a promise that a lawful parcel exists, and not necessarily one-to-one with legal parcels.
Record boundaryWhere the documents say the line is.
Occupied boundaryWhere people have been behaving as though it is.
SurveyThe location of record boundaries on the ground, showing where improvements sit relative to them.
Survey exceptionThe standard title policy's exception for matters a survey would disclose — meaning the policy does not insure boundary location.
EncroachmentAn improvement extending onto another's land.
Front footOne foot of frontage measured along the street.
Source-stated measurementThe professional form of a reported figure: the number, the unit, and where it came from.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A licensee prepares a deed describing the property as "1428 Elm Street, Burbank, California, APN 2445-018-032."

Identify both defects.

Solution. Neither element is a legal description, and a deed must describe the land well enough to identify it.

The address is assigned administratively, changes, is duplicated across jurisdictions, and describes a building rather than a parcel. A parcel with no building has no address; a parcel split in two has one address and two parcels.

The APN is an assessor's administrative identifier for taxation, changed, split, and combined as assessment practice requires. One APN may cover more than one legal parcel and one legal parcel may carry more than one APN. Critically, an APN is not a promise that a lawful parcel exists — the assessor taxes what is there and does not adjudicate whether it was lawfully created.

The correct practice: take the description from a recorded document — the deed or the preliminary report — copy it exactly and completely including the recording reference and any except or together-with language, and reconcile the description, the APN, and the map. Where they disagree, stop and route it to title.

Problem 2

Read this description and compute the area:

Beginning at the northeast corner of Section 14, T3N, R2W, SBB&M; thence South 00°00'00" East, 660.00 feet; thence North 90°00'00" West, 660.00 feet; thence North 00°00'00" West, 660.00 feet; thence North 90°00'00" East, 660.00 feet to the point of beginning.

Then say what would make it defective.

Solution. Read it as a walk: from the section corner, 660 feet due south, 660 feet due west, 660 feet due north, 660 feet due east — back to the start.

660 × 660 = 435,600 sq ft ÷ 43,560 = 10 acres.

What makes it sound: the point of beginning is tied to the survey system rather than to a perishable physical object, every course carries both a direction and a distance, and it closes.

A description that does not return to the point of beginning does not describe a closed parcel and is defective. Two other defects worth flagging: a bearing written as "East 45 degrees North," which is not a bearing at all and signals a transcription error, and a call that conflicts with a monument — which is a boundary question for a surveyor rather than something a licensee resolves.

Problem 3

Compute the acreage of each: (a) the NE¼ of the SW¼ of Section 22; (b) the S½ of the NW¼ of the SE¼; (c) the NE¼ of the SW¼ and the N½ of the SE¼ of Section 14.

Then state where Section 6 sits in its township and why some sections are not 640 acres.

Solution. Read backwards, dividing 640 by the denominators.

(a) 640 ÷ 4 ÷ 4 = 40 acres. (b) 640 ÷ 2 ÷ 4 ÷ 4 = 20 acres. (c) 40 acres + (640 ÷ 2 ÷ 4 = 80 acres) = 120 acres.

Sections are numbered 1 through 36 beginning in the northeast corner, running west across the top row, then dropping and reversing. So Section 6 is the northwest corner — with Section 1 northeast, 31 southwest, and 36 southeast.

Sections are a nominal 640 acres, not a guaranteed one. Range lines converge as they run north because the earth is curved, so correction lines and guide meridians are introduced to keep townships approximately square. The resulting irregularity falls on the north and west edges, which is where government lots appear — irregular parcels of odd acreage described by lot number, whose acreage must be read from the survey rather than computed from fractions.

Problem 4

A licensee copying a legal description from a prior listing omits "EXCEPT the westerly 20 feet thereof" and "TOGETHER WITH an easement for ingress and egress over Lot 15." The lot has no other street access.

What has been described?

Solution. A different parcel, in two directions at once — and the second omission is the one that destroys the property.

Dropping the "except" describes a parcel 20 feet wider than the seller owns. Someone else owns or has taken that strip.

Dropping the "together with" removes the access. The lot is landlocked, and the easement over Lot 15 is what makes it usable. A description without it conveys land no one can reach.

What transfers: a legal description is copied exactly and completely from a recorded document — including the recording reference, the county, and every except and together-with clause. Copying from a prior listing is not sourcing; it is repeating someone else's transcription.

Problem 5

A seller offers a rear portion of his lot as a separate building site. He produces a separate tax bill and an APN for it and says it "was split years ago."

Assess the evidence.

Solution. A separate tax bill is evidence of an assessment practice, not of a lawful split. The assessor taxes what is there and does not adjudicate whether a parcel was lawfully created. This is the most expensive misunderstanding an APN produces.

Two bodies of California law bear on it, doing different things. The Subdivision Map Act is the local land use mechanism governing the creation of parcels — the map process, conditions, dedications, and improvements — and it answers whether a parcel may lawfully be created and sold. The Subdivided Lands Law is the DRE-administered consumer protection mechanism governing the sale of subdivided interests, including the public report requirement.

"The seller says it was split years ago" is not evidence that it was lawfully split. Where a parcel's creation is in question, the matter goes to the local agency, to title, and to counsel — and it goes there before the property is marketed as a separate building site, because marketing it as one is a factual claim about entitlement.

Problem 6

A buyer asks whether the neighbor's fence is on the property line. The agent replies that the title report "came back clean" and that the fence has been there thirty years so "it's basically the line now anyway."

Correct both statements.

Solution. Both are wrong, and the second is worse.

A preliminary report and a title policy address record title — what the records show about ownership and encumbrances. They are not surveys, and the standard policy typically excepts matters a survey would disclose. Boundary location is precisely the excepted category, so nothing in the report speaks to where the fence is. "Clean" answers a question the buyer did not ask.

The thirty-year comment is a legal conclusion the licensee cannot make. The doctrines that could matter here — adverse possession, agreed boundaries, prescriptive rights, acquiescence — carry requirements and consequences a licensee cannot assess, and stating that long use "means it's theirs now" invites reliance on an opinion that may be exactly backwards.

The right answer: "The title report doesn't address that — it covers record title, and boundary location is specifically excepted. What answers it is a survey, and here's how to get one before your contingency expires. If it turns out the fence isn't on the line, that's a question for an attorney."

The distinction underneath it: the record boundary is where the documents say the line is; the occupied boundary is where people have been behaving as though it is. They frequently differ, and the difference is invisible until someone builds, sells, or measures.

Problem 7

Compute: (a) a 120 × 200 foot lot in acres; (b) the value of a 130 × 250 foot lot at $12,000 per acre; (c) the depth of a 5-acre rectangular parcel with 400 feet of frontage; (d) cubic yards of concrete for a driveway 60 feet long, 12 feet wide, 4 inches thick.

Then name the three unit errors that produce most wrong answers.

Solution. (a) 120 × 200 = 24,000 sq ft ÷ 43,560 = 0.551 acres. (b) 130 × 250 = 32,500 sq ft ÷ 43,560 = 0.746 acres × $12,000 = $8,957. (c) 5 × 43,560 = 217,800 sq ft ÷ 400 = 544.5 feet. (d) 4 in = 0.333 ft; 60 × 12 × 0.333 = 240 cu ft ÷ 27 = 8.9 cubic yards.

The three errors: mixing feet and inches without converting — four inches is 0.333 feet, not 4, which in (d) would have produced 106 cubic yards; confusing linear with square measure — a 100-foot lot line is not 100 square feet; and converting acres in the wrong direction — square feet ÷ 43,560 = acres, acres × 43,560 = square feet.

The reasonableness check catches all three. A residential lot of 0.55 acres is plausible; 550 acres is not. Ask whether the answer is the right order of magnitude before reporting it.

Problem 8

A listing sheet reads "2,340 sq ft." The assessor shows 1,840, an appraisal from 2019 shows 1,910, and the difference is a permitted-status-unknown garage conversion the seller counts as living area. The buyer is paying by the square foot.

What is wrong, and how should the figure be stated?

Solution. Three things, and the buyer is pricing on all of them.

The figure has no source and conflicts with two that exist. Square footage is the most litigated number in residential practice precisely because the assessor, the listing service, an appraisal, and an actual measurement routinely produce different figures for one house.

The conversion is being included without disclosure of its status. Areas the local convention excludes — garages, unfinished basements, unpermitted conversions — inflate the figure when included, and presenting an unpermitted conversion as living area is a factual claim about both area and entitlement.

How to state it: "1,840 sq ft (per assessor; buyer to verify). Approximately 500 additional sq ft of converted garage space; permit status unverified — buyer to investigate with the city." Where sources conflict, say so and invite verification.

And because the buyer is paying by the square foot, the number is a material term — which also makes this a survey and permit-research trigger rather than a marketing question.

What transfers: every measurement a licensee reports carries the number, the unit, and the source. A number without a source is an assertion; a number with a source is information the buyer can evaluate.

Chapter summary

A street address is not a legal description. Addresses are administrative, change, duplicate, and describe buildings; legal descriptions identify parcels permanently and locatably.

Metes and bounds traces a perimeter from a defined point of beginning, through courses of direction and distance, back to the start. A description that does not close is defective, and where calls conflict with monuments the priority order governs — recognition for the licensee, application for the surveyor.

Bearings read N or S first, E or W last, with an angle between.

The rectangular survey grids land from a base line and principal meridian. Thirty-six sections to a township, 640 nominal acres to a section, sections numbered serpentine from the northeast. Sectional descriptions are read backwards, and 640 divided by the denominators gives the acreage.

Convergence produces correction lines, irregular sections, and government lots, whose acreage is read rather than computed.

Most residential property is described by lot, block, and tract, with the recorded map carrying the detail and the recording reference identifying which map. Copy descriptions exactly, including every except and together-with clause.

The Subdivision Map Act governs lawful creation; the Subdivided Lands Law governs sale. A seller's recollection is not evidence of a lawful split.

An APN is not a legal description, is not one-to-one with legal parcels, and is not evidence that a lawful parcel exists.

Record boundaries and occupied boundaries frequently differ, and a title policy does not insure the difference — the standard policy excepts what a survey would disclose. Never state where a boundary is, never characterize an encroachment, and never tell a buyer that long use has settled it.

Recommend a survey where the buyer will build near a line, where improvements appear close to one, where the description is old or complex, where the property is rural or irregular, where easement location matters, where anyone mentions a neighbor disagreement, or where acreage drives the price.

Know the constants, decompose irregular shapes, and check reasonableness. Most measurement errors are unit errors, not arithmetic.

Every reported measurement carries a number, a unit, and a source — and square footage carries its source most of all.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Identify the description system in use and any defect in the description as given, state which sources you would obtain and in what order, perform and show any area or value calculation the scenario requires with its units, identify whether a survey is warranted and state the trigger, state what you would disclose and how you would source each measurement used in marketing, and identify what you would refer to a surveyor, to title, or to counsel and by what deadline. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 05

Encumbrances, liens, easements, and restrictions

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Classify encumbrances by whether they affect title or use, and distinguish voluntary from involuntary liens.
  • Explain how each common lien arises, what it secures, and how it is removed.
  • Distinguish an easement from a license, identify how easements are created and terminated, and explain appurtenant versus in gross.
  • Explain what recorded restrictions do, how they differ from zoning, and what a licensee may say about them.
  • Identify encroachments and boundary conflicts and route them correctly.
  • Apply recording and priority rules, and read a preliminary report's exceptions as allocations of risk.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: encumbrances and liens55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: easements and recorded restrictions50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: priority, recording, and title exceptions45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Voluntary and involuntary liens

1.1 What an encumbrance is

An encumbrance is any right or interest in land held by someone other than the owner that affects the property's title or its use. It does not prevent ownership or transfer; it travels with the property and burdens whoever holds it.

The organizing distinction:

CategoryWhat it affectsExamples
Money encumbrances (liens)Title — the property secures a debtDeed of trust, tax lien, judgment lien, mechanic's lien
Non-money encumbrancesUse — what may be done with the propertyEasements, recorded restrictions, encroachments

That division carries most of the lesson. A lien is about who gets paid; an easement or restriction is about what may be done.

1.2 Voluntary and involuntary

A voluntary lien is created by the owner's agreement — most commonly a deed of trust securing a loan.

An involuntary lien arises by operation of law without the owner's consent. Taxes, judgments, and mechanic's liens are the recurring examples.

1.3 General and specific

A second axis, cutting across the first:

A specific lien attaches to a particular property — a deed of trust, a property tax lien, a mechanic's lien.

A general lien attaches to all of the debtor's property in the jurisdiction — a judgment lien, most federal tax liens.

The practical consequence: a general lien against a seller reaches every parcel he owns in the county, which is why a judgment against a person with a similar name must be resolved rather than assumed away.

1.4 The four-way classification

Most liens can be placed on both axes at once, and doing so is a useful examination and practice skill.

LienVoluntary/involuntaryGeneral/specific
Deed of trustVoluntarySpecific
Property tax lienInvoluntarySpecific
Mechanic's lienInvoluntarySpecific
Judgment lienInvoluntaryGeneral
Federal tax lienInvoluntaryGeneral
Special assessmentInvoluntarySpecific

1.5 What a lien does and does not do

Does: secure a debt against the property, so the property can be reached if the debt is not paid; and generally must be satisfied or otherwise addressed before clear title passes.

Does not: prevent the owner from selling. A property with liens can be sold; the liens are ordinarily paid from the proceeds at closing, which is precisely why the payoff figures matter and why they exceed principal balances.

1.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to place a lien on both axes and explain the practical consequence of a general lien.

2. The common liens

2.1 Deed of trust

The dominant security instrument in California residential practice, taken up in detail in Lesson 11. For present purposes: it is a voluntary, specific lien created when the owner borrows and pledges the property.

It is removed from title by a reconveyance recorded when the debt is satisfied. The debt being paid does not clear the record — a recorded clearing document does. A seller who says a loan was paid off years ago has stated a fact about the debt, not about the title, and an unreleased deed of trust in the preliminary report must be cleared by document.

2.2 Property tax liens

Property taxes become a lien on the property. Two features make them exceptional:

Priority. Property tax liens generally take priority over other liens regardless of when they attached. This is the principal exception to the recording-order rules in Section 5, and it exists because the taxing power does not depend on the order in which private parties recorded.

Attachment without notice to anyone. They arise by operation of law on a schedule, not by a recorded instrument someone had to file.

Special assessments — levied for improvements benefiting particular property, such as a district's infrastructure — are also involuntary and specific, and a buyer inherits the remaining obligation.

2.3 Mechanic's liens

A mechanic's lien secures payment for labor or materials furnished for the improvement of real property. It is the lien licensees most often mishandle, for two reasons.

Priority may relate back. A mechanic's lien's priority may relate back to a time earlier than its recording — commonly tied to when work commenced or the improvement began — which means a lien recorded after a lender's deed of trust may nonetheless take priority over it. This defeats the ordinary "first recorded, first in right" intuition.

It arises from work, not from a contract with the owner. A subcontractor or supplier the owner never dealt with, and may never have heard of, can have lien rights arising from work on the property.

The statutory scheme includes notices and deadlines — preliminary notices, recording periods, and periods within which enforcement must begin. The specifics are legal, and a licensee's role is to recognize that recent construction on a property creates lien exposure, ensure it is routed to escrow, title, and counsel, and not to advise on whether a lien is valid or timely.

The intake question: has any work been done on this property recently, by whom, and is everyone paid? A seller who renovated last spring and has an unpaid dispute with a contractor has a title problem that has not appeared yet.

2.4 Judgment liens

A money judgment can be made a lien on the debtor's real property in a county by recording an abstract of the judgment. It is involuntary and general — it reaches all of the debtor's real property in that county, including property acquired afterward while the lien is effective.

Two practical points:

Similar names. A judgment against a person whose name resembles the seller's appears in the title search and must be resolved through title, not assumed away by the licensee.

A buyer's own judgments matter too. A buyer with an outstanding judgment lien will find it attaching to the property he is about to buy, which is a financing problem discovered at the worst time.

2.5 Other encumbrances affecting title

Federal and state tax liens, which arise from unpaid taxes and are general.

Lis pendens — a recorded notice that litigation affecting title to the property is pending. It is not itself a lien, and it is a powerful cloud: it gives notice to anyone acquiring an interest that the outcome may affect them, which in practice stops transactions.

Attachment, a pre-judgment remedy securing a claim against property pending the outcome.

2.6 Homestead, briefly

California provides a homestead exemption protecting a portion of a homeowner's equity from certain creditors. Its scope, amount, and effect are legal questions, and it does not protect against a voluntary lien the owner granted or against tax liens.

The licensee's role is to recognize the term when a client raises it and to route it to counsel rather than explaining its effect on a particular creditor.

2.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain why a mechanic's lien defeats the first-recorded intuition, and what clears a paid deed of trust from title.

3. Easements and licenses

3.1 What an easement is

An easement is a non-possessory right to use another's land for a specified purpose. The holder may use; the holder may not possess or exclude.

It is a non-money encumbrance — it affects use rather than securing a debt — and it runs with the land, binding successive owners.

3.2 Appurtenant and in gross

Easement appurtenantEasement in gross
BenefitsA parcel — the dominant tenementA person or entity
BurdensAnother parcel — the servient tenementA parcel
On transferPasses with the dominant parcel automaticallyDepends on the grant; commercial ones are generally transferable
Typical exampleA driveway easement over a neighbor's lotA utility company's line easement

The dominant tenement is benefited; the servient tenement is burdened. Students confuse these constantly, and the memory aid is that the servient parcel serves the other.

An easement appurtenant passes with the dominant parcel whether or not it is mentioned in the deed, which is why the "together with" language in Lesson 04 matters for clarity rather than for creation — and why a landlocked parcel's access must be traced in the records rather than assumed from the description.

3.3 How easements are created

MethodHow it arises
Express grantThe servient owner grants it in writing
Express reservationA grantor conveys land and reserves an easement over it
ImplicationArising from the circumstances of a division of land, where prior use was apparent and continuous
NecessityArising where a parcel would otherwise be landlocked by the division that created it
PrescriptionArising from long use that meets the legal requirements
DedicationA private owner devoting land to public use, accepted by the public body
CondemnationAcquired by a government body exercising eminent domain

The last several are legal doctrines with requirements a licensee cannot evaluate. Recognition is the skill; whether a prescriptive or implied easement exists is a question for counsel.

3.4 The licensee's practical problem

The recurring situation is not exotic:

The preliminary report shows a ten-foot utility easement along the rear property line. The buyer intends to build a workshop at the rear of the lot.

What the licensee does: obtain the underlying document, ensure the buyer reviews it — width, location, permitted use, maintenance obligations — and route the effect on his plans to counsel and to the jurisdiction, before the contingency expires.

What the licensee does not do: call the easement routine because most properties have one; ask the title company to "remove" it, which misunderstands what an exception is; or predict that the workshop "should be fine."

An easement along the rear line can prevent exactly the structure this buyer is buying the property to build. That is not a formality.

3.5 Licenses

A license is permission to use another's land that is generally revocable and does not create an interest in the land.

EasementLicense
Interest in landYesNo
RevocableGenerally notGenerally yes
Runs with the landYesNo
Requires writingGenerally yesNot necessarily

The neighbor who has been allowed to park in the driveway for years has, in the ordinary case, a license — not an easement. But long use can raise a prescriptive claim, which is why "we've always let them" is a sentence a licensee should route rather than resolve.

3.6 Termination

Easements can end by express release, by merger of the dominant and servient parcels in one owner, by expiration of a stated term, by abandonment where the requirements are met, or by the purpose becoming impossible.

Non-use alone generally does not terminate an easement. A client who says "nobody has used that road in twenty years, so it's gone" has stated a conclusion the law usually does not reach. Route it.

3.7 A worked easement problem

The property. A residential lot. The preliminary report lists: a public utility easement along the rear ten feet, recorded 1958; an easement for ingress and egress in favor of the adjoining parcel, recorded 1974, described as a twenty-foot strip along the northern boundary; and a drainage easement in favor of the county, recorded 1991.

The buyer intends to build a detached workshop in the rear yard and to fence the north side.

What each easement means for his plan.

The utility easement. Section 3.2's easement in gross — it benefits a utility rather than a neighboring parcel. A structure built within it is a structure built where someone else has a recorded right of access, and the practical consequence is not theoretical: excavation, repair, or replacement can require removal.

The ingress and egress easement. Appurtenant, benefiting the adjoining parcel, and it runs with the land — meaning it does not end because the current neighbor is friendly or because the strip is not currently used. The fence the buyer plans would obstruct a recorded right of access, which is the fact pattern of a neighbor dispute and possibly of an injunction.

The drainage easement. Benefiting a public entity, with its own scope defined by the instrument.

What the licensee does — and Section 3.4 defines the boundary precisely.

He obtains the recorded instruments, not the one-line summaries in the report. The summaries state that an easement exists; the instruments state what it permits, where exactly it runs, and what it prohibits. This is the single most valuable thing a licensee does with a preliminary report and the thing most often skipped.

He tells the buyer his stated plans are affected by items in the report, identifies which ones, and recommends in writing that he review them with counsel and, for location, obtain a survey.

He recommends the buyer confirm with the jurisdiction what may be built where, because Lesson 06's public controls operate independently of these private ones — and a plan can be defeated by either.

What the licensee does not do.

He does not interpret the instruments. Whether the 1974 easement permits a gate, whether the 1958 easement has been abandoned by non-use, and whether anything about the arrangement has changed by operation of law are legal questions, and Section 3.6's termination doctrines are exactly the kind of analysis a licensee must not perform for a client.

He does not reassure. "That easement is ancient, nobody uses it" is the sentence that appears in the complaint.

The professional summary. The buyer is buying a lot on which he cannot do two of the things he intends without answering questions first. He is entitled to know that before he removes his contingencies, and the licensee's contribution is that he learns it on day three rather than after closing.

3.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to identify the dominant and servient parcels, distinguish an easement from a license, and state the correct handling of an easement shown in a preliminary report.

4. Deed restrictions and covenants

4.1 What they are

Private restrictions limit what may be done with land, created by agreement and recorded so that they bind successive owners.

They appear as conditions or restrictions in individual deeds, and — far more commonly in modern practice — as a recorded declaration of covenants, conditions, and restrictions governing a subdivision or common interest development.

4.2 Private restrictions versus zoning

This distinction is worth stating precisely, because clients get it wrong in a costly direction.

Private restrictionsZoning
SourcePrivate agreement, recordedGovernment, by ordinance
Enforced byOwners, or the associationThe jurisdiction
Changed byThe procedure in the instrument, often requiring owner approvalThe land use process
Effect on each otherNeither overrides the other

The controlling proposition: where zoning and a recorded restriction differ, the more restrictive generally governs in practice.

Zoning permitting a duplex does not authorize one where the recorded restrictions prohibit it. A restriction permitting a second unit does not authorize one where zoning prohibits it. A buyer must satisfy both.

A licensee who tells a buyer the property is "zoned for it, so you're fine" has answered half the question.

4.3 What restrictions commonly cover

Use — residential only, no business, no short-term rental. Building — setbacks, height, materials, minimum size, architectural approval. Appearance — paint, landscaping, fencing, visible vehicles. Occupancy and animals. Rental limits, including caps on the number of rented units, which can affect both an investor buyer and financing availability.

4.4 Enforcement

Private restrictions are enforced by those entitled to enforce them — other owners in the scheme, or the association. Remedies commonly include injunctive relief and, in an association context, the association's own enforcement mechanisms.

Restrictions that violate law are not enforceable. Racially restrictive covenants, discussed in Lesson 14, still appear in the recorded language of older title chains and are unenforceable and void. Their presence in a chain of title is not a defect in the seller's title, and California provides a process for their restrictive language to be addressed.

A licensee encountering such language should recognize what it is, not repeat it, and route the question of what may be done about it.

4.5 What a licensee may and may not do

May: identify that restrictions exist; obtain the recorded documents; ensure the buyer receives and reviews them; point out that they bind him regardless of zoning; recommend counsel where their effect on his plans matters; and document the recommendation.

May not: summarize the declaration for the buyer as though the summary were the document; opine on whether a particular use is permitted; predict whether an association will approve something; or characterize a restriction as unenforceable.

A buyer asks: "Can I run my business out of the garage?"

Correct: "There are two separate questions — what the city allows and what the recorded restrictions allow, and you have to satisfy both. Here are the CC&Rs; the use article starts on page eleven. The city's planning counter answers the zoning half. I'd get both answers before your contingency expires, and I'll note that we discussed it."

Incorrect: "It's a quiet business, nobody will care."

4.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain why zoning permission is not sufficient, and answer a permitted-use question correctly.

5. Encroachments, priority, and recording

5.1 Encroachments

An encroachment is an improvement extending onto another's land — a fence, an eave, a driveway, a retaining wall, a structure.

Encroachments raise a mixture of questions no licensee can resolve: whose land it is on, whether the encroaching owner has acquired rights through long use, whether removal can be compelled, and what the effect is on marketability.

The licensee's obligations: disclose what is observed; recommend a survey where the facts call for one; recommend counsel where a claim may exist; document; and ensure it happens before the contingency expires.

What the licensee must not do: state where the boundary is, characterize the encroachment as minor, predict whether the neighbor has a claim, advise moving a fence to resolve it, or tell a buyer that decades of use have settled the question.

5.2 The recording system

California maintains a public record of documents affecting real property, and the system exists to answer one question: who has notice of what?

Type of noticeWhat it is
Actual noticeThe person in fact knows
Constructive noticeThe law charges the person with knowing, because it was recorded
Inquiry noticeFacts that would prompt a reasonable person to investigate — such as someone visibly in possession who is not the record owner

Recording gives constructive notice to the world. That is the whole mechanism: a recorded document is knowable by anyone who looks, so the law treats everyone as knowing it.

Inquiry notice deserves attention because it is the one that surprises people. A buyer who sees an occupant who is not the seller, or a well-worn path across the rear of the lot, has facts that would prompt investigation — and may be charged with what the investigation would have revealed, recorded or not.

5.3 Priority

The general rule: first in time, first in right — as measured by recording, for those entitled to the recording system's protection.

The exceptions are what matter in practice:

ExceptionEffect
Property tax liensGenerally take priority regardless of when they attached
Mechanic's liensPriority may relate back to when work commenced, ahead of instruments recorded earlier than the lien
Subordination agreementA lienholder agrees to take a lower position than it would otherwise hold
Purchase-money priorityCertain purchase-money security interests receive favored treatment

Subordination is the one licensees encounter most. A construction lender frequently requires the seller who carried financing to subordinate, moving the seller behind the construction loan. Whether to agree is a significant decision with real consequences, and it belongs to counsel — not to the licensee explaining that "it's standard."

5.4 Why priority determines outcomes

Priority determines who is paid first from a limited fund and whose interest survives a foreclosure. A junior lienholder wiped out by a senior foreclosure has lost its security, whatever the merits of the debt.

That is why the recording date on the preliminary report is not administrative detail. It is the order in which the property's value is claimed.

5.5 What escrow and title actually do with this

Escrow obtains payoff demands and clears what must be cleared; title determines what it will insure and what it will except. Neither advises the parties, and neither decides a dispute.

A licensee's contribution is to surface issues early — the recent construction, the similar-name judgment, the unreleased deed of trust — so there is time to resolve them, and to route the questions rather than answering them.

5.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain constructive and inquiry notice, state the priority rule and its principal exceptions, and describe the correct handling of an encroachment.

6. Title exceptions and risk allocation

6.1 What a preliminary report is

A preliminary report reports the condition of title as shown in the records and states the terms on which the title insurer would be willing to issue a policy, including the exceptions it would take.

Three things it is not:

  • Not a guarantee that title is clear.
  • Not the policy. It states terms; the policy is the contract.
  • Not a survey. It addresses record title, not the physical property.

6.2 What an exception means

The single most useful sentence in this lesson:

An exception is an item the policy will not insure against. The buyer bears that risk unless it is otherwise addressed.

That is why exceptions are read rather than skimmed, and why the underlying recorded documents must be obtained. An exception naming a recorded easement tells the buyer that an easement exists; only the document tells him where it runs, how wide it is, and what it permits.

6.3 Exclusions and requirements

Two related concepts:

Exclusions are categories the policy form does not cover at all — commonly including governmental regulation such as zoning, the exercise of eminent domain, and matters created or agreed to by the insured.

Requirements are what the insurer needs before issuing — a reconveyance, a resolution, a release, evidence of authority.

A licensee reading a preliminary report should distinguish the three: exceptions are risks not insured, exclusions are categories outside the policy, and requirements are things that must be done before closing. The requirements are the ones with deadlines attached.

6.4 Reading the report item by item

ItemCorrect handling
Utility easement along the rear lineObtain the document; width, location, permitted use. Can prevent structures the buyer intends
Recorded CC&RsObtain and review; bind the buyer regardless of zoning
Unreleased deed of trust the seller says was paidA recorded reconveyance must be obtained; recollection does not clear the record
Mechanic's lienRoute to escrow and title; note that priority may relate back
Judgment against a similarly named personRoute to title for resolution; do not assume it is or is not the seller
Lis pendensRecognize as a serious cloud; route to counsel
Item the buyer does not understandRefer

Three responses that are wrong in every case: treating an item as routine because most properties have one; asking the title company to "remove" an exception; and predicting the practical effect of an easement or restriction on the buyer's plans.

6.5 Owner's and lender's policies

The owner's policy insures the buyer's interest. The lender's policy insures the lender's lien and its priority. They insure different interests, which is why both commonly exist, and why a buyer who declines an owner's policy because "there's a title policy on the loan" has misunderstood what he is not buying.

Extended coverage may address matters a standard policy excepts, including some survey matters, typically requiring a survey or other evidence. Whether to obtain it is the buyer's decision, informed.

6.6 The timing point

The preliminary report and the underlying documents belong at the front of the transaction queue, with the association documents and the hazard-area insurance quotes.

An exception discovered late — an easement across the building site, an unreleased lien requiring a document from a lender that no longer exists — takes time to resolve. A buyer who receives the report on day three has options; one who receives it on day twelve of a fourteen-day review has a formality.

6.7 A worked preliminary report

The report on a suburban residence, in the order the items appear:

  1. General and special taxes for the current fiscal year, a lien not yet due and payable.
  2. A supplemental assessment.
  3. A bond or assessment district obligation.
  4. Covenants, conditions and restrictions recorded 1964, with a note that any provision restricting on a protected basis is void and unenforceable.
  5. An easement for public utilities, 1964.
  6. A deed of trust, 2019, securing an original amount, in favor of a named lender.
  7. A second deed of trust, 2021, in favor of a different lender.
  8. A notice of a pending action affecting the property, recorded four months ago.
  9. A judgment against a person whose name is similar to the seller's.
  10. Requirements: satisfactory evidence of the authority of the parties, and a statement regarding parties in possession.

How a licensee reads this, applying Sections 6.2 through 6.4.

Items 1 through 3 are cost items, and the buyer's monthly figure has probably not included the third. Section 6.4's discipline is to read every item rather than the ones that look important.

Item 4 is a private restriction, and it is separate from zoning. Section 4.2. The unenforceable-provision note is standard and does not make the remaining provisions unenforceable — which is precisely the misreading a licensee must not encourage. Obtain the document; route its interpretation.

Item 5 is located from the instrument rather than assumed.

Items 6 and 7 are monetary liens expected to be paid at closing. Two deeds of trust means two payoff demands, and Lesson 10 of the practice course explains why the good-through date on each matters.

Item 8 is the most serious item on the report. A recorded notice of a pending action puts the world on notice of a claim affecting the property. It is not cleared by paying it, its resolution depends on the underlying matter, and it can affect insurability and financing. This is escalated to the broker and the title officer immediately — and it raises a disclosure question for the seller that the licensee does not answer alone.

Item 9 is the similar-name judgment. Section 5.3's priority rules make this consequential if it is the seller. The title company resolves it through its process, and the licensee's contribution is flagging it early rather than discovering on day twenty-five that clearance takes time.

Item 10's requirements are not exceptions. Section 6.3's distinction. They are conditions the title company will insist on before issuing, and the authority requirement is Lesson 03's ownership question arriving from the title side.

What the licensee does with the whole document. Deliver it promptly with delivery evidenced; obtain the underlying recorded instruments for items 4 and 5; flag items 8, 9, and 10 to escrow, the broker, and the client in writing; recommend review with counsel; and record what was recommended and when.

What he does not do. Rank the items by seriousness for the client as legal advice, tell the buyer item 8 "will probably clear," characterize the 1964 restrictions, or forward the report with "let me know if you have questions." Section 6.6's timing point is the reason: a report delivered without the recommendation that it be reviewed has been delivered in a way that does not produce a review.

6.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain what an exception means, distinguish exceptions from exclusions and requirements, and state the correct response to each item on a preliminary report.

Key terms

TermDefinition
EncumbranceA right or interest held by someone other than the owner, affecting title or use. Travels with the property.
Money encumbrance (lien)An encumbrance securing a debt against the property.
Non-money encumbranceAn encumbrance affecting use — easements, recorded restrictions, encroachments.
Voluntary lienCreated by the owner's agreement, typically a deed of trust.
Involuntary lienArising by operation of law without consent — taxes, judgments, mechanic's liens.
Specific lienAttaching to a particular property.
General lienAttaching to all of the debtor's property in the jurisdiction, including property acquired later while effective.
ReconveyanceThe recorded document clearing a satisfied deed of trust. Payment of the debt does not clear the record — a document does.
Property tax lien priorityGenerally takes priority regardless of when it attached. The principal exception to first-in-time.
Special assessmentA levy for improvements benefiting particular property. Involuntary, specific, and inherited by the buyer.
Mechanic's lienSecuring payment for labor or materials improving real property. Priority may relate back to when work commenced, and rights can arise for parties the owner never dealt with.
Judgment lienA money judgment made a lien on the debtor's real property in a county by recording an abstract. Involuntary and general.
Lis pendensA recorded notice of pending litigation affecting title. Not a lien, and a powerful cloud.
Homestead exemptionProtection of a portion of a homeowner's equity from certain creditors. Does not protect against voluntary liens the owner granted or against tax liens. Effect is a legal question.
EasementA non-possessory right to use another's land for a specified purpose. Runs with the land.
Dominant tenementThe parcel benefited by an easement appurtenant.
Servient tenementThe parcel burdened — it serves the other.
Easement appurtenantBenefits a parcel and passes with it automatically on transfer.
Easement in grossBenefits a person or entity rather than a parcel — a utility line easement.
Easement by necessityArising where a parcel would otherwise be landlocked by the division that created it.
Easement by prescriptionArising from long use meeting the legal requirements. A doctrine to recognize and refer.
LicenseRevocable permission to use another's land, creating no interest and not running with the land.
Non-useGenerally does not terminate an easement.
Private restrictions (CC&Rs)Recorded limits on use binding successive owners. Neither overrides zoning nor is overridden by it — the more restrictive generally governs.
EncroachmentAn improvement extending onto another's land. Disclose, recommend a survey, refer — never characterize.
Constructive noticeKnowledge the law charges a person with because a document was recorded.
Inquiry noticeKnowledge charged from facts that would prompt investigation — an occupant who is not the record owner, a worn path across the lot.
First in time, first in rightThe general priority rule, measured by recording.
Subordination agreementA lienholder's agreement to take a lower position than it would otherwise hold. A significant decision for counsel, not a formality.
Preliminary reportA report of record title stating the terms on which a policy would issue. Not a guarantee, not the policy, not a survey.
ExceptionAn item the policy will not insure against. The buyer bears that risk unless otherwise addressed.
ExclusionA category the policy form does not cover at all — governmental regulation, eminent domain, matters created by the insured.
RequirementSomething the insurer needs before issuing. The items with deadlines attached.

Practice problems

Problem 1

Classify each on both axes and state one practical consequence of the classification: (a) a deed of trust; (b) a property tax lien; (c) a judgment lien; (d) a mechanic's lien.

Answer.

Solution. (a) Voluntary and specific. Created by the owner's agreement and attached to this property. Consequence: it can be sold subject to, or paid from proceeds at closing — which is why the payoff demand, including accrued interest, is obtained rather than assumed from the balance.

(b) Involuntary and specific. Consequence: it generally takes priority regardless of when it attached, which is the principal exception to first-in-time and exists because the taxing power does not depend on the order private parties recorded.

(c) Involuntary and general. Consequence: it reaches every parcel the debtor owns in the county, including property acquired later while the lien is effective — which is why a buyer with an outstanding judgment discovers it attaching to the property he is buying, and why a judgment against a similarly named person must be resolved through title rather than assumed away.

(d) Involuntary and specific. Consequence: its priority may relate back to when work commenced, so it can outrank instruments recorded before it.

Problem 2

A seller renovated the kitchen and rebuilt the deck last spring. He is in an unresolved dispute with the general contractor over the final $18,000, and says two subcontractors he never met have been calling. The preliminary report is clean.

What is the exposure, and what does the licensee do?

Solution. A clean report is a snapshot, not a forecast. Mechanic's lien exposure is exactly what a current report can fail to show.

Two features make this dangerous. Priority may relate back to when work commenced, so a lien recorded next month can outrank a deed of trust recorded before it — which is why lenders care about recent construction. And lien rights arise from work, not from a contract with the owner: the subcontractors and suppliers the seller never dealt with can have rights of their own.

What the licensee does: ask the intake question — has any work been done recently, by whom, and is everyone paid? — route it to escrow, title, and counsel immediately, and ensure it is addressed before the contingency expires.

What the licensee does not do: advise whether a lien would be valid or timely, or reassure the buyer that the dispute is between the seller and his contractor. The statutory scheme's notices and deadlines are legal questions.

Problem 3

A preliminary report shows a ten-foot utility easement along the rear property line. The buyer is purchasing the lot specifically to build a workshop at the rear. The agent tells him easements are routine, offers to ask title to remove it, and says the workshop should be fine.

Assess all three responses.

Solution. All three are wrong, and the third is the one the buyer will rely on.

"Routine" is not an analysis. Most properties have easements; this one may prevent exactly the structure the buyer is buying the property to build.

Title cannot "remove" an exception, and asking misunderstands what an exception is: an item the policy will not insure against. It is not a blemish the insurer chose to note.

Predicting the effect on his plans is outside the license, and it is a legal and land-use question at once.

What the licensee does: obtain the underlying recorded document — width, location, permitted use, maintenance obligations — ensure the buyer reviews it, and route the effect on his plans to counsel and to the jurisdiction before the contingency expires. The exception names the easement; only the document says where it runs.

Problem 4

A neighbor has parked in the seller's driveway for eleven years with permission. The seller says he can revoke it any time. The buyer's agent says eleven years means the neighbor now has an easement. Separately, an old access road across the property has not been used in twenty years, and the seller says it is therefore gone.

Assess each statement.

Solution. Three statements, and all three are conclusions a licensee must not deliver.

The permission is, in the ordinary case, a license — revocable permission creating no interest in the land and not running with it. So far the seller's description is the usual one.

But "eleven years" raises a prescriptive claim, which is a legal doctrine with requirements neither agent can evaluate — and permission itself bears on the analysis. Neither "it's just a license" nor "he has an easement now" is a statement a licensee can make.

Non-use alone generally does not terminate an easement. Easements end by express release, merger, expiration of a stated term, abandonment where the requirements are met, or impossibility of purpose — not by twenty years of nobody driving on it.

What transfers: "we've always let them" and "nobody has used it in years" are both sentences a licensee routes rather than resolves. Disclose the facts, obtain the documents, and refer.

Problem 5

A buyer wants to add a second dwelling unit. The city's zoning permits it. The recorded CC&Rs prohibit more than one dwelling per lot. The agent tells him he is "zoned for it, so you're fine," and adds that the restriction is probably unenforceable because nobody has enforced it in years.

Correct both.

Solution. Zoning permission answers half the question. Private restrictions and zoning come from different sources, are enforced by different parties, and are changed by different procedures — and neither overrides the other. In practice the more restrictive governs: zoning permitting a duplex does not authorize one where the recorded restrictions prohibit it. The buyer must satisfy both.
