The enforceability opinion is a legal conclusion the licensee cannot give, and it is the kind a buyer will spend money relying on. Restrictions are enforced by those entitled to enforce them — other owners in the scheme, or the association — and non-enforcement history is a legal argument, not a fact the licensee can assert.

The correct answer: "There are two separate questions — what the city allows and what the recorded restrictions allow, and you have to satisfy both. Here are the CC&Rs; the use article starts on page eleven. The city's planning counter answers the zoning half. Get both answers before your contingency expires, and I'll note that we discussed it."

Problem 6

A buyer notices a well-worn path crossing the rear of the lot and an occupant in the guest house who is not the seller. The seller says the occupant is "just a friend staying temporarily" and there is nothing recorded about the path.

What kind of notice is in play, and what should happen?

Solution. Inquiry notice — the one that surprises people, because nothing was recorded.

The recording system answers who has notice of what. Actual notice is knowing in fact. Constructive notice is what the law charges a person with because a document was recorded — a recorded document is knowable by anyone who looks, so everyone is treated as knowing it. Inquiry notice arises from facts that would prompt a reasonable person to investigate.

An occupant who is not the record owner and a worn path across the lot are the textbook facts. The buyer may be charged with what a reasonable investigation would have revealed, recorded or not — which could include an unrecorded lease, a claimed prescriptive right, or an occupancy the seller cannot terminate on schedule.

What should happen: the facts are disclosed and investigated rather than explained away. Ask for any written agreement with the occupant, route the path to a survey and to counsel, and do not accept "just a friend" as a description of a possessory interest.

Problem 7

A seller carrying financing on a parcel is asked to sign a subordination agreement so a construction lender can record in first position. His agent tells him it is standard and to go ahead. Separately, the buyer declines an owner's title policy because "the lender is getting a title policy anyway."

Assess both.

Solution. Subordination is a significant decision, not a formality. The seller is agreeing to take a lower position than he would otherwise hold, behind a construction loan — and priority determines who is paid first from a limited fund and whose interest survives a foreclosure. A junior lienholder wiped out by a senior foreclosure has lost its security whatever the merits of the debt. Whether to sign belongs to counsel, and "it's standard" is the single most expensive sentence a licensee can say about it.

The owner's and lender's policies insure different interests. The lender's policy insures the lender's lien and its priority — not the buyer's ownership. A buyer who declines an owner's policy has not economized on a duplicate; he has left his own interest uninsured.

Worth adding while the subject is open: extended coverage may address matters a standard policy excepts, including some survey matters, typically requiring a survey or other evidence. Whether to obtain it is the buyer's decision, made informed.

Problem 8

A preliminary report received on day twelve of a fourteen-day review shows: recorded CC&Rs; an unreleased deed of trust the seller says was paid in 2016; a judgment against a person with a name similar to the seller's; a lis pendens; and a requirement for a corporate resolution.

Handle each, and state what went wrong before any of it.

Solution. The timing failure came first, and it is the one that made everything else harder. The preliminary report and its underlying documents belong at the front of the queue with the association documents and hazard-area insurance quotes. An exception discovered on day twelve — an unreleased lien needing a document from a lender that may no longer exist — leaves a formality where a review should have been.

The CC&Rs: obtain and review; they bind the buyer regardless of zoning.

The unreleased deed of trust: a recorded reconveyance must be obtained. Payment of the debt does not clear the record; a document does, and the seller's recollection is not that document.

The similar-name judgment: route to title for resolution. Do not assume it is or is not the seller — a judgment lien is general and reaches everything the debtor owns in the county.

The lis pendens: recognize it as a serious cloud. It is not itself a lien, but it gives notice that pending litigation may affect anyone acquiring an interest, which in practice stops transactions. Route to counsel immediately.

The resolution: this is a requirement, not an exception — something the insurer needs before issuing, and therefore the item with a deadline attached. Distinguish the three throughout: exceptions are risks not insured, exclusions are categories outside the policy, and requirements must be satisfied before closing.

Chapter summary

An encumbrance affects title or use. Liens secure debts; easements, restrictions, and encroachments govern what may be done.

Classify liens on two axes — voluntary or involuntary, general or specific — and read the practical consequence off the classification.

A lien does not prevent a sale; it is ordinarily paid from proceeds, which is why payoff figures exceed principal balances.

Payment does not clear the record. A recorded reconveyance does.

Property tax liens generally take priority regardless of timing, and mechanic's lien priority may relate back — the two exceptions that defeat the first-recorded intuition. Recent construction is an intake question.

An easement is a non-possessory right that runs with the land. The servient parcel serves the dominant one; appurtenant easements pass with the dominant parcel automatically. Non-use generally does not terminate one, and long permitted use is a doctrine to route rather than resolve.

A license is revocable, creates no interest, and does not run with the land — but "we've always let them" belongs to counsel.

Zoning and recorded restrictions are independent, and the more restrictive governs. "Zoned for it, so you're fine" answers half the question, and enforceability is never the licensee's call.

Encroachments are disclosed, surveyed, and referred — never characterized.

Recording answers who has notice. Constructive notice comes from the record; inquiry notice comes from facts on the ground that would prompt investigation.

Priority determines who is paid first and whose interest survives foreclosure, which is why subordination is a decision for counsel rather than a standard formality.

An exception is a risk the policy will not insure against. Read exceptions, obtain the underlying documents, distinguish exceptions from exclusions and requirements, and never treat an item as routine, ask for its removal, or predict its effect.

Order the report early. Late discovery converts a review into a formality.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Classify each encumbrance on both axes, state how each arose and what would remove it, identify the priority questions and any exception to first-in-time that applies, distinguish the exceptions from the exclusions and requirements on the report given, state what you would obtain and in what order and by what deadline, identify what you would route to title, to a surveyor, and to counsel, and state what you would decline to characterize and why. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 06

Government powers, land use, and environmental controls

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Identify the four government powers over private property and distinguish them by what each takes and what compensation each requires.
  • Explain how a general plan, zoning, and the entitlement process relate, and identify what each answers.
  • Describe subdivision and building controls and explain what a permit record does and does not establish.
  • Identify the environmental hazards a California residential transaction commonly raises and state the licensee's role for each.
  • Explain natural hazard disclosure and water-related requirements at the level a licensee must handle them.
  • Build a land-use due diligence sequence that puts slow items first.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: government powers and the planning framework55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: subdivision, building controls, and environmental hazards50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: natural hazard context and due diligence45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Taxation, eminent domain, police power, and escheat

1.1 Fee simple absolute is not absolute against government

Lesson 03 established that fee simple absolute is the greatest estate the law recognizes. It is nonetheless subject to four government powers, conventionally remembered as PETE:

PowerWhat it doesCompensation
P — Police powerRegulates use for public health, safety, morals, and general welfareNone
E — Eminent domainTakes property for public useJust compensation required
T — TaxationLevies on property to fund governmentNone; it is a charge, not a taking
E — EscheatProperty passes to the state where an owner dies with no heirs and no willNone

The compensation column is the distinction that matters. Regulation under the police power reduces what an owner may do without paying him; eminent domain takes and pays.

1.2 Police power

The broadest of the four, and the source of zoning, building codes, subdivision regulation, environmental controls, health and safety requirements, and rent regulation where a jurisdiction has adopted it.

The power is exercised by state and local government. Its exercise must serve a legitimate public purpose and must not be arbitrary — but within those limits it can substantially reduce what an owner may do, and the owner is not compensated.

A parcel zoned for commercial use is rezoned residential, and its market value falls by half. That is regulation, not a taking, in the ordinary case. The owner has not lost the property; he has lost uses.

Where regulation goes so far as to deprive an owner of substantially all economically beneficial use, the question of a regulatory taking arises. Whether a particular regulation crosses that line is a legal question of real complexity, and a licensee's role is to recognize the concept, never to advise that a client has a claim.

1.3 Eminent domain

The power to take private property for public use upon payment of just compensation. The process is called condemnation.

Two situations a licensee should be able to name:

Partial taking. Where only part of a parcel is taken, compensation may address both the part taken and severance damages to what remains — for example, where the remainder is left an unusable shape or loses its access.

Inverse condemnation. Where government action effectively takes or damages property without a formal proceeding, and the owner brings the claim rather than the government.

A pending or threatened condemnation is a material fact. It affects value, marketability, and the buyer's plans, and it is disclosed. Whether the owner has a claim, what compensation is due, and how to respond are legal questions for counsel.

1.4 Taxation

Property taxation is taken up in Lesson 13. For present purposes:

  • Property taxes become a lien on the property, generally with priority over other liens regardless of when they attached.
  • Special assessments fund improvements benefiting particular property, and a buyer inherits the remaining obligation.
  • Unpaid property taxes can ultimately lead to sale of the property under the applicable process.

1.5 Escheat

Where a person dies owning property with no will and no heirs who take under the law of intestate succession, the property passes to the state. It is uncommon in residential practice and appears mainly as a concept and occasionally in a chain of title.

1.6 What a licensee actually does with this section

Three things:

  1. Recognize which power is operating. A rezoning is police power; a road widening that takes twelve feet of frontage is eminent domain.
  2. Treat pending government action as a material fact — a proposed rezoning, an announced project, a pending condemnation.
  3. Refer the legal questions. Whether compensation is owed, whether a regulation has gone too far, and what a taking is worth are all outside the license.

1.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to name the four powers, state which requires compensation, and distinguish regulation from a taking on a set of facts.

2. Zoning and general plans

2.1 The planning hierarchy

California land use is organized in layers, and each answers a different question.

LayerQuestion it answers
General planWhat does this jurisdiction intend this area to become?
Specific planHow will that intent be implemented in this particular area?
Zoning ordinanceWhat uses and development standards apply to this parcel now?
Entitlements and permitsWhat has this particular parcel actually been approved for?

The general plan is the long-range policy document, and California requires local general plans to address specified elements — land use, circulation, housing, conservation, open space, noise, and safety among them. Zoning is generally required to be consistent with the general plan.

For a licensee the operative point is that the general plan describes intent while zoning controls today, and a buyer asking "will this area change?" is asking a general plan question, answered by the jurisdiction rather than by the licensee.

2.2 What a zoning ordinance controls

Use — what may occur on the parcel: residential, commercial, industrial, agricultural, mixed, and the subcategories within each.

Development standards — the physical envelope: setbacks from each property line, maximum height, lot coverage, floor area ratio, minimum lot size, parking requirements, and open space.

Both halves matter. A parcel zoned to permit a second unit may still be unable to accommodate one within the setbacks, the height limit, and the parking requirement. Permitted use and physical feasibility are different questions, and clients routinely conflate them.

2.3 The relief mechanisms

Where a proposal does not fit the ordinance, three mechanisms are commonly available, and they are frequently confused.

MechanismWhat it addresses
VarianceRelief from a development standard — a setback, a height limit — usually requiring a hardship arising from the property itself
Conditional use permitPermission for a use the ordinance allows in that zone subject to review and conditions
RezoningA change to the ordinance itself, moving the parcel to a different zone

The distinction to hold: a variance addresses the envelope; a conditional use permit addresses the use; a rezoning changes the rule.

All three are discretionary. Nobody is entitled to one, and a licensee who tells a buyer a variance "won't be a problem" has predicted a discretionary decision — which Lesson 13 of the Practice course identifies as a prediction stated as certainty.

2.4 Nonconforming use

A legal nonconforming use is one that was lawful when established and no longer conforms because the ordinance changed. It is commonly permitted to continue — often described as being "grandfathered."

Three limits that surprise owners:

  • Expansion is generally restricted. The right is usually to continue, not to enlarge.
  • Discontinuance can end it. Many ordinances terminate the right after a period of abandonment.
  • Destruction can end it. Rebuilding after substantial destruction may not be permitted.

And a use that was never lawful is not a nonconforming use. It is an unpermitted use, which is a different and worse problem — and "it's been like that for thirty years" does not convert one into the other.

2.5 Recorded restrictions again

From Lesson 05, restated because it belongs here too: zoning and recorded private restrictions are independent, and the more restrictive generally governs in practice. Zoning permitting a use does not authorize it where the CC&Rs prohibit it. A buyer must satisfy both.

2.6 What a licensee may say

May: identify the current zoning designation with its source; explain that zoning governs use and development standards; explain that variances, conditional use permits, and rezonings exist and are discretionary; direct the buyer to the planning counter; and confirm that the buyer investigated before his contingency expired.

May not: state that a use is permitted; predict that an application will be approved; interpret setback or height provisions for the buyer's project; or characterize an existing condition as legally nonconforming.

A buyer asks: "Can I build a second unit here?"

Correct: "That's two questions — what the zoning allows and whether the lot can physically fit it within setbacks, height, and parking, plus whatever the recorded restrictions say. The city's planning counter answers the first two, and I'll get you the CC&Rs for the third. Let's get both before your contingency expires, and I'll note we discussed it."

Incorrect: "The zoning allows it, so you're fine."

2.7 A worked land-use inquiry

A buyer under contract on a residence tells the licensee he intends to convert the detached garage into a rental unit, run a small contracting business from the property, and add a second story.

Three plans, and each is governed by more than one system.

The public side — Section 2.2's ordinance. Whether the zone permits an accessory dwelling unit, what standards apply to it, whether a home occupation is permitted and on what conditions, and what height, setback, and coverage limits apply to a second story. Each has an answer, and the answers come from the jurisdiction rather than from the licensee.

The relief mechanisms — Section 2.3. Where the ordinance does not permit something as of right, a variance or a conditional use permit may exist as a path. They are processes with standards, timelines, hearings, and no guaranteed outcome, and "you can just get a variance" is one of the more damaging sentences a licensee can say.

The private side — Section 2.5 and Lesson 05, Section 4.2. Recorded restrictions can prohibit what zoning permits. A property in a development whose declaration forbids rentals or business use is a property where the zoning answer is irrelevant to the buyer's plan.

The building side — Section 3.2. Permits, plans, and inspections, and a conversion of existing space carries its own requirements. Section 3.3's unpermitted-work problem is what this becomes if the buyer proceeds without them.

The practical sequence the licensee recommends, in writing:

  1. Ask the jurisdiction — planning for use and standards, building for permit requirements — before removing contingencies.
  2. Obtain and read the recorded restrictions, and route their interpretation to counsel.
  3. Confirm with a design or construction professional what the existing structure will actually support.
  4. Confirm insurance and financing implications where a use changes.

What the licensee says, and what he does not.

He says: these are three separate questions with four sources of answers, here is where each answer comes from, and here is the deadline by which you need them.

He does not say: "ADUs are allowed everywhere now," "nobody enforces the home-occupation rules," "you can add a second story, the neighbor did," or any version of what the ordinance permits. Section 2.6 states the boundary, and the neighbor's second story is the least reliable evidence available — it may have been permitted under a different ordinance, granted relief, or built without permission.

The recurring error this prevents. A buyer removes his contingencies on the strength of a licensee's encouragement, closes, and discovers that the conversion he bought the property for requires a discretionary approval he will not get. The information was available on day two, from a phone call to a public counter, and the licensee's contribution was to say who to call.

2.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to distinguish a variance from a conditional use permit, explain the limits on a nonconforming use, and answer a buildability question correctly.

3. Subdivision and building controls

3.1 The two subdivision statutes, revisited

From Lesson 04, because it is the land use half that matters here:

The Subdivision Map Act governs the creation of parcels — the tentative and final map process, the conditions a local agency may impose, dedications, and required improvements. It answers whether a parcel may lawfully be created and sold.

The Subdivided Lands Law is the DRE-administered consumer protection statute governing the sale of subdivided interests, including the public report requirement before certain subdivided property may be offered.

The recurring practice point: a separate tax bill is not evidence of a lawful split. Where a parcel's creation is in question, it goes to the local agency, to title, and to counsel — before it is marketed as a separate building site, because marketing it as one is a factual claim about entitlement.

3.2 Building codes and permits

Building codes set minimum standards for construction, and work generally requires a permit and inspections culminating in a final approval.

What a permit record establishes and does not:

EstablishesDoes not establish
That a permit was issued for described workThat the work was completed
That inspections were performed, where finaledThat the work was done well
The scope as permittedThat subsequent work was permitted
A dateThat current condition matches the permit

A permit that was issued but never finaled is a real problem — an open permit can complicate a sale, and the obligation to close it out generally follows the property.

3.3 Unpermitted work

This is among the most common problems in California residential transactions, and licensees mishandle it in both directions.

The facts to establish: what work was done, when, whether a permit was issued, whether it was finaled, and whether the work is habitable and safe as built.

What the licensee does:

  1. Disclose what is known, including what the seller said and what the licensee observed.
  2. Recommend permit research at the jurisdiction — which is a public record and is where the answer actually lives.
  3. Do not include unpermitted area in advertised square footage without disclosing its status.
  4. Recommend the buyer investigate what legalization would require, since the cost can be substantial and sometimes the work cannot be legalized at all.
  5. Document the recommendation and the buyer's response.

What the licensee does not do: state that the work is legal because it looks fine, characterize the risk as low, predict what the city would require, or advise the seller to say nothing because "everyone does it."

The asymmetry worth teaching: unpermitted work does not automatically make a property unsaleable, and it does automatically make it a disclosure item. Licensees fail on both sides — some conceal it, some describe it as a disaster. The professional response is to make it visible and investigable.

3.4 Certificates and clearances

Depending on the jurisdiction and the property type, a transfer may trigger local requirements — retrofit obligations for smoke and carbon monoxide devices and water conservation fixtures, occupancy or resale inspections, sewer lateral requirements, or point-of-sale reports.

These are local and they vary. A licensee working across city lines who assumes uniformity will eventually miss one, and a missed point-of-sale requirement can delay a closing.

3.5 Accessibility in new construction

Certain multifamily housing is subject to accessible design and construction requirements, and California standards can exceed the federal baseline. A licensee marketing new construction should not represent that a property meets accessibility requirements without confirming it, and should direct specific questions to the developer, the architect, or the jurisdiction.

3.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to state what a permit record establishes, handle unpermitted work correctly, and explain why local transfer requirements need to be checked jurisdiction by jurisdiction.

4. Environmental hazards and disclosure

4.1 The licensee's role, stated once for all of them

For every hazard in this section the role is the same three steps:

  1. Disclose what is known, including the licensee's own observations.
  2. Recommend appropriate investigation by a qualified professional, matched to the property.
  3. Do not evaluate.

A licensee who tells a buyer that visible growth "is just surface mold, it'll clean off" has made an assessment they are not qualified to make, on which the buyer may rely.

4.2 Age-triggered: lead-based paint

For target housing built before 1978, the federal lead-based paint requirements apply. They are triggered by the age of the housing, not by whether children will occupy it, and licensees routinely misstate them in both directions.

The obligation isThe obligation is not
Disclose known lead-based paint and hazardsTest the property
Provide available records and reportsCreate records that do not exist
Provide the required pamphletCertify the property is free of lead
Give the buyer an opportunity to conduct an assessmentRemediate before transfer

4.3 The other common hazards

Asbestos. Present in older construction in insulation, flooring, roofing, and other materials. Generally of most concern when disturbed. Evaluation and abatement are specialist work.

Radon. A naturally occurring soil gas that can accumulate indoors. Testing is inexpensive relative to the transaction and is the only way to know.

Mold. Requires moisture, which means the underlying question is usually where the water is coming from. A licensee should disclose visible growth and moisture evidence as observations and recommend evaluation — never characterize the type or the risk.

Methamphetamine contamination. Where a property was used for manufacture, remediation obligations and notice requirements can apply. A licensee learning of a history routes it immediately.

Underground storage tanks. Common on older and rural property. Leaking tanks create soil and groundwater contamination questions and potentially substantial liability.

Soil and groundwater contamination. Arising from prior industrial or agricultural use, adjacent operations, or historic fill. On any property with an industrial or agricultural history, the question belongs at intake.

Formaldehyde, PCBs, and other building-material issues, which appear in particular construction eras and product types.

4.4 Proximity and off-site conditions

Some conditions are not on the property at all: a neighboring industrial use, an agricultural operation with spraying or odor, a rail corridor, a flight path, a former landfill, a high-voltage corridor.

These raise the same discipline. Disclose what is known, point the buyer to objective sources, and do not characterize. Whether an odor from an adjacent dairy is tolerable is the buyer's judgment to make with accurate information — not the licensee's to make for him.

4.5 The environmental site assessment, in outline

For commercial and some other transactions, environmental due diligence is conducted through a phased assessment: a Phase I records and site reconnaissance review to identify recognized environmental conditions, followed where warranted by a Phase II involving sampling.

A licensee should know the terms, know that these are specialist reports, and know that they take time — which puts them at the front of the due diligence queue rather than the back.

4.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to state the lead-based paint obligations correctly in both directions, and give the correct response to a buyer asking whether visible mold is serious.

5. Water conservation and natural-hazard context

5.1 Natural hazard disclosure

California requires disclosure of whether a property lies within certain mapped hazard zones — flood, fire, seismic, and related designations. The disclosure is commonly prepared by a third-party report provider.

What it does and does not do:

  • Does: tell the buyer the property is within a mapped zone, so he can investigate what that means for insurance, construction requirements, and risk.
  • Does not: certify the property is safe, predict whether an event will occur, or substitute for the buyer's own investigation.

It applies based on mapping, not on the property's event history. A property that has never flooded in the seller's twenty years of ownership is disclosed as within a flood zone if it is mapped in one, and a buyer's view that the mapping is outdated changes nothing about the obligation.

5.2 Why it matters more than buyers assume

Hazard zone status drives consequences buyers regularly discover late:

Insurance availability and cost. In some California areas this is now the determining factor in whether a transaction closes at all.

Construction and improvement requirements, which can materially affect renovation plans.

Financing, where lenders require particular coverage.

Disclosure obligations the buyer inherits as a future seller.

The single most preventable failure in this area: insurance quotes obtained at the end of the contingency period rather than at the beginning. A buyer who learns in week one that coverage will cost far more than assumed can act. A buyer who learns it two days before closing generally cannot.

5.3 Seismic context

California's seismic mapping includes zones related to fault rupture and to seismic hazards such as liquefaction and landslide. Older residential construction may present retrofit questions — foundation bolting, cripple wall bracing, chimney condition.

The licensee's role is the familiar one: disclose what is known and what is mapped, recommend evaluation by a qualified professional, and route the engineering questions.

5.4 Fire hazard severity

Mapped fire hazard severity zones carry consequences including defensible space obligations, construction and material requirements in some areas, and — most acutely in current California practice — insurance availability.

A licensee listing or selling in a mapped fire area should treat insurance as a first-week item, not an administrative closing task.

5.5 Water: supply, rights, and conservation

Three distinct topics that clients merge.

Supply. Where does the water come from — municipal, district, mutual water company, private well, shared well? For rural property this is the question that determines whether the property is usable as intended.

Rights. Riparian, appropriative, and groundwater rights are the specialist area from Lesson 02. Recognize, ask, refer.

Conservation requirements. California transactions increasingly involve water conservation obligations, including fixture requirements that can be triggered at transfer, and local restrictions on landscaping and use.

Groundwater management adds a layer on rural and agricultural property: a parcel within a managed basin may face pumping constraints that affect what can be grown or built.

5.6 The rural intake questions

For rural, agricultural, or unimproved property, at the listing appointment:

  1. What is the water source, and is it documented?
  2. If a well: production, quality, and date of the last test?
  3. Is any water right appurtenant to the parcel, and is it documented?
  4. Is the property within a groundwater management area?
  5. Are there shared-facility agreements — a shared well, a pipeline, an access easement?
  6. Is the supply sufficient for the buyer's intended use?
  7. What is the septic situation, and when was it last inspected?
  8. Is access legal and documented, or is it by long-standing custom?

That last question closes the loop with Lesson 05: a rural parcel reached by a road nobody has a recorded right to use has an access problem the buyer will discover after closing.

5.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain why hazard disclosure applies on mapping rather than history, and list the rural intake questions.

6. Public and private land-use due diligence

6.1 The two halves

Every land use question has a public half and a private half, and satisfying one does not satisfy the other.

PublicPrivate
General plan and zoningRecorded CC&Rs and restrictions
Permits and building codeAssociation rules and architectural approval
Subdivision and parcel legalityEasements burdening the parcel
Environmental and hazard requirementsPrivate agreements — shared wells, access, maintenance
Local transfer requirementsLeases and occupancy agreements

A buyer must clear both columns. The most common failure is checking the public half, receiving a "yes," and treating the question as answered.

6.2 The sequence

Order the work by how long it takes, not by how important it seems. The slow items are the ones that fail transactions.

Week one:

  • Order the preliminary report and the underlying documents.
  • Order association documents where applicable.
  • Obtain insurance quotes where the property is in a mapped hazard area.
  • Begin permit research at the jurisdiction.
  • Order the natural hazard report.

Week two:

  • General and specialist inspections, matched to the property.
  • Zoning and entitlement questions at the planning counter.
  • Survey where a boundary trigger is present.
  • Environmental assessment where the property's history calls for one.

Ongoing:

  • Track every item against the contingency deadline in writing.
  • Confirm receipt of each report and the buyer's review.
  • Escalate anything that will not arrive in time, while there is still time to act.

6.3 The questions that identify the work

A short intake interrogation identifies most of what a particular property needs:

  • What does the buyer intend to do with it beyond live in it?
  • What is the property's history — industrial, agricultural, prior uses, prior construction?
  • What is visibly present — additions, conversions, outbuildings, slopes, retaining walls, wells, tanks, solar?
  • What is adjacent — commercial, agricultural, rail, flight path, corridor?
  • What is mapped — fire, flood, seismic?
  • What is recorded — easements, restrictions, association?

Notice that the buyer's intent is the first question. A buyer who plans to build, subdivide, run a business, or keep animals needs due diligence a buyer who plans to move in does not.

6.4 What defensible due diligence looks like in the file

  • The buyer's stated intentions, recorded at intake.
  • Each investigation recommended, with the date.
  • Each report ordered, with the date ordered and the date received.
  • The buyer's confirmation that he received and reviewed each.
  • Recommendations to consult the jurisdiction, a specialist, or counsel — with the buyer's response.
  • Anything that could not be obtained in time, and what was done about it.

The last item is the one licensees omit. A report that never arrived, with no record of the escalation, looks in hindsight exactly like a report nobody ordered.

6.5 The recurring failure

Not that a licensee gave wrong land use advice. That a licensee gave land use advice at all — and the confident answer arrived faster than the correct referral would have.

The questions in this lesson are answered by the planning counter, the building department, the recorded documents, the specialist, and counsel. The licensee's professional contribution is knowing which question goes where, and getting it there while the buyer can still act on the answer.

6.6 The due diligence failure, restated for a new licensee

Section 6.5 names the recurring failure. Stated as a working rule, it is this: the buyer's plan was never tested against the property's actual constraints, and nobody noticed until after closing.

The pattern is always the same:

  1. The buyer says what he intends to do with the property.
  2. Everyone finds it reasonable, because it usually is.
  3. Nobody asks the jurisdiction, and nobody reads the recorded restrictions.
  4. Contingencies are removed on the strength of the plan being reasonable.
  5. The plan turns out to require an approval, a permit, or a use the restrictions forbid.

Step three is the whole failure, and it costs a phone call and a document request.

The three questions that prevent it, asked as soon as a buyer states an intention:

  • "Have you confirmed with the city or county that this is permitted here?"
  • "Have we read what the recorded restrictions say about it?"
  • "Do we know what permits this needs and what the existing structure will support?"

The licensee answers none of them. He asks them, in writing, with a date, before the contingency deadline — and records the answer.

Why this belongs at the end of a government-powers lesson. Every concept in Sections 1 through 5 — police power, zoning, subdivision control, building regulation, environmental and hazard frameworks — operates on a property whether or not anyone looked. The lesson's purpose is not to make a licensee an expert in any of them. It is to make him the participant who asks, on day two, whether anyone has checked.

6.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to build a due diligence sequence for a specific property, justify the ordering by lead time, and state what the file must show.

Key terms

TermDefinition
PETEPolice power, Eminent domain, Taxation, Escheat — the four government powers over private property.
Police powerRegulation of use for public health, safety, morals, and general welfare. No compensation. The source of zoning, building codes, and environmental controls.
Eminent domainThe power to take private property for public use upon just compensation. The process is condemnation.
Severance damagesCompensation for harm to the remainder where only part of a parcel is taken.
Inverse condemnationA claim brought by an owner where government action effectively took or damaged property without a formal proceeding.
Regulatory takingThe question arising where regulation deprives an owner of substantially all economically beneficial use. A legal question — recognize, never advise.
EscheatProperty passing to the state where an owner dies with no will and no heirs.
General planThe long-range local policy document, addressing required elements. Answers what an area is intended to become; zoning is generally required to be consistent with it.
Zoning ordinanceWhat uses and development standards apply to a parcel now.
Development standardsThe physical envelope — setbacks, height, coverage, floor area ratio, minimum lot size, parking, open space.
Permitted use versus physical feasibilityDifferent questions. A parcel may permit a second unit that cannot fit within the setbacks, height, and parking.
VarianceDiscretionary relief from a development standard, usually requiring hardship arising from the property itself.
Conditional use permitDiscretionary permission for a use the ordinance allows in that zone subject to review and conditions.
RezoningA change to the ordinance itself.
Legal nonconforming useA use lawful when established that no longer conforms. Generally may continue but not expand, and may be lost by discontinuance or destruction.
Unpermitted useA use that was never lawful. Not a nonconforming use, and time does not convert one into the other.
Subdivision Map ActGoverns the lawful creation of parcels.
Subdivided Lands LawDRE-administered consumer protection governing the sale of subdivided interests, including the public report.
Open permitA permit issued but never finaled. A real problem whose closeout obligation generally follows the property.
Target housingPre-1978 housing triggering the federal lead-based paint obligations — by age, not by occupancy by children.
Recognized environmental conditionWhat a Phase I environmental site assessment is designed to identify, with a Phase II involving sampling where warranted.
Natural hazard disclosureDisclosure of mapped flood, fire, seismic, and related zones. Applies on mapping, not event history, and certifies nothing about safety.
Defensible spaceVegetation management obligations in mapped fire hazard areas.
Groundwater management areaA managed basin where pumping constraints may affect what can be grown or built.
Point-of-sale requirementA local obligation triggered at transfer — retrofit, inspection, sewer lateral, or report. Varies by jurisdiction.
The two halvesPublic land use and private restrictions. A buyer must clear both, and satisfying one does not satisfy the other.
Lead-time orderingSequencing due diligence by how long each item takes rather than by how important it seems.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A city rezones a commercially zoned parcel to residential and the owner's market value falls by half. Separately, the city takes twelve feet of frontage from an adjacent parcel for a road widening, leaving the remainder without its former driveway access. A licensee tells the first owner he has "a taking claim worth half his value."

Identify the power in each and assess the advice.

Solution. The rezoning is police power — regulation of use for public health, safety, and general welfare, and no compensation is owed in the ordinary case. The owner has not lost the property; he has lost uses. Regulation reducing value substantially is still regulation.

The road widening is eminent domain, exercised through condemnation, requiring just compensation. Because only part was taken and the remainder lost its access, compensation may address both the part taken and severance damages to what remains.

The advice is wrong and outside the license. Whether regulation goes so far as to deprive an owner of substantially all economically beneficial use — a regulatory taking — is a legal question of real complexity, and telling a client he has a claim worth a specific amount is both a legal conclusion and a valuation of it.

What the licensee actually does: recognize which power is operating, treat pending government action as a material fact to disclose, and refer the legal questions.

Problem 2

A buyer asks whether he can build a second unit. Zoning permits second units in the district. The lot is 40 feet wide with 5-foot side setbacks, a 25-foot height limit, and a two-space parking requirement. The CC&Rs prohibit more than one dwelling per lot. His agent says "the zoning allows it, so you're fine."

Identify everything the answer missed.

Solution. Three separate questions, and the agent answered one of them.

Permitted use is not physical feasibility. Zoning controls both use and the development standards — setbacks, height, coverage, parking, open space. A 40-foot lot with 5-foot side setbacks leaves 30 feet of buildable width, and the parking requirement has to land somewhere. The zoning may permit a unit the lot cannot hold.

The CC&Rs prohibit it, and private restrictions and zoning are independent. The more restrictive generally governs — zoning permission does not authorize what the recorded restrictions forbid, and the buyer must satisfy both.

The correct answer: "That's two questions on the public side — what the zoning allows and whether the lot can physically fit it within setbacks, height, and parking — plus whatever the recorded restrictions say. The city's planning counter answers the first two; I'll get you the CC&Rs for the third. Let's have both before your contingency expires, and I'll note we discussed it."

Problem 3

Three owners want to do things the ordinance does not currently allow: one wants to build within the required rear setback because of an unusual lot shape; one wants to operate a childcare facility in a residential zone where the ordinance lists it as subject to review; one wants his industrial parcel treated as commercial. A licensee tells each that the approval "won't be a problem."

Name the correct mechanism for each and assess the reassurance.

Solution. A variance for the setback — relief from a development standard, usually requiring a hardship arising from the property itself, which an unusual lot shape may supply.

A conditional use permit for the childcare facility — permission for a use the ordinance already allows in that zone subject to review and conditions.

A rezoning for the industrial parcel — a change to the ordinance itself, moving the parcel to a different zone.

The distinction to hold: a variance addresses the envelope, a conditional use permit addresses the use, a rezoning changes the rule.

All three are discretionary, and nobody is entitled to one. "Won't be a problem" predicts a discretionary decision — a prediction stated as certainty, on which a buyer may commit hundreds of thousands of dollars.

Problem 4

A converted garage has been used as a bedroom for thirty years. The seller says it is grandfathered. Permit records show a permit issued in 1994 for a garage conversion, never finaled. The listing advertises 2,340 square feet including the converted space.

Assess each element.

Solution. "Grandfathered" is being used for the wrong concept. A legal nonconforming use was lawful when established and no longer conforms because the ordinance changed. A use that was never lawful is not a nonconforming use — it is unpermitted, which is a different and worse problem, and thirty years does not convert one into the other.

The open permit is a real problem, not a technicality. A permit issued but never finaled can complicate the sale, and the obligation to close it out generally follows the property to the buyer. Note also what the record does establish and does not: it shows a permit was issued for described work and gives a date. It does not establish that the work was completed, done well, or that current condition matches the permit.

The square footage is a misrepresentation as stated. Unpermitted area is not included in advertised living area without disclosing its status.

What the licensee does: disclose what is known including the seller's statement and the licensee's observations; recommend permit research at the jurisdiction, which is public record; recommend the buyer investigate what legalization would require, since the cost can be substantial and some work cannot be legalized at all; and document. What the licensee does not do: say it is legal because it looks fine, characterize the risk, predict what the city will require, or advise the seller to stay quiet.

Problem 5

A 1962 house is being sold to a buyer with no children. There is visible dark growth under a bathroom window and a rusted vent pipe in the rear yard the seller says served "an old oil tank, probably still down there." The agent tells the buyer lead rules do not apply without children, that the growth is "just surface mold," and that old tanks are common in the neighborhood.

Correct all three.

Solution. Lead-based paint obligations are triggered by the age of the housing, not by whether children will occupy it — pre-1978 target housing is covered. And the obligation is misstated in the other direction too: disclose what is known, provide available records and reports, provide the required pamphlet, and give the buyer an opportunity to conduct an assessment. It does not require testing, creating records that do not exist, certifying the property lead-free, or remediating.

"Just surface mold" is an assessment the licensee is not qualified to make, on which the buyer may rely. Mold requires moisture, so the underlying question is where the water is coming from. Disclose the observation — visible growth and its location — and recommend evaluation.

An underground storage tank is not made harmless by being common. Leaking tanks create soil and groundwater contamination questions and potentially substantial liability. Disclose what the seller said, recommend specialist investigation, and route it.

The rule covering all three: disclose what is known including your own observations, recommend appropriate investigation by a qualified professional, and do not evaluate.

Problem 6

A property has never flooded in the seller's twenty-two years but sits in a mapped flood zone and a mapped fire hazard severity zone. The buyer says the flood mapping is "obviously outdated." The licensee plans to have the buyer get insurance quotes after the inspection contingency closes.

Correct the buyer and assess the plan.

Solution. The disclosure applies on mapping, not on event history. A property that never flooded is disclosed as within a flood zone if it is mapped in one, and the buyer's view of the mapping's currency changes nothing. The disclosure tells him he is in a mapped zone so he can investigate what that means; it does not certify safety, predict an event, or substitute for his own investigation.

The insurance plan is the single most preventable failure in this area. Hazard zone status drives insurance availability and cost — in some California areas now the determining factor in whether a transaction closes at all — along with construction requirements, financing conditions, and the disclosure obligations the buyer inherits as a future seller.

Insurance is a first-week item in a mapped fire or flood area, not an administrative closing task. A buyer who learns in week one that coverage will cost far more than assumed can act; one who learns it two days before closing generally cannot.

Problem 7

A buyer is purchasing 20 rural acres to plant an orchard and build a house. The property has a well, a septic system, and is reached by a gravel road across a neighbor's land that the seller says "everyone has always used."

List the questions and identify the most serious issue.

Solution. The access is the most serious issue, and it is the one dressed up to sound harmless. "Everyone has always used it" describes a custom, not a right. A rural parcel reached by a road nobody has a recorded right to use has an access problem the buyer discovers after closing — and long permitted use raises a prescriptive question that is a legal doctrine to route, not a conclusion to assert.

The rural intake questions: What is the water source, and is it documented? For the well — production, quality, and the date of the last test? Is any water right appurtenant to the parcel and documented? Is the property within a groundwater management area, where pumping constraints could affect what can be grown? Are there shared-facility agreements — shared well, pipeline, access easement? Is the supply sufficient for the buyer's intended use, which here means irrigating an orchard rather than serving a household? What is the septic situation and when was it last inspected? And is access legal and documented, or by custom?

What transfers: the buyer's intent is the first due diligence question. A buyer planning to plant and build needs investigation a buyer planning to move in does not.

Problem 8

A buyer intends to build. On day eleven of a fourteen-day contingency, the association documents have not arrived, the permit research has not started, no survey was ordered despite a fence that appears to cross the rear line, and the preliminary report was received that morning. The agent's file contains a list of properties shown and the executed contract.

Diagnose the sequencing and state what the file should have shown.

Solution. Everything slow was started last. Order due diligence by lead time, not by apparent importance — the slow items are the ones that fail transactions.

Week one should have carried: the preliminary report and its underlying documents; association documents; insurance quotes if the property is in a mapped hazard area; permit research at the jurisdiction; and the natural hazard report. Week two: inspections matched to the property; zoning and entitlement questions at the planning counter, which this buyer needs because he intends to build; the survey, triggered here by a fence apparently crossing a line; and environmental assessment if the property's history called for one.

And the two halves were never separated. Public — general plan and zoning, permits, parcel legality, hazard requirements, local transfer requirements. Private — CC&Rs, association rules and architectural approval, easements burdening the parcel, private agreements, leases. A buyer must clear both columns, and the common failure is getting a "yes" from the public half and treating the question as answered.

What the file should show: the buyer's stated intentions recorded at intake; each investigation recommended with its date; each report ordered with the date ordered and received; the buyer's confirmation of receipt and review; recommendations to consult the jurisdiction, a specialist, or counsel with the buyer's responses; and anything that could not be obtained in time and what was done about it. That last item is the one licensees omit — and a report that never arrived, with no record of the escalation, looks in hindsight exactly like a report nobody ordered.

Chapter summary

Four government powers limit fee simple absolute, and the compensation column separates them: police power regulates without paying, eminent domain takes and pays, taxation charges, escheat collects where there are no heirs.

Regulation that reduces value is not a taking in the ordinary case. Whether it has gone far enough to become one is a legal question to recognize and refer, never to advise on.

Pending government action is a material fact — proposed rezonings, announced projects, threatened condemnation.

The general plan states intent; zoning controls today; entitlements record what a parcel has actually been approved for. Zoning governs use and development standards, and permitted use is not the same question as physical feasibility.

Variance for the envelope, conditional use permit for the use, rezoning to change the rule — all discretionary, and none is a formality.

A nonconforming use may continue but generally not expand, and may be lost. A use that was never lawful is not nonconforming, and time does not cure it.

Zoning and recorded restrictions are independent, and the more restrictive governs.

A permit record establishes issuance and a date — not completion, quality, or current condition. An open permit follows the property.

Unpermitted work is a disclosure item, not automatically a disaster. Disclose, recommend permit research and a legalization inquiry, keep it out of advertised square footage without disclosure, and never characterize the risk.

Local transfer requirements vary by jurisdiction and must be checked rather than assumed.

For every environmental hazard the role is the same: disclose what is known including your own observations, recommend appropriate investigation, and do not evaluate. Lead-based paint is triggered by housing age, not by children.

Natural hazard disclosure applies on mapping, not event history, and its most consequential downstream effect is insurance — a first-week item in mapped areas, never a closing task.

Water is three questions: supply, rights, and conservation. On rural property, ask all eight intake questions — and treat "everyone has always used that road" as an access problem, not an answer.

Every land use question has a public half and a private half, and clearing one does not clear the other.

Sequence due diligence by lead time, document what was recommended, ordered, received, and reviewed — and document what did not arrive and what you did about it.

The recurring failure is not wrong land use advice. It is land use advice at all.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Identify which government power is operating on each fact given, separate the public land use questions from the private restriction questions and state who answers each, build a due diligence sequence ordered by lead time with the reason for the ordering, identify every hazard or condition requiring disclosure and state what you would disclose and what you would decline to characterize, identify what you would refer to the jurisdiction, to a specialist, and to counsel, and state the evidence you would create and retain including for anything that could not be obtained in time. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 07

Agency and brokerage relationships

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Identify the three ways agency is created and explain why conduct is the route that surprises licensees.
  • State each fiduciary duty and identify the conduct that breaches it, including breaches involving no dishonesty.
  • Distinguish what is owed to a principal from what is owed to everyone, and locate the boundary between confidentiality and disclosure.
  • Apply California's disclose-elect-confirm sequence and state when each step must occur.
  • Explain the broker's supervisory duty and the salesperson's authority, and why the brokerage is the agent.
  • Disclose compensation conflicts in time to matter, and distinguish the power to terminate an agency from the right to do so without consequence.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: creation of agency and fiduciary duties55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: representation forms and the disclosure sequence50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: supervision, compensation conflicts, and termination45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Creation of agency

1.1 What agency is

Agency is the relationship in which one person is authorized to act on behalf of another in dealings with third parties.

PartyRole
PrincipalThe person represented. Also called the client
AgentThe person authorized to act for the principal
Third partyA participant who is not the agent's principal. In residential practice, the other side

In California residential practice, the broker is the agent and the salesperson acts for the broker. Section 5 develops why that matters.

1.2 The three ways agency arises

MethodHow
Express agreementA listing agreement, representation agreement, or other engagement — written or oral
Conduct (implication)The licensee acted as an agent and the consumer reasonably relied
RatificationThe principal accepts the benefit of acts performed on their behalf, adopting them after the fact

Conduct is the route that surprises licensees, and it is the reason the agency disclosure belongs early rather than at the offer.

A homeowner and a licensee have talked for five weeks. He has advised her on pricing, recommended repairs, and told her what he thinks buyers will accept. Nothing is signed and he has not been paid. He now represents a buyer making an offer on her home and says no agency existed.

He may be wrong. Agency can arise from conduct, and five weeks of advice she reasonably relied on looks like the conduct of an agent. Payment is not what creates duties, and the absence of a signed agreement does not prevent the relationship.

The consequence is not merely that he cannot claim a commission. He may have owed her fiduciary duties throughout, and representing the buyer without disclosure and consent breaches duties actually owed.

1.3 What does not create agency

Payment does not. A buyer's agent compensated from the seller's side still represents the buyer. Who pays and who is represented are separate questions, and this must be explained to clients rather than assumed.

A signed form alone does not, where conduct contradicts it. A form naming one relationship while the licensee behaves as the other party's advisor creates exactly the contradiction that later gets litigated.

1.4 Scope of authority

Agency comes with a scope, and a licensee should know its limits.

Actual authority is what the principal has granted, expressly or by implication from what the engagement requires.

Apparent authority arises where the principal's conduct leads a third party reasonably to believe the agent has authority the agent does not in fact have. It can bind the principal, which is why loose statements about what a licensee may commit to are dangerous.

What a residential licensee ordinarily may not do without specific authority: accept an offer on the client's behalf, sign documents for the client, or bind the client to terms.

1.5 Checkpoint

You should be able to name the three ways agency arises and explain why the disclosure belongs before the relationship has been operating.

2. Fiduciary duties

2.1 What a fiduciary duty is

A fiduciary owes the highest duties the law imposes in a commercial relationship: to act in the principal's interest rather than the fiduciary's own.

The conventional list, with the memory device OLD CAR:

DutyWhat it requires
O — ObedienceFollow the principal's instructions — lawful instructions only
L — LoyaltyPlace the principal's interests ahead of the agent's own
D — DisclosureDisclose material facts known to the agent
C — ConfidentialityProtect the principal's confidential information
A — AccountingAccount for all funds and property belonging to the principal
R — Reasonable care and diligencePerform with the skill of a competent licensee

2.2 Obedience and its limit

Obedience extends to lawful instructions and no further. An instruction to conceal a material defect, to refuse to show property to a category of buyer, or to misrepresent competing offers is not an instruction to be followed.

A principal instructs her agent not to show the property to a category of buyers. The agent believes refusing will cost her the listing.

Required: refuse the instruction, explain that it would be unlawful discrimination, escalate to the broker, and address whether the listing can continue.

Three failures: following it while documenting that it came from the client — documentation does not transfer responsibility for the act; following it informally by scheduling around those buyers — which achieves the same exclusion; and withdrawing quietly without explanation — which leaves the client free to repeat the instruction with the next licensee.

2.3 Loyalty

Loyalty means surfacing conflicts rather than silently resolving them in the agent's favor. The recurring breaches:

  • Acting as an undisclosed principal — buying the client's property, or having an interest in the buyer.
  • Accepting an undisclosed benefit from a third party.
  • Steering the client toward an outcome that pays the agent more.
  • Representing a competing interest without disclosure and consent.

2.4 Disclosure, and the confidentiality boundary

This is the most consequential line in agency practice, and it falls in the same place every time.

Confidentiality protects the principal's negotiating position. It never authorizes concealing a material fact about the property.

Protected as confidentialMust be disclosed
The seller's lowest acceptable priceA known material defect
The buyer's maximumThe condition of the property
The client's motivation and timelineFacts affecting value or desirability
The client's financial positionThe licensee's own observations

A seller's agent learns the foundation was underpinned after movement six years ago. The seller says the work was done properly, produces no records, and instructs the agent not to mention it because "it's fixed."

Disclosure controls. The instruction cannot be followed. Confidentiality protects the negotiating position, not concealment of a material physical condition — and the adequacy of a repair with no records is exactly what a buyer would want to evaluate. Careful silence while avoiding an affirmative false statement is still concealment.

2.5 Reasonable care and diligence

The duty breached most often, and the one that requires no dishonesty at all.

A licensee who repeats an unverified square footage figure, misses a contingency deadline, fails to deliver a disclosure, or does not perform the visual inspection has breached this duty without lying to anyone. Most claims against licensees are negligence claims.

2.6 What is owed to non-clients

Non-clients are not unprotected. Every licensee owes honesty, fair dealing, and disclosure of material facts to everyone in the transaction — the other party, their agent, lenders, escrow, appraisers.

"I don't represent them" answers the wrong question. It correctly identifies whose interests the licensee advances. It says nothing about whether the licensee may mislead them.

The distinction is whose interests you advance, not whether you owe anything.

2.7 Which duty was breached

Section 2.1 through 2.6 state the duties. The skill a principles course is trying to build is recognizing which duty a given failure breaches, because the answer determines the analysis, the remedy, and frequently whether the conduct is negligence or something worse.

The conductThe duty breached
Failing to present a competing offer to the sellerDisclosure
Telling a buyer's agent the lowest price the seller would takeConfidentiality
Buying the client's property through a relative without saying soLoyalty
Missing a contingency deadlineReasonable care
Following an instruction not to show the property to a category of buyersThe obedience limit — the instruction was unlawful, and following it is the licensee's own violation
Depositing an earnest money check into an operating accountAccounting
Recommending a vendor who pays a fee, without disclosing itLoyalty, and the conflict framework
Preparing a pricing analysis from three comparables in a market with twentyReasonable care
Concealing a known material defect at the seller's instructionHonesty to non-clients, and the obedience limit
Substituting the licensee's judgment on price for the seller'sObedience — the decision was the principal's
Telling a buyer client "the seller will take 700" without knowingReasonable care, and it is a misrepresentation
Continuing to use a former client's confidential informationConfidentiality, which survives termination — Section 6.8

Two observations worth carrying.

Reasonable care is the most frequently breached and the least dramatic. Section 2.5 notes it can be breached without dishonesty, and most rows on any real list of claims are careless rather than dishonest. A licensee who does careful work is protected from most of this table without ever facing an ethical dilemma.

Several rows breach two duties at once, and the second is usually the more serious. Concealing a defect at the seller's instruction breaches honesty to the buyer and describes the licensee performing an unlawful instruction. Recommending an undisclosed paid referral breaches loyalty and engages a separate framework with its own consequences.

The diagnostic question that resolves most cases: whose interest was subordinated, and to what? If the answer is "the principal's, to the licensee's," it is loyalty. If it is "the principal's, to nobody's — it was just done badly," it is care. If it is "a non-client's, to the principal's," it is the honesty duty in Section 2.6, and that duty does not bend to fiduciary loyalty.

2.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to name the six duties, state the confidentiality-disclosure boundary, and explain why a careless licensee can breach a fiduciary duty without any dishonesty.

3. Seller, buyer, and dual agency

3.1 The representation forms

FormThe brokerage represents
Seller's agentThe seller only
Buyer's agentThe buyer only
Dual agentBoth parties in the same transaction

A brokerage becomes a dual agent when it represents both sides — including where two different salespersons of the same brokerage represent the two parties, because the brokerage is the agent.

3.2 Dual agency requires informed written consent from both

Not one. Not implied. Not obtained afterwards.

Informed means each party understands what dual agency means and what it limits. Written means documented. From both means each party consents on their own behalf. And the consent must precede the brokerage acting on both sides rather than follow it.

3.3 What dual agency does and does not permit

The single most misunderstood point in agency, and the source of real breaches:

Consent to dual agency permits the brokerage to represent both parties. It does not convert either party's confidential negotiating position into shared information.

Both parties sign a dual agency consent. During negotiation the seller privately tells the broker the lowest number he would take. The buyer asks the broker directly what the seller will accept.

The broker may say nothing about the seller's floor without the seller's permission.

Two wrong answers worth naming: both are clients so both are entitled to what the broker knows — being a client entitles a party to loyalty about their own interests, not to the other client's confidences; and disclosing both sides' limits keeps it balanced — that does not cure the breach, it commits it twice.

A vague answer conveying the same information is still a disclosure. The form of words does not matter.

3.4 What a dual agent can still do

Dual agency is not paralysis. The brokerage can still deliver information, prepare documents, coordinate the transaction, disclose material facts, and advise each party on matters not involving the other's confidences.

What it cannot do is advocate one party's negotiating position against the other's, or move confidential information across.

3.5 The structural tension

A dual agent owes loyalty to two parties whose interests on price are directly opposed. That tension is why consent must be informed — the party is agreeing to a relationship in which the agent cannot advocate for them the way a single-side agent would.

A licensee explaining dual agency badly — "it just means we handle both sides, it's more efficient" — has obtained a signature rather than consent.

3.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to state what dual agency requires, what consent does and does not permit, and why assigning a second salesperson does not avoid it.

4. California agency disclosure

4.1 The three steps

California's agency disclosure operates in three steps that occur at three different times:

StepWhat it is
DiscloseProvide the statutory disclosure describing the relationship options and the duties in each
ElectThe relationship is chosen
ConfirmThe relationship actually elected is recorded, in or with the purchase agreement

4.2 The timing

RoleWhen the disclosure must be provided
Seller's agentBefore the listing agreement is signed
Buyer's agentAs soon as practicable before the buyer signs an offer
Agent presenting an offer to the sellerBefore or at the time the offer is presented

Every one of these is the same principle: the disclosure precedes the decision it informs.

A student who holds that principle can resolve unfamiliar timing questions without memorizing a table. Apply it and the answers fall out: the seller should know whose interests the presenting agent serves while evaluating the offer, not afterward.

4.3 Why late delivery is not cured by delivery

A buyer's agent presents an offer. The agency disclosure reaches the listing side the following week, after acceptance, and is signed and dated then.

The file now contains a signed disclosure and is still defective. The document exists; the protection it was meant to give does not, because the seller made the decision without it.

This is the shape of every timing defect in the course. The remedy for a late disclosure is not a later signature.

4.4 The confirmation

The agency confirmation records which relationship was actually elected. Two failure modes appear in file audits:

A confirmation contradicting the earlier disclosure — naming the brokerage as buyer's agent while the listing file shows seller's agent, with no dual agency consent anywhere. That is not a documentation problem. It describes the brokerage acting on both sides without either party's consent, and it is the most serious finding in a typical residential audit.

A confirmation left blank or unchecked, which leaves the file unable to show what relationship existed.

4.5 The disclosure is not the agreement

A recurring confusion: the agency disclosure describes the relationship options and the duties; the listing or representation agreement creates the engagement and its terms. They are different documents doing different work, and providing one does not accomplish the other.

The agency disclosure is required regardless of whether a representation agreement is signed. A licensee who requires a signed representation agreement before showing property is stating a brokerage policy, which is legitimate — but the disclosure obligation does not depend on it.

4.6 The dates, and what they prove

The three steps in Section 4.1 exist as a sequence, and their dates are what a file audit examines — not their presence.

The chain, with what each date has to precede:

StepIts date must precede
Disclosure to the sellerThe listing agreement. The seller must know what the relationship is before he commits to it.
Disclosure to the buyerThe advice. Section 4.2's timing runs from the outset of the working relationship, not from the offer.
Disclosure to the other partyThe presentation of the offer.
ElectionRecorded in the agreement itself.
Consent to dual agency, where applicableThe brokerage acting on both sides. Consent dated after the conduct is a record of the problem.
ConfirmationNothing — it is the last link, and its job is to be consistent with the four above.

Three defects a file audit finds, in descending order of seriousness:

1. A confirmation that contradicts the file. A purchase agreement confirming the brokerage as buyer's agent while the listing file shows it as seller's agent, with no consent anywhere, describes an undisclosed dual agency — a breach of duties actually owed rather than a documentation failure.

2. A disclosure dated after the agreement. The document exists and the protection it was designed to give never operated.

3. A signature with no evidence of delivery. Section 4.3 establishes that late delivery is not cured by delivery. A file that cannot show when a disclosure reached the party cannot show that it was timely, and the burden of that ambiguity does not fall on the consumer.

Why the buyer side fails more often than the listing side. A listing agreement forces the paperwork; nothing forces it on the buyer side. A licensee can work with a buyer for six weeks, give advice throughout, and produce a disclosure on the day the offer is written — and the file will contain a properly executed document that proves nothing about the six weeks.

The one habit that closes all three defects: date everything at the moment it happens, evidence delivery rather than relying on signature, and read the chain forward as a sequence rather than checking off documents as a set. If the dates do not run in order, something happened out of order — and that is worth knowing while the transaction can still respond to it.

4.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to state when each disclosure is due, explain why a signature after the decision does not cure lateness, and identify what an audit finding of a contradictory confirmation means.

5. Broker supervision and salesperson authority

5.1 The brokerage is the agent

Stated in Lesson 01 and applied here:

The salesperson's client is the broker's client. The listing is the brokerage's contract. The salesperson's compensation comes from the broker.

What follows:

  • Cancellation of a listing is a broker-level decision governed by the agreement, not the individual salesperson's to grant.
  • The transaction file belongs to the brokerage, whoever created the documents.
  • A second salesperson on the other side does not avoid dual agency, because the brokerage is the agent.
  • A salesperson may not contract in her own name for licensed activity.

5.2 The supervisory duty

The responsible broker must exercise reasonable supervision. Discharged by a system:

  1. Written policies covering the conduct required.
  2. File review, close enough in time to catch problems before they mature.
  3. Advertising review before publication.
  4. Trust fund controls, including reconciliation.
  5. Training, particularly for new licensees.
  6. An escalation path a salesperson can actually use.

A policy nobody follows is not supervision, and it is not a defense for anyone — not for the broker who wrote it and not for the salesperson who departed from it.

Independent contractor status does not reduce any of this. It characterizes the tax and compensation relationship and answers a different question.

5.3 Supervision from the receiving end

Licensees encounter supervision failures from below, and the correct responses are worth stating:

SituationResponse
A manager tells you to conceal an errorEscalate to the responsible broker. The instruction does not protect you
Office practice deviates from the written policyRaise it; a policy nobody follows protects no one
You are asked to handle work beyond your competence with no supportSay so in writing and ask for supervision or reassignment

Following an improper instruction and documenting that it came from someone else does not transfer responsibility for the act. It documents that you knew.

5.4 Delegation and unlicensed assistants
