Unlicensed assistants may perform administrative work. They may not perform licensed activity — soliciting, negotiating, showing property, or discussing terms — and they may not be compensated for referrals.

The boundary is not the job title. It is the activity.

5.5 Vicarious responsibility

The broker answers for the salesperson's conduct in the course of the agency, which is why the supervisory system exists and why brokerages care about advertising approval, file review, and policy compliance in ways that can feel bureaucratic to a new licensee.

The principal may also bear responsibility for the agent's conduct within the scope of the agency, which is one reason a seller cannot instruct an agent into unlawful conduct and stand clear of it.

5.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain why a listing is not the salesperson's to cancel, describe what real supervision consists of, and state the correct response to an improper instruction from a manager.

6. Compensation, conflicts, and termination

6.1 Compensation is negotiable

Brokerage compensation is set independently by each brokerage and negotiated with the client. It is not standard, not customary, and not set by any association or listing service.

Describing a rate as standard is both factually wrong and an antitrust exposure. The correct sentence:

"Compensation is negotiable between you and our brokerage. There is no standard rate, and no rate set by any association or listing service. Here is what our brokerage charges and what it covers."

6.2 Source of payment does not determine representation

Restated because clients and licensees both get it wrong: a buyer's agent compensated from the seller's side still represents the buyer. The arrangement must be explained rather than assumed, along with what happens if the seller's side offers less than the agreed compensation or offers none.

6.3 Conflicts of interest

Any interest that could reasonably influence the licensee's advice:

  • A referral fee or thing of value from a service provider.
  • An ownership or financial interest — including a relative's — in a recommended provider.
  • A bonus contingent on a sale or a price threshold.
  • The licensee's own interest, or a relative's, in a property.
  • Representation of another party with a competing interest.
  • A personal relationship a client would want to know about.

6.4 Disclosure must arrive in time to matter

Disclose before the client relies on the advice the conflict affects.

Inadequate approachWhy it fails
"The client is free to choose someone else"The client cannot weigh a recommendation without knowing what the recommender receives
"I'll disclose if they ask"The client does not know to ask
"I won't let it influence me"A private intention is not something the client can evaluate
"I'll disclose after acceptance"Information after the decision has no value
"The fee comes from the seller, not the buyer's pocket"Who pays does not change that the advice carries an interest

6.5 The referral fee prohibition is different

A conflict is ordinarily managed by disclosure. One category is not:

A licensee may not give or accept a fee, kickback, or thing of value for the referral of settlement service business, and may not split a fee where no services were performed. Disclosure does not cure it.

This reaches arrangements that feel like ordinary business courtesy — an escrow company paying per file, a lender covering marketing costs in exchange for referrals, a warranty company paying per policy.

Where the brokerage has an ownership interest in a provider it refers to, that is an affiliated business arrangement carrying its own disclosure and conditions.

6.6 The licensee as principal

Where a licensee buys or sells for their own account, or for a relative or an entity they have an interest in, the licensee's status must be disclosed. A licensee purchasing from a client, or holding an interest in a buyer of a client's property, occupies the highest-risk position in the business, and it requires disclosure, the client's informed consent, and the broker's involvement.

6.7 Termination of agency

Agency ends by:

MethodHow
CompletionThe purpose is accomplished
ExpirationThe stated term ends
Mutual agreementBoth parties agree
RevocationThe principal ends it
RenunciationThe agent ends it
Operation of lawDeath or incapacity of a party, destruction of the property, or similar events

The power to terminate and the right to terminate without consequence are different. A principal generally has the power to revoke, and exercising it may still breach the agreement and expose the principal to a claim. A licensee who tells a seller "you can cancel any time" has described the power and omitted the consequence — and has given a legal opinion about the agreement's terms.

6.8 What survives termination

Confidentiality generally survives. A licensee may not use or disclose a former principal's confidential information afterwards — including for a new client, and including after the property is relisted elsewhere.

A listing expires unsold. Six months later the same licensee represents a buyer interested in the property, now listed with another brokerage. He knows the former seller's lowest acceptable price. He may not use it.

A duty that evaporated when the engagement ended would not be worth having. This is why the obligation is described as surviving rather than lapsing.

Compensation provisions may also survive, including a protection clause applying to buyers identified during the term.

6.9 A worked agency audit

A brokerage reviews one closed file. It contains:

  • (a) A listing agreement dated March 4. An agency disclosure signed by the seller dated March 7.
  • (b) A buyer working with the same brokerage since February, with no disclosure in the buyer file until April 19 — the date of the purchase agreement.
  • (c) A purchase agreement confirming the brokerage as both the listing and the selling brokerage.
  • (d) No dual agency consent from either party.
  • (e) An email dated April 12 from the buyer's salesperson to the buyer: "I talked to our listing side — I think they'd take 690."
  • (f) A referral fee received from a home warranty company, disclosed nowhere in the file.
  • (g) A commission disbursement showing the brokerage paid from both sides.

The findings, ranked.

1. (c) and (d) together: an undisclosed dual agency. Section 3.2 requires informed written consent from both parties, and there is none. This is a breach of duties actually owed rather than a documentation failure, and it is the finding that changes the character of the whole file.

2. (e) The confidential information moved between sides. Section 3.3 states what dual agency does not permit, and this is the clearest possible violation of it: the seller's negotiating position, communicated by the brokerage to the party negotiating against the seller. It also proves item 1 was operating in fact rather than only on paper.

3. (f) The undisclosed referral fee. Section 6.3 and Section 6.4. Money received in connection with a recommendation the client relied on, with no disclosure at any point. Unlike the timing failures below, this involves value moving without the client's knowledge.

4. (b) The disclosure that arrived at the offer. Ten weeks of a working relationship, and the disclosure is dated the day of the purchase agreement. Section 4.3: late delivery is not cured by delivery. The document exists; nothing it was designed to accomplish occurred.

5. (a) The disclosure after the listing agreement. Three days late, and the seller committed before being informed.

6. (g) The two-sided payment. Section 6.2 establishes that source of payment does not determine representation, so this is not itself a violation. It is listed because it is the item an examiner uses to find item 1 — the disbursement is what makes the dual agency visible in thirty seconds.

What the brokerage does. Records every finding with its date; preserves the file including item (e); escalates to counsel, because items 1, 2, and 3 are not internal training matters; determines what obligation exists to the parties, which is a legal question; and changes the procedures that produced items (a), (b), and (d) — because a brokerage whose buyer files have no disclosure step and whose system does not detect that both sides are its own will produce this file again.

What the brokerage does not do. Produce a consent now, re-date item (b), or add a referral disclosure to the file today. A documented gap is a compliance problem. A manufactured document is a different proceeding, and every one of these findings is survivable in a way that fabrication is not.

6.10 The agency questions a licensee answers, and the ones he does not

This lesson has drawn several boundaries. Collecting them makes the working rule visible.

What a licensee explains, and should be able to explain well:

  • What the three disclosure steps are and when each happens. Section 4.1 and Section 4.2.
  • What representation means — that the brokerage owes the client fiduciary duties and owes everyone honesty, fair dealing, and disclosure of material facts.
  • That the brokerage, not the individual, is the agent. Section 5.1, which surprises consumers and licensees alike.
  • That who pays does not determine who is represented. Section 6.2, and consumers regularly assume the opposite.
  • What dual agency is, what it requires, and what it costs the parties — specifically that no one will advocate against the other side on price. Sections 3.2 through 3.5.
  • That compensation is negotiable. Section 6.1.
  • What conflicts exist in this transaction, disclosed before the client acts. Section 6.4.

What routes to counsel:

  • Whether a particular agency relationship arose on particular facts.
  • The legal effect of a defective or late disclosure.
  • Whether a duty was breached and what follows.
  • What a representation agreement's provisions mean for the client's obligations.
  • Whether the client can terminate, and what termination costs. Section 6.7 describes the mechanisms; the application is legal.

What routes to the broker:

  • Any dual agency, at the moment it becomes foreseeable rather than at the offer.
  • Any request to release a client from an agreement.
  • Any instruction the licensee believes is unlawful. Section 2.2's limit.
  • Any conduct by another licensee that engages a duty — including confidential information volunteered by the other side.
  • Any compensation arrangement that varies from the brokerage's standard.

The organizing principle across all three lists. A licensee explains how the framework works; counsel says what it means for these facts; the broker decides what the brokerage does. Almost every agency question a consumer asks fits one of those three, and a licensee who can sort them in the moment will handle correctly a great many situations he has never seen.

And the one question that appears in all three lists at once, which is why it is the hardest: "Whose side are you on?" The explanation is the licensee's, the legal characterization is counsel's, and if the honest answer is uncomfortable, that discomfort is information the broker needs before the transaction goes further.

6.11 Checkpoint

You should be able to state the negotiability sentence correctly, explain why the referral fee prohibition is not cured by disclosure, and say what survives the end of an agency.

Key terms

TermDefinition
AgencyThe relationship in which one person is authorized to act on behalf of another in dealings with third parties.
Principal / clientThe party represented.
AgentThe party authorized to act for the principal. In California residential practice, the broker.
Third partyA participant who is not the agent's principal. Owed honesty, fair dealing, and disclosure of material facts.
Agency by conductAgency arising because the licensee acted as an agent and the consumer reasonably relied. The route that surprises licensees.
RatificationAgency created when a principal accepts the benefit of acts performed on their behalf.
Actual authorityWhat the principal granted, expressly or by implication from what the engagement requires.
Apparent authorityAuthority a third party reasonably believes the agent has because of the principal's conduct. Can bind the principal.
OLD CARObedience, Loyalty, Disclosure, Confidentiality, Accounting, Reasonable care — the fiduciary duties.
ObedienceFollowing the principal's instructions — lawful instructions only.
LoyaltyPlacing the principal's interests ahead of the agent's own, and surfacing conflicts rather than silently resolving them.
The confidentiality boundaryConfidentiality protects the negotiating position. It never authorizes concealing a material fact about the property.
Reasonable care and diligenceThe duty breached most often, and breachable by carelessness alone with no dishonesty.
What is owed to everyoneHonesty, fair dealing, and disclosure of material facts. "I don't represent them" answers a different question.
Dual agencyOne brokerage representing both parties — including through two different salespersons, because the brokerage is the agent.
Informed written consentWhat dual agency requires from both parties, understanding what it means and limits, in writing, before the brokerage acts on both sides.
What consent permitsRepresentation of both parties. Not the movement of either party's confidential position to the other, and a vague answer conveying it is still a disclosure.
Disclose, elect, confirmCalifornia's three-step agency disclosure, occurring at three different times.
The timing principleThe disclosure precedes the decision it informs — before the listing is signed, before the buyer signs an offer, and before or when the offer is presented.
Agency confirmationThe record of the relationship actually elected. A confirmation contradicting the earlier disclosure with no dual agency consent is the most serious ordinary audit finding.
Reasonable supervisionAn affirmative duty discharged by a system: policies, file review, advertising review, trust fund controls, training, and a usable escalation path.
Documenting an improper instructionDoes not transfer responsibility for the act. It documents that you knew.
Negotiable compensationSet independently by each brokerage and negotiated with the client. Never standard, customary, or set by an association or listing service.
Source of paymentDoes not determine representation. A buyer's agent paid from the seller's side still represents the buyer.
Conflict of interestAny interest that could reasonably influence the licensee's advice. Managed by disclosure that arrives before the client relies on the advice.
Referral fee prohibitionNo fee, kickback, or thing of value for referring settlement service business, and no fee split where no services were performed. Disclosure does not cure it.
Affiliated business arrangementWhere the brokerage holds an ownership interest in a provider it refers to. Carries its own disclosure and conditions.
Power versus right to terminateA principal generally has the power to revoke; exercising it may still breach the agreement.
Survival of confidentialityConfidentiality generally survives the end of the agency — including for a new client and after the property is relisted.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A homeowner and a licensee have talked for five weeks. He advised her on pricing, recommended repairs, and told her what buyers would accept. Nothing is signed and he has not been paid. He now represents a buyer offering on her home and says no agency existed with her.

Is he right, and what is the consequence?

Solution. He may well be wrong. Agency can arise from conduct, and five weeks of advice she reasonably relied on looks like the conduct of an agent. Payment is not what creates duties, and the absence of a signed agreement does not prevent the relationship.

The consequence is not merely that he cannot claim a commission. He may have owed her fiduciary duties throughout, and representing the buyer without disclosure and consent breaches duties actually owed.

What transfers: this is precisely why the agency disclosure belongs early rather than at the offer. The relationship may already exist while the consumer is unaware of it — and the licensee frequently is too.

Problem 2

A seller's agent learns the foundation was underpinned after movement six years ago. The seller says the work was done properly, produces no records, and instructs the agent not to mention it because "it's fixed." Separately, the seller tells her the lowest price he will accept, and the buyer's agent asks her directly what the seller will take.

Which instruction binds her, and what may she say about the price?

Solution. The two facts fall on opposite sides of the same line, and the line is always in the same place.

The foundation must be disclosed. Confidentiality protects the seller's negotiating position, not concealment of a material physical condition — and the adequacy of a repair with no records is exactly what a buyer would want to evaluate. Obedience extends to lawful instructions only, so this one cannot be followed. She tells the seller it must be disclosed and why, involves the broker, and does not follow the instruction. Careful silence while avoiding an affirmative false statement is still concealment.

The lowest price is confidential and stays confidential. She may say nothing about it without the seller's permission.

What transfers: the confidentiality-disclosure boundary is the most consequential line in agency practice, and it resolves the majority of agency questions.

Problem 3

A brokerage lists a home. A buyer the same brokerage has been working with wants to offer. Both parties sign a dual agency consent. During negotiation the seller privately tells the broker his floor. The buyer asks directly what the seller will accept. The broker considers three answers: telling him, since both are clients; disclosing both sides' limits to keep it balanced; and giving a vague answer that gets the point across.

Assess each.

Solution. All three breach the duty, and the third is the one licensees think is safe.

Consent to dual agency permits the brokerage to represent both parties. It does not convert either party's confidential negotiating position into shared information.

Both are clients so both are entitled to what I know — being a client entitles a party to loyalty about their own interests, not to the other client's confidences.

Disclosing both sides' limits keeps it balanced — that does not cure the breach, it commits it twice.

A vague answer conveying the same information is still a disclosure. The form of words does not matter.

What the broker may say: nothing about the seller's floor without the seller's permission. Dual agency is not paralysis — the brokerage can still deliver information, prepare documents, coordinate, disclose material facts, and advise each party on matters not involving the other's confidences. What it cannot do is advocate one negotiating position against the other or move confidences across.

Problem 4

A buyer's agent presents an offer. The agency disclosure reaches the listing side the following week, after acceptance, and is signed and dated then. The file also contains a confirmation naming the brokerage as buyer's agent, while the listing file shows it as seller's agent, and there is no dual agency consent anywhere.

Rank the two findings and state the correct response.

Solution. The contradiction is the more serious, and it is different in kind. It describes the brokerage acting on both sides without either party's informed written consent — a breach of duties actually owed, not a documentation failure. In an ordinary residential file audit this is the most serious finding available.

The late disclosure is second. It must be provided before or at the time the offer is presented, so the seller knows whose interests the presenting agent serves while evaluating it. The file now contains a signed disclosure and is still defective: the document exists, the protection it was meant to give does not, because the seller decided without it. A later signature does not cure lateness.

The correct response: record both findings, escalate to the broker, and obtain anything that legitimately exists. What must not happen is creating the consent that was never obtained or re-dating the disclosure. A documented gap is survivable; a manufactured document is falsification.

Problem 5

A seller wants to cancel her listing two weeks in. The salesperson who took it says he is willing to release her. Separately, the brokerage's manager tells him to leave a square footage error uncorrected because escrow closes in four days, and an unlicensed assistant has been calling expired listings to solicit business, paid $300 per listing obtained.

Assess all three.

Solution. The listing is not his to release. It is the brokerage's contract with the seller, and cancellation is a broker-level decision governed by the agreement's terms. The same principle determines who owns the file and why a second salesperson would not cure dual agency.

The manager's instruction must be escalated above him. A licensee told to conceal a known error escalates to the responsible broker — the instruction does not protect her, and following an improper instruction while documenting that it came from someone else does not transfer responsibility for the act. It documents that she knew.

The assistant is performing licensed activity and being compensated for it. Soliciting listings is licensed activity, and the boundary is the activity, not the job title. Unlicensed assistants may perform administrative work; they may not solicit, negotiate, show property, discuss terms, or be compensated for referrals.

What ties them together: reasonable supervision is a system — policies, file review, advertising review, trust fund controls, training, and a usable escalation path. Two of these three failures are supervision failures at the manager level.

Problem 6

A seller asks why the listing states a negotiated compensation figure rather than "the standard rate." The licensee's brokerage also receives $300 per file from its preferred escrow company, and the licensee's brother owns the home warranty provider she recommends. She plans to mention both after the offer is accepted, noting that clients are always free to use someone else.

Answer the seller and assess the two arrangements.

Solution. The answer to the seller: "Compensation is negotiable between you and our brokerage. There is no standard rate, and no rate set by any association or listing service. Here is what our brokerage charges and what it covers." Describing a rate as standard or customary is both factually wrong and an antitrust exposure.

The $300 per file is prohibited, not merely undisclosed. A licensee may not give or accept a fee, kickback, or thing of value for referring settlement service business, or split a fee where no services were performed — and disclosure does not cure it. This is what makes it different from an ordinary conflict, and its resemblance to routine business courtesy is exactly why it catches people.

The brother's company is a conflict requiring disclosure before the client relies on the advice it affects. Both of her justifications fail: "free to choose someone else" ignores that a client cannot weigh a recommendation without knowing what the recommender receives, and disclosure after acceptance arrives after the decision it should have informed.

Problem 7

A listing expires unsold. Six months later the same licensee represents a buyer interested in the property, now listed with another brokerage. He knows the former seller's lowest acceptable price and that she needed to close by a certain date.

May he use either fact?

Solution. No, to both. Confidentiality generally survives the end of the agency. He may not use or disclose a former principal's confidential information afterwards — including for a new client, and including after the property is relisted elsewhere.

Her floor and her timeline are both negotiating-position information, protected for the same reason.

Why the rule takes this shape: a duty that evaporated when the engagement ended would not be worth having. That is why the obligation is described as surviving rather than lapsing.

Worth noting alongside it: compensation provisions may also survive, including a protection clause applying to buyers identified to the seller during the term.

Problem 8

A seller under an exclusive listing tells her agent she is unhappy and wants out. The agent says "you can cancel any time — just let me know." A week later the brokerage sends her an invoice for marketing costs and asserts a compensation claim.

What was wrong with the agent's statement?

Solution. He conflated the power to terminate with the right to terminate without consequence.

A principal generally has the power to revoke an agency. Exercising it may nonetheless breach the agreement and expose the principal to a claim — which is exactly what happened here.

And the statement was a legal opinion about the agreement's terms, given confidently by someone not entitled to give it, on which she acted.

What he should have done: state that the decision is a broker-level one governed by the agreement's cancellation and compensation terms, escalate to the broker, and — a useful first step — find out what she actually wants. "I want to cancel" often means "nothing is happening and no one is telling me why," which is a communication problem with a different fix.

Agency ends by completion, expiration, mutual agreement, revocation, renunciation, or operation of law. None of those routes automatically extinguishes what the agreement says about compensation.

Chapter summary

Agency arises by express agreement, by conduct, or by ratification. Conduct is the route that surprises licensees, and it is why the disclosure belongs early.

Payment does not create or determine agency. A buyer's agent paid from the seller's side still represents the buyer.

Six fiduciary duties — OLD CAR — run to the principal. Obedience covers lawful instructions only, and reasonable care is the duty most often breached, by carelessness alone.

Confidentiality protects the negotiating position and never authorizes concealing a material fact. That single boundary resolves most agency questions.

Honesty, fair dealing, and disclosure of material facts run to everyone. "I don't represent them" answers a different question.

A brokerage is a dual agent when it represents both sides, including through two salespersons, and it requires informed written consent from both, before it acts on both sides.

Consent permits representation, not disclosure. Neither party's confidences cross, disclosing both does not balance it, and a vague answer conveying the information is still a disclosure.

Disclose, elect, confirm — at three different times, each preceding the decision it informs. A signature after the decision does not cure a late disclosure, and a confirmation contradicting the disclosure with no consent is the most serious ordinary audit finding.

The brokerage is the agent and the party. The listing is not the salesperson's to cancel, the file is the brokerage's, and a salesperson may not contract in her own name.

Supervision is a system, not a manual, and independent contractor status does not reduce it. Documenting an improper instruction does not transfer responsibility — it documents that you knew.

Compensation is negotiable and never standard. Say the sentence correctly.

Conflicts are managed by disclosure that arrives before reliance — with one exception: paying or accepting anything of value for a settlement service referral is prohibited outright, and disclosure does not cure it.

Agency ends six ways, and the power to terminate is not the right to do so without consequence. Confidentiality, and often compensation provisions, survive the ending.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Identify how any agency relationship arose and with whom, state which fiduciary duties are engaged and by what conduct, locate any confidentiality-versus-disclosure question and resolve it, state what disclosure was required and by when and whether the timing was satisfied, identify any conflict and when it had to be disclosed, state what you would decline to do and why, identify what you would escalate to the broker and what you would refer to counsel, and state the evidence you would create and retain. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 08

Contracts and transaction formation

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Identify the elements required to form a contract and classify contracts by the standard categories.
  • Apply the offer, acceptance, and counteroffer rules to determine whether a contract exists and on whose terms.
  • Explain consideration, capacity, and legality, and identify what makes a contract void, voidable, or unenforceable.
  • State what the Statute of Frauds requires in real estate and identify the recurring exceptions.
  • Explain what a contingency does inside a formed contract and how performance obligations are discharged.
  • Distinguish the remedies available for breach and explain what a licensee may say about them.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: contract elements and formation55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: consideration, capacity, legality, and the Statute of Frauds50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: contingencies, performance, and remedies45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Contract elements

1.1 The four elements

A contract is an agreement the law will enforce. Four elements are required:

ElementWhat it requires
Mutual assentAn offer and an acceptance — a meeting of the minds on the same terms
ConsiderationSomething of value exchanged, or a legally recognized substitute
CapacityParties legally able to contract
Lawful objectA purpose the law permits

Where any is missing, the analysis in Section 3 determines whether what exists is void, voidable, or unenforceable — three different outcomes that students routinely merge.

1.2 The classifications

Contracts are described along several axes, and the vocabulary appears throughout the transaction.

AxisTypes
FormationExpress — stated in words. Implied — inferred from conduct
ObligationBilateral — a promise for a promise. Unilateral — a promise for an act
PerformanceExecutory — something remains to be done. Executed — fully performed
EnforceabilityValid, void, voidable, unenforceable

1.3 Bilateral and unilateral, in practice

A residential purchase agreement is bilateral. The buyer promises to buy; the seller promises to sell. Both are bound on formation.

An open listing or a reward is unilateral. The offeror promises to pay if someone performs an act; nobody is bound to perform.

An exclusive right to sell listing is bilateral — the brokerage promises to market and the seller promises to pay on the terms agreed.

The distinction matters because in a unilateral contract there is no breach by failing to act. Nobody promised to.

1.4 Executory and executed

A purchase agreement is executory from acceptance until closing. Both parties still owe performance.

It becomes executed at closing, when both have fully performed.

The vocabulary trips students because "executed" also colloquially means "signed." In contract classification it means fully performed, and a signed but unperformed agreement is executory.

1.5 What a licensee's role in contracts actually is

A licensee prepares transaction documents within the scope of the transaction and explains what provisions do in the transaction.

A licensee does not advise on legal effect, on rights and remedies, on whether a party may cancel or refuse to perform, or on entitlement to money. The test from Lesson 01 governs: would answering require a legal conclusion?

This lesson teaches contract principles so a licensee can recognize what is happening and route it — not so the licensee can advise on it.

1.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to name the four elements, classify a purchase agreement on all four axes, and state what "executed" means in classification.

2. Offer, acceptance, and counteroffer

2.1 The three formation rules

Three rules resolve most questions in practice:

  1. An offer is a proposal that, if accepted without change, forms a contract.
  2. An acceptance must match the offer. Any change to a material term is not an acceptance.
  3. Acceptance must be communicated to the offeror in the manner the offer or the law contemplates.

The third rule is the one that gets skipped. A signed acceptance sitting in a drawer, or in a signing platform nobody delivered, has not been communicated — and no contract has formed.

2.2 What makes an offer an offer

An offer must show present intent to be bound, be reasonably definite in its terms, and be communicated to the offeree.

Statements that are not offers: an invitation to negotiate, an advertisement in the ordinary case, a listing, or a statement of what someone might accept. A listing invites offers; it is not one.

2.3 The counteroffer

The single most misunderstood formation rule, and it is worth stating in full:

A counteroffer rejects the original offer and substitutes a new one. The original can no longer be accepted unless it is renewed.

Two consequences licensees regularly get wrong:

  • It is not an "acceptance with modifications" forming a contract on the counterofferor's terms.
  • It does not suspend the original offer, leaving it available if the counteroffer is rejected. The original is gone.

And it works in both directions. A party who signs a counteroffer but writes a change on it before returning it has made a further counteroffer, however small the change. Signing does not convert a modification into an acceptance.

2.4 Revocation, rejection, lapse, and death

An offer can end without ever being accepted:

MethodEffect
RevocationThe offeror withdraws it before acceptance — generally permitted even where the offer states a deadline, unless supported by consideration as an option
RejectionThe offeree declines; a counteroffer is a rejection
LapseThe stated time expires, or a reasonable time passes where none is stated
Death or incapacityOf either party, before acceptance, generally terminates the offer

The revocation rule surprises people in both directions. A buyer's offer stated to be open until Friday can be withdrawn Wednesday, and a buyer relying on the deadline should know that. A seller intending to accept should not assume the offer will still be there.

2.5 The option

An option is a contract in which the optionor gives the optionee the right to buy on stated terms within a stated time, supported by consideration.

The difference from an ordinary offer is precisely that consideration makes it irrevocable for the period. That is what the optionee paid for.

An option is generally unilateral in the sense that the optionee is not obligated to exercise it — but the option contract itself binds the optionor.

2.6 Electronic signature and delivery

Electronic signature is ordinary in California residential practice. Two points matter:

Delivery still has to happen and still has to be evidenced. An executed document in a signing platform that no one delivered has not been delivered, and the platform's audit trail is frequently the best evidence of when it was.

The contract's own notice provisions govern what constitutes delivery for a communication carrying legal effect. Delivery by a method the contract does not authorize may not count.

2.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to trace a sequence of signed documents and say at which point, if any, a contract formed and on whose terms.

3. Consideration, capacity, and legality

3.1 Consideration

Consideration is what each party gives in exchange — a benefit conferred or a detriment incurred. It is what distinguishes a contract from a gift promise.

Points that recur:

Adequacy is generally not examined. Courts do not ordinarily weigh whether the price was a good deal. Gross inadequacy may be evidence of something else — fraud, duress, or incapacity — but by itself it is not a defect.

Past consideration is generally not consideration. A promise to pay for something already done, with nothing new exchanged, is generally not enforceable as a contract.

Forbearance can be consideration. Agreeing not to do something you have a right to do can support a promise.

3.2 Capacity

Parties must be legally able to contract. The recurring categories:

CategoryEffect
MinorsContracts of a minor are generally voidable by the minor
Mental incapacityDepending on the circumstances, a contract may be voidable, or void where a guardianship or conservatorship applies
EntitiesCapacity depends on existence and on internal authorization

A licensee's practical concern is authority, not diagnosis. A licensee does not assess mental capacity. Where a party's capacity is genuinely in question, the licensee's role is to stop, escalate to the broker, and route it to counsel — never to proceed on the theory that the person seemed fine, and never to characterize a person's capacity in the file.

3.3 Lawful object

A contract for an unlawful purpose is not enforceable, and a contract can be unlawful in part.

Two forms recur in real estate:

Contracts to accomplish an unlawful act — an agreement to conceal a material defect, or to discriminate in the sale or rental of housing.

Provisions the law will not enforce — a lease clause purporting to waive the habitability obligation, or a restriction that discriminates on a protected basis. The provision fails whether or not both parties agreed to it.

3.4 Void, voidable, and unenforceable

Three outcomes students merge, with three different consequences.

What it meansExample
VoidNo contract exists and never did. Nobody can enforce itAn agreement for an unlawful purpose
VoidableA valid contract that one party may elect to avoidA minor's contract; one induced by fraud or duress
UnenforceableA contract the court will not enforce, though it may have other effectsAn oral agreement within the Statute of Frauds

The critical distinction is between void and voidable. A voidable contract is valid and binding until the party entitled to avoid it does so — and that party may instead choose to affirm it. A void agreement was never a contract at all.

3.5 Defects in assent

Even where the four elements appear present, assent can be defective:

Fraud — a knowing misstatement or concealment of a material fact, intended to be relied on and relied on.

Misrepresentation — including negligent misrepresentation, made without reasonable basis or care. Most claims against licensees are here rather than in fraud.

Duress and undue influence — coercion, or improper pressure exploiting a relationship of trust or a party's weakness.

Mistake — mutual mistake about a material fact may affect a contract; a unilateral mistake generally does not, with exceptions.

Whether any of these applies is a legal conclusion. A licensee who recognizes the pattern escalates and refers; a licensee who tells a client "you were defrauded, you can get out of this" has practiced law and may be wrong.

3.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to distinguish void from voidable and state the practical difference, and explain what a licensee does when capacity is in question.

4. Statute of Frauds

4.1 The requirement

The Statute of Frauds requires certain contracts to be in writing and signed by the party to be charged in order to be enforceable.

In real estate the categories that matter:

  • Contracts for the sale of real property or an interest in it.
  • Leases exceeding one year.
  • Agreements to pay compensation for services in connection with the sale or purchase of real property — the reason a listing agreement's compensation provision is written.
  • Agreements not to be performed within one year.

4.2 What "in writing" requires

The writing must generally identify the parties, describe the property, state the price or the method of determining it, and reflect the essential terms — signed by the party against whom enforcement is sought.

It need not be a single elegant document in every case; the essential point is a signed writing containing the essential terms. But a transaction assembled from fragments is a transaction in trouble, which is why practice uses a complete written agreement.

4.3 The consequence of non-compliance

An oral agreement within the statute is unenforceable, not void. That distinction has consequences: the agreement may have other effects, a party may perform voluntarily, and doctrines exist under which a court may enforce despite the absence of a writing.

Part performance is the recurring one: where a buyer has taken possession, paid, and made improvements in reliance, a court may enforce an oral agreement. Estoppel doctrines may operate similarly where one party relied to their detriment.

These are legal doctrines with requirements. A licensee should know they exist — so as not to tell a client that an oral agreement is worthless — and should route the question rather than answering it.

4.4 Why this matters to a licensee daily

Because the practical rule that flows from it is one licensees violate constantly:

Every agreement affecting the transaction goes in a signed writing.

An extension of the closing date, a change to what conveys, an agreed repair, a price adjustment — each is a change to a term of the contract, made by written amendment signed by both parties and delivered to escrow.

What does not change a contract: an email between the agents, a verbal agreement between the parties, an instruction to escrow alone, or a shared understanding that a short extension "won't matter."

The parties agree during escrow to move closing by a week. The agents confirm by email. Escrow is told. No amendment is signed.

If the transaction later fails, the parties are litigating on a contract whose closing date is the original one, with an email chain between non-parties as the evidence of a change.

4.5 Oral agreements that are enforceable

Not everything must be written. Many agreements are enforceable orally, including agreements outside the statute's categories and short leases. The professional practice of writing everything down is a risk practice, not a legal requirement in every case — and it is worth doing precisely because the boundary is not obvious in the moment.

4.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to state which real estate agreements must be written, explain why an oral agreement is unenforceable rather than void, and state what actually amends a contract.

5. Contingencies and performance

5.1 What a contingency is

A contingency conditions a party's obligation on a defined event or approval, inside a contract that has already been formed.

Three things it is not:

  • It does not delay formation. The contract exists.
  • It is not a general right to cancel for any reason.
  • It does not transfer the underlying responsibility to the other party.

5.2 The common residential contingencies

ContingencyWhat it protects
Investigation / inspectionThe buyer's ability to evaluate condition
LoanThe buyer's ability to obtain the financing described
AppraisalThe buyer's exposure if value does not support the price
Sale of buyer's propertyThe buyer's dependence on another closing
Review of documentsTime to review disclosures, reports, association documents
Title reviewThe buyer's ability to evaluate title exceptions

5.3 How removal actually works

The single most misunderstood mechanic in California residential practice:

Under the standard California residential purchase agreement form, contingencies remain in effect until the buyer removes them in writing. They do not expire automatically when the date passes.

Consequences:

  • A buyer who lets the date pass without delivering a removal has not removed the contingency.
  • A seller cannot treat the transaction as firm merely because the date passed.
  • The seller's route is to deliver the notice the contract requires, giving the buyer a defined period to act, after which the seller may cancel per the contract.

The habit is more durable than the rule: read the contract's own removal mechanism. Different forms and negotiated terms can operate differently, and the contract in front of you governs.

5.4 Time and performance

Nearly every obligation carries a date or a period, and those are terms of the contract rather than administrative targets.

Contracts define how time is counted, and the definitions vary: calendar days versus business days, whether the triggering day counts, when a period ending on a weekend or holiday rolls, and what time of day a deadline falls. Read the definitions in the contract in front of you. A licensee who assumes and is wrong by one day has cost a client a right.

5.5 Discharge of obligations

A contractual obligation can end several ways:

MethodHow
PerformanceThe obligation is carried out
Substantial performancePerformance falling short in a minor respect may discharge the obligation, subject to an allowance
AgreementThe parties release, rescind, or substitute — including novation, where a new party or contract is substituted with consent
ImpossibilityPerformance becomes objectively impossible
Operation of lawIncluding the running of limitations periods
Breach by the other partyWhich may excuse further performance

Assignment and novation are worth distinguishing. Assignment transfers rights, and the assignor may remain liable. Novation substitutes a party with all parties' consent and generally releases the original. A seller who "let the buyer assign" and assumes he is released has assumed a novation he did not obtain.

5.6 Waiver

A party may waive a contract right — a contingency, a condition, a requirement. Two points the client must understand:

  1. Waiving the contingency removes the contractual path to act, not the ability to investigate. A buyer may still inspect; he simply has no contractual route to respond to what he finds.
  2. The exposure is concrete. Where an appraisal contingency is waived and the appraisal is low, the buyer's options narrow to bringing funds, renegotiating without leverage, or defaulting with his deposit exposed.

The licensee's obligation is to put that in writing before the buyer decides, then follow the decision. Both failure modes are wrong: refusing to write what the client instructs overstates the licensee's role; writing it without comment abandons the advisory duty.

5.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain what a contingency does inside a formed contract, state how removal works under the standard form, and distinguish assignment from novation.

6. Breach, rescission, and remedies

6.1 What breach is

A breach is a failure to perform a contractual obligation without a legal excuse. A material breach goes to the essence of the bargain and may excuse the other party's further performance; a minor breach generally does not.

Whether a given failure is material is a legal question, and it is one clients ask licensees constantly.

6.2 The remedies

RemedyWhat it is
DamagesMoney compensating the injured party
Specific performanceA court order requiring the actual performance promised
RescissionUnwinding the contract and restoring the parties to their prior positions
Liquidated damagesAn amount the parties agreed in advance would be the recovery on default
ReformationCorrecting a writing to reflect what the parties actually agreed

6.3 Specific performance and why real estate is different

Specific performance is available for real property in a way it is not for most goods, and the reason traces to Lesson 01: uniqueness. No two parcels occupy the same location, so damages may not make a buyer whole — only the parcel will.

This is the clearest example in the course of a legal rule flowing directly from a physical characteristic of land.

6.4 Liquidated damages

Where the parties agree in advance that a specified amount — commonly the deposit, subject to limits — will be the seller's recovery on the buyer's default, that is a liquidated damages provision.

Three points:

  • It must be properly agreed, which typically requires specific formalities.
  • Its enforceability is constrained by law. It is not a penalty the seller may simply impose.
  • It generally limits the recovery rather than adding to it.

6.5 Rescission

Rescission unwinds the contract, and it typically requires restoration — each party giving back what they received. It is available on grounds including fraud, mistake, duress, undue influence, and failure of consideration, and it is sometimes available by agreement.

A licensee should recognize that rescission is a remedy with requirements, not a synonym for cancellation. Cancelling under a contract's own contingency provision is not rescission; it is performing the contract according to its terms.

6.6 What the licensee must not do

This section produces more improper legal advice than any other in the course, because the questions arrive in emotional moments.

Must not:

  • Tell a party whether they may cancel, rescind, or refuse to perform.
  • Tell a party whether they are entitled to the deposit.
  • Characterize a breach as material or minor.
  • Predict what a court would do.
  • Advise a party to stop performing.
  • Instruct escrow on a party's behalf, or attempt to resolve a dispute between the parties.

Must:

  • Explain the mechanism — that escrow acts only on mutual written instructions, that the contract has a cancellation procedure, that the deposit is held pending resolution.
  • Follow the contract's procedures for any notice the licensee's client wishes to give.
  • Document communications and their dates.
  • Escalate to the broker and recommend counsel, before any deadline passes.

A buyer whose loan is denied asks whether he gets his deposit back.

Correct: "That's a legal question and I can't answer it. What I can tell you is the mechanism: the contract has a cancellation procedure with a written notice requirement, escrow can only act on instructions both parties sign, and if you disagree the deposit stays put until it's resolved. Let's get the notice right and get you an answer from an attorney before the date runs."

6.7 Cancellation is a procedure

Cancellation is accomplished by exercising a right the contract provides, following the procedure the contract specifies. It is not accomplished by announcing an intention, by ceasing to perform, or by instructing escrow.

What forfeits a contingency's protection: notifying the other agent verbally, instructing escrow unilaterally to return the deposit, or simply ceasing to perform and letting the other party cancel for non-performance — which is a materially worse position.

6.8 What actually prevents disputes

Most transaction disputes are not about substance. They are about whether something was delivered, to whom, and when — which is why the practices that prevent them are documentary:

  1. Build the deadline calendar from the contract and share it in writing.
  2. Deliver notices by the authorized method and keep the evidence.
  3. Confirm materially important conversations in writing.
  4. Amend in writing, signed, and deliver to escrow.
  5. Correct errors immediately, in writing, to everyone who received the original.
  6. Keep superseded drafts, so the sequence of versions shows how terms changed.
  7. Audit the file at closing while memories are fresh.

6.9 The contract questions a licensee must route

This lesson describes contract law because a licensee works with contracts daily. It does not qualify him to answer questions about them, and the boundary is harder here than anywhere else in the course, because the questions arrive constantly and they sound like transaction questions.

The questionWhy it routes
"Is this contract still enforceable?"Enforceability is the legal conclusion, and it depends on facts and law rather than on the document's appearance
"Did they breach?"A legal characterization with consequences attached to it
"Can I cancel and get my deposit back?"Two legal questions — the right to cancel and the disposition of the deposit
"What are my damages?"Section 6.1's territory, and none of it is the licensee's
"Does the liquidated damages clause apply here?"Section 6.4 explains the concept; whether it operates on these facts is counsel's
"Can I sue for specific performance?"Section 6.3 explains why the remedy exists; whether to pursue it is legal advice
"Is this counteroffer still open?"Formation and revocation, applied to facts — the practice course's worked formation problem shows how quickly this gets hard
"Was that email an acceptance?"Same
"Can I add a clause saying they'll pay if the appraisal is low?"Drafting a provision that alters rights. Section 4.1 of Lesson 01
"They signed but wrote a note in the margin — what does that do?"Formation, again, and the answer is genuinely uncertain
"Do I have to disclose that the last deal fell through?"A disclosure question with a legal answer

What the licensee can do with every one of these, and it is substantial:

  1. Explain what the document provides for procedurally — what step comes next, what the deadline is, what the form requires.
  2. Identify that a legal question exists, which is a service most clients cannot perform for themselves.
  3. Recommend counsel in writing, with a date, before the deadline that makes the answer moot.
  4. Preserve the facts — the dates, the communications, the sequence — so that counsel is answering from a record rather than from recollection.
  5. Escalate to the broker, who decides what the brokerage does.

The distinction that makes this workable. "Your contingency period ends Friday at 5" is a fact about the document. "Your contingency protects you if you cancel Friday" is a legal opinion about your rights, and the two sentences are one word apart.

And the reason the boundary is protective rather than restrictive. Section 4.3 of Lesson 01 makes the general point; here it is specific. A licensee who answers a contract question and is wrong has caused a loss he is answerable for, on a subject where he had no authority to speak and where the client had a right to a qualified answer. The referral costs nothing and transfers the question to the person who can answer it.

6.10 What actually goes wrong in formation

The elements in Section 1 and the mechanics in Section 2 describe how a contract forms. Real transactions fail formation in a small number of recognizable ways, and each is prevented by a habit rather than by knowledge.

The failureWhat happenedThe habit that prevents it
Acceptance not communicatedA signature exists; nothing shows it reached the offeror before expirationTransmit and confirm receipt the moment a document is signed
Signed after expirationThe offer lapsed before the signatureCalendar every expiration, on both sides
A counteroffer treated as an acceptanceA term was changed; the original offer is terminated and a new one existsRead what was returned rather than assuming it came back the way it went out
Two documents both signedAn offer and a counteroffer both executed, in an unclear orderOne live document at a time, and the sequence dated
A term left blankFilled in later, by someone, off the pageNever transmit an incomplete document, and never complete one after signature
An oral agreement to change a termSection 4's problem, and the operative document still says the originalSame-day written amendment, without exception
Delivery to the wrong personSent to a party rather than through the agreed channel, or to an address never consented toUse the transmission method the agreement specifies, and evidence it
Signature by someone without authorityA co-owner, a trustee, an entity signatory, or an attorney-in-fact whose authority was assumedLesson 03's intake questions, asked before the offer
A margin noteAn annotation on a signed page that may or may not be a change of termsAnything that changes a term goes in an amendment, not in a margin

Two of these deserve emphasis because they recur most.

Communication of acceptance. Formation turns on it, and licensees consistently treat the signature as the event. A signed acceptance in an outbox has not formed a contract, and the practice course's worked formation problem shows how quickly that fact becomes consequential when circumstances change in the intervening hour.

The incomplete document. A blank in a material term is not a formality. The document the party signed is not the document in the file, and no explanation of good intent survives that fact.

What a licensee should take from this section. Section 6.9 establishes that the legal questions route to counsel. This table is not legal analysis — it is a list of operational habits, and every one of them is performed by the licensee rather than by anyone else in the transaction. The contract questions belong to counsel; the reasons counsel gets called usually belong to the file.

6.11 What a licensee actually does with contract law

This lesson is the longest legal treatment in the course, and its practical yield is smaller and more specific than students expect.

It does not qualify a licensee to advise on contracts. Section 6.9 lists what routes.

What it does is make four things possible.

One: recognizing that a legal question exists. A client asks something that sounds procedural and is not. A licensee who has studied formation, capacity, the Statute of Frauds, and remedies will notice — and noticing is the whole service, because a client who does not know he needs counsel will not call one.

Two: performing the operational work correctly. Section 6.10's table is entirely operational: transmitting, confirming, calendaring, refusing to send an incomplete document, reducing agreements to writing the same day. None of that requires legal judgment and all of it prevents legal problems.

Three: preserving the facts an answer will depend on. When a contract question does arise, its answer turns on dates, sequence, what was communicated, and what each party received. A licensee who has kept that record has given counsel something to work with; one who has not has given counsel four recollections.

Four: explaining the document's own terms without characterizing their effect. "Your contingency period ends Friday at five, and here is what the agreement says the removal has to look like" is a service. "You're protected until Friday" is an opinion about rights. The two sentences are close, and the distance between them is the entire boundary.

The three habits that carry most of the value:

  1. Everything material in writing, the same day.
  2. Every deadline in a system, counted from the event the agreement names rather than from the day everyone remembers.
  3. Every transmission evidenced, because formation and performance both turn on communication rather than on signature.

The test of whether this lesson worked. Not whether a student can recite the elements — but whether, handed a real situation, he can say which parts are his to handle, which parts are counsel's, and what the file needs to show either way. Those three judgments are what the lesson was for, and they are what a first year in practice will ask for repeatedly.

6.12 Checkpoint

You should be able to name the remedies, explain why specific performance is available for real property, and give the correct answer to a client asking about a deposit.

Key terms

TermDefinition
The four elementsMutual assent, consideration, capacity, and lawful object.
Express / impliedStated in words, or inferred from conduct.
Bilateral / unilateralA promise for a promise, or a promise for an act. A purchase agreement is bilateral; an open listing is unilateral.
Executory / executedSomething remains to be done, or fully performed. "Executed" in classification means performed, not signed.
OfferA proposal showing present intent to be bound, reasonably definite, and communicated. A listing invites offers; it is not one.
Mirror-image acceptanceAn acceptance must match the offer; any change to a material term is not an acceptance.
Communication of acceptanceThe rule most often skipped. A signed acceptance nobody delivered has not been communicated.
CounterofferA rejection of the original offer and the substitution of a new one. The original is gone, not suspended — including where the change is handwritten on a signed form.
RevocationWithdrawal before acceptance, generally permitted even where the offer states a deadline.
OptionA contract making an offer irrevocable for a period, supported by consideration.
ConsiderationValue exchanged. Adequacy is generally not examined; past consideration generally is not consideration; forbearance can be.
CapacityThe legal ability to contract. A licensee's concern is authority, not diagnosis — where capacity is in question, stop, escalate, and refer.
Lawful objectA purpose the law permits. Provisions the law will not enforce fail whether or not both parties agreed.
VoidNo contract exists and never did.
VoidableA valid contract one party may elect to avoid — and may instead affirm.
UnenforceableA contract a court will not enforce, though it may have other effects.
Fraud / negligent misrepresentationA knowing misstatement intended to be relied on; or a statement made without reasonable basis or care. Most claims against licensees are the second.
Statute of FraudsThe requirement that certain contracts be in writing and signed by the party to be charged — including sales of real property, leases over one year, and compensation agreements.
Part performanceA doctrine under which a court may enforce an oral agreement where a party took possession, paid, and improved in reliance.
Written amendmentThe only thing that changes a contract term. Agent emails, verbal agreements, and escrow instructions alone change nothing.
ContingencyA condition on a party's obligation inside a formed contract — not a delay of formation, not a general right to cancel.
Written removalHow contingencies end under the standard California form. A passed date removes nothing; the seller's route is the contractual notice.
AssignmentTransfer of contract rights, with the assignor frequently remaining liable.
NovationSubstitution of a party or contract with all parties' consent, generally releasing the original.
WaiverGiving up a contract right. Removes the contractual path to act, not the ability to investigate.
Material breachA failure going to the essence of the bargain, which may excuse further performance. Whether a breach is material is a legal question.
Specific performanceA court order requiring the promised performance. Available for real property because of uniqueness.
Liquidated damagesAn amount agreed in advance as the recovery on default. Must be properly agreed, is constrained by law, and generally limits rather than adds.
RescissionUnwinding the contract with restoration. Not a synonym for cancellation under a contingency.
CancellationExercising a right the contract provides, by the procedure it specifies. Not accomplished by announcing, ceasing to perform, or instructing escrow.

Practice problems

Problem 1

Classify a residential purchase agreement on all four axes at the moment of acceptance. Then classify an open listing offering compensation to whoever produces a buyer.

Answer.

Solution. The purchase agreement at acceptance: express — stated in words; bilateral — the buyer promises to buy and the seller promises to sell, and both are bound on formation; executory — both still owe performance until closing; and valid, assuming the four elements are present.

It becomes executed at closing, when both have fully performed. The vocabulary trips students because "executed" colloquially means signed — in classification it means fully performed, so a signed but unperformed agreement is executory.

The open listing is unilateral — a promise to pay if someone performs an act. Nobody is bound to perform, and therefore nobody breaches by not performing. An exclusive right to sell listing, by contrast, is bilateral: the brokerage promises to market and the seller promises to pay on the agreed terms.

Problem 2

A buyer offers $880,000, open until 5:00 p.m. Friday. Wednesday, he withdraws it. Thursday, the seller signs it and puts it in his file. Friday morning the seller's agent emails the signed copy.

Was a contract formed?

Solution. No, on two independent grounds — either one is enough.

The offer was revoked before acceptance. An offer may generally be revoked before acceptance even where it states a deadline, unless supported by consideration as an option — which is precisely what an option buys: irrevocability for the period.

And acceptance was not communicated on Thursday. A signed acceptance sitting in a file has not been communicated to the offeror, so nothing formed then; by Friday there was nothing left to accept.

What transfers, in both directions: a buyer relying on "open until Friday" should know it can be withdrawn Wednesday, and a seller intending to accept should not assume the offer will still be there. The communication rule is the one most often skipped — including in signing platforms, where an executed document nobody delivered has not been delivered.

Problem 3

A buyer offers $880,000. The seller strikes the price to $895,000 and the closing date to March 20, signs, and returns it. The buyer signs it but crosses out March 20 and writes March 25 before returning. The seller now says he prefers the original $880,000 offer and will accept it.

Where do the parties stand?

Solution. No contract, and the original offer is gone.

The seller's changes were a counteroffer, which rejected the $880,000 offer and substituted a new one. It was not an "acceptance with modifications" forming a contract on his terms, and it did not suspend the original leaving it available — the original can no longer be accepted unless the buyer renews it.

The buyer's handwritten date change is a further counteroffer, however small. Signing does not convert a modification into an acceptance.

The buyer's counteroffer at $895,000 / March 25 is outstanding. The seller may accept it, reject it, or counter again — or ask the buyer to renew the original terms, which the buyer is free to decline.

Problem 4

Four situations: (a) a seller and buyer agree to conceal a known foundation defect from the lender; (b) a 17-year-old signs a purchase agreement; (c) an oral agreement to sell a house, where the buyer has taken possession, paid $60,000, and rebuilt the kitchen; (d) a lease clause waiving the landlord's habitability obligation.

Classify each as void, voidable, or unenforceable, and state the practical consequence.

Solution. (a) Void. A contract for an unlawful purpose. No contract exists and never did; nobody can enforce it.

(b) Voidable by the minor. This is a valid, binding contract until the minor elects to avoid it — and the minor may instead choose to affirm it. That is the practical difference from void, and the one students merge: voidable contracts operate until someone acts.

(c) Unenforceable under the Statute of Frauds — but not worthless. Unenforceable is not void, and part performance is exactly the doctrine these facts raise: possession, payment, and improvements in reliance. Whether it applies is a legal question. A licensee should know the doctrine exists so as not to tell a client an oral agreement is worthless, and should route it rather than answer it.

(d) The provision is unenforceable, and the rest of the lease may stand. The habitability obligation is not waivable, and the clause fails whether or not both parties agreed to it.

Problem 5

A seller in her late eighties seems confused during the listing appointment, asks the same questions repeatedly, and at one point does not recall that her husband died. Her son, who brought the licensee in, says she is "sharp as ever" and urges signing today. The licensee notes in the file that the seller "appeared to lack capacity."

What should the licensee do, and what is wrong with the file note?

Solution. Stop, escalate to the broker, and route it to counsel. Do not sign today, and do not proceed on the theory that she seemed fine or that a family member vouched for her.

A licensee's concern is authority, not diagnosis. A licensee is not qualified to assess mental capacity, and the file note characterizes her capacity — which is exactly the conclusion the licensee may not draw and which will be read later by people who were not there.

What the note should record instead is observation: what was said, what was asked repeatedly, what the son said and when, that the licensee did not proceed, and that the matter was escalated on a stated date. Observation, not inference — the same discipline as recording ceiling staining rather than diagnosing a roof leak.

Two further flags: the son's pressure to sign today is exactly the pattern that raises undue influence, and depending on the circumstances a contract with an incapacitated person may be voidable, or void where a conservatorship applies. All of that is for counsel.

Problem 6

The parties agree during escrow to move closing back one week. The agents confirm by email, escrow is told, and everyone proceeds. The seller also verbally agrees to leave the washer and dryer. The transaction fails and the parties dispute who breached.

What is the operative contract?

Solution. The original closing date, and no obligation about the appliances.

A change to a contract term is made by written amendment signed by both parties and delivered to escrow so the instructions match the agreement. An email between the agents does not amend the contract — the agents are not the parties. Neither does a verbal agreement between the parties, an instruction to escrow alone, or a shared understanding that a short extension "won't matter."

The parties are now litigating on a contract whose closing date is the original one, with an email chain between non-parties as the evidence of a change.

The appliances fail for the same reason plus one more: the agreement concerns an interest in real property within the Statute of Frauds, and personal property that does not convey unless included has not been included in any writing.

The practical rule: every agreement affecting the transaction goes in a signed writing. The boundary of what strictly must be written is not obvious in the moment, which is exactly why the practice is to write everything.

Problem 7

The buyer's investigation contingency period ends on the 17th. Nothing is delivered. On the 18th the seller's agent tells the seller the contingency expired, the buyer is committed, and she may accept a better offer that arrived overnight. Separately, the buyer waived the appraisal contingency to win the property, and the appraisal comes in $20,000 low.

Correct the first, and state what the licensee owed the buyer on the second.

Solution. All three statements about the contingency are wrong. Under the standard California form, contingencies remain in effect until the buyer removes them in writing — they are not self-executing on a date. The buyer has not removed anything, so his protection is intact, he is not committed, and the seller is not free to sell elsewhere. The seller's route is the contractual notice, which starts the period the contract specifies; if the buyer removes during it the transaction continues, and if not the seller may cancel by the contract's procedure.

The durable habit is better than the rule: read the contract's own removal mechanism. Different forms and negotiated terms operate differently.

On the waiver, what was owed before he decided: an explanation in writing of what the contingency protected and what waiving it exposed him to — that he could still investigate but would have no contractual path to act on what he found, and that a low appraisal would narrow him to bringing funds, renegotiating without leverage, or defaulting with his deposit exposed. Then follow his informed decision.

Both failure modes are wrong: refusing to write the offer overstates the licensee's role; writing it without comment abandons the advisory duty.

Problem 8

A buyer's loan is denied. He phones the listing agent, emails escrow demanding his deposit, and stops responding. The listing agent tells her seller the contract is over, that she may keep the deposit, and that the buyer "clearly materially breached." The buyer's agent tells him he will "definitely get it back" and should sue for specific performance if the seller resists.

Identify every error and state the correct posture.

Solution. Errors on both sides, and almost all of them are legal conclusions.

The buyer forfeited his protection rather than exercising it. A denial does not end the contract by itself; the loan contingency provides a path with requirements — the written notice the contract requires, within its terms, with supporting documentation. A phone call to the other agent is not that notice; a unilateral instruction to escrow is not cancellation, since escrow acts only on mutual written instructions; and ceasing to perform invites cancellation for non-performance, a materially worse position. Cancellation is a procedure, not an announcement.

The listing agent gave three wrong answers. Whether a breach is material is a legal question. Whether the seller may keep the deposit is a legal question — and any liquidated damages provision must have been properly agreed, is constrained by law, and generally limits recovery rather than adding to it. And the contract is not "over" because a date passed.

The buyer's agent gave two. "You'll definitely get it back" is a legal conclusion he is not entitled to give. And specific performance is a remedy for compelling performance — available for real property because of uniqueness, which is Lesson 01's physical characteristic producing a legal rule — not a route to recovering a deposit.

The correct posture, for both: explain the mechanism — the contract has a cancellation procedure with a written notice requirement, escrow acts only on mutual instructions, and a disputed deposit stays put until resolved. Get the notice right, document everything with dates, escalate to the broker, and get the client an answer from counsel before the deadline runs.

Chapter summary

Four elements form a contract: mutual assent, consideration, capacity, lawful object.

Classify on four axes — express or implied, bilateral or unilateral, executory or executed, and by enforceability. "Executed" in classification means performed, not signed.

Three formation rules resolve most questions: an offer accepted without change forms a contract; an acceptance must match the offer; acceptance must be communicated.

A counteroffer rejects and replaces. The original is gone, not suspended — and a signature with a change written on it is a further counteroffer.

An offer can be revoked before acceptance even where it states a deadline, unless consideration made it an option.

Adequacy of consideration is generally not examined; past consideration generally is not consideration.

Capacity is a matter for authority and referral, never diagnosis. Record observations, not characterizations, and escalate.

Void, voidable, and unenforceable are three different outcomes. A voidable contract is binding until the entitled party elects to avoid it — and may be affirmed instead.

The Statute of Frauds requires a signed writing for sales of real property, leases over a year, and compensation agreements. Non-compliance makes an agreement unenforceable, not void, and part performance may still reach it.

Only a signed written amendment changes a contract term. Agent emails, verbal agreements, and escrow instructions alone change nothing.

A contingency conditions an obligation inside a formed contract, and under the standard California form it ends only by written removal — never by a passed date.

Read the contract's definitions of how time is counted. Being wrong by one day costs a client a right.

Distinguish assignment from novation, and explain a waiver's real exposure in writing before the client decides.

Specific performance is available for real property because land is unique — the clearest case in the course of a legal rule flowing from a physical characteristic.

Liquidated damages must be properly agreed, are constrained by law, and generally limit recovery.

Cancellation is a procedure; rescission is a remedy. They are not synonyms.

Never tell a client whether they may cancel, whether a breach is material, or who gets the deposit. Explain the mechanism, follow the contract's procedures, document, escalate, and refer before the deadline.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Trace the formation sequence and state whether a contract exists and on whose terms, identify any element or assent defect and classify the result as void, voidable, or unenforceable, state which agreements in the scenario must be in a signed writing and what actually amends the contract, identify each contingency in effect and what would be required to remove it, state the notice the next step requires and its delivery method, identify every question you would decline to answer and why, and state what you would escalate to the broker and refer to counsel and by what deadline. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 09

Transfer, deeds, title, and recording
