Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • State the requirements of a valid deed and identify which common assumptions about deeds are wrong.
  • Distinguish a grant deed from a quitclaim deed and other deed types by what each warrants.
  • Explain delivery and acceptance and why a signed, recorded deed can still fail to transfer title.
  • Explain recording, the notice it gives, and how priority is determined.
  • Describe title insurance and read a preliminary report as an allocation of risk.
  • Distinguish voluntary from involuntary transfer and identify what each requires.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: deed requirements and deed types55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: delivery, acceptance, and recording50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: title insurance and involuntary transfer45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Deed requirements

1.1 What a deed is

A deed is the written instrument by which an owner conveys an interest in real property to another. The parties have specific names:

  • Grantor — the party conveying.
  • Grantee — the party receiving.

A memory device that survives the examination: the grantor gives; the grantee takes.

1.2 The requirements

A valid deed must generally:

RequirementDetail
Be in writingThe Statute of Frauds applies to conveyances
Identify a competent grantorLegally capable, and with authority to convey
Identify the granteeSufficiently to be ascertainable
Describe the propertyAdequately — not by street address alone
Contain granting languageWords showing present intent to convey
Be signed by the grantorThe grantee's signature is generally not required
Be delivered and acceptedSection 3 — and the requirement most often misunderstood

1.3 What a deed does not require

Students and clients believe several things a deed does not need, and each misbelief causes real problems.

Consideration is not required for validity. A deed may be a gift. Consideration matters for other purposes — including whether a party qualifies as a bona fide purchaser under the recording system — but a deed reciting "love and affection" can be perfectly valid.

The grantee need not sign. The grantor conveys; the grantee's acceptance is required but is generally presumed where the conveyance is beneficial.

Recording is not required for validity. A deed transfers title between grantor and grantee on delivery and acceptance, recorded or not. Recording protects against third parties — it does not create the transfer.

Notarization is not required for validity, but it is required in practice: a deed must generally be acknowledged before a notary to be recorded. So an unacknowledged deed may be valid and unrecordable, which is a problem the grantee will discover later.

A perfect legal description is not required if the property is otherwise adequately described, but adequacy is a legal question and the practice is to use the recorded legal description exactly.

1.4 The granting clause

The words showing intent to convey — "grant," "convey," "quitclaim." In California the word "grant" carries statutory significance, discussed in Section 2, which is why deed type follows from the operative word rather than from the document's title.

1.5 The habendum and other clauses

The habendum clause — historically "to have and to hold" — defines the extent of the estate conveyed. Where it conflicts with the granting clause, the resolution is a legal question.

Reservations and exceptions carve something out: a reserved life estate, reserved mineral rights, an excepted strip. These are the "except" and "reserving" phrases from Lesson 04, and dropping one while copying describes a different parcel.

Conditions and restrictions may appear, and conditional language in a deed is a title matter for counsel, not a curiosity.

1.6 The licensee's practice

  1. Take the legal description from a recorded document, not from a prior listing.
  2. Copy it exactly and completely, including recording references and every reservation, exception, and "together with."
  3. Never substitute an address or APN.
  4. Escrow prepares the deed; the licensee confirms the vesting instruction came from the client after the client consulted counsel.
  5. Route conditional or unusual language to title and to counsel.

1.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to list the requirements of a valid deed and name four things a deed does not require.

2. Grant deed and quitclaim deed

2.1 The spectrum

Deeds differ in what the grantor warrants about the title being conveyed. That is the organizing idea; the names follow from it.

DeedWhat the grantor warrants
General warranty deedThe broadest warranties, extending back through prior ownership. Uncommon in California residential practice
Grant deedLimited implied warranties (Section 2.2). The standard California residential instrument
Quitclaim deedNothing. Conveys whatever interest the grantor has, which may be none
Trustee's deedGiven following a trustee's sale under a deed of trust
Sheriff's deedGiven following an execution sale
Gift deedA conveyance for love and affection rather than value
Deed in lieu of foreclosureA borrower conveying to the lender to avoid foreclosure

2.2 The grant deed's implied warranties

By use of the word "grant," a California grantor impliedly warrants two things unless the deed says otherwise:

  1. That the grantor has not previously conveyed the same estate to another.
  2. That the estate is free from encumbrances made or suffered by the grantor — not by prior owners.

Both warranties are limited to the grantor's own period of ownership. A grant deed does not warrant against a defect created by someone who owned the property in 1974.

That limit is exactly why title insurance exists in California residential practice: the deed's warranties do not reach the risks the buyer actually faces.

2.3 The quitclaim deed

A quitclaim conveys whatever interest the grantor has, with no warranty that the grantor has any.

Its legitimate uses are specific and common:

  • Clearing a cloud on title — a possible claimant releases whatever interest they might have.
  • Transfers between related parties where warranties are unnecessary.
  • Correcting a defect in a prior conveyance.
  • Releasing an easement or other interest.

What a quitclaim does not do: it does not clear liens against the property, and it does not make the grantee's title better than the grantor's was. A buyer receiving a quitclaim in an arm's-length purchase should understand that if the grantor owned nothing, the buyer received nothing.

A licensee must not advise a client to accept a quitclaim, or to give one, as a solution to a title problem. Whether a quitclaim resolves a particular cloud is a legal question, and quitclaims are also used to accomplish frauds precisely because they warrant nothing.

2.4 Deed types encountered after default

A trustee's deed conveys title following a trustee's sale. A buyer at such a sale takes what the sale conveys — which may extinguish junior liens but not senior ones — and the property is generally sold without the protections of an ordinary transaction.

A deed in lieu of foreclosure transfers title to the lender voluntarily. It does not automatically extinguish junior liens, which is the reason a lender may decline one, and its consequences for the borrower are legal and tax questions.

Both belong to Lesson 11's default material. Here the point is recognition: a chain of title containing one of these tells a story the buyer's counsel and title company should read.

2.5 Reconveyance

Not a transfer of ownership but of a security interest: the reconveyance is the recorded document releasing a deed of trust when the debt is satisfied.

Payment of the debt does not clear the record; the recorded document does. A seller who says a loan was paid off years ago has stated a fact about the debt and nothing about the title, and an unreleased deed of trust in a preliminary report requires a document.

2.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to state what a grant deed impliedly warrants and its limit, and explain what a quitclaim does and does not accomplish.

3. Delivery and acceptance

3.1 The requirement that surprises everyone

A deed transfers title on delivery and acceptance. Signing does not transfer title. Notarizing does not transfer title. Recording does not transfer title.

A deed that is signed, notarized, and sitting in a drawer has conveyed nothing.

3.2 What delivery means

Delivery is not primarily physical. It is the grantor's present intent to pass title now, manifested by conduct.

The recurring evidence of intent:

EvidenceWeight
Handing the deed to the granteeStrong
Recording the deedRaises a presumption of delivery
Delivering to escrow with instructions to record on closingDelivery on the conditions being met
Handing it to a third party for the granteeDepends on the instructions and the grantor's control
Keeping it, with instructions to record after deathGenerally not effective delivery — the grantor retained control and intended a testamentary transfer

The failure mode is intent, not custody. A grantor who hands over a deed while saying "this takes effect when I die" has not manifested present intent to pass title now, and the attempted transfer generally fails as an ineffective testamentary act.

3.3 Delivery to escrow

Escrow supplies the ordinary mechanism. The grantor deposits the deed with instructions to deliver on the conditions being satisfied. That is a conditional delivery, and it is one of the two elements — with a binding contract — that make an arrangement an escrow at all.

Critically, once delivered into escrow under a binding contract, the grantor generally cannot unilaterally recall it. The escrow holder is not the grantor's agent for that purpose.

3.4 Acceptance

The grantee must accept. Acceptance is generally presumed where the conveyance is beneficial, and it can be shown by conduct — recording, taking possession, paying taxes, encumbering the property.

Acceptance matters most where it is refused. A grantee can decline a conveyance, and there are real reasons to: contaminated property carrying remediation liability, property carrying assessments exceeding its value, or an unwanted interest in a dispute.

3.5 What a void delivery cannot be cured by

Recording does not cure a defective delivery. A recorded deed raises a presumption of delivery, and a presumption can be rebutted. A deed recorded by a grantee who took it from a drawer without the grantor's intent to convey did not become effective by being recorded.

This is also the mechanism behind deed fraud: a forged or improperly delivered deed appears in the record and looks like a conveyance. A forged deed is void and conveys nothing, no matter how many transfers follow it — which is why the title industry treats forgery as a category of loss distinct from an ordinary defect.

3.6 The licensee's role

Delivery and acceptance questions arise in specific situations a licensee should be able to spot:

  • A client says a relative "signed the house over" years ago but nothing was recorded and nothing changed.
  • A deed appears in the record that the seller says he never signed.
  • A parent wants to deed the property to a child "but keep it until I die."
  • A grantee wants to refuse a conveyance.

In every one, the licensee's role is the same: recognize, stop, escalate to the broker, and route it to title and to counsel. Whether a delivery was effective is a legal conclusion with enormous consequences.

3.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain why a signed and notarized deed may convey nothing, and identify a delivery that fails for lack of present intent.

4. Recording and constructive notice

4.1 What the recording system is for

California maintains a public record of documents affecting real property. The system exists to answer one question:

Who has notice of what?

Recording does not make a document valid, and failing to record does not make it invalid between the parties. Recording protects the recorded party against third parties.

4.2 The three kinds of notice

TypeWhat it is
Actual noticeThe person in fact knows
Constructive noticeThe law charges the person with knowing, because it was recorded
Inquiry noticeFacts that would prompt a reasonable person to investigate

Constructive notice is the system's engine. A recorded document is knowable by anyone who looks, so the law treats everyone as knowing it. That is why a buyer cannot claim to be surprised by a recorded easement he never read.

Inquiry notice is the one that surprises people, because nothing was recorded. Someone visibly in possession who is not the record owner, a well-worn path across the rear of a lot, an occupant paying rent to a person other than the seller — each is a fact that would prompt investigation, and a purchaser may be charged with what the investigation would have revealed.

4.3 The bona fide purchaser

The recording system's protection generally runs to a bona fide purchaser: one who takes for value, in good faith, and without notice of the prior interest — actual, constructive, or inquiry.

Each element does work:

  • For value is why a donee is treated differently from a buyer, and one reason consideration matters even though a deed does not require it for validity.
  • In good faith excludes a party who knew of the problem.
  • Without notice includes what the records showed and what an inquiry would have revealed.

4.4 Priority

The general rule: first in time, first in right, as measured by recording, for those entitled to the system's protection.

The exceptions from Lesson 05, restated because they belong to the recording material:

ExceptionEffect
Property tax liensGenerally take priority regardless of when they attached
Mechanic's liensPriority may relate back to when work commenced
Subordination agreementA lienholder agrees to take a lower position
Purchase-money priorityCertain purchase-money security interests receive favored treatment

Priority determines who is paid first from a limited fund and whose interest survives a foreclosure. A junior lienholder wiped out by a senior foreclosure has lost its security whatever the merits of the debt — which is why a recording date on a preliminary report is not administrative detail.

4.5 The chain of title and the abstract

The chain of title is the successive recorded conveyances tracing ownership. A break in the chain — a conveyance from someone who never appears as a grantee — is a defect requiring resolution.

A wild deed is a recorded document outside the chain: because a searcher following the chain would not find it, recording it may not give constructive notice.

An abstract of title is a summary of the recorded documents affecting a parcel. In modern California residential practice the preliminary report and the title policy have largely replaced reliance on an abstract, but the vocabulary persists.

4.6 What is recorded and what is not

Recorded: deeds, deeds of trust, reconveyances, easements, declarations of restrictions, judgment abstracts, mechanic's liens, lis pendens, maps, and many other instruments.

Frequently not recorded, and therefore invisible to a records search: leases, occupancy agreements, unrecorded easements arising by implication or prescription, side agreements between parties, and personal property arrangements.

That gap is what inquiry notice covers, and it is why physical inspection of the property is a separate obligation from reading the title report.

4.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain what recording does and does not accomplish, define the three kinds of notice, and state the elements of a bona fide purchaser.

5. Title insurance and preliminary reports

5.1 What a preliminary report is

A preliminary report reports the condition of title as shown in the records and states the terms on which the title insurer would be willing to issue a policy, including the exceptions it would take.

Three things it is not:

  • Not a guarantee that title is clear.
  • Not the policy. It states terms; the policy is the contract.
  • Not a survey. It addresses record title, not the physical property.

5.2 Exceptions, exclusions, requirements

Three categories a licensee must distinguish:

CategoryWhat it is
ExceptionAn item the policy will not insure against. The buyer bears that risk unless it is otherwise addressed
ExclusionA category the policy form does not cover at all — governmental regulation such as zoning, eminent domain, matters created or agreed to by the insured
RequirementSomething the insurer needs before issuing — a reconveyance, a resolution, a release, evidence of authority

The requirements are the items with deadlines attached, and the ones most likely to delay a closing.

5.3 What the policy does

Title insurance indemnifies the insured against covered losses from title defects, within the policy's terms and excluding what it excepts. It also typically obligates the insurer to defend covered claims.

Two features distinguish it from other insurance:

It looks backward, not forward. Other insurance covers future events; title insurance covers defects that already exist in the record or the chain, discovered later.

The premium is generally paid once, at closing, for coverage that continues.

5.4 Owner's and lender's policies

The owner's policy insures the buyer's interest. The lender's policy insures the lender's lien and its priority.

They insure different interests, which is why both commonly exist — and why a buyer who declines an owner's policy because "there's a title policy on the loan" has not economized on a duplicate. He has left his own interest uninsured.

Extended coverage may address matters a standard policy excepts, including some survey matters, typically requiring a survey or other evidence. Whether to obtain it is the buyer's decision, made informed.

5.5 What title insurance does not cover

The list is as important as the coverage:

  • Zoning and governmental regulation — excluded.
  • Physical condition of the property.
  • Boundary location, under a standard policy — the survey exception.
  • Matters the insured created or agreed to.
  • Matters known to the insured and not disclosed to the insurer.
  • Anything the policy excepted.

A buyer asks whether the neighbor's fence is on the line and the agent says the title report "came back clean."

That answer is wrong. The report addresses record title, and boundary location is exactly what the standard policy excepts. Nothing in the report speaks to where the fence is. A survey does.

5.6 Reading the report item by item

ItemCorrect handling
Utility easement along a boundaryObtain the document — width, location, permitted use. Can prevent structures the buyer intends
Recorded CC&RsObtain and review; bind the buyer regardless of zoning
Unreleased deed of trust the seller says was paidA recorded reconveyance must be obtained
Mechanic's lienRoute to escrow and title; priority may relate back
Judgment against a similarly named personRoute to title; do not assume it is or is not the seller
Lis pendensA serious cloud; route to counsel
A requirement for a corporate resolution or trustee evidenceThe authority verification from Lesson 03, with a deadline

Three responses wrong in every case: treating an item as routine because most properties have one; asking title to "remove" an exception; and predicting an easement's or restriction's effect on the buyer's plans.

5.7 The timing point

The preliminary report and the underlying documents belong at the front of the transaction queue, with association documents and hazard-area insurance quotes.

An exception discovered late — an easement across the building site, an unreleased lien requiring a document from a lender that no longer exists — takes time to resolve. A buyer who receives the report on day three has options; one who receives it on day twelve of a fourteen-day review has a formality.

5.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain what an exception means, distinguish it from an exclusion and a requirement, and state what title insurance does not cover.

6. Voluntary and involuntary transfer

6.1 The two categories

Voluntary transfer occurs with the owner's intent: sale, gift, dedication, and transfer at death by will.

Involuntary transfer occurs without it: intestate succession, escheat, eminent domain, foreclosure, execution sale, tax sale, adverse possession, and transfers by operation of natural forces.

6.2 Transfer at death

Testate — the decedent left a valid will. Real property passes as the will directs, generally through probate unless a mechanism avoids it.

Intestate — no valid will. Property passes by intestate succession to heirs in the order the law specifies. That order is statutory and its application is legal work.

Escheat — no will and no heirs who take. The property passes to the state.

Probate is the court-supervised process of administering a decedent's estate. Sales during probate may require court involvement and may follow particular procedures — which is why the personal representative's authority must be verified against the appointment, exactly as Lesson 03 required.

Mechanisms that can avoid probate — a trust holding title, survivorship forms of ownership, and certain statutory instruments — have different consequences and are estate planning matters for counsel.

6.3 Adverse possession

Title acquired through possession meeting statutory requirements. The elements are conventionally described as possession that is open and notorious, hostile to the true owner's interest, actual, exclusive, and continuous for the statutory period — and in California, payment of taxes is also required.

Two points for a licensee:

The tax payment requirement is the practical filter. It defeats most casual claims, because a person occupying without paying the taxes generally cannot satisfy it.

It is a legal doctrine, never a licensee's conclusion. A client who says "we've used that strip for twenty years, so it's ours" has stated a claim requiring legal analysis — and the neighbor who says "they've used it so long there's nothing I can do" has stated the mirror image. Both go to counsel.

Prescriptive easements arise from the same family of doctrines and produce a use right rather than ownership.

6.4 Transfer by natural forces

Accretion — the gradual addition of land by deposit — generally benefits the owner. Erosion is its gradual counterpart. Avulsion — a sudden change, such as a river changing course in a storm — generally does not shift boundaries. Reliction is land exposed by a permanent recession of water.

These matter on waterfront and watercourse property and are boundary questions for a surveyor and counsel.

6.5 Involuntary transfer after default

Foreclosure — judicial or nonjudicial — transfers title following default under a security instrument, and is the subject of Lesson 11.

Execution sale follows a judgment, transferring title by sheriff's deed.

Tax sale follows unpaid property taxes under the applicable process.

Each produces a deed type carrying its own limitations, and each appears in a chain of title as a signal that the buyer's counsel and title company should look closely.

6.6 Dedication

Dedication is a private owner devoting land to public use, accepted by the public body. It appears most often on subdivision maps, where streets and easements are offered for dedication as a condition of approval.

A licensee reading a recorded map should recognize dedicated areas as land the owner no longer controls — which occasionally surprises a buyer who measured the lot to the pavement.

6.7 What ties this lesson together

Every mechanism in this lesson answers one of three questions, and a licensee who keeps them separate will handle unfamiliar situations correctly:

  1. Did title actually transfer? — the deed requirements, and delivery and acceptance.
  2. Who has notice, and whose interest prevails? — recording and priority.
  3. What risk is insured, and what is not? — the policy, its exceptions, and its exclusions.

A licensee who can place a problem into one of those three, and route it accordingly, is doing the job. A licensee who answers it is not.

6.8 A worked title problem

A licensee takes a listing. The preliminary report reveals:

  • Title vested in a person who died two years ago.
  • A deed from that person to the current occupant, signed and notarized but never recorded.
  • A deed of trust recorded eighteen months ago, signed by the occupant.
  • A lien recorded against the deceased owner's name eleven months ago.

The occupant says: "My uncle gave me the house before he died. I have the deed."

What each element means, applying this lesson.

An unrecorded deed can still be effective between the parties. Section 3 establishes that delivery and acceptance are what transfer title, not recording. The occupant may well own the property.

Recording is about notice to the world. Section 4. An unrecorded deed does not give constructive notice, which means subsequent parties dealing with the record owner may take without knowledge of it — and the consequences depend on rules this course states and does not resolve on particular facts.

The eleven-month-old lien is the problem the unrecorded deed created. It was recorded against a person the record still showed as the owner, after the deed was signed and before it was recorded. Whether it attaches is exactly the question the recording system exists to answer, and it is a legal question with real stakes.

The deed of trust signed by the occupant was presumably underwritten on some showing of ownership, which raises its own questions about what the lender was given.

The delivery question is live. Section 3.2's requirements — was the deed delivered during the uncle's lifetime, with intent to pass title presently? "He gave it to me" describes a belief. A deed found in a drawer after death is a different fact pattern from a deed handed over with intent, and the difference determines whether title passed at all or whether the property is part of the estate.

What the licensee does.

He does not take the listing on the occupant's description. Lesson 03's intake questions apply with force: how is title held, may I see the document, who must sign.

He routes it — to counsel and to the title company — before marketing. Every one of the questions above is legal, and the title company will not insure until they are resolved.

He records what he was told and when, and he does not repeat the occupant's characterization of ownership in any advertisement.

What he must not do. Conclude that the unrecorded deed settles it, record the deed himself, tell the occupant the lien "won't matter because the deed is older," or market the property while the questions are open. The fourth is the one that turns a solvable title problem into a transaction with a buyer whose deadlines are running.

Why this lesson puts it here. Sections 1 through 5 supply every concept required to see the issues — deed requirements, delivery, recording, notice, priority, and what a preliminary report is. What they do not supply is the answer, and knowing the difference is the professional skill.

6.9 Checkpoint

You should be able to distinguish voluntary from involuntary transfer, state the adverse possession elements including California's tax requirement, and place a title problem into one of the three questions.

Key terms

TermDefinition
DeedThe written instrument conveying an interest in real property. The grantor gives; the grantee takes.
Deed requirementsWriting, competent grantor, ascertainable grantee, adequate property description, granting language, grantor's signature, and delivery and acceptance.
What a deed does not requireConsideration for validity, the grantee's signature, recording, or notarization for validity — though acknowledgment is generally required to record.
Granting clauseThe words showing present intent to convey. In California, "grant" carries statutory significance.
Habendum clauseDefines the extent of the estate conveyed.
General warranty deedThe broadest warranties, extending through prior ownership. Uncommon in California residential practice.
Grant deedThe standard California residential instrument, impliedly warranting that the grantor has not previously conveyed the same estate and that the estate is free of encumbrances made or suffered by the grantor — limited to the grantor's own ownership.
Quitclaim deedConveys whatever interest the grantor has, warranting nothing. Does not clear liens and cannot make title better than the grantor's.
Trustee's deedGiven following a trustee's sale under a deed of trust.
Deed in lieu of foreclosureA borrower's voluntary conveyance to the lender. Does not automatically extinguish junior liens.
ReconveyanceThe recorded document releasing a deed of trust when the debt is satisfied. Payment does not clear the record; the document does.
DeliveryThe grantor's present intent to pass title now, manifested by conduct. Not primarily physical, and not accomplished by signing, notarizing, or recording.
Failed testamentary deliveryA deed handed over or held with instructions to take effect at death — generally ineffective, because the grantor retained control.
Conditional deliveryDeposit into escrow with instructions to deliver on conditions being met. One of the two elements making an arrangement an escrow.
AcceptanceRequired of the grantee, generally presumed where beneficial, and refusable for real reasons — contamination liability, assessments exceeding value.
Forged deedVoid, conveying nothing regardless of how many transfers follow.
Actual noticeThe person in fact knows.
Constructive noticeKnowledge the law charges a person with because a document was recorded. The system's engine.
Inquiry noticeKnowledge charged from facts that would prompt investigation — an occupant who is not the record owner, a worn path, rent paid to someone else.
Bona fide purchaserOne who takes for value, in good faith, and without notice. The party the recording system protects.
First in time, first in rightThe general priority rule, measured by recording, subject to tax lien priority, mechanic's lien relation-back, subordination, and purchase-money priority.
Chain of titleThe successive recorded conveyances tracing ownership. A break is a defect.
Wild deedA recorded document outside the chain, which a searcher following the chain would not find — so recording it may not give constructive notice.
Preliminary reportA report of record title stating the terms on which a policy would issue. Not a guarantee, not the policy, not a survey.
ExceptionAn item the policy will not insure against.
ExclusionA category the policy form does not cover at all.
RequirementSomething the insurer needs before issuing — the item with a deadline attached.
Backward-looking coverageTitle insurance covers defects already existing in the record or chain, discovered later, for a premium generally paid once.
Survey exceptionWhy a standard policy does not insure boundary location.
Testate / intestate / escheatDeath with a valid will; without one, passing by intestate succession; or to the state where there are no heirs.
Adverse possessionTitle by possession that is open and notorious, hostile, actual, exclusive, and continuous for the statutory period — and in California, with payment of taxes.
Accretion / erosion / avulsion / relictionGradual deposit; gradual loss; sudden change, which generally does not shift boundaries; and land exposed by permanent recession of water.
DedicationA private owner devoting land to public use, accepted by the public body. Common on subdivision maps.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A deed recites consideration of "love and affection," is signed only by the grantor, was never recorded, and was never notarized. A licensee tells the grantee it is "not a real deed."

Assess each element.

Solution. The licensee is wrong on all four, and right about one practical consequence he did not identify.

Consideration is not required for validity. A deed may be a gift. Consideration matters elsewhere — including whether someone qualifies as a bona fide purchaser under the recording system — but a deed reciting love and affection can be perfectly valid.

The grantee need not sign. The grantor conveys; the grantee's acceptance is required and is generally presumed where the conveyance is beneficial.

Recording is not required for validity. A deed transfers title between grantor and grantee on delivery and acceptance, recorded or not. Recording protects against third parties; it does not create the transfer.

Notarization is not required for validity — but a deed must generally be acknowledged before a notary to be recorded. So this deed may be valid and unrecordable, which is the real problem the grantee will discover later, and the one the licensee missed.

Whether it is effective is a legal conclusion in any event. Route it.

Problem 2

A seller offers a quitclaim deed, saying it will "clear up" a recorded judgment lien and an unreleased 1998 deed of trust. The buyer's agent says a quitclaim is fine since the seller obviously owns the property.

Assess.

Solution. A quitclaim conveys whatever interest the grantor has, warranting nothing — and it does not clear liens against the property. The judgment lien and the unreleased deed of trust survive it untouched.

The 1998 deed of trust requires a recorded reconveyance. Payment of the debt does not clear the record; the document does.

The agent's reasoning is backwards. A quitclaim is risky precisely because it makes no representation about what the grantor owns — if the grantor owned nothing, the buyer receives nothing. In an arm's-length purchase the buyer normally receives a grant deed, which impliedly warrants that the grantor has not previously conveyed the same estate and that it is free of encumbrances made or suffered by the grantor.

Note the limit that explains title insurance: those warranties reach only the grantor's own period of ownership. A grant deed warrants nothing about a defect created in 1974.

And a licensee must not advise a client to give or accept a quitclaim as a solution to a title problem. Whether it resolves a particular cloud is legal, and quitclaims are used to accomplish frauds precisely because they warrant nothing.

Problem 3

A father signs and notarizes a deed to his daughter, puts it in his safe, and tells her "this is yours when I'm gone." He dies four years later. She finds the deed and records it.

Did title transfer?

Solution. Generally not — and recording it did not fix anything.

A deed transfers title on delivery and acceptance. Signing does not. Notarizing does not. Recording does not.

Delivery is not primarily physical; it is the grantor's present intent to pass title now. "This is yours when I'm gone" is the opposite: he retained the deed and retained control, and manifested an intent to transfer at death. That is an attempted testamentary transfer, which generally fails because it was not made with the formalities a will requires.

Recording raises a presumption of delivery — and a presumption can be rebutted. A deed recorded by a grantee who took it from a drawer without the grantor's present intent to convey did not become effective by being recorded.

What the licensee does: recognize the pattern, stop, escalate to the broker, and route it to title and to counsel. Whether delivery was effective is a legal conclusion with enormous consequences — and this property may in fact pass through the father's estate instead.

Problem 4

A buyer's title search is clean. On the property he notices a tenant in the guest house paying rent to someone other than the seller, and a gravel path worn across the rear of the lot to the neighbor's barn. The seller calls the tenant "a friend staying temporarily."

What notice is in play, and what should happen?

Solution. Inquiry notice — the kind that surprises buyers, because nothing was recorded.

Constructive notice is the system's engine: a recorded document is knowable by anyone who looks, so everyone is treated as knowing it. But leases, occupancy agreements, unrecorded easements arising by implication or prescription, and side agreements are frequently not recorded and are therefore invisible to a records search.

That gap is what inquiry notice covers. An occupant who is not the record owner, rent paid to a third party, and a worn path to a neighbor's structure are textbook facts that would prompt a reasonable person to investigate — and a purchaser may be charged with what the investigation would have revealed, recorded or not.

This also threatens bona fide purchaser status, which requires taking for value, in good faith, and without notice — actual, constructive, or inquiry.

What should happen: investigate rather than accept the explanation. Ask for any written agreement with the occupant, route the path to a survey and to counsel, and do not treat "a friend staying temporarily" as a description of a possessory interest. Physical inspection is a separate obligation from reading the title report, precisely because of this gap.

Problem 5

A buyer asks whether the neighbor's fence is on the line. His agent says the title report "came back clean" and that the owner's policy will cover it if there is a problem. The buyer also declines an owner's policy because the lender is getting one.

Correct all three.

Solution. "Clean" answers a question he did not ask. A preliminary report and a policy address record title — what the records show about ownership and encumbrances. They are not surveys, and the standard policy excepts matters a survey would disclose. Nothing in the report speaks to where the fence is.

The policy will not cover it either. Boundary location under a standard policy is the survey exception, and an exception is an item the policy will not insure against. Also outside coverage: zoning and governmental regulation, physical condition, matters the insured created or agreed to, and matters known to the insured and not disclosed.

Declining the owner's policy is not economizing on a duplicate. The owner's policy insures the buyer's interest; the lender's insures the lender's lien and its priority. They insure different interests, which is why both exist — and declining leaves his own interest uninsured.

What to say: the report doesn't address that, a survey does, here is how to get one before the contingency expires — and extended coverage may address some survey matters, typically requiring a survey or other evidence, which is his decision to make informed.

Problem 6

A preliminary report shows: recorded CC&Rs; a utility easement along the rear line where the buyer plans to build; an unreleased deed of trust from 2016; a judgment against a similarly named person; a lis pendens; and a requirement for evidence of the trustee's authority. It arrives on day twelve of a fourteen-day review.

Handle each, and state what went wrong first.

Solution. The timing failure came first. The preliminary report and its underlying documents belong at the front of the queue, with association documents and hazard-area insurance quotes. An unreleased lien needing a document from a lender that may no longer exist, discovered on day twelve, leaves a formality where a review should have been.

CC&Rs: obtain and review; they bind the buyer regardless of zoning.

The easement: obtain the document — width, location, permitted use. It may prevent exactly what he is buying the lot to build. Do not call it routine, ask title to remove it, or predict its effect on his plans.

The 2016 deed of trust: a recorded reconveyance must be obtained.

The similar-name judgment: route to title. A judgment lien is general and reaches everything the debtor owns in the county — do not assume it is or is not the seller.

The lis pendens: a serious cloud giving notice that pending litigation may affect anyone acquiring an interest. Route to counsel immediately.

The trustee evidence is a requirement, not an exception — something the insurer needs before issuing, and therefore the item with a deadline attached. It is the Lesson 03 authority verification arriving through the title company.

Problem 7

A client says her family has farmed a 40-foot strip beyond her fence line for twenty-two years, so it is "obviously ours now." The adjoining owner's agent says there is nothing his client can do about it. Neither family has ever paid taxes on the strip.

Assess both positions.

Solution. Both agents have stated legal conclusions, and both may be wrong — but the tax fact is decisive against the claim.

Adverse possession requires possession that is open and notorious, hostile, actual, exclusive, and continuous for the statutory period — and in California, payment of taxes. Nobody paid taxes on the strip.

The tax requirement is the practical filter. It defeats most casual claims exactly like this one, because a person occupying without paying the taxes generally cannot satisfy it.

Neither agent may say so as a conclusion. "It's obviously ours" and "there's nothing you can do" are the mirror images of the same error, and the elements are fact-intensive.

Worth naming alongside it: prescriptive easements arise from the same family of doctrines and produce a use right rather than ownership — so a claim that fails as adverse possession may still raise a use question.

Both positions go to counsel, and the boundary itself goes to a surveyor.

Problem 8

Three chains of title cross a licensee's desk: one containing a trustee's deed from 2019; one where a conveyance appears from a grantor who never appears as a grantee; and one where a subdivision map shows a 15-foot strip along the frontage offered for dedication, which the buyer measured as part of his lot.

Identify the issue in each and place it in the lesson's three questions.

Solution. The trustee's deed signals a completed foreclosure. A buyer at a trustee's sale takes what the sale conveyed — which may have extinguished junior liens but not senior ones — and the property was sold without an ordinary transaction's protections. It is a "who has notice and whose interest prevails" question: the buyer's counsel and title company should read what the sale did to the surrounding interests.

The conveyance from a stranger to the chain is a break in the chain — a defect requiring resolution. If the instrument sits entirely outside the chain it may be a wild deed, which a searcher following the chain would not find, so recording it may not have given constructive notice. That is a "did title actually transfer" question.

The dedicated strip is land the owner no longer controls. Dedication is a private owner devoting land to public use, accepted by the public body, and it appears routinely on subdivision maps where streets and easements are offered as a condition of approval. The buyer measured to the pavement and bought less than he thinks. That is a "what risk is insured and what is not" question — and a description and survey question besides.

What transfers: every problem in this lesson is one of three — did title transfer, who has notice and whose interest prevails, and what is insured. A licensee who can place a problem and route it is doing the job. One who answers it is not.

Chapter summary

A deed requires a writing, a competent grantor, an ascertainable grantee, an adequate description, granting language, the grantor's signature, and delivery and acceptance.

It does not require consideration, the grantee's signature, recording, or notarization for validity — though acknowledgment is generally needed to record, so an unacknowledged deed may be valid and unrecordable.

Deeds differ by what the grantor warrants. A California grant deed impliedly warrants no prior conveyance of the same estate and no encumbrances made or suffered by the grantor — limited to the grantor's own ownership, which is precisely why title insurance exists.

A quitclaim warrants nothing, clears no liens, and cannot make title better than the grantor's. Never advise giving or accepting one as a title solution.

Title transfers on delivery and acceptance. Signing, notarizing, and recording do not transfer title, and a deed held with instructions to take effect at death generally fails.

Recording raises a presumption of delivery that can be rebutted, and a forged deed is void no matter how many transfers follow.

Recording answers who has notice. Constructive notice comes from the record; inquiry notice comes from facts on the ground — and the unrecorded world of leases, occupancies, and implied easements is exactly why physical inspection is a separate obligation.

The recording system protects the bona fide purchaser — for value, in good faith, without notice.

First in time, first in right, subject to tax lien priority, mechanic's lien relation-back, subordination, and purchase-money priority. Priority decides who is paid and whose interest survives foreclosure.

A preliminary report states the terms on which a policy would issue. Distinguish exceptions (risks not insured), exclusions (categories outside the policy), and requirements (the items with deadlines). Order it early.

Title insurance looks backward, covers defects already in the record or chain, and does not cover zoning, physical condition, boundary location under a standard policy, or what the insured created or concealed. Owner's and lender's policies insure different interests.

Voluntary transfer occurs with intent; involuntary transfer does not — intestate succession, escheat, eminent domain, foreclosure, execution and tax sales, adverse possession, and natural forces.

Adverse possession in California requires payment of taxes, which defeats most casual claims — and it is never a licensee's conclusion.

Every problem in this lesson is one of three questions: did title transfer, who has notice and whose interest prevails, and what is insured. Place it, and route it.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. State whether title transferred and identify which deed requirement is in question, identify the deed type involved and what it does and does not warrant, identify every form of notice in play including anything giving rise to inquiry notice, distinguish the exceptions from the exclusions and requirements on the report given and state what must be obtained and by when, identify what you would route to title, to a surveyor, and to counsel, and state what you would decline to conclude and why. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 10

Escrow, settlement, and transaction chronology

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Explain what makes an arrangement an escrow and what the escrow holder's neutrality forbids.
  • Describe how escrow instructions are created, how they relate to the purchase agreement, and what happens when they conflict.
  • Explain the handling of deposits and documents, including what escrow may and may not do with a disputed deposit.
  • Compute prorations and read a closing statement, identifying which party is debited and credited for each item.
  • Sequence signing, funding, recording, and disbursement, and explain why the order is deliberate.
  • Build the post-closing record and state what must never be done to a file.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: the escrow holder's role and instructions55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: deposits, documents, and prorations50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: recording, disbursement, and post-closing records45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. The escrow holder's role

1.1 What escrow is

Escrow is a neutral third party holding funds and documents under the parties' conditional delivery, authorized to act only according to their mutual written instructions.

Two elements make an arrangement an escrow, and both are required:

  1. A binding contract between the parties.
  2. Conditional delivery to a neutral holder.

Without both, what exists is a stakeholder arrangement, not an escrow. The conditional delivery element connects directly to Lesson 09: a deed deposited with escrow, to be delivered when conditions are met, is the textbook conditional delivery.

1.2 Why escrow exists

The problem escrow solves is simultaneity. A buyer will not release funds before receiving title; a seller will not release title before receiving funds. Neither will go first.

Escrow breaks the impasse by having both go first to a neutral party, who releases to each only when the conditions are satisfied. That single function explains every rule that follows.

1.3 Neutrality, and what it forbids

Escrow's neutrality is not a courtesy. It is the structural feature that lets both parties rely on the same holder.

Escrow mayEscrow may not
Follow mutual written instructionsAct on one party's instruction alone
Hold funds and documentsAdvise either party
Prepare and circulate documents per the instructionsDecide a dispute between the parties
Record and disburse when conditions are metRelease funds because one party says they are entitled
Report status to both partiesTake a position on who is right

Escrow is sometimes described as a limited dual agent — owing duties to both parties simultaneously, bounded strictly by the instructions. That description is useful because it explains why escrow stops rather than deciding when the parties disagree.

1.4 A worked test of neutrality

The day before recording, the buyer emails escrow: hold back $3,000 from the seller's proceeds for a repair the seller promised verbally. The seller knows nothing about it. The buyer's agent emails escrow confirming the request is legitimate.

Escrow may not act on it. A change to the disbursement requires mutual written instructions; one party cannot redirect funds.

Nor may escrow simply disburse in full, which also acts on one side's position where a genuine dispute has been raised.

Nor does the agent's confirmation supply the missing consent. An agent is not the other party.

The correct outcome: escrow stops. The parties resolve it in writing and the resolution reaches escrow as a mutual instruction. The agents' job is to get that done before the closing date, not to argue with escrow about its role.

1.5 Who may act as escrow holder

California regulates escrow activity, and escrow services in real estate transactions are provided by licensed escrow companies and by other entities operating under exemptions applicable to their own business — including certain financial institutions, title companies, and attorneys and brokers acting in transactions in which they are otherwise involved.

A broker performing escrow in a transaction the broker is handling is operating under an exemption with limits. Whether a particular arrangement fits within one is a legal and licensing question, and it is not a decision an individual licensee makes.

1.6 Selection and the referral prohibition

Selection of the escrow holder is negotiable between the parties and is often addressed in the purchase agreement. Two points:

A licensee must not accept anything of value for directing settlement business. Referrals are ordinary; payment for them is a serious federal problem, and disclosure does not cure it.

Where the brokerage has a financial interest in an escrow or title provider, that is an affiliated business arrangement, carrying disclosure obligations and other conditions.

1.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to state the two elements of an escrow, explain what neutrality forbids, and say what escrow does when one party instructs it alone.

2. Escrow instructions

2.1 What they are

Escrow instructions are the parties' written directions to the escrow holder: what to collect, what to prepare, what conditions must be satisfied, and what to do when they are.

They are how the purchase agreement gets executed operationally. Everything escrow does traces to an instruction.

2.2 The relationship to the purchase agreement

This is the point licensees must be able to explain.

Escrow's instructions implement the purchase agreement. Where they conflict, the parties' agreement governs, and the discrepancy is reconciled by the parties in writing — not by escrow deciding.

An escrow opened on an incomplete contract, or without the amendment that changed a term, will produce instructions that do not match the agreement. That surfaces later, usually at the worst time, and the parties then discover they have two documents saying different things about what they agreed.

2.3 Opening escrow properly

What escrow needs at opening:

  • The fully executed purchase agreement and all amendments.
  • The parties' contact and identification details.
  • The deposit, per the contract's terms and within its time.
  • Lender contact where financing is involved.
  • Title order details.

The licensee's contribution is accuracy and completeness. Delivering a partially executed contract, or omitting the amendment signed last week, guarantees a mismatch.

2.4 Amending instructions

Instructions are amended the same way the contract is: in writing, signed by both parties.

An email from an agent does not amend escrow instructions any more than it amends the contract. Where the parties change a term, the sequence is: signed amendment to the contract, then delivery to escrow so the instructions match.

2.5 What escrow will not do with instructions

  • Interpret ambiguous instructions to favor one side. Escrow asks for clarification.
  • Follow an instruction from one party that changes another party's rights.
  • Advise a party whether an instruction is a good idea.
  • Proceed where the instructions conflict with the agreement without the parties reconciling it.

2.6 Reconciliation is the licensee's job

Nobody else compares the instructions against the agreement line by line. Escrow prepares them from what it received; the lender reads its own documents; the parties read the summary.

A licensee should read the instructions against the contract and every amendment, and raise discrepancies immediately. Price, deposit, closing date, what conveys, cost allocation, credits, and possession are the recurring places where a mismatch appears.

2.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to state what governs when instructions conflict with the agreement, and describe what a licensee should reconcile and when.

3. Deposit and document handling

3.1 The deposit

The deposit — earnest money — performs two functions: it signals commitment, and it is the fund from which a seller's remedy is typically drawn if the buyer defaults.

Points the buyer must understand before he offers it:

Where it goes. To escrow, per the contract's terms, within the time the contract specifies. A deposit that never reached escrow on schedule is a performance problem.

When it is at risk. Not on any change of mind. A buyer acting within a contingency generally has a path to recover it. Once contingencies are removed, the exposure changes materially.

Liquidated damages. Where the parties agreed in advance that a specified amount will be the seller's recovery on default, that provision must have been properly agreed, its enforceability is constrained by law, and it generally limits rather than adds to the recovery. It is not a penalty the seller may simply impose.

3.2 The disputed deposit

Buyers and sellers both assume the deposit follows automatically from who was right. It does not.

Escrow may act only on mutual written instructions. Where the parties disagree, escrow holds — pending mutual instruction, or resolution through the contract's dispute resolution provisions or the courts.

A licensee's role is to explain the mechanism, not to predict the outcome or advise on entitlement. Whether a party is entitled to a deposit is a legal question, and it arrives in an emotional moment when a confident answer is exactly what the client wants.

3.3 Trust funds and the licensee

Where a deposit passes through the brokerage rather than directly to escrow, it is a trust fund, and the rules from Lesson 15 apply in full: separation from the brokerage's own funds, complete records identifying whose funds they are, and timely handling.

A deposit check held in a desk drawer over a weekend is a trust fund handling problem, whatever the intention, and the requirement runs to the broker.

3.4 Documents escrow handles

Escrow collects, prepares, and circulates the documents the instructions call for: the deed, the loan documents from the lender, payoff demands, statements of information, transfer tax declarations, and the closing statements.

Two categories deserve attention.

Payoff demands. Escrow obtains from each lienholder a statement of what satisfies the obligation. A payoff includes accrued interest to the payoff date and other amounts owed under the note — routinely more than the principal balance, and routinely a surprise to sellers who were never told.

The statement of information. A confidential form collecting identifying details so the title company can distinguish the parties from similarly named people in the records. It is how the judgment-against-a-similar-name problem from Lesson 05 gets resolved, and clients who resist providing it should be told what it is for.

3.5 Identity, fraud, and verification

Escrow verifies identity, and licensees should understand why: seller impersonation fraud — in which someone purporting to own a property, often unimproved or non-owner-occupied, attempts to sell it — is a recurring pattern.

The transaction-side signals from Lesson 13 of the Practice course apply here: a seller who never appears and acts only through an intermediary, identification that does not match other documents, urgency to close combined with changing parties or funding sources, and instructions to send proceeds somewhere unexpected.

A licensee who notices the pattern escalates to the broker. A licensee does not investigate alone, confront, or warn a suspected participant.

3.6 Wire fraud

The highest-risk moment in the transaction is when funds move, and it belongs in every escrow discussion:

  1. Warn the client in writing, at the start of escrow, that wire instructions will never be changed by email and that they must verify by voice before sending.
  2. Verify by calling escrow at an independently obtained number — from the engagement documents or the company's published listing, never a number from the email carrying the instructions.
  3. Treat any change to instructions as presumptively fraudulent until verified through that independent channel.

Sent early, the warning costs nothing and prevents the largest single loss a residential client can suffer. Sent the day funds move, it arrives after the attacker has established the thread.

3.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain what happens to a disputed deposit, why a payoff exceeds the principal balance, and what the wire fraud warning must say and when it must be sent.

4. Proration and closing statements

4.1 What proration is

Proration allocates continuing charges and credits between seller and buyer at the ownership boundary, so each bears the portion attributable to their period.

Items commonly prorated: property taxes, association dues, special assessment installments, prepaid or accrued rents on income property, tenant security deposits (transferred rather than prorated), and interest on an assumed loan.

4.2 The mechanics

Three questions produce every proration:

  1. What period does the charge cover?
  2. Has it been paid, or is it owed?
  3. Where does the ownership boundary fall?

The two outcomes:

SituationEffect
Seller prepaid beyond the boundarySeller is credited; buyer is debited — the buyer reimburses the seller
Charge is owed for the seller's periodSeller is debited; buyer is credited — the buyer will pay it

Escrow computes the final figures. A licensee should be able to explain the concept and check that an estimate is in the right range, and should not attempt to produce the operative numbers.

4.3 Worked prorations

Taxes. Annual property taxes of $7,300 are paid through June 30. Closing is May 31, and the parties prorate on a 365-day basis with the day of closing to the buyer.

The seller prepaid July 1 through June 30 but owns only through May 30. The buyer's period is May 31 through June 30 = 31 days.

Daily rate: $7,300 ÷ 365 = $20.00 per day. 31 × $20.00 = $620.00.

Seller credited $620.00; buyer debited $620.00. The buyer reimburses the seller for the period the seller paid but the buyer owns.

Association dues. Monthly dues of $450 are paid for the current month. Closing is the 12th of a 30-day month, day of closing to the buyer.

Daily rate: $450 ÷ 30 = $15.00. Buyer's days: the 12th through the 30th = 19 days. 19 × $15.00 = $285.00. Seller credited, buyer debited.

Rent on income property. A tenant paid $2,400 rent on the 1st for a 30-day month. Closing is the 10th, day of closing to the buyer.

The seller collected rent covering days the buyer will own: the 10th through the 30th = 21 days. $2,400 ÷ 30 = $80.00 per day × 21 = $1,680.00.

Seller debited $1,680.00; buyer credited $1,680.00 — the opposite direction from the tax example, because here the seller holds money for the buyer's period rather than having paid for it.

4.4 The conventions that change the answer

The day-count basis. A 365-day year and a 360-day year with 30-day months produce different daily rates. The contract or local custom specifies which.

Who gets the day of closing. Assigning it to the buyer or the seller shifts one day's worth.

The tax year. California's property tax year and installment schedule do not align with the calendar year, and a licensee who prorates as though they do will be wrong.

Read the convention rather than assuming it. This is the same discipline as reading the contract's definition of how time is counted.

4.5 The closing statement

The closing statement — and, where the transaction involves a consumer mortgage, the lender-required closing disclosure — shows each party's debits and credits and the resulting cash position.

The structure:

SideTypical entries
Seller's debitsLoan payoffs, brokerage compensation, seller-paid closing costs, prorated items owed, credits to buyer, liens satisfied
Seller's creditsSale price, prorated items prepaid
Buyer's debitsPurchase price, buyer-paid closing costs, prorated items reimbursed
Buyer's creditsDeposit, loan proceeds, seller credits, prorated items owed by seller

The single reliable check: the purchase price is a credit to the seller and a debit to the buyer. The deposit is a credit to the buyer. Everything else follows from asking who benefits and who pays.

4.6 The licensee's role with the statement

Does: review it against the contract and every amendment before closing; check that the price, deposit, credits, commission, and agreed repairs match what the parties agreed; and raise discrepancies immediately.

Does not: prepare the operative figures, explain a lender's charges as though qualified to, or tell a client a fee is "standard" or "about right." Those are lender and escrow questions.

A discrepancy found the day before closing is a problem; the same discrepancy found after disbursement is a dispute.

4.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to compute a proration in both directions, state which party is credited in each, and describe what a licensee checks on a closing statement.

5. Recording and disbursement

5.1 The four events, in order

Licensees regularly conflate these, and clients rely on the conflation.

#EventWhat happens
1SigningThe parties execute the closing documents. The transaction is not closed
2FundingThe lender releases loan proceeds to escrow, subject to its conditions
3RecordingThe deed and security instrument are recorded, establishing the interests of record and giving constructive notice. Generally when the buyer becomes owner of record
4DisbursementEscrow disburses once recording is confirmed

5.2 Why recording precedes disbursement

Deliberately: it prevents a gap in which money has moved but the interests are not of record. If funds disbursed first and recording failed, the seller would hold the proceeds and the buyer would hold nothing enforceable against third parties.

The order is a protection, not a workflow preference.

5.3 Recording order and priority

Where multiple instruments record, the order establishes lien priority. An out-of-sequence recording can place a new lender behind an interest it was to be ahead of.

This is why closing is sequenced rather than simultaneous, and it is not clerical.

5.4 Funding conditions

An approval is commonly conditional, and prior-to-funding conditions are not formalities. An unsatisfied condition means the loan does not fund, whatever the schedule says.

Lenders also commonly re-verify credit and employment before funding, which is why the start-of-escrow warning from Lesson 11 — do not open credit, change jobs, or move large sums without telling your lender — prevents more failed closings than any other communication a licensee sends.

5.5 The final verification

Before closing, the buyer's final verification of condition — the walkthrough — confirms the property is in the condition the contract requires: agreed repairs completed against the amendment that agreed them, included items present, systems operating, no new damage including from the move-out, and vacancy where required.

It is not an inspection, and it is not a new opportunity to renegotiate accepted condition — but it is not informational either.

Where a problem appears:

  1. Document it — photographs, dated notes.
  2. Address it through the contract before closing, because the ordinary leverage disappears at closing.
  3. Do not instruct escrow to withhold funds — escrow cannot act without mutual instruction.
  4. Do not close intending to pursue the seller afterward, which converts a solvable issue into a claim.

5.6 Possession is not ownership

Ownership transfers at recording. Possession transfers when the contract says it does — frequently the same day and frequently not.

Where they diverge, the arrangement must be documented. A post-closing occupancy agreement should address duration and end date, payment, condition on delivery, insurance and in whose name, utilities and maintenance, a deposit or holdback, and the remedy for failure to vacate.

A brief term does not reduce the need for terms. "They only need three days" is how a holdover with no agreed remedy begins — and the buyer now owns a property occupied by someone with no obligation he can enforce quickly.

Early possession before closing is riskier still and generally to be discouraged: a buyer who occupies and then fails to close leaves the seller with an occupant and a property to re-market.

5.7 Transfer of control

Practical items that generate complaints when handled loosely: when keys transfer and from whom; garage and gate remotes, mailbox keys, association access devices; changing codes on smart locks and alarms; and confirming the seller's remote access to cameras and smart devices is removed.

A seller who retains app access to interior cameras after closing is a real problem, and it is addressed explicitly rather than assumed.

5.8 Confirming closing to the client

When recording is confirmed, tell the client in writing, with the date and what it means. Clients frequently do not know whether they own the property, and the licensee is the person they will ask.

5.9 Checkpoint

You should be able to sequence the four events, explain why recording precedes disbursement, and state the correct response to a problem found at the walkthrough.

6. Post-closing records

6.1 Audit while memories are fresh

The most useful review of a transaction file happens shortly after closing. Its purpose is to confirm the file can answer questions later, and to obtain anything still obtainable while the participants respond to messages.

6.2 The audit questions

  1. Completeness. Every required document present — agency disclosures, the agreement, offers and counteroffers, disclosures, notices, amendments, closing documents — including for the parts that did not go smoothly.
  2. Dates. Every document dated, and delivery evidenced where timing matters.
  3. Consistency. Names, dates, amounts, and property descriptions reconcile across documents.
  4. Chronology. The sequence makes sense against the contract and against the disclose-before-decide principle — the agency disclosure precedes the agreement, disclosures precede contingency removal, amendments precede the dates they change.
  5. Nothing improper present. No unexecuted draft mistaken for final, no superseded figure presented as current.
  6. Retention. Filed where it will be found, for the required period.

6.3 Keep the superseded versions

The instinct to keep only final documents should be resisted.

The sequence of versions is frequently what shows when a term changed and who agreed to it.

A file containing only executed finals can prove what was agreed but not how the parties arrived there — which is the question a dispute usually asks.

6.4 What to do with findings

  • Obtain what legitimately exists — a missing copy, an unrecorded acknowledgment.
  • Record what does not, plainly.
  • Escalate to the broker.
  • Address the process that produced the gap, not only the instance.

6.5 The line

Creating a document now and dating it to match the transaction, or removing surrounding records so an inconsistency does not show, is falsification.

A documented gap is a compliance problem a brokerage can survive. A manufactured document is a question about integrity, and it ends careers.

Corrections are made visibly, with the original preserved and the change attributable. A record that can be silently altered is not a record.

6.6 Retention

The file is retained for the required period, which runs from closing. Cancelled and failed transactions are retained too — and are disproportionately the ones that later produce disputes, which is exactly why they must not be discarded as uninteresting.

Records belong to the brokerage, and the responsible broker answers for them, whoever created them.

6.7 What actually prevents disputes

Most transaction disputes are not about substance. They are about whether something was delivered, to whom, and when. The practices that prevent them are documentary:

  1. Build the deadline calendar from the contract and share it in writing.
  2. Deliver notices by the authorized method and keep the evidence.
  3. Confirm materially important conversations in writing.
  4. Amend in writing, signed, and deliver to escrow.
  5. Correct errors immediately, in writing, to everyone who received the original.
  6. Keep superseded drafts.
  7. Audit at closing while memories are fresh.

6.8 The escrow file, item by item

Section 6.2 supplies the audit questions. This section supplies the inventory, because an audit without a list of what should be present can only find defects in what is there.

A complete escrow file contains:

  1. The purchase agreement, fully executed, with acceptance evidenced and dated.
  2. Every counteroffer, in sequence, each executed — including any that were superseded.
  3. Escrow instructions, executed by both parties.
  4. Every amendment, executed, dated, and — critically — with evidence it reached escrow.
  5. The deposit receipt, and evidence of its handling.
  6. The seller's disclosures, with delivery evidenced to the buyer.
  7. The licensee's own disclosure, as a separate document.
  8. The preliminary report, with delivery evidenced.
  9. Association documents where applicable, with delivery evidenced and the date of delivery recorded.
  10. Inspection and evaluation reports obtained, and the written recommendations that preceded them.
  11. Every contingency removal or waiver, dated and with delivery evidenced.
  12. Every notice, with what it said, when, to whom, and by what method.
  13. Payoff demands, with their good-through dates.
  14. The loan documents' key facts — approval, conditions cleared, and the terms actually closed.
  15. The estimated and final closing statements, with the review that compared them to the agreement.
  16. Recording confirmation and disbursement records.
  17. Possession arrangements, where anything other than possession at closing was agreed.
  18. The correspondence that evidences a decision, moved out of an inbox and into the file.
  19. The close-out memorandum — what was obtained, what is missing, and what was escalated.

The five items most often absent, and they are not random: 4, 9, 11, 12, and 18. Every one of them is about transmission rather than execution — a document that exists somewhere and whose journey to the person who needed it is not recorded.

Section 6.3's superseded versions belong in items 2, 4, and 15. The sequence of versions is what proves when a term changed and who agreed, which is the question a dispute usually asks.

The audit's real question, restated: could a person who was not present reconstruct what happened, when, and on what basis? Item 19 is what makes the answer yes even where items are missing, because a documented gap is a file that was checked, and an undocumented gap is a file that was not — and from the outside those two look identical until someone asks.

6.9 The chain of decisions escrow cannot make

Section 6.7 addresses what prevents disputes. This section states the structural reason escrow does not prevent them, because licensees consistently assume it will.

Escrow is neutral, and neutrality is a constraint rather than a service level. It means the escrow holder:

  • Follows the instructions it has, and does not act on what it was told verbally or on what one party says was agreed.
  • Does not decide between the parties. Conflicting instructions produce a hold, not a resolution.
  • Does not police the transaction. It will not notice that a disclosure was late, that a contingency period was miscounted, or that an amendment was never sent to it.
  • Does not advise. Not either party, on anything, including on whether a figure looks wrong.

The consequence is a list of things that will not happen unless a licensee does them:

What must happenWho does it if the licensee does not
Every amendment reaches escrowNobody
The closing statement is checked against the agreementNobody
The deadlines are calendared and watchedNobody
Someone notices that a credit was agreed and is not shownNobody
Someone notices that the payoff demand expires before closingNobody
Someone confirms delivery of the documents rights run fromNobody
Someone tells the client what he is about to signNobody

Seven rows, one answer. This is the concrete version of Section 6.4's coordinator description, and it is why the file audit in Section 6.2 finds what it finds: the transaction's most failure-prone elements are the ones with no assigned owner outside the brokerage.

Two implications for a new licensee.

Do not read escrow's silence as approval. An escrow that has not raised an issue has not reviewed for it. Its silence carries no information.

Do not read a smooth closing as a well-run one. Most transactions close without anyone testing whether the amendments arrived, the deadlines were counted correctly, or the disclosures were delivered on time. The file finds out later, and by then the answer is fixed.

The one habit that covers most of the table: at every point where something is agreed, ask two questions — who has to receive this, and how will the file show they did? Both answers are usually the licensee, and both take a minute.

6.10 Checkpoint

You should be able to run the post-closing audit, state what may legitimately be obtained after closing and what may never be created, and explain why failed transactions are retained.

Key terms

TermDefinition

The two elementsA binding contract between the parties, and conditional delivery to a neutral holder. Without both, it is a stakeholder arrangement.

Limited dual agentThe description explaining escrow's shape — duties to both parties, bounded strictly by the instructions, which is why it stops rather than deciding.

Affiliated business arrangementWhere the brokerage holds a financial interest in a settlement provider. Carries disclosure and conditions. Accepting anything of value for directing settlement business is separately prohibited and not cured by disclosure.
Escrow instructionsThe parties' written directions to the escrow holder. They implement the purchase agreement; where they conflict, the agreement governs and the parties reconcile it in writing.
ReconciliationReading the instructions against the contract and every amendment. Nobody else does it line by line.
Deposit (earnest money)The fund signaling commitment and from which a seller's remedy is typically drawn on default.
Liquidated damagesAn amount agreed in advance as the recovery on default. Must be properly agreed, is constrained by law, and generally limits recovery.
The disputed depositHeld by escrow pending mutual instruction or resolution through the contract's dispute provisions or the courts. Entitlement is a legal question.
Payoff demandA lienholder's statement of what satisfies the obligation — including accrued interest and other amounts under the note, routinely more than the principal balance.
Statement of informationA confidential form letting the title company distinguish the parties from similarly named people in the records.

FundingThe lender's release of proceeds to escrow, subject to its conditions.
RecordingRecording of the deed and security instrument. Generally when the buyer becomes owner of record.
DisbursementEscrow's release of funds after recording is confirmed — deliberately, to prevent a gap where money moved but interests are not of record.
Recording orderThe sequence establishing lien priority. Not clerical.
Prior-to-funding conditionA requirement that must be satisfied or the loan does not fund. Not a formality.
Final verification of conditionThe walkthrough confirming contract compliance. Not an inspection, not a renegotiation, and not informational.
