Post-closing occupancy agreementThe written agreement where possession diverges from recording, covering duration, payment, condition, insurance, utilities, deposit, and the remedy for failure to vacate.
Superseded versionsEarlier drafts kept because the sequence of versions shows when a term changed and who agreed.
FalsificationCreating a document now and dating it to match, or removing records so an inconsistency does not show. Distinct from a documented gap, which is survivable.

Practice problems

Problem 1

Two days before recording, the seller emails escrow instructing it to release $5,000 of the buyer's deposit to him immediately because the buyer "clearly breached." The seller's agent emails escrow confirming the seller is entitled to it. The buyer says nothing.

What may escrow do, and what should the agents do?

Solution. Escrow stops. It may act only on mutual written instructions, and each alternative fails for its own reason.

It may not release the funds — one party cannot redirect them. It may not take a position on whether the buyer breached, because escrow does not decide disputes. And the agent's confirmation supplies nothing: an agent is not the other party.

Whether the seller is entitled to the deposit is a legal question, and neither agent may answer it. Buyers and sellers both assume the deposit follows automatically from who was right; it does not. Escrow holds pending mutual instruction or resolution through the contract's dispute resolution provisions or the courts.

What the agents do: explain the mechanism to their clients, follow the contract's procedures for any notice their client wishes to give, document communications with dates, escalate to the broker, and recommend counsel before any deadline passes.

Problem 2

Escrow's instructions show a closing date of March 20 and a $12,000 seller credit. The purchase agreement says March 20, but an amendment signed two weeks ago moved closing to March 27 and reduced the credit to $8,000. Escrow was never sent the amendment. Escrow asks the agents which figure to use.

What governs, and who failed?

Solution. The parties' agreement governs. Escrow's instructions implement the purchase agreement, and where they conflict the discrepancy is reconciled by the parties in writing — not by escrow deciding and not by an agent telling escrow which number to use.

The failure is at opening and afterward. Escrow needs the fully executed agreement and all amendments, and an escrow opened without one produces instructions that do not match. Then nobody reconciled: escrow prepares instructions from what it received, the lender reads its own documents, the parties read the summary, and nobody compares the instructions against the contract line by line except the licensee.

The fix: deliver the signed amendment to escrow so the instructions can be amended — in writing, signed by both parties, exactly as the contract is amended. An agent's email amends neither.

The recurring mismatch points to check: price, deposit, closing date, what conveys, cost allocation, credits, and possession.

Problem 3

A seller is surprised at closing that his payoff is $412,600 when his statement showed a principal balance of $404,800. He also objects to providing a statement of information, calling it intrusive.

Explain both.

Solution. A payoff demand states what satisfies the obligation — including accrued interest to the payoff date and other amounts owed under the note. It is routinely more than the principal balance, and it is routinely a surprise to sellers who were never told.

The failure was at the listing appointment. Explaining this on the net sheet, months earlier, is one of the small things separating a licensee who prepared the client from one who did not.

The statement of information is confidential and has a specific purpose: it lets the title company distinguish the parties from similarly named people in the records. It is how the judgment-against-a-similar-name problem gets resolved rather than becoming his problem. A client who resists should be told what it is for — not pressured, and not left to assume the worst.

Problem 4

Compute and state the direction of each: (a) annual taxes of $7,300 paid through June 30, closing May 31, 365-day basis, day of closing to the buyer; (b) monthly association dues of $450 paid for the current month, closing the 12th of a 30-day month; (c) a tenant's $2,400 rent paid on the 1st of a 30-day month, closing the 10th.

Answer.

Solution. (a) The seller prepaid through June 30 but owns only through May 30. Buyer's period: May 31 – June 30 = 31 days. $7,300 ÷ 365 = $20.00/day × 31 = $620.00. Seller credited, buyer debited — the buyer reimburses the seller.

(b) $450 ÷ 30 = $15.00/day. Buyer's days: the 12th through the 30th = 19 days × $15.00 = $285.00. Seller credited, buyer debited.

(c) Opposite direction. The seller collected rent covering days the buyer will own: the 10th through the 30th = 21 days. $2,400 ÷ 30 = $80.00/day × 21 = $1,680.00. Seller debited, buyer credited — the seller holds money for the buyer's period rather than having paid for it.

The rule underneath all three: prepaid beyond the boundary means seller credited; accrued or held for the buyer's period means seller debited. And three conventions change the answer — the day-count basis, who gets the day of closing, and the fact that California's property tax year and installment schedule do not align with the calendar year. Read the convention rather than assuming it.

Problem 5

A licensee reviewing a closing statement the day before closing finds the brokerage compensation is $2,000 higher than the listing agreement, an agreed $3,500 repair credit is missing, and a lender fee he does not recognize. He decides to raise it after closing so as not to delay it, and tells the seller the unfamiliar fee is "pretty standard."

Assess both decisions.

Solution. Both are wrong, and the timing decision is the expensive one.

A discrepancy found the day before closing is a problem; the same discrepancy found after disbursement is a dispute. Once funds are disbursed the money is gone and the leverage with it. The compensation error and the missing credit are exactly what a licensee is positioned to catch — by reviewing the statement against the contract and every amendment and checking price, deposit, credits, commission, and agreed repairs.

"Pretty standard" is a lender question he answered. A licensee does not explain a lender's charges as though qualified to, and does not tell a client a fee is standard or about right.

A quick check on the rest: the price is a credit to the seller and a debit to the buyer; the deposit is a credit to the buyer. Everything else follows from asking who benefits and who pays.

Problem 6

A licensee tells his client the transaction "closed" the afternoon the parties signed. Funding occurs the next morning, recording that afternoon, and disbursement the following day. The client had already given notice on her apartment and scheduled movers based on the signing date. Separately, the buyer opened a furniture credit account the week before.

Sequence the events and identify both failures.

Solution. Signing — the documents are executed and the transaction is not closed. Funding — the lender releases proceeds to escrow subject to its conditions. Recording — the deed and security instrument are recorded, establishing the interests of record and giving constructive notice; generally when the buyer becomes owner of record. Disbursement — escrow releases funds once recording is confirmed.

Recording precedes disbursement deliberately, preventing a gap in which money has moved but the interests are not of record. And where multiple instruments record, the order establishes lien priority, which is why closing is sequenced rather than simultaneous.

The furniture credit threatens the closing itself. Prior-to-funding conditions are not formalities, and lenders commonly re-verify credit and employment before funding. The start-of-escrow warning — do not open credit, change jobs, or move large sums without telling your lender — prevents more failed closings than any other communication a licensee sends.

And when recording is confirmed, tell the client in writing with the date and what it means. Clients frequently do not know whether they own the property, and the licensee is who they will ask.

Problem 7

At the final verification the buyer finds an agreed repair was never done and a named built-in refrigerator removed. The listing agent suggests closing on schedule and sorting it out afterward; the buyer's agent suggests instructing escrow to hold back $6,000. The seller then asks to stay three extra days after closing, and both agents agree it is too short to need paperwork.

Assess all three.

Solution. Closing and pursuing afterward converts a solvable issue into a claim — the ordinary leverage to resolve it disappears at closing. Escrow cannot hold back funds on one party's instruction; it acts only on mutual written instructions.

Correct: document the findings with photographs and dated notes, and address them through the contract before closing. The walkthrough confirms the property is in the condition the contract requires — it is not an inspection and not a renegotiation of accepted condition, but it is not informational either.

The three-day occupancy is the failure that will cost the most. Ownership transfers at recording; possession transfers when the contract says — and any divergence is documented. A written occupancy agreement covers duration and end date, payment, condition on delivery, insurance and in whose name, utilities and maintenance, a deposit or holdback, and the remedy for failure to vacate.

A brief term does not reduce the need for terms. "They only need three days" is how a holdover with no agreed remedy begins — and the buyer now owns a property occupied by someone with no obligation he can enforce quickly. Add the transfer-of-control items too: keys and from whom, remotes and access devices, smart lock and alarm codes, and removal of the seller's app access to interior cameras, which is a real problem addressed explicitly rather than assumed.

Problem 8

A post-closing audit finds a missing signed copy of an amendment, a closing statement figure inconsistent with an earlier version, and only executed final documents — the drafts were discarded. A colleague suggests recreating the amendment with a matching date and deleting the superseded statement so nothing conflicts. The file is for a transaction that closed; a second file, for a transaction that fell apart in escrow, was thrown away as "not a real file."

State the correct response and identify both errors about retention.

Solution. The correct response: obtain what legitimately exists — the missing signed copy may still be retrievable, which is exactly why the audit happens while participants still respond to messages. Record what does not exist, plainly. Escalate to the broker. And address the process that produced the gap, not only the instance.

The colleague's suggestion is falsification. Creating a document now and dating it to match, or removing records so an inconsistency does not show, converts a compliance failure into a question about integrity. A documented gap is survivable; a manufactured document ends careers. Corrections are made visibly, with the original preserved and the change attributable — a record that can be silently altered is not a record.

Discarding the drafts was the first retention error. The sequence of versions is frequently what shows when a term changed and who agreed to it. A file of executed finals proves what was agreed but not how the parties got there, which is the question a dispute usually asks.

Throwing away the failed transaction is the worse one. Files are retained for the required period from closing — including cancelled and failed transactions, which are disproportionately the ones that later produce disputes. That is precisely why they must not be discarded as uninteresting. And the records belong to the brokerage, with the responsible broker answering for them.

Chapter summary

Escrow requires a binding contract and conditional delivery to a neutral holder, and it exists to solve simultaneity — neither party will go first, so both go first to someone neutral.

Neutrality forbids acting on one party's instruction, advising either party, and deciding disputes. Where the parties disagree, escrow stops, and an agent's confirmation is not a party's consent.

Never accept anything of value for directing settlement business, and disclose affiliated business arrangements.

Instructions implement the agreement; the agreement governs a conflict, reconciled by the parties in writing. Open escrow on the complete executed contract and every amendment, and reconcile the instructions line by line — nobody else does.

The deposit is at risk differently before and after contingency removal, liquidated damages are constrained by law and generally limit recovery, and a disputed deposit is held pending mutual instruction or resolution. Entitlement is a legal question.

A payoff exceeds the principal balance because it includes accrued interest and other amounts under the note — explain it at the listing appointment, not at closing.

Warn about wire fraud in writing at the start of escrow, and verify any change through a channel the attacker does not control.

Prorations run in two directions: prepaid beyond the boundary credits the seller; accrued or held for the buyer's period debits the seller. Read the day-count convention, who gets the day of closing, and California's tax year rather than assuming any of them.

On a closing statement the price is a seller credit and a buyer debit, and the deposit is a buyer credit. Review it against the contract before closing — afterward it is a dispute.

Signing, funding, recording, disbursement, in that order. Recording generally transfers ownership, disbursement follows it deliberately, and recording order sets lien priority.

Prior-to-funding conditions are not formalities, and the start-of-escrow warning about credit, employment, and moving funds prevents more failures than anything else.

The walkthrough confirms contract compliance, and problems are resolved before closing, while leverage exists.

Possession transfers when the contract says, and any divergence is documented in full — including keys, access devices, and the seller's remote camera access.

Audit shortly after closing, keep superseded versions, obtain what exists, record what does not, and retain failed transactions — they are disproportionately the ones that produce disputes. A documented gap is survivable; a manufactured document is not.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. State what escrow may and may not do on the facts given and why, identify every discrepancy between the escrow instructions and the agreement and state what governs, compute each proration with its convention stated and identify which party is credited, identify the closing statement entries you would verify against the contract, sequence the closing events and identify any that occurred out of order, state what you would document at the final verification and by when any problem must be addressed, and identify what you would escalate to the broker and refer to counsel. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 11

Real estate finance foundations

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Distinguish the promissory note from the security instrument and explain what each does.
  • Explain how a deed of trust operates in California and how it differs from a mortgage.
  • Describe the primary and secondary markets and identify the sources of real estate financing.
  • Compute interest, amortization components, points, and the effect of each on cost.
  • Compute loan-to-value and qualifying ratios and explain what each measures.
  • Describe default, the nonjudicial and judicial foreclosure paths, and deficiency and redemption at orientation depth.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: notes, security instruments, and clauses55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: markets, sources, and loan mathematics50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: ratios, default, and foreclosure45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Promissory notes

1.1 Two documents, two jobs

Every secured real estate loan involves at least two instruments, and confusing them is the single most common error in this material.

InstrumentWhat it does
Promissory noteThe promise to repay. It creates the debt and states its terms
Security instrumentPledges the property as security for that debt

The note is the debt. The security instrument is the remedy.

A note without a security instrument is an unsecured obligation — enforceable, but with no claim against the property. A security instrument without a note secures nothing, because there is no obligation to secure.

1.2 What the note contains

The principal amount, the interest rate and how it is determined, the payment amount and schedule, the maturity date, the parties, and the consequences of default.

Parties: the maker (or borrower) promises to pay; the payee (or holder) is entitled to receive.

1.3 Negotiability

A note meeting certain requirements is a negotiable instrument, transferable so that a subsequent holder may take it free of certain defenses the maker could have raised against the original payee.

Practically, this is why loans are sold. A borrower's obligation follows the note into the secondary market, and the borrower's payments may be directed to a servicer they never chose.

A licensee's role is recognition, not analysis. Whether a particular note is negotiable and what defenses survive are legal questions.

1.4 The clauses that matter

Several note and security instrument provisions come up constantly and are frequently misdescribed.

ClauseWhat it does
AccelerationOn default, makes the entire balance immediately due. Without it, a lender could only sue for missed payments — which is why foreclosure depends on it
Alienation (due-on-sale)Permits the lender to call the loan due on transfer of the property. This is why loans are generally not assumable without lender consent
PrepaymentAddresses whether and on what terms the borrower may pay early. A prepayment penalty is a charge for doing so, and its permissibility is regulated
Late chargeA charge for a payment received after a stated period
SubordinationAn agreement to take a lower lien position than the instrument would otherwise hold
ReleaseIn a blanket loan, permits individual parcels to be released as they are sold

Acceleration and alienation are the two students merge. Acceleration responds to default; alienation responds to transfer. They do different work and are triggered by different events.

1.5 Straight and amortized notes

A straight note — sometimes called an interest-only or term note — provides for interest payments with the principal due at maturity. The principal does not reduce along the way.

An amortized note provides for payments that retire both interest and principal over the term, so the balance reaches zero at maturity. A fully amortized loan retires completely; a partially amortized loan leaves a balloon payment due at maturity.

1.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to state what each instrument does, distinguish acceleration from alienation, and explain what a balloon payment signals about the amortization.

2. Deeds of trust and mortgages

2.1 The California instrument

California residential lending overwhelmingly uses the deed of trust, and it involves three parties rather than two.

PartyRole
TrustorThe borrower, who conveys title in trust as security
BeneficiaryThe lender, for whose benefit the security is held
TrusteeA neutral third party holding the power of sale

A mortgage by contrast involves two parties: mortgagor (borrower) and mortgagee (lender).

The memory device that survives the exam: the trustor gives the security; the trustee holds the power; the beneficiary benefits.

2.2 Why the third party matters

The trustee holds a power of sale — the authority to sell the property on default without going to court.

That single feature is why California residential foreclosure is predominantly nonjudicial, and it drives everything in Section 6: the timeline, the borrower's reinstatement window, and the deficiency consequences.

2.3 Title theory and lien theory

Jurisdictions describe the security relationship differently. Some treat the security instrument as passing legal title to the lender until the debt is repaid; others treat it as creating a lien while the borrower retains title.

California's deed of trust is generally treated as a lien for most practical purposes, notwithstanding its form as a conveyance in trust. The borrower possesses, uses, and can transfer the property, subject to the security interest.

For a licensee the practical point is what it produces: the borrower is the owner, and the lender holds a claim.

2.4 Satisfaction and the reconveyance

When the debt is satisfied, the beneficiary directs the trustee to reconvey, and a reconveyance is recorded releasing the deed of trust.

Payment does not clear the record; the recorded document does. A seller who says a loan was paid off in 2016 has stated a fact about the debt and nothing about the title, and an unreleased deed of trust in a preliminary report requires a document — the same point Lessons 05 and 09 make from the title side.

2.5 Priority

First, second, and junior describe recording position rather than loan type. A "second" is simply a deed of trust recorded after another.

Priority determines who is paid first from a limited fund and whose interest survives a foreclosure. A junior lienholder wiped out by a senior foreclosure has lost its security whatever the merits of the debt.

The exceptions from Lesson 05 apply here: property tax liens generally take priority regardless of timing, mechanic's lien priority may relate back, and subordination moves a lienholder down voluntarily.

Subordination is a significant decision, not a formality, and it belongs to counsel — most often when a seller who carried financing is asked to move behind a construction lender.

2.6 Purchase money and seller financing

A purchase money loan is one used to acquire the property. California treats certain purchase money loans differently for deficiency purposes, addressed in Section 6.

Seller financing — where the seller carries all or part of the purchase price — creates a note and deed of trust in the seller's favor. It is legitimate and common, and it raises specific questions: priority, what happens if the buyer defaults, servicing, and whether any disclosure or regulatory requirement applies to the arrangement.

A licensee arranging seller financing should route the structure to counsel rather than assembling it from a form.

2.7 Other structures

Assumption — the buyer takes over the existing loan. Generally requires lender consent because of the alienation clause, and the seller may remain liable unless released by novation. A seller who "let the buyer assume" and assumes he is released has assumed a novation he did not obtain.

Subject to — the buyer takes title without assuming the loan, which remains the seller's obligation and remains secured by the property. It triggers the alienation clause and carries real risk for both parties.

Blanket loan — one loan secured by more than one parcel, typically with a release clause permitting individual parcels to be sold.

Construction loan — advanced in stages as work progresses, with its own priority concerns against mechanic's liens.

Home equity line — a revolving credit facility secured by the property.

2.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to name the three parties to a deed of trust, explain what the power of sale produces, and distinguish assumption from taking subject to.

3. Loan markets and sources

3.1 Primary and secondary

The primary market is where loans are originated — the lender and the borrower.

The secondary market is where existing loans are bought and sold between investors.

The secondary market's function is liquidity. A lender who can sell a loan recovers its capital and can lend again, so the supply of mortgage credit does not depend on any one lender's balance sheet.

Two consequences a licensee should be able to explain:

Underwriting standards flow backward from the secondary market. Lenders originate to standards that make loans saleable, which is why documentation requirements can feel disconnected from a particular borrower's obvious ability to pay.

Servicing frequently transfers. A borrower's loan may be sold and the servicing directed elsewhere, and the borrower's obligations follow the note.

3.2 Sources of financing

SourceCharacter
Depository institutionsBanks and credit unions lending from deposits and other funds
Mortgage bankersOriginate and fund loans, frequently selling them into the secondary market
Mortgage brokersArrange loans between borrowers and lenders; do not lend their own funds
Private and hard money lendersIndividual or fund lenders, typically shorter term and higher rate, often asset-focused
Seller financingThe seller carries all or part of the price
Government-backed programsAn agency insures or guarantees the lender, supporting terms qualifying borrowers might not otherwise obtain

3.3 Government-backed programs, and three things licensees misstate

Government-backed programs share a structure: an agency insures or guarantees the lender against certain losses, reducing risk and supporting terms — notably lower down payments.

  • The government is not the lender. Private lenders make the loans.
  • The loans bear interest on ordinary terms.
  • They are not restricted to new construction, and not to first-time buyers as a general matter.

Government programs commonly carry property condition requirements, which matters to a seller evaluating an offer: a property with deferred maintenance may not satisfy them. That is a closing-probability question, not a preference.

3.4 Conventional financing and mortgage insurance

A conventional loan is one not insured or guaranteed by a government agency. Where the down payment is below a program threshold, mortgage insurance is commonly required, adding to the monthly cost and protecting the lender rather than the borrower.

Students consistently get that last point wrong. Mortgage insurance protects the lender. The borrower pays for it, and receives access to financing at a lower down payment in exchange.

3.5 The Federal Reserve and rates, in outline

Monetary policy influences the cost and availability of credit, and mortgage rates respond to broader capital market conditions rather than being set by any single institution.

What a licensee should take from this: rates move for reasons outside any participant's control, and predicting them is not within the license. "Rates will be lower in the spring" is a prediction stated as certainty, on which a client may act.

3.6 What a licensee may say about financing

May: explain the general characteristics of common program types; explain what preapproval is; explain how the appraisal interacts with the loan; explain how a credit affects the transaction; recommend the buyer speak with more than one lender; explain what tends to make an offer read as strong.

May not: recommend a specific program as suitable for this buyer; predict what a lender will approve; predict the direction of rates; advise on structuring the buyer's finances to qualify; or suggest anything be omitted or characterized differently on an application.

And never accept anything of value for referring a buyer to a settlement service provider. Referrals happen; payment for them does not, and disclosure does not cure it.

3.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain what the secondary market does and why it shapes underwriting, and state who mortgage insurance protects.

4. Interest, amortization, and points

4.1 Simple interest

Real estate loans generally use simple interest — interest computed on the outstanding principal, not on accumulated interest.

Interest = Principal × Rate × Time

A $400,000 loan at 6% for one year: 400,000 × 0.06 = $24,000 annual interest.

One month's interest: $24,000 ÷ 12 = $2,000.

4.2 How an amortized payment splits

Each payment on an amortized loan covers the interest accrued since the last payment, and the remainder reduces principal.

$400,000 at 6%, monthly payment $2,398.20.

Month 1: interest = 400,000 × 0.06 ÷ 12 = $2,000.00. Principal = 2,398.20 − 2,000.00 = $398.20. New balance: $399,601.80.

Month 2: interest = 399,601.80 × 0.06 ÷ 12 = $1,998.01. Principal = $400.19. New balance: $399,201.61.

The pattern is the lesson. Early payments are almost entirely interest; the principal portion grows each month while the interest portion shrinks. This is why a borrower five years into a thirty-year loan has repaid far less principal than the elapsed fraction suggests — and why a seller's payoff surprises him.

4.3 Points

One point equals one percent of the loan amount.

Discount points are paid to reduce the interest rate. Origination points are a charge for making the loan. Both are quoted the same way and do different things.

2 points on a $400,000 loan = 400,000 × 0.02 = $8,000.

The break-even calculation is the useful one:

Paying 2 points ($8,000) reduces the payment by $140 per month.

$8,000 ÷ $140 = 57 months, about 4 years 9 months.

A borrower who expects to hold the loan longer than that comes out ahead; one who expects to sell or refinance sooner does not.

A licensee may explain the arithmetic. Whether this borrower should pay points depends on his plans, tax situation, and alternatives — which is a lender and advisor question.

4.4 Rate structures

StructureCharacteristicThe borrower's exposure
Fixed rateRate and payment fixed for the termNone to rate movement; pays for that certainty
Adjustable rateRate changes per an index plus a margin, within capsPayment changes over time
Interest-only periodPayments cover interest only for a periodPrincipal is not retired during it
BalloonPayments do not retire principal by maturityA large sum due at maturity

For an adjustable-rate loan, the rate at each adjustment is the index plus the lender's margin, subject to the note's caps — not the lender's discretion, and not the borrower's credit at that time.

Caps limit movement: a periodic cap limits change at any single adjustment; a lifetime cap limits total movement over the loan's life.

4.5 Nominal and effective rates

The nominal rate is the rate stated in the note. The effective rate reflects the actual cost including points and certain fees, which is why a loan with a lower stated rate can cost more.

The annual percentage rate is the disclosed measure designed to make loans comparable by including certain costs beyond interest. It is a comparison tool with defined contents, and a licensee should point a borrower to it rather than computing or interpreting it for him.

4.6 Impounds

Many loans require an impound or escrow account, into which the borrower pays monthly amounts toward property taxes and insurance, which the servicer then pays when due.

Two practical consequences: the monthly obligation is larger than principal and interest alone, and an impound shortage after a tax or insurance increase produces a payment increase on a loan the borrower believed was fixed. Explaining that in advance prevents an angry call the licensee will otherwise receive.

4.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to compute a month's interest and the principal split, compute points and a break-even, and explain what determines an adjustable rate at adjustment.

5. Loan-to-value and qualifying ratios

5.1 Loan-to-value

LTV = loan amount ÷ value, where value is the lower of appraised value and purchase price.

A $920,000 purchase with a $736,000 loan: 736,000 ÷ 920,000 = 80% LTV.

If the appraisal comes in at $900,000, the lender lends against $900,000. At 80%: 900,000 × 0.80 = $720,000 — $16,000 less than planned.

That single rule — lenders lend against the lower of value or price — explains every consequence of a low appraisal, and it is worth memorizing in exactly that form.

The buyer's options are then: bring the additional funds, renegotiate the price, negotiate another resolution, or cancel if a contingency permits and its procedure is followed.

What does not happen automatically: the price does not adjust to the appraised value by operation of any loan program, the loan is not denied, and no one is entitled to a second appraisal as of right.

5.2 Combined loan-to-value

Where more than one loan is secured by the property, CLTV measures the total.

A $920,000 property with a $736,000 first and a $46,000 second: (736,000 + 46,000) ÷ 920,000 = 782,000 ÷ 920,000 = 85% CLTV.

5.3 Equity

Equity = value − liens. It changes with value, with principal reduction, and with new borrowing — which is why a homeowner who takes a home equity line has converted equity into debt without any change in the property.

5.4 Qualifying ratios

Lenders measure a borrower's capacity with ratios. The two conventional ones:

The front-end (housing) ratio = housing expense ÷ gross monthly income. Housing expense conventionally includes principal, interest, taxes, and insurance — PITI — plus association dues where applicable.

The back-end (total debt) ratio = total monthly obligations ÷ gross monthly income, including the housing expense plus other recurring debt.

Gross monthly income $9,000. Proposed PITI $2,520. Car payment $480, student loan $300, credit minimums $120.

Front-end: 2,520 ÷ 9,000 = 28%. Back-end: (2,520 + 480 + 300 + 120) ÷ 9,000 = 3,420 ÷ 9,000 = 38%.

Thresholds vary by program, by compensating factors, and over time. A licensee should be able to compute the ratios and explain what they measure, and should never state a threshold as a rule — that is a lender question, and stating it is the kind of confident error that costs a buyer a property.

5.5 The three numbers, restated

From the Practice course, because it belongs to the mathematics:

What the buyer can borrow — the lender's number. What cash is available — down payment, closing costs, reserves, moving costs, and the immediate costs of occupying a home. Buyers routinely plan to the down payment and forget the rest. What the buyer is willing to spend — often lower than the first, and the number that should drive the search.

A licensee who searches to the lender's maximum is showing properties the buyer will feel unable to afford.

5.6 What jeopardizes an approval

Advise the buyer of these in writing at the start of escrow, because by the time they surface it is usually late:

Buyer actionEffect
Opening new credit, financing furniture or a carChanges the debt-to-income analysis the approval rested on
Changing or leaving a jobEmployment is a condition; a change may end the approval
Large unexplained depositsRequires sourcing; may look like undisclosed borrowed funds
Moving money between accountsComplicates asset verification
Paying off debt with funds needed for closingReduces verified cash to close
Missing a payment on any obligationAffects credit at the re-check

A short written note at the start of escrow prevents more failed transactions than any other single communication a licensee sends.

5.7 Occupancy is a material fact

Occupancy — owner-occupied, second home, or investment — affects pricing, program eligibility, and which consumer protections apply. It is therefore a material fact the lender relies on.

A buyer who asks a licensee to record owner-occupancy on a property he intends to rent is asking for participation in loan fraud. Decline plainly, explain why, and record the loan as it actually is. Whether he can afford the payment either way is beside the point: the misstatement is about obtaining terms on a false representation of the risk being priced.

5.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to compute LTV, CLTV, and both qualifying ratios, and explain why the lower of value or price governs.

6. Default, foreclosure, and deficiency

6.1 What default is

Default is a failure to perform an obligation under the note or the security instrument. Non-payment is the common form; others include failure to pay taxes, failure to maintain insurance, and unauthorized transfer triggering the alienation clause.

Acceleration is what makes default consequential. Without an acceleration clause a lender could pursue only missed payments; with one, the entire balance becomes due.

6.2 The two paths

Nonjudicial foreclosureJudicial foreclosure
MechanismThe trustee's power of saleA court action
SpeedFasterSlower
Court involvementNoneYes
Deficiency judgmentGenerally not availableMay be available, subject to limits
Redemption after saleGenerally noneA statutory redemption period may apply

The trade-off is the point. A lender proceeding nonjudicially gets speed and gives up the deficiency; a lender proceeding judicially may preserve a deficiency claim and accepts a slower, costlier process. In California residential practice, nonjudicial foreclosure predominates.

6.3 The nonjudicial sequence, in outline

The process is statutory and its details are legal. In outline:

  1. Default occurs, and the beneficiary elects to proceed.
  2. A notice of default is recorded, beginning the statutory period.
  3. A reinstatement period runs, during which the borrower may generally cure by paying the arrearage and permitted costs — not the accelerated balance.
  4. A notice of sale is recorded, published, posted, and mailed, after the applicable period.
  5. The trustee's sale occurs, and title passes by trustee's deed.

The reinstatement window is the point most misunderstood by borrowers. For a period, curing the arrearage restores the loan; after that window closes, the borrower's route is generally redemption before sale — paying the full accelerated amount — which is a materially different sum.

California also imposes borrower-protective requirements on servicers in the residential foreclosure context, including contact and single-point-of-contact obligations. The specifics are legal, and a licensee's role is to know they exist and route the borrower to counsel and to a HUD-approved housing counselor.

6.4 Deficiency

A deficiency is the shortfall where the sale proceeds do not satisfy the debt. California restricts deficiency judgments substantially:

  • Generally unavailable after a nonjudicial sale, whatever the loan type.
  • Restricted for purchase money loans in defined circumstances — protection connected to the loan having been used to acquire the property.
  • Subject to limits even in judicial foreclosure, including valuation-based constraints.

Whether a deficiency is available in a specific case is a legal question with significant consequences, and it is exactly the question a distressed client will ask a licensee. The answer is a referral.

6.5 Alternatives to foreclosure

AlternativeWhat it is
ReinstatementCuring the arrearage within the window
Forbearance or modificationThe lender agrees to pause, restructure, or change terms
Short saleA sale for less than the balance, with lienholder approval
Deed in lieu of foreclosureA voluntary conveyance to the lender

Short sales and deeds in lieu carry credit, tax, and deficiency consequences that are legal and tax questions. A licensee who tells a distressed seller that a short sale "won't hurt as much" or that a deficiency "gets wiped out" has given advice in the client's most consequential moment, on a question the licensee cannot answer.

And a deed in lieu does not automatically extinguish junior liens, which is one reason a lender may decline one.

6.6 Junior liens and the sale

A senior foreclosure generally extinguishes junior liens as claims against the property — while the underlying debts may survive as personal obligations, subject to the deficiency limits above.

That is why priority matters and why a junior lienholder monitors senior loans: a second wiped out by a first's foreclosure has lost its security.

6.7 The licensee working with distressed sellers

Distressed transactions concentrate every risk in this course, and three practices control most of it:

  1. Refer early and repeatedly. Counsel for the legal consequences, a tax professional for the tax consequences, and a HUD-approved housing counselor for the process. Do it in writing.
  2. Never predict an outcome — approval of a short sale, forgiveness of a deficiency, the credit effect, or the timeline.
  3. Recognize the fraud patterns. Distressed situations attract equity-stripping schemes, rescue arrangements, and transfers that leave the owner worse off. A licensee who sees one escalates to the broker and does not participate, however the request is framed.

6.8 Two loans, compared honestly

A borrower is offered two structures on the same purchase.

  • Loan A: fixed rate for the full term, no points, higher rate.
  • Loan B: fixed for an initial period and adjustable afterward, two points paid at closing, lower initial rate.

The comparison a licensee is not qualified to perform, and the analysis that shows why it matters anyway.

Section 4's mechanics supply the vocabulary. Points are paid now to reduce the rate later, which makes the comparison a question about how long the borrower holds the loan. The shorter the holding period, the worse the points look; the longer it is, the better.

Section 4's amortization supplies the second half. Early payments on an amortizing loan are mostly interest, so a borrower who sells in four years has paid down very little principal — which means the difference between the two structures is nearly all rate and points rather than equity.

The adjustable feature is the third variable, and it is the one presented worst. The initial payment on Loan B is the number the borrower will be shown. It is the one figure guaranteed to be true for the shortest time, and the meaningful number is the worst case: what the payment becomes if the rate adjusts to its maximum.

What determines the right answer, and none of it is on the rate sheet: how long the borrower expects to hold the property, how stable his income is, what reserves he holds, and what he would do if the payment rose to its maximum.

What the licensee does.

He explains the mechanics — what points are, what amortization means early in a loan, what an adjustment does — because these are concepts, and Section 4 exists so that he can.

He tells the borrower to ask the lender for both structures on a common basis, including the worst-case payment on Loan B and a total cost over the holding period the borrower actually expects.

He asks the questions the rate sheet does not — holding period, income stability, reserves — because those are the facts that decide it and nobody else in the transaction is asking.

He records the conversation.

What he does not do. Recommend either loan, characterize one as cheaper, tell the borrower "you can always refinance," or opine that rates will move in a direction. The first three are the lender's work and the fourth is a prediction stated as a plan — which is, across this entire course, the single most damaging sentence available in a financing conversation.

6.9 Checkpoint

You should be able to compare the two foreclosure paths on speed and deficiency, explain the reinstatement window, and state what a licensee says to a distressed client asking about a deficiency.

Key terms

TermDefinition
Promissory noteThe promise to repay. The note is the debt.
Security instrumentThe pledge of property as security. The security instrument is the remedy.
Maker / payeeThe party promising to pay; the party entitled to receive.
Negotiable instrumentA note transferable so a subsequent holder may take free of certain defenses. Why loans are sold.
Acceleration clauseMakes the entire balance due on default. Without it a lender could pursue only missed payments.
Alienation (due-on-sale) clausePermits the lender to call the loan due on transfer. Why loans are generally not assumable without consent.
Straight noteInterest payments with principal due at maturity.
Amortized notePayments retiring interest and principal. Fully amortized retires completely; partially amortized leaves a balloon.
Deed of trustCalifornia's dominant security instrument. Three parties: trustor (borrower), beneficiary (lender), trustee (holds the power of sale).
MortgageA two-party security instrument: mortgagor and mortgagee.
Power of saleThe trustee's authority to sell on default without court action. The feature producing nonjudicial foreclosure.
ReconveyanceThe recorded release when the debt is satisfied. Payment does not clear the record; the document does.
Purchase money loanA loan used to acquire the property, treated differently for deficiency purposes.
AssumptionThe buyer takes over the loan, generally requiring consent — and the seller remains liable unless released by novation.
Subject toThe buyer takes title without assuming; the loan remains the seller's obligation and remains secured by the property.
Blanket loanOne loan secured by multiple parcels, typically with a release clause.
Primary / secondary marketWhere loans are originated; where existing loans are bought and sold. The secondary market supplies liquidity and shapes underwriting standards backward.
Mortgage brokerArranges loans; does not lend its own funds.
Government-backed programAn agency insures or guarantees the lender. The government is not the lender, the loans bear ordinary interest, and property condition requirements are a closing-probability fact.
Mortgage insuranceCommonly required below a down payment threshold. Protects the lender, paid by the borrower.
Simple interestInterest = Principal × Rate × Time, computed on outstanding principal.
Amortization splitEach payment covers accrued interest first; the remainder reduces principal. Early payments are almost entirely interest.
PointOne percent of the loan amount. Discount points buy down the rate; origination points charge for making the loan.
Break-evenCost of points ÷ monthly saving = months to recover.
Index plus marginWhat sets an adjustable rate at each adjustment, subject to the note's periodic and lifetime caps. Not lender discretion, not the borrower's later credit.
Nominal / effective rateThe rate stated in the note; the actual cost including points and certain fees.
Impound accountMonthly collections for taxes and insurance. A shortage produces a payment increase on a loan the borrower thought was fixed.
Loan-to-valueLoan ÷ the lower of appraised value and purchase price. The rule explaining every consequence of a low appraisal.
Combined loan-to-valueTotal secured debt ÷ value.
EquityValue − liens.
Front-end ratioHousing expense (PITI plus dues) ÷ gross monthly income.
Back-end ratioTotal monthly obligations ÷ gross monthly income. Thresholds vary and are never stated by a licensee as a rule.
OccupancyA material fact the lender relies on. Misstating it is loan fraud, regardless of affordability.
Nonjudicial foreclosureSale under the power of sale. Faster; generally no deficiency and generally no post-sale redemption.
Judicial foreclosureA court action. Slower; a deficiency may be available subject to limits, and a statutory redemption period may apply.
ReinstatementCuring the arrearage and permitted costs within the statutory window — not the accelerated balance.
Redemption before salePaying the full accelerated amount after the reinstatement window closes.
DeficiencyThe shortfall where sale proceeds do not satisfy the debt. Substantially restricted in California, and always a legal question.
Short saleA sale for less than the balance, requiring lienholder approval, with credit, tax, and deficiency consequences for counsel.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A seller carrying financing asks his agent whether the buyer can "just take over the loan," and whether the seller will still owe anything. Separately, the buyer proposes taking title "subject to" the existing first, and the agent says the two arrangements are the same thing.

Correct the agent and answer the seller.

Solution. They are not the same thing, and the difference is who owes.

Assumption: the buyer takes over the loan. It generally requires lender consent because of the alienation (due-on-sale) clause — and critically, the seller remains liable unless released by novation. A seller who "let the buyer assume" and assumes he is released has assumed a novation he did not obtain.

Subject to: the buyer takes title without assuming. The loan remains the seller's obligation and remains secured by the property — so the seller has transferred the property, kept the debt, and lost control of whether it gets paid. It also triggers the alienation clause, with real risk for both parties.

What the licensee does: explain the mechanism, and route the structure to counsel. Whether the seller is released, and what the lender may do on transfer, are legal questions.

Problem 2

A note contains an acceleration clause and an alienation clause. The borrower misses three payments; separately, another borrower transfers the property to her adult son. A licensee says both clauses do the same thing and that a lender "can always call the loan."

Distinguish them.

Solution. They respond to different events and do different work.

Acceleration responds to default. On default it makes the entire balance immediately due — and without it, a lender could pursue only the missed payments. That is why foreclosure depends on it: the lender is enforcing an accelerated debt, not three payments.

Alienation responds to transfer. It permits the lender to call the loan due when the property is transferred, which is why loans are generally not assumable without lender consent. The transfer to the son triggers this clause, not the acceleration clause.

"A lender can always call the loan" is wrong. The lender's rights come from the clauses actually in the instrument and the events that trigger them.

Problem 3

Compute for a $400,000 loan at 6% with a monthly payment of $2,398.20: (a) the first month's interest and principal split; (b) the second month's; (c) the cost of 2 points; (d) the break-even if those points reduce the payment by $140 per month.

Then state what the pattern in (a) and (b) explains.

Solution. (a) Interest = 400,000 × 0.06 ÷ 12 = $2,000.00. Principal = 2,398.20 − 2,000.00 = $398.20. Balance: $399,601.80.

(b) Interest = 399,601.80 × 0.06 ÷ 12 = $1,998.01. Principal = $400.19. Balance: $399,201.61.

(c) 400,000 × 0.02 = $8,000. One point is one percent of the loan amount — not the price.

(d) 8,000 ÷ 140 = 57 months, about 4 years 9 months. A borrower holding longer comes out ahead; one selling or refinancing sooner does not.

The pattern: early payments are almost entirely interest, and the principal portion grows only slowly. That is why a borrower five years into a thirty-year loan has repaid far less principal than the elapsed fraction suggests — and it is the arithmetic behind the payoff surprise a seller gets at closing.

On (d): a licensee may explain the arithmetic. Whether this borrower should pay points depends on his plans, tax situation, and alternatives — a lender and advisor question.

Problem 4

A buyer with an adjustable-rate loan asks his agent what his rate will be at the first adjustment. The agent says it depends on the lender and on whether the buyer's credit has improved, and adds that rates "will definitely be lower by spring."

Correct each statement.

Solution. All three are wrong.

The rate at adjustment is the index plus the lender's margin, subject to the note's caps. Not the lender's discretion.

Not the borrower's credit at that time either — the margin was set when the loan was made.

Caps limit the movement: a periodic cap limits change at any single adjustment; a lifetime cap limits total movement over the loan's life. Those are in the note, and the borrower should read them.

The rate prediction is outside the license. Mortgage rates respond to broader capital market conditions and are not set by any single institution. "Rates will be lower in the spring" is a prediction stated as certainty, on which a client may act.

Worth adding while the subject is open: an impound shortage after a tax or insurance increase raises the payment on a loan the borrower thought was fixed. Explaining that in advance prevents an angry call.

Problem 5

A $920,000 purchase with 20% down. The appraisal comes in at $900,000. The buyer's agent tells him the price will adjust automatically to the appraised value, that the loan is now denied, and that he can demand a second appraisal.

Compute the shortfall and correct all three statements.

Solution. Lenders lend against the lower of appraised value and purchase price — so against $900,000. At 80%: 900,000 × 0.80 = $720,000, against the $736,000 planned. A $16,000 shortfall in loan proceeds.

All three statements are wrong. The price does not adjust to appraised value by operation of any loan program. The loan is not denied. And no one is entitled to a second appraisal as of right.

The buyer's actual options: bring the additional $16,000, renegotiate the price in whole or part, negotiate another resolution such as a credit or a term change, or cancel if a contingency permits and its procedure is followed.

The rule worth memorizing in exactly that form — the lower of value or price — explains every consequence of a low appraisal.

Problem 6

Gross monthly income $9,000. Proposed PITI $2,520. Car payment $480, student loan $300, credit card minimums $120. The buyer's preapproval says $1.1 million; his comfortable payment supports about $860,000. A relative has offered $100,000 of help.

Compute both ratios, and address the three numbers and the gift.

Solution. Front-end: 2,520 ÷ 9,000 = 28%. Back-end: (2,520 + 480 + 300 + 120) ÷ 9,000 = 3,420 ÷ 9,000 = 38%.

A licensee computes and explains the ratios and never states a threshold as a rule. Thresholds vary by program, by compensating factors, and over time — that is a lender question, and stating one is the kind of confident error that costs a buyer a property.

Search to $860,000, not $1.1 million. The three numbers are what he can borrow, what cash is available — down payment, closing costs, reserves, moving costs, and the immediate costs of occupying a home, which buyers routinely forget — and what he is willing to spend, which is the number that should drive the search. Searching to the lender's maximum shows him properties he will feel unable to afford.

On the $100,000: confirm with the lender first. Ask him to confirm how the assistance would be treated and documented, do not build the search around the higher figure until confirmed, and do not contact the relative directly. A gift and a loan are treated differently, because an undisclosed loan is a hidden obligation that undermines the lender's capacity analysis.

Problem 7

Two weeks into escrow a buyer finances $9,000 of furniture, moves $22,000 from a relative into his checking account, and pays off his car with part of his down payment funds. He also asks the licensee to record the property as owner-occupied, though he intends to rent it, noting he can afford either payment.

Assess each act.

Solution. All three financial moves attack the file the approval rested on, and the occupancy request is a different category entirely.

Financing furniture changes the debt-to-income analysis. The $22,000 deposit requires sourcing and may look like undisclosed borrowed funds. Paying off the car with closing funds solves one ratio by breaking another — it reduces verified cash to close.

Underlying all three: an approval rests on the file as underwritten, and lenders commonly re-verify credit and employment before funding. The written start-of-escrow warning — do not open credit, change jobs, or move large sums without telling your lender first — prevents more failed transactions than any other communication a licensee sends. Also on that list: leaving a job and missing a payment on any obligation.

The occupancy request is loan fraud, and affordability is beside the point. Occupancy affects pricing, program eligibility, and which consumer protections apply, and is therefore a material fact the lender relies on. The misstatement is not about whether he will pay; it is about obtaining terms on a false representation of the risk being priced. Decline plainly, explain why, and record the loan as it actually is.

Problem 8

A distressed homeowner two payments behind asks her agent: can she still save the house, will a short sale be approved, will she owe the difference, and would a deed in lieu clear her second mortgage. The agent tells her a short sale "won't hurt as much," that the deficiency "gets wiped out in California," and that a deed in lieu will clear everything.

Correct the advice and state the correct posture.

Solution. Every answer the agent gave was a legal or tax conclusion, and at least two are wrong as stated.

On saving the house: for a period after a notice of default is recorded, a reinstatement window lets the borrower cure by paying the arrearage and permitted costs — not the accelerated balance. After that window closes, the route is generally redemption before sale, paying the full accelerated amount, which is a materially different sum. That distinction is the one borrowers misunderstand most.

On approval: never predicted. A short sale requires lienholder approval.

On deficiency: California restricts deficiency judgments substantially — generally unavailable after a nonjudicial sale, and restricted for purchase money loans in defined circumstances. But whether a deficiency is available in her case is a legal question with significant consequences, and a short sale's deficiency treatment depends on what the lienholders agree to. "It gets wiped out" is not an answer a licensee may give.

On the deed in lieu: it does not automatically extinguish junior liens — which is one reason a lender may decline one, and it means her second may survive.

The correct posture: refer early and repeatedly, in writing — counsel for the legal consequences, a tax professional for the tax consequences, and a HUD-approved housing counselor for the process. Predict nothing. And recognize that distressed situations attract equity-stripping and rescue schemes: a licensee who sees one escalates to the broker and does not participate, however the request is framed.

Chapter summary

The note is the debt; the security instrument is the remedy. A note without security is unsecured; security without a note secures nothing.

Acceleration responds to default; alienation responds to transfer. They do different work.

California uses a three-party deed of trust, and the trustee's power of sale is what produces nonjudicial foreclosure, the reinstatement window, and the deficiency consequences.

Payment does not clear the record; a recorded reconveyance does.

Priority determines who is paid first and whose interest survives foreclosure, and subordination is a decision for counsel rather than a formality.

Assumption leaves the seller liable without a novation; taking subject to leaves the debt entirely with the seller. Route both to counsel.

The secondary market supplies liquidity and shapes underwriting backward, which is why documentation requirements can feel disconnected from an obvious ability to pay.

Government-backed does not mean government-lent, and mortgage insurance protects the lender while the borrower pays for it.

Interest is simple: principal × rate × time. Each amortized payment covers accrued interest first, which is why early payments are nearly all interest and why the payoff surprises sellers.

One point is one percent of the loan. Compute the break-even; leave the decision to the borrower and the lender.

An adjustable rate is index plus margin within the note's caps — never lender discretion, never the borrower's later credit — and never predict the direction of rates.

Lenders lend against the lower of appraised value and purchase price. That sentence explains every consequence of a low appraisal, and nothing about the resolution is automatic.

Compute the ratios; never state a threshold as a rule. Search to what the buyer is willing to spend, not the lender's maximum.

Send the start-of-escrow warning in writing about credit, employment, and moving funds — and refuse any request to misstate occupancy, whatever the buyer can afford.

Nonjudicial foreclosure trades the deficiency for speed; judicial foreclosure trades speed for a possible deficiency and a redemption period. Reinstatement cures the arrearage; redemption requires the accelerated balance.

With distressed clients, refer early and in writing, predict nothing, and recognize the fraud patterns.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Identify the instruments involved and what each does, identify every clause triggered by the facts and by what event, perform each computation the scenario requires — showing the interest split, points, break-even, loan-to-value, and qualifying ratios as applicable — and state what the lower-of-value-or-price rule produces, identify each event during escrow that jeopardizes the approval and the written warning you would have sent, state what you would decline to answer and why, and identify what you would refer to a lender, to a tax professional, to a housing counselor, and to counsel and by what deadline. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 12

Valuation and appraisal foundations

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Distinguish value, price, and cost, and state the elements and economic principles that create value.
  • Explain highest and best use and apply its four tests.
  • Apply the sales comparison approach, adjust in the correct direction, and reconcile indications into a supported range.
  • Apply the cost approach, compute depreciation, and identify when the approach is most and least reliable.
  • Apply the income capitalization approach and compute value, rate, and income from the IRV relationship.
  • State the boundary between an appraisal and a comparative market analysis, and explain appraiser independence.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: value concepts and highest and best use55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: the sales comparison and cost approaches50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: income capitalization and the appraisal boundary45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Value, price, and cost

1.1 Three different things

Students merge these constantly, and every valuation error downstream begins here.

TermDefinition
ValueWhat the property is worth — an opinion, formed as of a date, for a stated purpose
PriceWhat someone actually paid. A historical fact
CostWhat it took to create the improvements. Also a historical fact

Value is an opinion; price and cost are facts. All three can differ, and frequently do.

A property can cost $900,000 to build, be worth $700,000, and sell for $760,000 — all at the same time, without anything being wrong.

1.2 Market value

The appraisal standard is market value, and its definition contains conditions that do the work.

Market value is, in outline, the most probable price a property should bring in a competitive and open market under conditions requisite to a fair sale, where:

  • Buyer and seller are each typically motivated — neither is forced.
  • Both are well informed and acting in their own interests.
  • A reasonable exposure time is allowed in the open market.
  • Payment is in cash or its equivalent, with financing terms not affecting the price.
  • The price is unaffected by special financing or concessions.

Each condition excludes something. A forced sale, an uninformed buyer, a two-week marketing period, or seller-paid concessions inflating the number each produce a price that is not market value.

That is why a distressed sale may be evidence of a transaction and poor evidence of value, and why the analysis in Section 3 asks whether a comparable reflects this market.

1.3 The elements of value

Four elements must be present for value to exist, remembered as DUST:

ElementWhat it requires
D — DemandSomeone wants it, with purchasing power
U — UtilityIt satisfies a need or use
S — ScarcityIt is in limited supply
T — TransferabilityOwnership can be transferred

Demand without purchasing power is not demand. That single qualification explains why desire alone does not create value, and why affordability constraints move markets.

Transferability is the one students forget. A property nobody can convey — clouded title, unresolved authority — has impaired value for that reason alone.

1.4 The economic principles

These recur throughout the approaches, and each has a practical application.

PrincipleWhat it says
Supply and demandPrice responds to their relationship in this market
SubstitutionA buyer will pay no more than the cost of an equally desirable substitute. The foundation of all three approaches
Highest and best useValue reflects the most productive legally permitted use — Section 2
ConformityMaximum value arises where a property conforms reasonably to its surroundings
ProgressionA lesser property benefits from surrounding better ones
RegressionA better property is dragged down by surrounding lesser ones
ContributionAn improvement adds what the market pays for it, not what it cost
Increasing and diminishing returnsSuccessive investment adds value up to a point, then less than it costs
AnticipationValue reflects expected future benefits
ChangeValue is a snapshot; markets and neighborhoods move
BalanceValue is maximized where the factors of production are in equilibrium
ExternalitiesFactors outside the property affect its value

1.5 Substitution is the engine

Every approach in this lesson is an application of substitution:

  • Sales comparison — a buyer will pay no more than a similar property sold for.
  • Cost — a buyer will pay no more than the cost of building an equivalent, less depreciation.
  • Income — an investor will pay no more than a comparable income stream costs.

A student who holds substitution understands why there are three approaches and why they should converge.

1.6 Contribution is not cost

The most common client misconception, and one a licensee must be able to explain without giving offense:

Money spent on a property does not become value in the property. The market decides what an improvement contributes, and for specialized improvements the contribution is usually well below the cost.

A $150,000 recording studio built into a garage in a neighborhood of family homes may contribute a fraction of that, because the pool of buyers who want it — and would pay for it — is small. That is over-improvement relative to the neighborhood, and it is a recognized phenomenon rather than a rhetorical device for arguing a seller down.

The complement is also true: some modest improvements contribute more than they cost, because they remove a specific buyer objection.

1.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to distinguish value, price, and cost; name the elements of value; and explain contribution to a seller without giving offense.

2. Highest and best use

2.1 The concept

Highest and best use is the reasonably probable use that produces the highest value. Appraisal of the land assumes it; appraisal of an improved property considers whether the existing improvement represents it.

2.2 The four tests

A use must satisfy all four, applied in order:

TestQuestion
Legally permissibleIs the use allowed — zoning, private restrictions, entitlements?
Physically possibleCan the site support it — size, shape, soils, access, utilities?
Financially feasibleDoes it produce a positive return?
Maximally productiveAmong the uses passing the first three, which produces the highest value?

The order matters. A use that is not legally permissible is excluded before feasibility is considered, however profitable it would be.

And "legally permissible" has two halves, from Lessons 05 and 06: zoning and recorded private restrictions, with the more restrictive governing. Zoning permitting a use does not authorize it where the CC&Rs prohibit it.

2.3 Interim use

Where the highest and best use is not yet feasible, the current use may be an interim use — economically reasonable now, expected to change.

A single-family home on a parcel in a corridor transitioning to commercial is the standard example: the house is the interim use, and the land may be worth more than the improved property.

2.4 When the improvement subtracts

Where the value of the land as if vacant exceeds the value of the improved property, the improvement has negative contribution — its removal cost is a deduction rather than an addition.

This is counterintuitive and real. A functionally obsolete building on a valuable site can make the property worth less than the bare land.

2.5 Why a licensee needs this

Three situations:

Pricing a property whose use is changing. A licensee who prices a transitioning property as a house may be leaving the land value on the table — or, in the other direction, may be pricing land value into a property whose entitlement does not support it.

Advising on improvements. A seller asking whether to add a fourth bedroom is asking a contribution and conformity question.

Recognizing where the analysis exceeds the license. Highest and best use for a development site is appraisal and entitlement work. A licensee who tells a buyer the parcel's highest and best use is a fourplex has made a legal, physical, and financial determination in one sentence.

2.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to apply the four tests in order and explain when an improvement has negative contribution.

3. The sales comparison approach

3.1 What it does

The sales comparison approach estimates value by analyzing closed sales of comparable properties, adjusting each for its differences from the subject, and reconciling the adjusted indications.

It is the primary approach for residential property, because residential buyers behave by substitution — they compare.

3.2 The direction of adjustment

The most-missed mechanic in valuation, and worth stating in a form that removes ambiguity.

Adjustments are made to the comparable, never to the subject.

The question each adjustment answers is: what would this comparable have sold for if it had been like the subject?

Therefore:

  • If the comparable is superior, its price is adjusted downward.
  • If the comparable is inferior, its price is adjusted upward.

The memory aid: CBS — Comparable Better, Subtract. And its complement: comparable worse, add.

3.3 A worked grid

Subject: 1,800 sq ft, 3 bed, 2 bath, 6,000 sq ft lot, 2-car garage, updated kitchen, no view.

Comp AComp BComp C
Sale price$845,000$902,000$810,000
Closed2 months ago5 months ago1 month ago
Living area1,8001,9801,700
Bed / bath3 / 24 / 23 / 2
Lot6,1006,0005,400
Garage2-car2-car1-car
KitchenUpdatedUpdatedOriginal
Adjustments
Market conditions—+$9,000—
Living area—−$27,000+$15,000
Bedroom count—−$10,000—
Lot size−$1,000—+$4,000
Garage——+$12,000
Kitchen——+$20,000
Net adjustment−$1,000−$28,000+$51,000
Adjusted value$844,000$874,000$861,000

Read what each line does:

  • Comp B closed five months ago in a rising market, so it is adjusted up — it would sell for more today.
  • Comp B is larger and has a fourth bedroom, both superior, so both are adjusted down toward the subject.
  • Comp C has an original kitchen and a one-car garage, both inferior, so both are adjusted up.

3.4 Reconciliation

Three adjusted values — $844,000, $874,000, $861,000 — are not averaged. Averaging treats a comparable requiring $51,000 of adjustment as exactly as reliable as one requiring $1,000, which it is not, and silently discards that information.

Reconciliation weights by reliability:

  1. Adjustment size, gross as well as net. A small net can conceal a large gross, and reliability tracks gross.
  2. Recency.
  3. Similarity in the characteristics driving this market.

A defensible conclusion: the supported range is approximately $845,000 to $870,000, most probable near $850,000 to $855,000, weighted toward Comp A as the least-adjusted and most similar indicator.

A range, a most-probable point, and a stated reason for the weighting — not a single number presented as fact.

3.5 Where adjustment amounts come from

Adjustment values are derived, in order of preference:

  1. Paired sales analysis — comparing sales differing in one characteristic to isolate its contribution.
  2. Market-derived rates — an established rate within this submarket, applied to modest differences only.
  3. Cost with depreciation — for a specific improvement, recognizing that contribution rarely equals cost.
  4. Reasoned judgment, disclosed as such.

The last is legitimate when the first three are unavailable, provided it is identified as judgment rather than presented as measurement.

3.6 What each data category proves

CategoryWhat it provesWhat it does not
Closed salesWhat buyers actually paidCurrent conditions, if the market moved
Pending salesWhere the market is moving nowThe final price; terms can change
Active listingsWhat sellers are asking; the competitionValue — asking prices are hopes
Expired and withdrawnWhat the market rejectedMuch alone, but a useful ceiling indicator

Active listings never establish value. A seller can ask any number, and three homes listed at a price for over ninety days without an offer are evidence the market has declined to pay it three separate times.

3.7 Common errors

ErrorWhy it is wrong
Adjusting the subject instead of the comparableReverses the entire logic
Averaging adjusted valuesTreats unreliable indicators as equal to reliable ones
Discarding every comparable with a differenceLeaves nothing to analyze; differences are what adjustments exist for
Applying a price-per-square-foot rate across large differencesContribution per square foot is not linear across a large gap
Many small adjustments reaching a targetThe grid becomes an argument for a predetermined number
Automatic exclusion or non-adjustment of a distressed saleWhether it reflects this market is an analytical question

3.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to adjust in the correct direction, reconcile without averaging, and say what an active listing proves.

4. The cost approach

4.1 What it does

The cost approach estimates value as:

Land value (as if vacant, at highest and best use) + cost of improvements new − accrued depreciation = indicated value

It applies substitution directly: a buyer will pay no more than the cost of acquiring a site and building an equivalent improvement.

4.2 Reproduction and replacement cost

Reproduction cost is the cost to construct an exact replica, including any obsolete features.

Replacement cost is the cost to construct a building of equivalent utility using current materials and methods.

Replacement cost is generally the more useful measure, because a buyer does not want the obsolete features. Reproduction cost matters for historic and unusual structures.

4.3 Cost estimating methods

MethodHow
Square foot (comparative)Applying a cost per square foot from comparable construction. Fast, and the common approach
Unit-in-placeCosting each component — foundation, framing, roofing — installed
Quantity surveyItemizing every material and labor cost. The most detailed and least practical

4.4 Depreciation: the three categories

Depreciation here means loss in value from any cause, and it comes in three forms. Distinguishing them is the examinable and practical skill.

TypeSourceCurable?
Physical deteriorationWear, age, deferred maintenanceOften curable — worn carpet, a failed roof. Sometimes not — structural aging
Functional obsolescenceSomething about the improvement itself — poor layout, one bathroom, an outdated system, an over-improvementSometimes curable
External (economic) obsolescenceSomething outside the property — a new freeway, a declining neighborhood, an adjacent nuisanceGenerally incurable, because the owner cannot change it

Curable means the cost to cure is justified by the value it adds. Incurable means it is not — either because the fix costs more than it returns or because it cannot be fixed at all.

External obsolescence is the one to recognize by its source. If the cause is off the property, the owner cannot cure it, and the loss is real.

4.5 A worked cost approach

Subject: 2,000 sq ft home, 12 years old, on a lot worth $340,000 as if vacant. Replacement cost new: $260 per square foot. Estimated total economic life: 60 years. Effective age: 12 years. Additional functional obsolescence for a single bathroom: $18,000. External obsolescence from a new arterial behind the lot: $25,000.

Cost new: 2,000 × $260 = $520,000.

Physical deterioration, straight-line (age-life): effective age ÷ total economic life = 12 ÷ 60 = 20%. $520,000 × 0.20 = $104,000.

Total accrued depreciation: $104,000 + $18,000 + $25,000 = $147,000.

Indicated value: $340,000 + $520,000 − $147,000 = $713,000.

Note that land is not depreciated. Depreciation applies to improvements only.
