4.6 Effective age versus actual age

Actual age is years since construction. Effective age reflects condition and updating.

A well-maintained, updated 30-year-old house may have an effective age of 12. A neglected 12-year-old house may have an effective age of 25. The age-life computation uses effective age, which is why the same chronological age produces different depreciation.

4.7 Where the approach is strong and weak

Strongest: new or nearly new construction, where depreciation is minimal and the estimate is close to actual cost. Special-purpose properties with no comparable sales — a school, a church, a fire station. Insurance valuation, where the question is replacement rather than market.

Weakest: older improvements, where depreciation must be estimated and the estimate carries the whole answer. Properties where land value is itself hard to establish.

The recurring misuse: treating the cost approach as a floor. A property is not worth what it cost to build, and telling a seller "you have $900,000 in it, so it's worth $900,000" is the contribution error at building scale.

4.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to run the cost approach with all three depreciation types and explain why external obsolescence is generally incurable.

5. The income capitalization approach

5.1 What it does

The income approach estimates value from the income a property produces. It is the primary approach for investment property, because investors buy income streams.

5.2 Building the income statement

The sequence, and each line matters:

LineComputation
Potential gross income (PGI)All income at full occupancy
− Vacancy and collection lossAn allowance, market-derived
= Effective gross income (EGI)
− Operating expensesFixed, variable, and reserves for replacement
= Net operating income (NOI)

Two exclusions students get wrong, and they are the examinable ones:

Debt service is not an operating expense, and depreciation is not an operating expense.

NOI measures the property's performance, independent of how any particular owner financed it or what their tax position is. Two buyers with different loans buy the same NOI.

Reserves for replacement are included — the annualized allowance for roofs, systems, and other items that will need replacing. Omitting them overstates NOI and therefore overstates value.

5.3 The IRV relationship

I = R × V — Income equals Rate times Value

V = I ÷ R — Value equals Income divided by Rate R = I ÷ V — Rate equals Income divided by Value

The capitalization rate expresses the relationship between a property's NOI and its value, and it is derived from the market — from sales of comparable income properties where both NOI and price are known.

5.4 Worked problems

A property produces NOI of $84,000. Comparable sales indicate a 6% capitalization rate.

V = 84,000 ÷ 0.06 = $1,400,000.

A property sold for $1,250,000 with NOI of $81,250. What rate does it indicate?

R = 81,250 ÷ 1,250,000 = 6.5%.

Full income statement. 12 units at $2,100 monthly. Vacancy and collection 5%. Operating expenses $118,000 including reserves. Market cap rate 5.75%.

PGI = 12 × 2,100 × 12 = $302,400. Less vacancy: 302,400 × 0.05 = 15,120. EGI = $287,280. Less expenses: 287,280 − 118,000 = NOI = $169,280. V = 169,280 ÷ 0.0575 = $2,943,999, say $2,944,000.

5.5 The inverse relationship

Rate and value move in opposite directions for a given income.

The same $169,280 NOI at 5.0% = $3,385,600. At 7.0% = $2,418,286.

A two-point movement in the rate changes the indicated value by nearly a million dollars on identical income. That is why the rate's derivation matters more than the arithmetic, and why a licensee should never select a cap rate to reach a number.

A higher cap rate signals higher perceived risk or lower expected growth; a lower rate signals the opposite.

5.6 Gross rent and gross income multipliers

A simpler screening tool.

GRM = sale price ÷ gross monthly rent (residential) GIM = sale price ÷ gross annual income (commercial)

A property sold for $840,000 with gross monthly rent of $6,000. GRM = 840,000 ÷ 6,000 = 140. Applying that to a subject renting for $6,400: 6,400 × 140 = $896,000.

The limitation is the lesson. A multiplier uses gross income and therefore ignores expenses entirely. Two properties with identical gross rents and very different expense structures produce identical multiplier indications and very different values. It is a screening tool, not a valuation.

5.7 Reconciling the three approaches

An appraiser applies the approaches that fit the property and reconciles them — weighting by reliability and relevance, not averaging.

Property typeTypically weighted toward
Single-family residenceSales comparison
New constructionCost, supported by sales comparison
Apartment or commercialIncome capitalization
Special-purpose with no salesCost

Reconciliation is judgment, stated with its reasoning — the same discipline as reconciling comparables within the sales comparison approach.

5.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to build an income statement excluding debt service and depreciation, solve IRV in all three directions, and explain what a multiplier ignores.

6. Appraisal independence and CMA limits

6.1 The boundary

Comparative market analysisAppraisal
Prepared byA real estate licenseeA licensed or certified appraiser
PurposeTo advise a client on pricing or offer strategyAn independent opinion of value for a defined use, commonly lending
StandardsProfessional practice; not appraisal standardsProfessional appraisal standards
IndependenceAdvises a client the licensee representsIndependent of the parties
May be called an appraisalNoYes

The difference is not the data used. A licensee's analysis and an appraiser's report may rely on the same closed sales. The difference is who prepares it, under what standards, with what independence, and for what purpose.

6.2 What a licensee may and may not do

May: prepare a comparative market analysis; give an opinion of value to advise a client; explain how the opinion was formed; discuss what an appraisal is likely to consider.

May not: hold the analysis out as an appraisal; use it where an appraisal is required; represent that it satisfies a lender's valuation requirement; or perform an appraisal without the required license or certification.

6.3 Appraiser licensing, in outline

California licenses and certifies appraisers at levels tied to the complexity and value of property an appraiser may appraise, administered by the state's appraiser regulatory agency. Federally related transactions carry their own requirements.

A licensee's practical takeaway: appraiser qualification is a real credential with levels, and a valuation for a lending purpose requires an appraiser rather than a licensee, however experienced the licensee.

6.4 Independence

A licensee may provide genuinely relevant information to an appraiser through the proper channel — a list of sales the licensee believes relevant, information about condition and improvements, a copy of the contract.

A licensee may not communicate a target. "The deal dies without another $25,000" is exactly what independence rules exist to prevent, and a value that moves after that conversation is unreliable for the purpose the appraisal serves.

The distinction is between supplying information and supplying a target — and requests to "take another look" that arrive with a number attached are the pattern to refuse, including when the request comes from your own client.

6.5 When the appraisal disagrees

A lender's appraisal below the contract price is, first, a financing event rather than a verdict on the licensee's analysis. Lenders lend against the lower of appraised value and purchase price.

The options are practical rather than automatic: the buyer brings additional funds; the parties renegotiate; the parties negotiate another resolution; or the transaction is cancelled if a contingency permits and its procedure is followed.

What does not happen automatically: the price does not adjust to the appraised value by operation of any loan program, the loan is not denied, and no one is entitled to a second appraisal as of right.

Where the appraisal contingency has been removed, the practical options remain but the contractual rights are limited — and whether the buyer may cancel and what happens to his deposit is a legal question for counsel, not for the licensee.

6.6 Appraisal bias

Valuation is a judgment, and judgment is where bias operates. Concerns about appraisal bias — including in the treatment of neighborhoods and of the people occupying properties — are a recognized issue, and Lesson 14 takes up the fair housing framework.

Two points here. Objective, market-derived adjustment support is the structural control, because a derived adjustment can be examined and an impression cannot. And a licensee who believes an appraisal reflects bias should escalate to the broker and follow the reconsideration process available through the lender — not confront the appraiser and not advise the client on a claim.

6.7 What makes a licensee's opinion defensible

The test is reconstructability as of the date given: could someone reviewing this later see what data was used, what adjustments were made, what reasoning connected them, and when?

What the file should contain:

  1. The comparables used, with data and sources, as of the date of the analysis.
  2. The adjustment grid, with the direction and basis of each adjustment.
  3. The reconciliation, including what was weighted and why.
  4. Comparables considered and rejected, with the reason.
  5. The market conditions evidence — months of supply, days on market, direction.
  6. The recommendation, as a range with a most-probable point.
  7. The date and the licensee who prepared it.
  8. The written advice given, and the client's decision.

What is not on that list: that the property eventually sold near the estimate, which is luck rather than method; that the client signed an acknowledgment, which proves delivery rather than soundness; and that the brokerage's template was used, which proves formatting.

6.8 When the client chooses a different number

Price is the client's decision; the analysis and advice are the licensee's obligation. Where a seller lists above the supported range, the file needs the analysis, the written advice, the client's decision, and any agreed revisit strategy.

What must not happen: the client's reasoning being adopted as the brokerage's opinion of value, or the analysis being quietly revised upward to match the list price.

An analysis that follows the price rather than informing it is worse than no analysis. It documents nothing and looks, later, like an opinion manufactured to fit.

6.9 A worked reconciliation

Three approaches, one property: a forty-year-old single-family residence in an established neighborhood, owner-occupied, being valued for a listing decision.

  • Sales comparison indicates a range, with the three closest comparables clustering and one outlier requiring large gross adjustment.
  • Cost approach indicates a figure well above the comparison range.
  • Income capitalization indicates a figure well below it.

The reconciliation is not an average, and Section 3's reconciliation discussion makes the point. It is a judgment about which approach the market for this property actually uses.

Why sales comparison dominates here. Buyers of owner-occupied single-family homes decide by comparison to other homes. The approach mirrors the actual decision process of the actual buyers, which is the criterion.

Why the cost approach comes in high, and why that is expected. Section 4's method estimates replacement cost less depreciation plus land. Depreciation on a forty-year-old improvement is the hardest element to estimate, and it is systematically understated — physical wear is visible, functional obsolescence is easy to miss, and external obsolescence is frequently ignored entirely. A high cost indication on an older property usually means the depreciation estimate is light, not that the property is worth more than comparable sales say.

Why the income approach comes in low, and why it is nearly irrelevant here. Section 5's method converts income to value. Owner-occupied homes in most markets sell for more than their rent supports, because the buyers are not buying income. Applying the approach produces an accurate answer to a question no participant in this market is asking.

The outlier comparable. Section 3's adjustment discussion governs: a comparable requiring large gross adjustment is a weak comparable regardless of where its adjusted figure lands. It is given little weight, and the reasoning is stated rather than the comparable being quietly dropped.

The reconciliation, written. Primary weight to sales comparison, supported by the three clustering comparables; the cost approach as a check with its depreciation limitation stated; the income approach reported and given minimal weight with the reason. A range, with a most-probable figure inside it, and the reasoning for each on the page.

Why this matters for a licensee who will never appraise. Section 6.7's requirement is that a licensee's own opinion be defensible, and defensibility is exactly this: the reasoning is visible, the weight given to each indication is explained, and a reader who disagrees can see where. An opinion that states a number and shows nothing is not defensible even when it is right — and Section 6.1's boundary means the licensee's opinion is a comparative market analysis, never an appraisal, however carefully it was constructed.

6.10 The three sentences that create appraisal problems

Section 6.4 states the independence requirement and Section 6.6 addresses bias. In practice, licensee involvement in valuation goes wrong through a small number of sentences, each of which sounds harmless.

"The contract is at 640, so we need it to come in there." A target, communicated. The politeness of the phrasing does not change what was conveyed, and Section 6.4's requirement does not distinguish between pressure and a helpful heads-up.

"Here are a few comps I pulled that support the price." Supplying sales an appraiser may not have is legitimate when the sales are supplied because they are relevant. Supplying sales selected because they support a number is the same instruction with an extra step. The test is how the set was assembled, and a set assembled by working backward from a figure is a target in a different form.

"Let's use Whitfield — his numbers come in." The most serious of the three, because it is systemic. Selecting appraisers by favorable history builds the pressure into the ordering pattern, it affects every future file, and it is visible in the brokerage's own ordering records.

What is permissible, and it is substantial: providing the contract, the property's improvements and their dates, permits, and comparable sales offered as data. Information, never a target — and where the licensee cannot tell which one he is providing, the question is whether he assembled it before or after knowing the number the file needs.

And where the appraisal comes in low, Section 6.5 governs: it is a fact, not a problem to solve. It may support a reconsideration through the prescribed channel with additional data, and the appraiser remains free to reject it. What it does not support is pressure, and "my manager told me to" does not transfer responsibility for making the call.

6.11 Checkpoint

You should be able to state the appraisal-CMA boundary, distinguish supplying information from supplying a target, and list what makes an opinion reconstructable.

Key terms

TermDefinition
Value / price / costWhat it is worth — an opinion; what someone paid — a fact; what it took to create — a fact. All three can differ.
Market valueThe most probable price under conditions requisite to a fair sale — typically motivated and well-informed parties, reasonable exposure, cash-equivalent payment, unaffected by special concessions.
DUSTDemand (with purchasing power), Utility, Scarcity, Transferability — the elements of value.
SubstitutionA buyer will pay no more than the cost of an equally desirable substitute. The foundation of all three approaches.
Conformity, progression, regressionValue is maximized by reasonable conformity; a lesser property benefits from better neighbors; a better property is dragged by lesser ones.
ContributionWhat the market pays for an improvement, not what it cost.
Over-improvementAn improvement whose contribution falls well below cost because the buyer pool for it is small.
AnticipationValue reflects expected future benefits.
Highest and best useThe reasonably probable use producing the highest value, tested as legally permissible, physically possible, financially feasible, and maximally productive — in that order.
Interim useAn economically reasonable current use expected to change.
Negative contributionWhere land as if vacant exceeds the improved value, so removal cost is a deduction.
Direction of adjustmentMade to the comparable, never the subject. Comparable better, subtract; comparable worse, add.
Gross versus net adjustmentA small net can conceal a large gross; reliability tracks gross.
ReconciliationWeighting indications by reliability to reach a range and a most-probable point. Not averaging.
Paired sales analysisDeriving an adjustment by comparing sales differing in one characteristic. The preferred source.
Active listingsEvidence of asking prices and competition — never of value.
Reproduction versus replacement costCost of an exact replica including obsolete features; cost of equivalent utility using current methods. Replacement is generally the more useful measure.
Physical deteriorationLoss from wear, age, and deferred maintenance.
Functional obsolescenceLoss from something about the improvement itself — layout, one bathroom, an outdated system, an over-improvement.
External obsolescenceLoss from something outside the property. Generally incurable, because the owner cannot change it.
Curable / incurableWhether the cost to cure is justified by the value it adds.
Effective ageAge as reflected by condition and updating. The age-life computation uses effective age, not actual age.
Land is not depreciatedDepreciation applies to improvements only.
Potential gross incomeAll income at full occupancy.
Effective gross incomePGI less vacancy and collection loss.
Net operating incomeEGI less operating expenses including reserves for replacement — and excluding debt service and depreciation.
IRVI = R × V; V = I ÷ R; R = I ÷ V.
Capitalization rateThe relationship between NOI and value, derived from the market. Rate and value move in opposite directions for a given income.
Gross rent multiplierPrice ÷ gross monthly rent. A screening tool that ignores expenses entirely.
Comparative market analysisA licensee's opinion of value to advise a client. Not an appraisal and may never be called one.
Appraiser independenceA licensee may supply information to an appraiser and never a target — including at a client's request.
ReconstructabilityThe test of a defensible opinion: could a reviewer later see the data, the adjustments, the reasoning, and the date.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A seller says the house "cost $900,000 to build four years ago plus $150,000 for the recording studio in the garage, so it's worth at least $1,050,000." Comparable closed sales support $760,000.

Explain the analysis without giving offense.

Solution. Value, price, and cost are three different things. Value is an opinion formed as of a date; price and cost are historical facts. A property can cost $900,000 to build, be worth $700,000, and sell for $760,000 — all at once, without anything being wrong.

Contribution is the concept for the studio. Money spent on a property does not become value in the property; the market decides what an improvement contributes. Here the pool of buyers who want a recording studio — and would pay for one — is small, and the studio also consumed the garage, which most buyers in a neighborhood of family homes do want. That is over-improvement relative to the neighborhood, a recognized phenomenon rather than a device for arguing him down.

Say the complement honestly in the same conversation: the relationship runs both ways, and some modest improvements contribute more than they cost because they remove a specific buyer objection.

And the cost approach is not a floor. "You have $1,050,000 in it, so it's worth that" is the contribution error at building scale.

Problem 2

A parcel in a corridor transitioning to commercial holds a 60-year-old single-family house. Zoning now permits neighborhood commercial; the recorded CC&Rs from the original 1958 tract restrict the parcel to residential use. A licensee tells the owner the highest and best use is a retail building.

Apply the tests and assess the advice.

Solution. The four tests run in order, and the analysis stops at the first.

Legally permissible has two halves: zoning and recorded private restrictions, with the more restrictive governing. Zoning permits commercial; the CC&Rs prohibit it. Unless the restrictions can be addressed, retail is not legally permissible — and the analysis never reaches physically possible, financially feasible, or maximally productive, however profitable retail would be.

The advice is also outside the license. Telling an owner the highest and best use is a retail building makes a legal, physical, and financial determination in one sentence. Highest and best use for a development site is appraisal and entitlement work.

What is true and worth saying: the house may be an interim use — economically reasonable now and expected to change — and if the land as if vacant is worth more than the improved property, the improvement has negative contribution and its removal cost is a deduction. Both are reasons to get an appraiser and counsel involved rather than to price the property as a house by default.

Problem 3

Adjust each toward a subject of 1,800 sq ft, 3 bed, 2 bath, updated kitchen: (a) a comparable of 1,980 sq ft with 4 bedrooms; (b) a comparable with an original kitchen and a one-car garage; (c) a comparable that closed five months ago in a rising market.

State the direction and the reason for each.

Solution. Every adjustment answers one question: what would this comparable have sold for if it had been like the subject? Adjustments are made to the comparable, never the subject.

(a) Downward, twice. Larger living area and an extra bedroom are both superior — comparable better, subtract.

(b) Upward, twice. An original kitchen and a one-car garage are both inferior — comparable worse, add.

(c) Upward. The market has risen since it closed, so it would sell for more today. Note this is a market conditions adjustment, driven by whether the market changed rather than by the calendar — a five-month-old sale in a flat market may need none.

CBS: Comparable Better, Subtract.

Problem 4

Three comparables produce adjusted values of $844,000 (net adjustment $1,000), $874,000 (net $28,000), and $861,000 (net $51,000 gross, and the most recent sale). A licensee averages them to $859,667 and presents it as the value. He also cites three active listings at $1.3 million that have sat 94, 112, and 130 days.

Correct both.

Solution. Averaging treats the $51,000-adjustment comparable as exactly as reliable as the $1,000 one — it is not, and the average silently discards that information.

Reconciliation weights by reliability: adjustment size, gross as well as net since a small net can conceal a large gross; then recency; then similarity in what drives this market.

A defensible conclusion: a supported range of roughly $845,000 to $870,000, most probable near $850,000 to $855,000, weighted toward the least-adjusted and most similar indicator. A range, a point, and a stated reason — not a single number presented as fact.

The active listings prove asking prices, not value. A seller can ask anything. What these three additionally prove is that the market has declined to pay $1.3 million three separate times, over three months each. Citing them as support inverts what they show.

Problem 5

Compute the cost approach: 2,000 sq ft home, effective age 12, total economic life 60, land worth $340,000 as if vacant, replacement cost new $260 per sq ft, $18,000 of functional obsolescence for a single bathroom, and $25,000 of external obsolescence from a new arterial behind the lot. The owner asks whether he can cure the arterial problem by landscaping.

Answer both.

Solution. Cost new: 2,000 × $260 = $520,000. Physical deterioration (age-life): 12 ÷ 60 = 20% × $520,000 = $104,000. Total accrued depreciation: 104,000 + 18,000 + 25,000 = $147,000. Indicated value: $340,000 + $520,000 − $147,000 = $713,000.

Note that land is not depreciated — depreciation applies to improvements only. And the age-life computation uses effective age, not actual age: a well-maintained updated 30-year-old house may have an effective age of 12, while a neglected 12-year-old house may have an effective age of 25.

On the arterial: no. External obsolescence arises outside the property, which is exactly why it is generally incurable — the owner cannot change it. Landscaping may soften the experience and will not remove the arterial. The single bathroom, by contrast, is functional obsolescence arising from the improvement itself and may be curable if the cost to cure is justified by the value it adds.

Problem 6

A 12-unit building rents at $2,100 per unit monthly. Vacancy and collection loss 5%. Operating expenses $118,000 including reserves. The market cap rate is 5.75%. The owner's accountant produced a "net income" of $141,000 by deducting debt service of $96,000 and adding back $23,000 of depreciation from the tax return.

Compute the value correctly and explain the accountant's error.

Solution. PGI = 12 × 2,100 × 12 = $302,400. Less vacancy 5% = 15,120. EGI = $287,280. Less operating expenses 118,000. NOI = $169,280. V = 169,280 ÷ 0.0575 = $2,944,000 (rounded).

The accountant mixed two different measures. Debt service is not an operating expense, and depreciation is not an operating expense. NOI measures the property's performance, independent of how any particular owner financed it or what their tax position is — two buyers with different loans buy the same NOI.

Reserves for replacement, by contrast, belong in. Omitting them overstates NOI and therefore overstates value.

Using his $141,000 figure at the same rate would indicate $2,452,174 — nearly half a million dollars off, from a definitional error rather than an arithmetic one.

Problem 7

The same $169,280 NOI is capitalized by three parties at 5.0%, 5.75%, and 7.0%. Separately, a licensee values a fourplex by applying a GRM of 140 derived from a nearby sale, without examining either property's expenses.

Show the value spread and state what the multiplier ignores.

Solution. At 5.0%: 169,280 ÷ 0.05 = $3,385,600. At 5.75%: $2,944,000. At 7.0%: 169,280 ÷ 0.07 = $2,418,286.

Nearly a million dollars of spread on identical income. Rate and value move in opposite directions, and that sensitivity is why the rate's derivation matters more than the arithmetic — and why a licensee must never select a cap rate to reach a number. A higher rate signals higher perceived risk or lower expected growth; a lower rate the opposite.

The GRM ignores expenses entirely, because it uses gross income. Two properties with identical gross rents and very different expense structures — one with all utilities paid by the owner, one without — produce identical multiplier indications and materially different values.

It is a screening tool, not a valuation.

Problem 8

A listing agent's analysis supports $845,000 to $870,000. The seller lists at $1,020,000. Two weeks later the agent revises her written analysis upward to match the list price, tells a buyer's agent the price "reflects our brokerage's opinion of value," and emails the lender's appraiser three sales with a note that "the deal dies without another $25,000."

Assess each act.

Solution. Listing at the seller's number was correct. Price is the client's decision; the analysis and advice are the licensee's obligation. She discharged both.

The revision is the failure, and it is worse than having no analysis. An analysis that follows the price rather than informing it documents nothing and looks, later, like an opinion manufactured to fit. What the file needed instead: the comparables with sources as of the date, the adjustment grid with direction and basis, the reconciliation and its weighting, comparables rejected and why, the market conditions evidence, the range with a most-probable point, the date, the written advice, and the seller's decision.

Representing the list price as the brokerage's opinion of value adopts the seller's reasoning as the brokerage's — the one thing the file must not do.

The email mixes the permitted with the prohibited. Sending relevant sales through the proper channel is information and is fine. "The deal dies without another $25,000" is a target, and a value that moves after that conversation is worthless for the purpose the appraisal exists to serve — including when the request serves her own client.

Chapter summary

Value is an opinion; price and cost are facts, and market value's conditions — typical motivation, informed parties, reasonable exposure, cash-equivalent payment — each exclude something.

DUST names the elements, and demand without purchasing power is not demand.

Substitution is the engine of all three approaches, and contribution is not cost.

Highest and best use is tested in order — legally permissible, physically possible, financially feasible, maximally productive — and "legally permissible" means zoning and private restrictions. Where land as if vacant exceeds improved value, the improvement subtracts.

Adjust the comparable, never the subject. Comparable better, subtract.

Reconcile, do not average. Weight by adjustment size — gross as well as net — recency, and similarity, and state a range with a most-probable point and a reason.

Closed sales prove price; pending sales show direction; active listings never establish value.

The cost approach is land plus cost new less accrued depreciation, with land never depreciated and effective age driving the age-life computation. Physical, functional, and external — and external is generally incurable because its cause is off the property.

NOI excludes debt service and depreciation and includes reserves. Two buyers with different loans buy the same NOI.

IRV in all three directions, with rate and value moving in opposite directions — which is why the rate's derivation matters more than the arithmetic.

Multipliers ignore expenses and are a screening tool.

A CMA is not an appraisal and may never be called one. The difference is not the data but the preparer, standards, independence, and purpose.

Supply an appraiser information, never a target — including at your own client's request. A low appraisal is a financing event, and nothing about the resolution is automatic.

Defensibility is reconstructability, and an analysis quietly revised to match a list price is worse than no analysis at all.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. State the highest and best use analysis and where it stops, build the adjustment grid with the direction and basis of each adjustment and reconcile to a range with a most-probable point and stated weighting, perform the cost approach identifying each type of depreciation and whether it is curable, build the income statement and solve for value, rate, or income as the facts require, identify which approach you would weight and why, state what you would decline to characterize and why, and list what the file must retain for the opinion to be reconstructable as of its date. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 13

Taxation, investment, and real estate mathematics

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Explain California property taxation, the assessment mechanism, and how assessments change on transfer and improvement.
  • Identify the transfer and income tax concepts a licensee must recognize and route, without advising on them.
  • Compute cash flow, cash-on-cash return, and the measures an investor uses to compare properties.
  • Explain leverage in both directions and identify the risks an investor carries.
  • Perform the commission, proration, area, and percentage calculations a licensee needs daily.
  • Compare investments and document the analysis so the basis of a recommendation is reconstructable.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: property taxation and tax orientation55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: cash flow, return, and leverage50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: transaction mathematics and investment comparison45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Property taxation and assessments

1.1 The California framework

California property taxation operates on an assessed value system with a constitutional limit on the general tax rate and on annual increases in assessed value.

The framework's operative features:

  • A base year value is established, generally on a change in ownership or on new construction.
  • Annual increases in assessed value are limited by an inflation factor, subject to the constitutional cap.
  • The general tax rate is capped, with additional voter-approved indebtedness levied on top of it.
  • Reassessment to current market value occurs on a change in ownership or on new construction, rather than continuously.

1.2 The consequence that matters most in practice

Two identical houses side by side can carry very different property tax bills, because their base year values were set in different years.

This is not an anomaly; it is how the system works. And it produces the single most common tax misstatement a licensee makes:

A buyer's property tax will not be the seller's property tax. It will be based on a new base year value established on the change in ownership.

A licensee who tells a buyer "the taxes are about $4,200 a year" — quoting the seller's bill on a property held since 1998 — has given a number the buyer will budget against and that will be wrong by a multiple. The correct statement quotes the seller's current bill as the seller's, explains that the property will be reassessed, and directs the buyer to the county assessor and to a tax professional for an estimate.

1.3 New construction and improvements

New construction generally triggers reassessment of the new construction, added to the existing base year value rather than replacing it. Ordinary maintenance and repair generally do not.

The line between an improvement that triggers reassessment and maintenance that does not is an assessor question, and a licensee advising a client that a renovation "won't affect your taxes" has answered it without authority.

1.4 Exemptions and exclusions

California provides various exemptions and exclusions, including a homeowners' exemption for an owner-occupied residence, exemptions for certain veterans, and exclusions from reassessment for certain transfers.

The rules governing transfer exclusions have changed, and their application depends on the relationship, the property's use, and value thresholds.

This is a referral, not a summary. A licensee who tells a client that a transfer "won't trigger reassessment" has given tax advice on a question whose rules have moved and whose application is fact-specific. The correct response names the issue and routes it to the assessor and to a tax professional before the transfer, because the structure of a transaction can determine the outcome.

1.5 Supplemental assessments

When a property is reassessed on transfer or new construction, a supplemental assessment captures the difference between the old and new assessed value for the remainder of the tax year.

Buyers regularly do not know this exists. A supplemental tax bill arriving months after closing, for an amount nobody mentioned, is a preventable client surprise — and telling the buyer to expect one is a small thing that separates a prepared client from an angry one.

1.6 Special assessments and Mello-Roos

Special assessments fund improvements benefiting particular property, and they are levied in addition to the general tax.

Community facilities district (Mello-Roos) special taxes fund infrastructure and services in defined districts and can add substantially to the annual obligation. They have their own disclosure requirements in a residential transaction, and they can continue for many years.

A buyer inherits the remaining obligation, which is why the total annual carrying cost — not the general tax rate alone — is what a buyer should be looking at.

1.7 Payment, delinquency, and the tax lien

Property taxes are payable on a schedule of installments with defined delinquency dates, and unpaid taxes become a lien on the property.

Two features from Lesson 05, restated: the tax lien is involuntary and specific, and it generally takes priority over other liens regardless of when they attached.

Continued nonpayment leads ultimately to sale of the property under the applicable process.

1.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain why two neighbors pay different taxes, state what a licensee may say about a buyer's future tax bill, and describe what a supplemental assessment is.

2. Transfer and income tax orientation

2.1 The scope of this section

Every topic below is a referral. The purpose is to let a licensee recognize an issue, use the vocabulary accurately, and route it to a tax professional — not to advise.

A licensee giving tax advice is doing two things wrong at once: exceeding the license, and answering a question whose rules change and whose application depends on facts the licensee does not hold.

2.2 Documentary transfer tax

California counties and some cities impose a documentary transfer tax on conveyances, computed on the consideration or value transferred, with exemptions for certain transfers.

Practical points: the tax is collected at recording; the allocation between buyer and seller is a matter of the contract and local custom; and some cities impose additional transfer taxes that can be substantial. A licensee working across city lines who assumes uniformity will misstate closing costs.

2.3 Income tax concepts a licensee should recognize

ConceptIn outline
BasisGenerally what was paid, adjusted over time
Adjusted basisBasis increased by capital improvements and reduced by depreciation taken
Capital gainGenerally the amount realized less adjusted basis
Depreciation (tax)A deduction available for income-producing property, taken over a recovery period. Land is not depreciable
Depreciation recaptureDepreciation previously taken may be treated differently on sale
Principal residence exclusionAn exclusion from gain on the sale of a principal residence, subject to ownership, use, and amount conditions
Like-kind exchangeA deferral mechanism for qualifying investment or business property, with strict identification and completion timing
Installment saleRecognition of gain over time where payments are received over time
Passive activity rulesLimits on the use of losses from rental activity
Interest and tax deductionsAvailable on a personal residence subject to limits and conditions

2.4 The three that generate the most improper advice

The principal residence exclusion. Clients ask whether they will "pay tax on the profit," and the answer depends on ownership and use periods, prior use of the exclusion, the amount of gain, and filing status. A licensee who says "you're fine, it's under the limit" has computed a client's tax.

The like-kind exchange. The identification and completion deadlines are strict, the structure requires a qualified intermediary, and a mistake is generally not fixable after the fact. A licensee's role is to identify early that a client may want an exchange and get a tax professional and intermediary involved before the sale closes — because after closing, the opportunity is usually gone.

Depreciation and recapture. Investors frequently do not distinguish tax depreciation from the appraisal depreciation in Lesson 12, and they are entirely different concepts sharing a word.

2.5 The two depreciations

Worth stating explicitly because the collision is guaranteed:

Appraisal depreciationTax depreciation
What it isLoss in value from any causeA deduction taken over a recovery period
Depends onActual condition, function, and externalitiesA schedule
Applies toImprovementsImprovements on income-producing property
LandNot depreciatedNot depreciable
PurposeEstimating valueComputing taxable income

A property can be appreciating in value while being depreciated for tax purposes. Both statements are true simultaneously.

2.6 What a licensee says

"That's a tax question and I'm not able to advise on it — the rules depend on facts I don't have and they change. What I can do is flag it now, while there's still time to structure things, and get you to a tax professional before we go further."

Two features of that answer matter: it names the limit honestly rather than deflecting, and it connects the referral to timing. Most tax opportunities in a real estate transaction close when the transaction does.

2.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to use the vocabulary accurately, distinguish the two depreciations, and explain why the exchange referral must happen before closing.

3. Cash flow and return

3.1 Building from NOI

Lesson 12 built net operating income. Investment analysis continues from there.

LineComputation
Potential gross incomeAll income at full occupancy
− Vacancy and collection loss
= Effective gross income
− Operating expenses (including reserves)
= Net operating incomeThe property's performance
− Annual debt service
= Before-tax cash flowThe investor's performance

NOI is the property; cash flow is the investor. NOI excludes debt service precisely so two buyers with different loans can compare the same property. Cash flow includes it because that is what the investor actually receives.

3.2 A worked cash flow

12 units at $2,100 monthly. Vacancy and collection 5%. Operating expenses $118,000 including reserves. Annual debt service $126,000.

PGI = 12 × 2,100 × 12 = $302,400 Less vacancy: −15,120 → EGI = $287,280 Less operating expenses: −118,000 → NOI = $169,280 Less debt service: −126,000 → Before-tax cash flow = $43,280

3.3 The return measures

MeasureFormulaWhat it measures
Capitalization rateNOI ÷ valueThe property's unleveraged return
Cash-on-cash returnBefore-tax cash flow ÷ cash investedThe return on the money actually put in
Gross rent multiplierPrice ÷ gross monthly rentA screening ratio ignoring expenses
Operating expense ratioOperating expenses ÷ effective gross incomeExpense efficiency
Debt coverage ratioNOI ÷ annual debt serviceThe cushion between income and the loan payment
Break-even occupancy(Operating expenses + debt service) ÷ PGIThe occupancy needed to cover everything

3.4 Worked returns

Using the property above, purchased for $2,944,000 with $736,000 down and $60,000 of closing and acquisition costs — $796,000 of cash invested.

Cap rate: 169,280 ÷ 2,944,000 = 5.75%

Cash-on-cash: 43,280 ÷ 796,000 = 5.44%

Debt coverage ratio: 169,280 ÷ 126,000 = 1.34

Operating expense ratio: 118,000 ÷ 287,280 = 41.1%

Break-even occupancy: (118,000 + 126,000) ÷ 302,400 = 244,000 ÷ 302,400 = 80.7%

3.5 Reading the numbers

The debt coverage ratio of 1.34 means NOI covers debt service 1.34 times. Lenders require a cushion above 1.00, and the required level varies by lender and property type — which is a lender question a licensee does not answer with a threshold.

The break-even occupancy of 80.7% is the operating leverage in one number: below about 81% occupancy, the property does not cover its expenses and its loan. An investor looking at a submarket where occupancy has run at 88% has a 7-point cushion; one where it has run at 84% has three.

Cash-on-cash below the cap rate — 5.44% against 5.75% — tells the investor the financing is costing more than the property earns on the borrowed portion. That is negative leverage, and Section 4 develops it.

3.6 The measures that mislead

Cap rate ignores financing, which is a feature when comparing properties and a limitation when evaluating an investor's actual return.

Cash-on-cash ignores appreciation, principal reduction, and tax effects — three of the four sources of return on real estate. An investor comparing a real estate cash-on-cash return to a bond yield is comparing incomparable things.

GRM ignores expenses entirely. Two properties with identical gross rents and very different expense structures produce identical multipliers and materially different values.

None of these is a valuation. They are comparison tools with defined limits, and a licensee should be able to state each limit when quoting the number.

3.7 The four sources of return

Real estate return comes from four places, and an analysis quoting only one is incomplete:

  1. Cash flow — what the property pays out.
  2. Principal reduction — the loan balance falling with each amortized payment.
  3. Appreciation — value change, which may be negative.
  4. Tax effects — which are a tax professional's territory.

3.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to build a cash flow from PGI, compute each return measure, and state each measure's limitation.

4. Leverage and risk

4.1 What leverage is

Leverage is the use of borrowed funds to increase the return on invested capital. It works in both directions, and the second direction is the one investors underweight.

4.2 Positive and negative leverage

Positive leverage: the property's return exceeds the cost of the borrowed money, so borrowing raises the return on equity.

Negative leverage: the cost of borrowing exceeds the property's return, so borrowing lowers the return on equity.

The comparison is between the property's unleveraged return — roughly the cap rate — and the cost of debt.

4.3 Worked both ways

A $1,000,000 property producing $60,000 NOI — a 6% cap rate.

All cash: return = 60,000 ÷ 1,000,000 = 6.0%.

75% financing at 5%: loan $750,000, interest-only for simplicity = $37,500. Cash flow = 60,000 − 37,500 = $22,500 on $250,000 invested = 9.0%. Positive leverage — borrowing at 5% to earn 6% raised the equity return by three points.

75% financing at 7.5%: interest = $56,250. Cash flow = 60,000 − 56,250 = $3,750 on $250,000 = 1.5%. Negative leverage — the same property, the same NOI, and a return well below the all-cash 6%.

The property did not change. The financing did, and it moved the equity return from 9.0% to 1.5%.

4.4 Leverage amplifies losses too

The same mechanism magnifies downside. A 10% decline in the value of an all-cash $1,000,000 property costs the investor 10% of his capital. The same decline on $250,000 of equity costs 40% of it.

That is the whole risk of leverage in one sentence, and it is why a licensee should never present leverage as free return.

4.5 The risks an investor carries

RiskWhat it is
Market riskValue and rent movement
Vacancy and credit riskUnits empty or tenants not paying
Operating riskExpenses rising faster than income
Interest rate riskRate movement on adjustable debt, and at refinance
Liquidity riskReal estate cannot be sold quickly at a known price
Legislative and regulatory riskRent regulation, tax changes, land use changes
Management riskPerformance depends on operations
Physical riskCasualty, deferred maintenance, hazard exposure
Refinance riskA balloon or maturity arriving in an unfavorable market

Liquidity risk traces straight back to Lesson 01. Immobility and uniqueness are why real estate cannot be sold quickly at a known price, which is why it is not comparable to a listed security however attractive the return looks.

4.6 Diversification and concentration

An investor holding one property in one submarket carries concentration risk: the specific building, the specific tenant mix, and the specific local economy.

A licensee should be able to name that as a consideration. A licensee should not tell a client how much of their net worth belongs in real estate, which is investment advice requiring information and qualification the licensee does not have.

4.7 What a licensee may and may not do with investment analysis

May: compute and explain the measures; explain what each includes and excludes; explain how leverage works in both directions; gather and present the operating data and its sources; and identify the questions the client should take to their advisors.

May not: project future appreciation or rent growth as fact; state a required return or a suitable allocation; advise on the tax consequences; predict interest rates or market direction; or present a pro forma as anything other than a set of assumptions.

4.8 Pro formas and the assumption problem

A pro forma is a projected operating statement. It is built from assumptions — rent growth, vacancy, expense inflation, and an exit value — and the assumptions carry the answer.

Three disciplines:

  1. Label it as projected, never as actual.
  2. State each assumption and its source, so a reader can substitute their own.
  3. Distinguish actual historical operating figures from projections on the same page, because a pro forma presented as an operating statement is a misrepresentation.

A seller's pro forma showing full occupancy at above-market rents is not evidence of income. It is a set of assumptions, and it should be presented as one.

4.9 Checkpoint

You should be able to demonstrate positive and negative leverage numerically, explain how leverage amplifies loss, and state what a pro forma must be labeled.

5. Commissions, prorations, and area math

5.1 The percentage relationships

Nearly every transaction calculation is one relationship in three forms:

Part = Whole × Rate Whole = Part ÷ Rate Rate = Part ÷ Whole

Identifying which quantity is missing is most of the work.

5.2 Commission calculations

A property sells for $840,000 at a 5% total compensation. What is the total? 840,000 × 0.05 = $42,000.

The listing and selling sides split it evenly, and the selling salesperson's brokerage splits 60/40 with her. What does she receive? Selling side: 42,000 × 0.50 = $21,000. Her share: 21,000 × 0.60 = $12,600.

A salesperson received $9,450, being 60% of her brokerage's share, which was half the total at 4.5%. What was the sale price? Brokerage share: 9,450 ÷ 0.60 = $15,750. Total compensation: 15,750 ÷ 0.50 = $31,500. Price: 31,500 ÷ 0.045 = $700,000.

Work backward one step at a time, and identify at each step which quantity you have and which you need.

5.3 Net to seller

A seller wants to net $600,000 after 5% compensation and $9,000 of closing costs. What must the price be?

The error is multiplying $600,000 by 1.05. The compensation is a percentage of the price, not of the net.

Price − 0.05(Price) − 9,000 = 600,000 0.95(Price) = 609,000 Price = 609,000 ÷ 0.95 = $641,052.63

Check: 641,052.63 × 0.05 = 32,052.63. 641,052.63 − 32,052.63 − 9,000 = $600,000. ✓

The net-to-seller problem is the most reliably missed calculation in this material, and it is missed the same way every time: applying the rate to the wrong base.

5.4 Prorations

From Lesson 10, with the direction rule restated because it is what students reverse:

Seller prepaid beyond the boundary → seller credited, buyer debited. Charge accrued or money held for the buyer's period → seller debited, buyer credited.

Annual taxes $7,300 paid through June 30; closing May 31; 365-day basis; day of closing to the buyer. Buyer's period: May 31 – June 30 = 31 days. 7,300 ÷ 365 = $20.00/day × 31 = $620.00 credited to the seller.

A tenant paid $2,400 rent on the 1st of a 30-day month; closing the 10th. Buyer's days: the 10th – 30th = 21. 2,400 ÷ 30 = $80/day × 21 = $1,680.00 credited to the buyer.

Read the convention rather than assuming it: the day-count basis, who gets the day of closing, and the fact that California's property tax year does not align with the calendar year.

5.5 Area and volume

ConstantValue
Acre43,560 sq ft
Mile5,280 ft
Section1 mile square = 640 acres
Square yard9 sq ft
Cubic yard27 cu ft

A 130 × 250 ft lot at $12,000 per acre. 130 × 250 = 32,500 sq ft ÷ 43,560 = 0.746 acres × 12,000 = $8,957.

A 5-acre rectangular parcel with 400 ft of frontage — how deep? 5 × 43,560 = 217,800 ÷ 400 = 544.5 ft.

Concrete for a drive 60 ft × 12 ft × 4 in. 4 in = 0.333 ft. 60 × 12 × 0.333 = 240 cu ft ÷ 27 = 8.9 cubic yards.

5.6 Interest and loan calculations

One month's interest on a $400,000 balance at 6%: 400,000 × 0.06 ÷ 12 = $2,000.

A payment of $2,398.20 on that balance: interest $2,000.00, principal $398.20, new balance $399,601.80.

Two points on a $400,000 loan: 400,000 × 0.02 = $8,000. One point is one percent of the loan, not the price.

A $920,000 purchase, 20% down, appraisal $900,000: the lender lends against the lower of value or price. 900,000 × 0.80 = $720,000, a $16,000 shortfall against the $736,000 planned.

5.7 The three errors that produce most wrong answers

  1. Applying a rate to the wrong base — the net-to-seller error, and the same error whenever a percentage is taken of the result rather than the source.
  2. Unit errors — mixing feet and inches, confusing linear with square measure, dividing when multiplying by 43,560 was required.
  3. Skipping the reasonableness check.

The reasonableness check catches nearly all of them. A residential lot of 0.55 acres is plausible; 550 acres is not. A commission of $42,000 on an $840,000 sale is plausible; $420,000 is not. Before reporting a number, ask whether it is the right order of magnitude.

5.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to work a commission problem backward, compute net to seller correctly, prorate in both directions, and apply a reasonableness check.

6. Investment comparison and documentation

6.1 Comparing on a common basis

Two properties cannot be compared until the numbers describe the same thing.

The recurring failures:

  • One NOI includes reserves and the other does not.
  • One set of expenses is actual and the other is projected.
  • One property's income is at market rent and the other's is at in-place rent.
  • One analysis deducts debt service and the other does not.
  • Acquisition costs are counted as cash invested in one and omitted in the other.

A licensee's contribution is to normalize the presentation and state what was normalized — not to declare a winner.

6.2 A worked comparison

Property A: price $2,944,000; NOI $169,280; debt service $126,000; cash invested $796,000. Property B: price $2,400,000; NOI $132,000; debt service $102,000; cash invested $660,000.

MeasureAB
Cap rate169,280 ÷ 2,944,000 = 5.75%132,000 ÷ 2,400,000 = 5.50%
Cash flow169,280 − 126,000 = $43,280132,000 − 102,000 = $30,000
Cash-on-cash43,280 ÷ 796,000 = 5.44%30,000 ÷ 660,000 = 4.55%
Debt coverage169,280 ÷ 126,000 = 1.34132,000 ÷ 102,000 = 1.29

A leads on every measure computed — and that is not the answer. The comparison is incomplete without: whether both NOIs include reserves on the same basis; whether both sets of income are actual or projected; the condition and deferred maintenance of each; the lease structure and tenant quality; the submarket's occupancy history against each property's break-even; the loan terms, including whether either has a balloon; and the exit assumptions.

A licensee who presents the table alone has presented four numbers as a decision.

6.3 What the client decides and what the licensee supplies

The division is the same one that governs listing price and offer terms:

The investment decision is the client's. The analysis, the data, its sources, and the risks are the licensee's obligation.

The licensee supplies: the operating data with its sources, the computed measures with their limitations stated, the risks identified, the questions to take to a tax professional and to counsel, and the written record of what was recommended.

The licensee does not supply: a projection presented as fact, a required return, an allocation recommendation, a tax conclusion, or a prediction about rates or market direction.

6.4 Verifying the data

Investment analysis rests on operating figures, and the figures come from a seller with an interest in them.

What to verify and against what:

ItemSource
RentsLeases and a rent roll, not a summary
OccupancyRent roll and payment history
Operating expensesActual statements and bills, not a percentage estimate
Property taxesThe assessor — and reassessed on transfer, not the seller's current bill
InsuranceA quote for this buyer, especially in mapped hazard areas
Deferred maintenanceInspection, not the seller's characterization
Utility responsibilityThe leases

The property tax line is the one most often carried forward wrongly. A seller's pro forma using the seller's tax bill understates the buyer's expense, sometimes by a large multiple — and it flows straight through NOI into value.

6.5 Documenting the analysis

The test is the same one Lesson 12 applied to a valuation: reconstructability. Could a reviewer see what data was used, from what source, what assumptions were applied, and when?

The file should contain:

  1. The operating data and its source for each line.
  2. What was actual and what was projected, distinguished.
  3. Every assumption in any projection, with its basis.
  4. The computed measures and their date.
  5. The risks identified and communicated.
  6. Referrals made to a tax professional, to counsel, and to specialists — with the client's response.
  7. The client's decision, and any instruction that departed from the advice.

6.6 The recurring failure

Not that a licensee computed something wrong. That a licensee presented a seller's pro forma as though it were operating data, and the client bought the assumptions.

The discipline that prevents it is small and consistent: label projections, source every figure, verify what can be verified, and state what could not be.

6.7 Why the comparison is the deliverable

Sections 6.1 through 6.6 describe how to compare investments and what the file must hold. The point worth ending on is why the comparison — rather than the recommendation — is what a licensee actually delivers.

Three reasons.

The decision is the client's, and it depends on facts the licensee does not have. Tax position, other holdings, risk tolerance, liquidity needs, and time horizon are the inputs that decide between two properties with similar returns. A licensee who recommends has substituted assumptions about those inputs for the client's actual answers.

A recommendation cannot be checked; a comparison can. Section 6.5's documentation requirement exists so that a client — or anyone later — can see the inputs, the arithmetic, and the assumptions. A client who disagrees with an assumption can say so and the analysis is rebuilt. A client who disagrees with a recommendation has only the licensee's judgment to argue with.

The comparison survives being wrong. Projections are wrong routinely — vacancy, expenses, and appreciation all move. An analysis whose assumptions are on the page is defensible when the outcome disappoints, because the reader can see what was assumed and that the assumption was reasonable when made. A number with no assumptions attached is indefensible the moment it is off.

The one thing that must never appear in an investment analysis: a projected appreciation rate presented as an expectation rather than as an assumption being tested. Section 6.4's verification requirement covers the inputs that are knowable. Future value is not one of them, and the sentence "these have been going up about six percent a year" is a prediction stated as a plan.

Section 6.6's recurring failure, restated: the analysis that was performed carefully, delivered verbally, and never written down. The client made a good decision on good information, and the file cannot say so — which is the same finding this course reaches in agency, in disclosure, in valuation, and in escrow, and it is the finding that is entirely within the licensee's control.

6.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to normalize two properties for comparison, state what the table does not capture, and list what the file must show.

Key terms

TermDefinition
Base year valueThe assessed value established generally on a change in ownership or new construction, with annual increases limited thereafter.
Reassessment on transferWhy a buyer's property tax will not be the seller's, and why two identical neighboring houses carry different bills.
Supplemental assessmentThe bill capturing the difference between old and new assessed value for the remainder of the tax year after a transfer or new construction.
Special assessment / Mello-RoosLevies beyond the general tax, funding improvements or district infrastructure and services. Inherited by the buyer.
Property tax lienInvoluntary and specific, and generally takes priority regardless of when it attached.
Documentary transfer taxA county and sometimes city tax on conveyances, with some cities imposing substantial additional taxes.
Basis / adjusted basisGenerally what was paid; adjusted upward by capital improvements and downward by depreciation taken.
Tax depreciationA deduction for income-producing property over a recovery period. Land is not depreciable, and this is a different concept from appraisal depreciation.
Depreciation recaptureDepreciation previously taken may be treated differently on sale.
Principal residence exclusionAn exclusion from gain subject to ownership, use, amount, and filing conditions. A referral, never a computation.
Like-kind exchangeA deferral mechanism with strict identification and completion timing and a required intermediary. The referral must happen before closing.
Net operating incomeThe property's performance — excluding debt service and depreciation, including reserves.
Before-tax cash flowNOI less annual debt service. The investor's performance.
Cash-on-cash returnBefore-tax cash flow ÷ cash invested. Ignores appreciation, principal reduction, and tax effects.
Debt coverage ratioNOI ÷ annual debt service. The cushion between income and the loan payment.
Operating expense ratioOperating expenses ÷ effective gross income.
Break-even occupancy(Operating expenses + debt service) ÷ potential gross income. Operating leverage in one number.
The four sources of returnCash flow, principal reduction, appreciation, and tax effects. An analysis quoting one is incomplete.
LeverageBorrowed funds used to increase return on invested capital. Positive where the property's return exceeds the cost of debt; negative where it does not.
Amplification of lossThe other half of leverage: a 10% value decline costs 10% of an all-cash investor's capital and 40% of a 25%-equity investor's.
Liquidity riskReal estate cannot be sold quickly at a known price — traceable to immobility and uniqueness.
Concentration riskOne property, one submarket, one tenant mix.
Pro formaA projected operating statement. Labeled as projected, with every assumption and its source stated, and never presented as operating data.
Part, whole, ratePart = Whole × Rate; Whole = Part ÷ Rate; Rate = Part ÷ Whole.
Net to sellerPrice − (rate × price) − costs = net. The rate applies to the price, not to the net — the most reliably missed calculation in the material.
The reasonableness checkAsking whether an answer is the right order of magnitude before reporting it.
NormalizationRestating two properties' figures on a common basis before comparing, and stating what was normalized.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A buyer asks what the property taxes will be. The seller has owned the house since 1998 and pays $4,200 a year. The listing agent tells the buyer "about $4,200," and adds that the planned kitchen remodel "won't affect your taxes." The property is in a Mello-Roos district.

Identify every error.

Solution. Three errors, and the first will be wrong by a multiple.

A buyer's property tax will not be the seller's. California establishes a base year value generally on a change in ownership, with annual increases limited thereafter — which is exactly why two identical neighboring houses carry very different bills. A property held since 1998 carries a base year value from 1998, and this buyer's will be set on transfer.

The correct statement quotes the seller's current bill as the seller's, explains that the property will be reassessed on the change in ownership, and directs the buyer to the county assessor and to a tax professional for an estimate.

The remodel answer is an assessor question he answered without authority. New construction generally triggers reassessment of the new construction, added to the existing base year value; ordinary maintenance and repair generally do not — and the line between them is not the licensee's to draw.

The Mello-Roos was never mentioned. A community facilities district special tax can add substantially to the annual obligation, continues for many years, carries its own disclosure requirements, and is inherited by the buyer. The number a buyer should be looking at is the total annual carrying cost, not the general rate.

And nobody told him about the supplemental assessment — the bill capturing the difference between old and new assessed value for the remainder of the tax year, which will arrive months after closing for an amount nobody mentioned.

Problem 2

A seller asks whether she will owe tax on her gain, whether she can "do a 1031" on the rental she is also selling, and whether the depreciation she has taken on the rental means it has lost value. Her agent says the gain is under the limit so she is fine, that they can set up the exchange after closing, and that depreciation means the building is worth less.

Correct each.

Solution. All three are wrong, and the second is the one that cannot be undone.

The principal residence exclusion depends on ownership and use periods, prior use of the exclusion, the amount of gain, and filing status. "You're under the limit so you're fine" computes a client's tax on facts the licensee does not hold, under rules that change.

A like-kind exchange generally cannot be arranged after the sale closes. The identification and completion deadlines are strict, the structure requires a qualified intermediary, and a mistake is generally not fixable after the fact. The referral must happen before closing — which is why the licensee's real job here is to identify early that she may want an exchange and get a tax professional and intermediary involved now.

The two depreciations are entirely different concepts sharing a word. Tax depreciation is a deduction taken over a recovery period on income-producing property; appraisal depreciation is loss in value from any cause. A property can be appreciating in value while being depreciated for tax purposes — both true simultaneously. Her building has not lost value because she deducted depreciation, and recapture means that depreciation may be treated differently on sale, which is a further referral.

What the licensee says: "That's a tax question and I'm not able to advise on it — the rules depend on facts I don't have and they change. What I can do is flag it now, while there's still time to structure things, and get you to a tax professional before we go further."

Problem 3

Build the analysis: 12 units at $2,100 monthly, vacancy and collection 5%, operating expenses $118,000 including reserves, annual debt service $126,000, price $2,944,000, cash invested $796,000.

Compute PGI, EGI, NOI, cash flow, cap rate, cash-on-cash, debt coverage, and break-even occupancy — then say what the last two tell the investor.

Solution. PGI = 12 × 2,100 × 12 = $302,400. Less vacancy 5% (15,120) → EGI = $287,280. Less operating expenses → NOI = $169,280. Less debt service → Before-tax cash flow = $43,280.

Cap rate: 169,280 ÷ 2,944,000 = 5.75%. Cash-on-cash: 43,280 ÷ 796,000 = 5.44%. Debt coverage: 169,280 ÷ 126,000 = 1.34. Break-even occupancy: (118,000 + 126,000) ÷ 302,400 = 80.7%.

NOI is the property; cash flow is the investor. NOI excludes debt service precisely so two buyers with different loans can compare the same property.

The debt coverage of 1.34 means NOI covers the payment 1.34 times. Lenders require a cushion above 1.00 and the required level varies — a lender question a licensee does not answer with a threshold.

The break-even of 80.7% is the operating leverage in one number. Below about 81% occupancy the property covers neither its expenses nor its loan. An investor in a submarket running 88% has a seven-point cushion; one running 84% has three.

And cash-on-cash below the cap rate — 5.44% against 5.75% — signals negative leverage.

Problem 4

A $1,000,000 property produces $60,000 NOI. Compare: all cash; 75% financing at 5% interest-only; and 75% financing at 7.5% interest-only. Then state what a 10% value decline costs the investor in the all-cash and 75%-financed cases.

Show the arithmetic and name the principle.

Solution. All cash: 60,000 ÷ 1,000,000 = 6.0%.

75% at 5%: interest = 750,000 × 0.05 = $37,500. Cash flow = 60,000 − 37,500 = $22,500 on $250,000 = 9.0%. Positive leverage — borrowing at 5% to earn 6% raised the equity return by three points.

75% at 7.5%: interest = $56,250. Cash flow = 60,000 − 56,250 = $3,750 on $250,000 = 1.5%. Negative leverage — the same property, the same NOI, and a return well below the all-cash 6%.

The property did not change. The financing did, and it moved the equity return from 9.0% to 1.5%.

On the downside: a 10% decline costs the all-cash investor 10% of his capital ($100,000 of $1,000,000). It costs the leveraged investor 40% of his ($100,000 of $250,000).

That is the whole risk of leverage in one sentence, and it is why leverage is never presented as free return.

Problem 5

A seller wants to net $600,000 after 5% compensation and $9,000 of closing costs. A salesperson received $9,450, being 60% of her brokerage's share, which was half of a 4.5% total.

Compute the required price and the sale price, and name the error the first problem invites.

Solution. Net to seller. The error is multiplying $600,000 by 1.05 — the compensation is a percentage of the price, not of the net.

Price − 0.05(Price) − 9,000 = 600,000 0.95(Price) = 609,000 Price = 609,000 ÷ 0.95 = $641,052.63

Check: 641,052.63 × 0.05 = 32,052.63; 641,052.63 − 32,052.63 − 9,000 = $600,000. ✓

The commission problem, worked backward one step at a time. Brokerage share: 9,450 ÷ 0.60 = $15,750. Total compensation: 15,750 ÷ 0.50 = $31,500. Price: 31,500 ÷ 0.045 = $700,000.

The net-to-seller problem is the most reliably missed calculation in this material, and it is missed the same way every time: applying the rate to the wrong base.

Problem 6

Compute and state the direction: (a) annual taxes $7,300 paid through June 30, closing May 31, 365-day basis, day of closing to the buyer; (b) a tenant's $2,400 rent paid on the 1st of a 30-day month, closing the 10th. Then compute (c) a 130 × 250 ft lot at $12,000 per acre and (d) concrete for a 60 × 12 ft drive 4 inches thick.

Answer, and name the three errors that produce most wrong results.

Solution. (a) Buyer's period May 31 – June 30 = 31 days. 7,300 ÷ 365 = $20.00/day × 31 = $620.00 credited to the seller, debited to the buyer — he reimburses her for the period she paid and he owns.

(b) Buyer's days the 10th – 30th = 21. 2,400 ÷ 30 = $80/day × 21 = $1,680.00 credited to the buyer, debited to the seller — the opposite direction, because she holds money for his period.

(c) 130 × 250 = 32,500 sq ft ÷ 43,560 = 0.746 acres × $12,000 = $8,957.

(d) 4 in = 0.333 ft. 60 × 12 × 0.333 = 240 cu ft ÷ 27 = 8.9 cubic yards.

The three errors: applying a rate to the wrong base (the net-to-seller error); unit errors — mixing feet and inches, which in (d) would have produced 106 cubic yards, confusing linear with square measure, or dividing where multiplying by 43,560 was required; and skipping the reasonableness check.

Also read the convention rather than assuming it: the day-count basis, who gets the day of closing, and the fact that California's property tax year does not align with the calendar year.

Problem 7

An investor is comparing two buildings. Property A: price $2,944,000, NOI $169,280, debt service $126,000, cash invested $796,000. Property B: price $2,400,000, NOI $132,000, debt service $102,000, cash invested $660,000. A leads on every computed measure, and the licensee recommends A.

Compute the table and state why the recommendation is premature.

Solution. Cap rate: A 5.75%, B 5.50%. Cash flow: A $43,280, B $30,000. Cash-on-cash: A 5.44%, B 4.55%. Debt coverage: A 1.34, B 1.29.

A leads on all four — and that is not the answer.

The comparison is incomplete without: whether both NOIs include reserves on the same basis; whether both sets of income are actual or projected; whether both are at market rent or in-place rent; condition and deferred maintenance; lease structure and tenant quality; each submarket's occupancy history against each property's break-even; the loan terms including whether either carries a balloon; and the exit assumptions.

A licensee who presents the table alone has presented four numbers as a decision. The investment decision is the client's; the analysis, the data, its sources, and the risks are the licensee's obligation — along with normalizing the presentation and stating what was normalized.

Note also what the measures themselves omit. Cash-on-cash ignores appreciation, principal reduction, and tax effects — three of the four sources of return.

Problem 8

A seller's marketing package shows an operating statement with 100% occupancy, market rents 8% above the current rent roll, expenses stated as "35% of gross," and the seller's current property tax bill. The buyer's agent forwards it with the comment "the numbers work."

Identify every defect and state the discipline that prevents this.

Solution. This is a pro forma presented as an operating statement, which is a misrepresentation whoever authored it — and forwarding it with "the numbers work" adopts it.

100% occupancy is an assumption, not a figure. Actual occupancy comes from the rent roll and payment history.

Rents 8% above the rent roll are projected. Actual rents come from the leases, not a summary.

"35% of gross" is an estimate substituting for data. Operating expenses come from actual statements and bills.

The seller's property tax bill is the single most consequential error, because the property will be reassessed on transfer. Carrying the seller's bill forward understates the buyer's expense, sometimes by a large multiple — and it flows straight through NOI into value.

Two more to verify: insurance quoted for this buyer, especially in mapped hazard areas, and deferred maintenance from an inspection rather than the seller's characterization.

The discipline: label projections as projected, source every figure, verify what can be verified, and state what could not be. Distinguish actual from projected on the same page — and record the assumptions and their basis, the measures and their date, the risks communicated, the referrals made, and the client's decision.

The recurring failure is not a computation error. It is presenting a seller's pro forma as operating data, and the client buying the assumptions.

Chapter summary

California taxes on a base year value system, so a buyer's tax will not be the seller's, two identical neighbors pay differently, and a supplemental assessment follows the transfer. Quote the seller's bill as the seller's, and route the estimate to the assessor and a tax professional.

Special assessments and Mello-Roos are inherited, and the number that matters is total annual carrying cost.

Every income and transfer tax topic is a referral. Use the vocabulary accurately, recognize the issue, and connect the referral to timing — most tax opportunities close when the transaction does, and the like-kind exchange is the clearest case.

Tax depreciation and appraisal depreciation are different concepts sharing a word.

NOI is the property; cash flow is the investor. Cap rate, cash-on-cash, debt coverage, operating expense ratio, and break-even occupancy each measure something specific — and each has a limitation to state when quoting it.

Return comes from four places: cash flow, principal reduction, appreciation, and tax effects.

Leverage works both directions. Positive where the property's return exceeds the cost of debt, negative where it does not — and it amplifies losses in exactly the same proportion.

Name the risks, including liquidity risk, which traces to immobility and uniqueness — and never state a suitable allocation.

A pro forma is a set of assumptions. Label it, source every assumption, and never present it as operating data.

Part, whole, rate answers most transaction math. Work commissions backward one step at a time, and compute net to seller by applying the rate to the price rather than the net.

Prorations run in two directions, and the conventions change the answer.

Most wrong answers come from the wrong base, unit errors, and a skipped reasonableness check.

Normalize before comparing, state what was normalized, and verify the data against leases, statements, the assessor, and an inspection. The client decides; the licensee supplies the analysis, the sources, the risks, and the record.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. State what you would tell the client about property taxation and what you would route to the assessor and to a tax professional, identify every tax issue present and the deadline that makes each referral urgent, build the operating statement and compute each return measure with its limitation stated, demonstrate whether the financing produces positive or negative leverage, perform every transaction calculation the scenario requires showing your bases and units, identify each figure you would verify and against what source, state what you would decline to project or conclude, and list what the file must show for the analysis to be reconstructable. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items.
