An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 14

Fair housing, civil rights, and consumer protection

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Identify the federal protected classes and the statutes that establish them.
  • Identify California's additional protections and explain why the federal list alone is insufficient.
  • Recognize prohibited conduct in its obvious and subtle forms, and apply the advertising rules.
  • Distinguish a reasonable accommodation from a reasonable modification and apply each correctly.
  • Explain fair lending obligations and appraisal bias, and identify the licensee's role in each.
  • Respond correctly to a complaint, document the response, and build practices that constrain discretion.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: federal and California protections55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: prohibited conduct, advertising, and accommodations50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: fair lending, appraisal bias, and complaint response45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Federal protected classes

1.1 The framework

Federal fair housing law prohibits discrimination in the sale, rental, and financing of housing, and in the provision of related services, on the basis of specified protected characteristics.

The federal protected classes:

Class
Race
Color
Religion
Sex
National origin
Familial status
Disability (handicap)

The first four and national origin came earlier; familial status and disability were added later, which is why some older material lists five.

1.2 The statutes to know by name

The Civil Rights Act of 1866 prohibits racial discrimination in property transactions. Its significance is its reach: it is not subject to the exemptions that apply under later fair housing law, and it applies to all property.

The Fair Housing Act — Title VIII of the Civil Rights Act of 1968, amended in 1988 — is the principal federal housing discrimination statute, establishing the seven classes above and the enforcement framework.

The Americans with Disabilities Act addresses public accommodations and commercial facilities. It reaches a brokerage's own office as a place of public accommodation; it is not the statute governing accessibility in a private dwelling, which is the Fair Housing Act's domain.

The Equal Credit Opportunity Act governs discrimination in credit, addressed in Section 5.

1.3 What familial status protects

Familial status protects households with children under 18, persons who are pregnant, and persons in the process of obtaining custody of a child.

Consequences licensees regularly miss:

  • "Adults only" housing is generally prohibited, and the exemptions for housing for older persons are narrow and have specific requirements that must actually be satisfied.
  • Occupancy standards that are set restrictively enough to exclude families with children function as familial status discrimination however they are phrased.
  • Steering families toward particular buildings, floors, or areas is steering.

1.4 What disability protection covers

Disability protection reaches physical and mental impairments that substantially limit major life activities, along with a record of such an impairment and being regarded as having one.

Three obligations flow from it, developed in Section 4: reasonable accommodations, reasonable modifications, and accessible design and construction requirements for certain multifamily housing.

One clarification that prevents a common error: current illegal drug use is generally not protected, while a record of addiction may be.

1.5 Exemptions, and why they matter less than licensees think

Federal fair housing law contains limited exemptions — for certain owner-occupied dwellings of a small number of units, certain single-family sales by an owner without a broker, and certain religious organizations and private clubs.

Three reasons a licensee should not rely on them:

  1. The exemptions generally do not apply where a real estate licensee is involved. That is the point: they cover an owner acting alone, not a transaction with a broker in it.
  2. The 1866 Act's prohibition on racial discrimination is not subject to them.
  3. California's protections are broader, and a federal exemption does not create a state one.

A licensee who hears "this one is exempt" should treat that as a reason to escalate, not as a reason to proceed.

1.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to name the seven federal classes, name the four statutes, and explain why exemptions are rarely available in a transaction involving a licensee.

2. California fair housing protections

2.1 The principle to carry

California protects the federal classes and adds further characteristics. A licensee who knows only the federal list will engage in conduct that is lawful federally and unlawful in California.

That single sentence is the section's operative content, and it should shape every practice decision.

2.2 The California framework

Two bodies of California law do most of the work.

The Fair Employment and Housing Act is California's principal housing discrimination statute, covering the federal characteristics and adding others.

The Unruh Civil Rights Act prohibits discrimination by business establishments, and its reach extends beyond housing to business generally — which is why it can apply to conduct that would not fall under a housing-specific statute.

California's protections extend beyond the federal seven to characteristics including marital status, sexual orientation, gender identity and gender expression, ancestry, source of income, age, genetic information, citizenship, immigration status, primary language, military and veteran status, and others.

The list is not static. It has been added to repeatedly, which is the practical reason a licensee should not work from a memorized list at all.

2.3 Source of income

The addition licensees encounter most often in practice.

An instruction to refuse applicants using housing assistance is an unlawful instruction. The obligations from the agency lesson apply in full: refuse it, explain why, escalate to the broker, and address whether the engagement can continue.

Advertising and screening practices that exclude assistance recipients — "no vouchers," or a minimum income multiple applied to the full rent rather than the tenant's portion — fail for the same reason.

2.4 Why the practice does not depend on the list

Because the list grows, and because copy that describes people is a risk regardless of which characteristic it touches, the reliable practice is not memorization:

Describe the property. Do not describe the people who should live in it.

Property-focused copy never requires reference to any protected characteristic at all, which is why it is safe against a list that changes. And "it's not one of the federal seven" is not clearance — it is the reasoning that produces California violations.

2.5 Enforcement

Complaints may be pursued through the state civil rights agency, through federal agencies, and through the courts. Remedies can include damages, injunctive relief, civil penalties, and attorney fees.

A licensee's discipline exposure is separate and additional. Discriminatory conduct is grounds for action against a license independent of any civil outcome, which means "the case settled" does not close the matter.

2.6 The obligation runs to everyone

Fair housing obligations are not limited to the consumer in front of the licensee.

A licensee who accepts a seller's instruction to limit who sees a property has violated the obligation as to buyers he never met. A licensee whose marketing reaches a narrow audience by design has done the same.

The client relationship is one surface among several, and the harm to a person who never learned the property existed is exactly the harm the law addresses.

2.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to name California's principal fair housing statutes, explain why the federal list is insufficient, and state the practice that does not depend on the list.

3. Prohibited conduct and advertising

3.1 The prohibited practices

PracticeWhat it is
Refusal to sell or rentDeclining to deal on a protected basis
Different terms or conditionsDifferent price, deposit, terms, or services
SteeringDirecting a person toward or away from areas, buildings, or units
BlockbustingInducing sales or rentals by representations about the entry of a protected group into an area
RedliningDenying or varying the terms of financing or insurance by area
Discriminatory advertisingCopy or targeting indicating a preference, limitation, or discrimination
Refusal of accommodation or modificationDenying a required reasonable accommodation or modification
Falsely denying availabilityTelling a person a unit is unavailable when it is
Retaliation or interferenceAgainst a person exercising fair housing rights or assisting another
DiscouragementDeterring an inquiry or application rather than denying it

3.2 The forms that are committed with good intentions

The obvious practices are the ones licensees already know not to do. The subtle ones are where violations actually occur.

FormExample
Redirection"You'd be more comfortable over here"
Curated balanceSelecting a "representative mix" chosen by the licensee rather than by the buyer's criteria
CharacterizationDescribing residents rather than the property or the area's objective features
Differential enthusiasmShowing everything, but presenting some properties warmly and others flatly
Differential effortPrompt follow-up on some inquiries, slower on others
Assumed suitabilityDeciding a two-story home "won't work" for a buyer with a disability and not showing it
Soliciting preferencesAsking which protected characteristics matter so they can be "honored"
Discouragement"You probably wouldn't qualify" instead of taking the application

The last four are the ones committed with good intentions, and they are prohibited conduct all the same. Steering is defined by whose criteria drove the selection, not by the licensee's intent.

Discouragement deserves particular attention: it achieves a denial while producing no record of one, which is exactly why it is hard to detect and dangerous to permit.

3.3 The advertising rule

Advertising may not indicate a preference, limitation, or discrimination based on protected characteristics, and may not express an intention to make one.

The workable form:

Describe the property. Do not describe the people who should live in it.

Copy that sorts people, however warmly intended, signals who is and is not welcome. That is the harm, and intent is not a defense.

3.4 Rewriting copy

Problem copyWhy it failsProperty-focused rewrite
"Family-friendly neighborhood"Familial status"Cul-de-sac location; park two blocks away"
"Perfect for a young couple"Age and familial status"Two bedrooms, one bath, 940 square feet"
"Walking distance to St. Anne's parish"Religion"Walking distance to the town center"
"Not suitable for anyone with mobility issues"Disability"Second-floor unit; access by stairs, no elevator"
"Ideal for professionals, no students"Sorts occupants"Quiet building; on-site laundry; assigned parking"
"Mature adults only"Age and familial statusOmit; the older-persons exemptions are narrow and must actually apply
"English-speaking building"National origin, primary languageOmit entirely
"Great for empty nesters"Familial status and age, softened"Single-story, low-maintenance yard"
"No vouchers"Source of income — CaliforniaOmit; screen on uniform written criteria

Every rewrite is more informative than the original. "Second-floor unit; access by stairs, no elevator" tells a buyer with a mobility concern exactly what they need, without deciding for them whether the property will work.

3.5 Accessibility information belongs in the copy

A common overcorrection is to omit accessibility features entirely, on the theory that any mention of disability is prohibited. That withholds information consumers need.

**The rule is about who the property is for, not about what the property has.**

Do describe: single-level layout, step-free entry, wide doorways, roll-in shower, elevator, grab bars, accessible parking. Do not characterize: who the property is or is not suitable for.

3.6 Targeting is part of the advertisement

The most consequential digital point, and it is easy to miss because it does not appear in the copy.

Platforms permit advertisers to select who sees an advertisement — by age, by location, by inferred interests, by proxies for demographic characteristics.

Restricting who may see a housing advertisement can deny groups access to the housing opportunity, and that is the harm advertising rules exist to prevent.

Neutral copy delivered selectively excludes as effectively as discriminatory wording. Two consequences: targeting choices must be examined with the same care as the words, and using a platform's tool does not shift responsibility. "The platform offered that option" is not an answer.

3.7 Imagery, disclaimers, and instructions

Imagery communicates preference as effectively as words. Marketing that consistently depicts one group can signal who is welcome.

An equal housing statement does not cure discriminatory content. A discriminatory advertisement with a fair housing logo is still a discriminatory advertisement.

A client's instruction does not authorize it. Documenting that copy came from the seller does not transfer responsibility, and publishing a softened version conveying the same preference is the same violation in gentler words. The professional response is to explain why the copy cannot run, offer the property-focused rewrite, and escalate if the client insists.

3.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to name the prohibited practices, rewrite copy that sorts people, and explain why targeting is part of the advertisement.

4. Reasonable accommodations and modifications

4.1 Two different requests

Using the wrong framework produces the wrong answer, and the error is extremely common.

Reasonable accommodationReasonable modification
What it changesA rule, policy, practice, or serviceThe physical premises
ExamplesWaiving a no-pet policy for an assistance animal; a reserved accessible parking space; permitting a live-in aide; accepting rent on a different date tied to benefit timingInstalling a grab bar; widening a doorway; adding a ramp; lowering a countertop
Who requestsThe person with the disability, or someone on their behalfThe same
AnalysisIs it reasonable, and is it necessary because of the disability?The same

The classic error is evaluating an accommodation request under the ordinary policy — treating a request to waive a no-pet rule as a pet question — which produces a denial that never engaged the actual standard.

4.2 Assistance animals are not pets

For fair housing purposes, an assistance animal is not a pet. Three consequences frequently mishandled:

  1. A no-pet policy does not, by itself, justify denying the request.
  2. A pet deposit or pet rent may not be charged. Charging one imposes a condition on a protected group that others do not bear.
  3. A request cannot be refused merely because the animal is not "certified." Reliance on a certification requirement is a common and serious error.

The provider may still evaluate reasonableness, and the resident remains responsible for damage the animal causes and for the animal's behavior.

4.3 What "reasonable" means

Reasonableness is an actual analysis, not a formality, and it can produce a denial. Factors that legitimately bear on it include whether the request would impose an undue financial and administrative burden, whether it would fundamentally alter the nature of the operation, and whether an alternative would equally meet the need.

Two points about process:

  • The interactive process matters. Where a request as made is not workable, the provider engages about alternatives rather than simply refusing.
  • Delay functions as denial. A request that receives no response is effectively refused, and the absence of a decision is not a defense.

4.4 Modifications: who pays and what conditions apply

In private housing, a reasonable modification is generally made at the requester's expense. The provider may impose reasonable conditions — workmanlike performance, permits, and in some circumstances restoration when the tenancy ends.

What the provider may not do is treat the request as an unauthorized alteration and refuse it on that basis. That applies the lease framework to a fair housing question.

4.5 Verification, and its limits

Where a disability or the disability-related need is not obvious, a provider may generally seek information sufficient to establish that the person has a disability and that the requested accommodation is necessary because of it.

What may not be sought is a diagnosis, medical records, or detail about the nature or severity of the condition. The question is whether the need exists, not what the condition is.

4.6 Design and construction

Certain multifamily housing is subject to accessible design and construction requirements, and California standards can exceed the federal baseline.

A licensee marketing new construction should not represent that a property meets accessibility requirements without confirming it, and should direct specific questions to the developer, the architect, or the jurisdiction.

4.7 Accessibility in how service is delivered

The obligation is not limited to the property. It extends to how a licensee provides service:

  • Offering information in a format a consumer can use.
  • Not assuming a consumer's needs or capacities from an observation.
  • Not steering a consumer toward or away from properties based on an assumption about what would suit their disability.

The consumer decides what works for them; the licensee provides accurate information about the property.

That last point is the one licensees most often get wrong with the best intentions. Deciding on a buyer's behalf that a two-story house "won't work for him" and not showing it is steering, however kindly meant.

4.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to classify a request, state the standard applied, and identify what may and may not be asked in verification.

5. Fair lending and appraisal bias

5.1 The fair lending framework

The Equal Credit Opportunity Act prohibits discrimination in any aspect of a credit transaction on protected bases, and requires that an applicant who is denied credit receive a statement of the reasons or notice of the right to obtain one.

The Fair Housing Act separately prohibits discrimination in residential real-estate-related transactions, including lending.

The Home Mortgage Disclosure Act requires certain lenders to collect and report data on mortgage applications, which supports detection of lending patterns no individual decision reveals.

The Community Reinvestment Act addresses depository institutions' obligations to help meet the credit needs of the communities they serve.

5.2 Redlining and its modern forms

Redlining — denying or varying the terms of financing or insurance by area — is prohibited, and its historical operation is part of why present-day patterns exist.

Federally influenced appraisal and lending practice once graded neighborhoods for lending risk, with grades correlated to racial composition. Areas graded poorly received less mortgage capital for decades, which suppressed maintenance, values, and the accumulation of housing equity in exactly the neighborhoods already excluded.

Modern forms include marketing that reaches some areas and not others, branch and service distribution, and — increasingly — algorithmic and data-driven decision-making that reproduces historical patterns without anyone intending it.

5.3 Why this compounds

Housing is the principal vehicle of wealth accumulation for most American families. Wealth built through housing is inherited, and inherited wealth becomes the down payment for the next generation.

Exclusion from housing markets therefore does not end when the exclusionary practice ends. It compounds — which explains why equal treatment starting now does not by itself produce equal outcomes.

What this does and does not imply for a licensee. It does not imply that a licensee should attempt to correct history through their own decisions about who lives where — selecting properties for a buyer based on a protected characteristic is steering regardless of the intent behind it, including a corrective intent.

It does imply that a licensee should recognize inherited patterns rather than reading them as natural facts about places, refuse to transmit them as market wisdom, apply uniform practices, and give consumers objective information so they can apply their own criteria.

5.4 The licensee's role in lending

May: explain in general terms how loan program types work; explain what a preapproval is; recommend the buyer speak with more than one lender; and provide the same information and referrals to every consumer.

May not: steer a consumer toward or away from a lender or program on a protected basis; assume from an observation what a consumer can afford or qualify for; discourage an application; or participate in an arrangement that varies terms by area or by borrower characteristic.

"They probably wouldn't qualify" is not an assessment a licensee is entitled to make, and acting on it is discouragement, which produces a denial with no record of one.

5.5 Appraisal bias

Valuation is a judgment, and judgment is where bias operates. Concerns about appraisal bias — including in the treatment of neighborhoods and of the people occupying properties — are a recognized issue, and both federal and California attention has increased.

The structural control is objective, market-derived support. A derived adjustment can be examined; an impression cannot. Comparable selection, adjustment derivation, and neighborhood description are each places where a stated basis constrains discretion.

5.6 The licensee's role with an appraisal

May: provide genuinely relevant information through the proper channel — sales the licensee believes relevant, information about condition and improvements, a copy of the contract.

May not: communicate a target. "The deal dies without another $25,000" is exactly what independence rules exist to prevent — including when the request comes from the licensee's own client.

Where a licensee believes an appraisal reflects bias: escalate to the broker and follow the reconsideration process available through the lender. Do not confront the appraiser, and do not advise the client that they have a claim — that is a legal conclusion.

5.7 Insurance

Discrimination in the provision of property insurance by area or by protected characteristic raises the same concerns as lending, and insurance availability is increasingly the practical constraint on transactions in mapped hazard areas.

A licensee's role is the familiar one: ensure the buyer investigates early in the contingency period, provide the same information and referrals to every consumer, and escalate anything that looks like a pattern rather than a pricing decision.

5.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to name the fair lending statutes, explain why exclusion compounds, and state what a licensee does when an appraisal appears to reflect bias.

6. Complaints, documentation, and professional response

6.1 Structure beats effort

The central practical finding, and the reason this material belongs in a principles course rather than an ethics seminar:

Conscious effort to be fair does not reliably reduce the effects of bias. Structure does.

Effort fails because it depends on noticing, and much of what produces unequal outcomes operates outside awareness. Structure works because it removes the discretion in which bias operates.

6.2 The practices that constrain discretion

PracticeWhat it constrains
Written criteria applied uniformlyThe decision itself, in screening and selection
Uniform service scriptsDifferential treatment at first contact
The consumer's criteria drive the searchWhose judgment selects properties
Objective sources for subjective questionsThe licensee substituting characterization for data
Uniform showing and access protocolsSelective supervision, and the judgments behind it
Documented evaluationsMakes the basis reviewable, which itself changes behavior
Slowing decisions under time pressureBias operates most strongly when fast
Reviewing outcomes, not intentionsDetects patterns no individual decision reveals

6.3 Declining characterizations well

Licensees steer partly because declining is hard in the moment. Having language ready removes the difficulty. Each answer declines the characterization, gives an honest reason, and leaves the consumer better equipped.

"What kind of people live here?" "I don't describe neighborhoods by who lives in them — that's not something I'm permitted to do, and honestly my impression wouldn't be worth much. Census data and the city's own information are public, and here's where to find them."

"Is this a safe area?" "Safety isn't something I can characterize for you. Local law enforcement publishes incident data by area, and here's where. I'd also suggest visiting at different times of day."

"Which schools are good?" "School quality is something to evaluate against your own priorities. The district publishes performance data — here's the link. I'd avoid ranking them, because what matters in a school varies a lot by family."

What not to do instead: refuse and change the subject, which leaves a real informational need to be answered somewhere less careful; answer "in general terms," since a general characterization is still a characterization; or answer by showing rather than telling, redirecting the search toward what you would have said.

6.4 The unlawful instruction

Write this out in advance, because it is the hardest thing in the lesson to do in the moment — the client is in front of you, the listing is at stake, and the instruction is usually delivered casually.

"I can't do that, and I want to be straight with you about why. [Characteristic] is protected under California law, and screening or showing on that basis isn't something I'm able to do — it would put your listing and my license at risk. What I can do is [the lawful alternative addressing the underlying concern]. I'm also going to let my broker know we discussed this, because that's the process."

Four components: refuses plainly, explains without lecturing, offers a lawful alternative addressing the real concern, and escalates transparently.

Three failures: following it while documenting that it came from the client — documentation does not transfer responsibility for the act; complying informally by scheduling around those consumers — which achieves the same exclusion; and withdrawing quietly without explanation — which leaves the client free to repeat the instruction with the next licensee.

6.5 Responding to a complaint

When a consumer alleges discrimination, the first response frequently determines the trajectory:

  1. Acknowledge promptly. Silence reads as evasion.
  2. Notify the broker immediately.
  3. Preserve everything — records, communications, drafts, texts, and the marketing as it ran.
  4. Respond factually, without admitting or denying liability before the file has been reviewed.
  5. Do not offer a settlement or a fee reduction on your own initiative.
  6. Report to the carrier where the matter could become a claim.

Why the wrong responses are wrong: an immediate detailed defense commits a position before anyone reviewed the file; an apology accepting fault may be treated as an admission and can compromise coverage; a fee reduction commits the brokerage and rarely ends the matter; waiting for a formal demand hardens the matter and may make coverage notice late; and handling it quietly compounds the original problem with a supervision failure.

Deleting a record that reflects poorly is far worse than the record. It converts a defensible dispute about professional judgment into a question about integrity, and it is generally discoverable anyway.

6.6 Complaints that are not about you

A consumer who reports that another brokerage discriminated should be taken seriously:

  • Record what they report, without characterizing it as established.
  • Inform them that enforcement agencies accept complaints, and how.
  • Notify your broker.

What not to do: investigate the other brokerage, tell the consumer such complaints rarely succeed, or take no action because the conduct was someone else's.

6.7 Outcome review

The practice most likely to detect a problem is also the least used: periodically look at your own outcomes rather than your intentions.

  • Where are the properties you showed, mapped?
  • How quickly did you respond to inquiries, grouped by source?
  • Which applicants did you encourage, and which did you discourage?
  • What does your marketing reach, and whom does it reach?

Individual decisions rarely reveal anything. Patterns do — and a licensee who reviews their own patterns finds problems while they are still correctable.

6.8 What the file must show

For a fair housing question, the record is the defense:

  • The consumer's stated criteria, recorded at intake and updated.
  • The search parameters used.
  • Properties presented and shown, with dates.
  • Where a matching property was not shown, why.
  • Screening criteria and documented evaluations against them.
  • Advertising as it ran, where, when, and with whose approval — including the audience targeting.
  • Instructions declined, and the escalation.

A list of properties shown, without the criteria that produced it, shows what happened without showing why. The criteria are what make it defensible.

6.9 Checkpoint

You should be able to produce the declining scripts, write the unlawful instruction response, state the complaint sequence, and describe what outcome review examines.

Key terms

TermDefinition
The federal protected classesRace, color, religion, sex, national origin, familial status, disability.
Civil Rights Act of 1866Prohibits racial discrimination in property transactions, not subject to the later exemptions, and applies to all property.
Fair Housing ActTitle VIII of the 1968 Act as amended, the principal federal housing discrimination statute.
Americans with Disabilities ActAddresses public accommodations and commercial facilities — including a brokerage's own office.
Familial statusHouseholds with children under 18, pregnant persons, and persons obtaining custody. Older-persons exemptions are narrow and must actually apply.
FEHACalifornia's principal housing discrimination statute, covering the federal characteristics and adding others.
Unruh Civil Rights ActProhibits discrimination by business establishments, reaching beyond housing.
Source of incomeA California-protected characteristic. An instruction to refuse housing assistance is an unlawful instruction.
"Not one of the federal seven"Not clearance. It is the reasoning that produces California violations.
SteeringDirecting a person toward or away from areas, buildings, or units. Defined by whose criteria drove the selection, not by intent.
BlockbustingInducing sales or rentals by representations about the entry of a protected group into an area.
RedliningDenying or varying financing or insurance terms by area.
DiscouragementDeterring an inquiry or application rather than denying it. Achieves a denial with no record of one.
Assumed suitabilityDeciding on a consumer's behalf that a property will not work for them. Steering, committed with good intentions.
Describe the property, not the peopleThe rule that works because it never requires reference to any protected characteristic — and therefore survives a list that changes.
Accessibility informationBelongs in the copy. The rule governs who the property is for, not what it has.
Audience targetingPart of the advertisement. Restricting who may see a housing advertisement denies access as effectively as the words, and a platform's tool does not shift responsibility.
Equal housing statementAppropriate and expected. Does not cure discriminatory content.
Reasonable accommodationA change to a rule, policy, practice, or service because of a disability.
Reasonable modificationA change to the physical premises, generally at the requester's expense with reasonable conditions.
Assistance animalNot a pet. No pet deposit or pet rent, no certification requirement — and the resident remains responsible for damage and behavior.
Interactive processEngaging about workable alternatives. Delay functions as denial.
Verification limitsEnough to establish the disability and the disability-related need — not diagnosis, records, or nature and severity.
Equal Credit Opportunity ActProhibits credit discrimination and requires reasons for adverse action.
HMDA / CRAMortgage application data reporting supporting pattern detection; and depository obligations to community credit needs.
CompoundingWhy exclusion outlasts the practice: housing wealth is inherited and becomes the next generation's down payment.
Corrective steeringSelecting properties on a protected basis with a corrective intent. Still steering.
Appraiser independenceSupply information, never a target — including at your own client's request.
Structure beats effortEffort depends on noticing; structure removes the discretion in which bias operates.
Outcome reviewExamining your own patterns — showings mapped, response times by source, who was encouraged, whom your marketing reached.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A seller tells a licensee that his fourplex is "exempt from all that fair housing stuff" because he lives in one unit, and instructs the licensee not to rent to families with children or to anyone using a housing voucher. He adds that he would never discriminate on race, so there is no real issue.

Assess every element.

Solution. The exemption claim fails first, and for three reasons. Federal exemptions are limited and generally do not apply where a real estate licensee is involved — that is the point of them: they cover an owner acting alone. The 1866 Act's prohibition on racial discrimination is not subject to them at all. And California's protections are broader; a federal exemption does not create a state one.

"This one is exempt" is a reason to escalate, not a reason to proceed.

Excluding families with children is familial status discrimination, federally and in California — and the older-persons exemptions are narrow and must actually be satisfied.

Excluding voucher holders is source of income discrimination, protected in California. So is a minimum income multiple applied to the full rent rather than the tenant's portion.

And his framing is the error the whole lesson addresses. Race is one of seven federal characteristics, and California protects far more. A licensee who reasons "it's not race, so it's fine" will engage in conduct that is unlawful.

Problem 2

A licensee shows a buyer three homes in a different area than the two he asked about, tells him he'd "be more comfortable over here," follows up on some inquiries within an hour and others in two days, presents certain listings warmly and others flatly, and does not schedule a two-story listing for a buyer with a mobility impairment because the stairs "won't work for him."

Classify each and state what makes them all the same violation.

Solution. All five are steering, and steering is defined by whose criteria drove the selection, not by the licensee's intent.

Redirection — showing a different area than the one asked about. Differential effort — allocating access unequally by response time, a distinction that cannot be defended afterward in neutral terms. Differential enthusiasm — shaping the selection as effectively as declining to show it. Assumed suitability — the two-story home, and the one committed with the best intentions.

On the last: the consumer decides what works for them; the licensee provides accurate information about the property — here, that access is by stairs with no elevator. Deciding on his behalf and omitting the property is steering however kindly meant, and it is precisely why accessibility information belongs in the copy rather than being used to filter for him.

The defense is the record: the buyer's stated criteria, the search parameters used, properties presented with dates, and the reason where a matching property was not shown. A list of properties shown without the criteria that produced it shows what happened without showing why.

Problem 3

Rewrite each and say what makes the rewrite better: (a) "family-friendly neighborhood"; (b) "not suitable for anyone with mobility issues"; (c) "mature adults only, no vouchers"; (d) "walking distance to St. Anne's parish." The seller insists on (a) and offers to sign a statement that it was his idea.

Answer.

Solution. (a) "Cul-de-sac location; park two blocks away." (b) "Second-floor unit; access by stairs, no elevator." (c) Omit both clauses; screen on uniform written criteria. (d) "Walking distance to the town center."

Every rewrite is more informative than the original. (b) especially tells a buyer with a mobility concern exactly what they need to know without deciding for them whether the property will work — which is also the answer to the common overcorrection of omitting accessibility features entirely. The rule governs who the property is for, not what it has: describe the step-free entry, the roll-in shower, the elevator.

On (c): "mature adults only" touches age and familial status and the older-persons exemptions are narrow; "no vouchers" is source of income discrimination in California.

The seller's offer is worthless and the licensee should say so plainly. A client's instruction does not authorize the copy, documenting that it came from him does not transfer responsibility, and publishing a softened version conveying the same preference is the same violation in gentler words. Explain why it cannot run, offer the rewrite, and escalate if he insists.

Problem 4

A licensee posts a listing with fully neutral copy and an equal housing logo, then boosts it with targeting set to homeowners aged 25–45 in three ZIP codes. Her marketing images consistently depict one group of people. She says the copy is clean so there is no exposure.

Assess.

Solution. The targeting is the serious problem, and it is easy to miss because it does not appear in the copy at all.

Platforms permit selection by age, location, inferred interests, and proxies for demographic characteristics. Restricting who may see a housing advertisement can deny groups access to the housing opportunity — the exact harm advertising rules exist to prevent. Age-based selection plus geographic proxies is that. Neutral copy delivered selectively excludes as effectively as discriminatory wording, and "the platform offered that option" is not an answer: using the tool does not shift responsibility from the advertiser.

The imagery is a second exposure. Imagery communicates preference as effectively as words, and marketing that consistently depicts one group can signal who is welcome.

The logo cures nothing. A discriminatory advertisement with a fair housing logo is still a discriminatory advertisement, and the same is true of a disclaimer stating that all are welcome.

Targeting choices must be examined with the same care as the words — and the archive must record the audience, not only the creative.

Problem 5

A tenant with a disability asks the property manager to waive the no-pet policy for her assistance animal and to install grab bars in her bathroom at her own cost. The manager replies that the building does not allow pets, that certification would be required in any event, that a $500 pet deposit would apply, that the grab bars are an unauthorized alteration under the lease, and asks for her medical records. Three weeks later he has not decided.

Classify each request and identify every error.

Solution. The animal is a reasonable accommodation — a change to a rule or policy. The grab bars are a reasonable modification — a change to the physical premises, generally at the requester's expense, subject to reasonable conditions such as workmanlike performance and permits.

Six errors. He evaluated the accommodation under the ordinary pet policy — the classic mistake, producing a denial that never engaged the actual standard. An assistance animal is not a pet, so a no-pet policy does not by itself justify refusal. Certification may not be required. A pet deposit or pet rent may not be charged, because it imposes a condition on a protected group others do not bear. Treating the modification as an unauthorized alteration applies the lease framework to a fair housing question. Medical records may not be sought — a provider may seek enough to establish the disability and the disability-related need, not a diagnosis, records, or the nature or severity of the condition.

And the delay is a seventh. Reasonableness is a genuine analysis that can produce a denial — but the interactive process requires engaging about alternatives where a request as made is not workable, and delay functions as denial. The absence of a decision is not a defense.

What remains true: the resident is responsible for damage the animal causes and for its behavior.

Problem 6

A buyer's agent tells his client that a particular lender "probably wouldn't approve someone in your situation" and steers him elsewhere without an application. Separately, the agent notices that a lender's marketing appears to reach some neighborhoods and not others, and that an appraisal on a home in a historically underinvested area came in far below his supported range.

Address each.

Solution. The first is discouragement, and it is the form that produces a denial with no record of one — which is exactly why it is hard to detect and dangerous. "They probably wouldn't qualify" is not an assessment a licensee is entitled to make, and acting on it steers the consumer away from credit. What a licensee may do is explain in general terms how program types work, explain what a preapproval is, recommend speaking with more than one lender, and provide the same information and referrals to every consumer.

The marketing pattern is a modern redlining question. Denying or varying financing or insurance terms by area is prohibited, and modern forms include marketing reach, service distribution, and algorithmic decision-making that reproduces historical patterns without anyone intending it. Escalate to the broker rather than concluding.

On the appraisal: escalate to the broker and follow the reconsideration process available through the lender. Do not confront the appraiser, and do not advise the client they have a claim — a legal conclusion. And whatever the licensee sends must be information — relevant sales, condition, the contract — never a target.

Problem 7

An owner tells a licensee, while signing the listing, that he does not want the property shown to a particular category of buyer. The listing is worth several months of income.

Write the response and name what each part accomplishes. Then identify three ways licensees fail here.

Solution. "I can't do that, and I want to be straight with you about why. That's protected under California law, and screening or showing on that basis isn't something I'm able to do — it would put your listing and my license at risk. What I can do is apply the same written criteria and the same showing protocol to everyone, which is what actually protects you if a decision is ever questioned. I'm also going to let my broker know we discussed this, because that's the process."

Four components: it refuses plainly, explains without lecturing, offers a lawful alternative addressing the underlying concern, and escalates transparently rather than behind the client's back.

Three failures. Following it while documenting that it came from the client — documentation does not transfer responsibility for the act. Complying informally by scheduling around those buyers — which achieves the same exclusion. And withdrawing quietly without explanation — which leaves the client free to repeat the instruction with the next licensee.

Why this is written in advance: it is the hardest thing in the lesson to do in the moment. The client is in front of you, the listing is at stake, and the instruction is delivered casually — exactly the conditions under which people improvise something softer and comply informally.

And the obligation runs to people he will never meet. A licensee who accepts an instruction limiting who sees a property has violated it as to buyers he never met.

Problem 8

A consumer emails alleging she was discouraged from applying for a rental. The licensee sends a detailed defense within the hour, apologizes for "how it came across," offers to waive the application fee, deletes a text thread with the owner that reads badly, and decides to tell his broker only if it escalates. He also mentions that another brokerage did something similar to a client last month but that "those complaints never go anywhere."

State the correct sequence and address the other-brokerage report.

Solution. Five errors, and the deletion is categorically the worst.

The immediate defense commits a position before anyone reviewed the file. The apology may be treated as an admission and can compromise coverage. The fee waiver commits the brokerage and rarely ends the matter. Waiting to tell the broker compounds the original problem with a supervision failure. And deleting the thread converts a defensible dispute about professional judgment into a question about integrity — and it is generally discoverable anyway.

The correct sequence: acknowledge promptly, because silence reads as evasion; notify the broker immediately; preserve everything including drafts, texts, and the marketing as it ran with its targeting; respond factually without admitting or denying liability before the file is reviewed; offer no settlement on your own initiative; and report to the carrier where the matter could become a claim.

On the other brokerage: record what the consumer reported without characterizing it as established, inform her that enforcement agencies accept complaints and how, and notify the broker. What he must not do is investigate the other brokerage, tell her such complaints rarely succeed, or take no action because the conduct was someone else's.

And note the separate exposure: discriminatory conduct is grounds for action against a license independent of any civil outcome. "The case settled" does not close the matter.

Chapter summary

Seven federal protected classes, four statutes to know by name — and the 1866 Act's racial prohibition is not subject to the exemptions.

Exemptions generally do not apply where a licensee is involved. "This one is exempt" is a reason to escalate.

California protects the federal classes and adds many others, including source of income, and the list keeps growing — which is why the practice cannot be memorization.

Describe the property; do not describe the people who should live in it. Property-focused copy never requires reference to any protected characteristic, so it survives a changing list — and "it's not one of the federal seven" is not clearance.

The prohibited practices include the subtle forms, and redirection, curated balance, characterization, differential enthusiasm and effort, assumed suitability, and discouragement are the ones committed with good intentions. Steering is defined by whose criteria drove the selection.

Accessibility information belongs in the copy, because the rule governs who the property is for, not what it has.

Targeting is part of the advertisement, imagery communicates preference, an equal housing statement cures nothing, and a client's instruction authorizes nothing.

Accommodations change rules; modifications change premises. Evaluating an accommodation under the ordinary policy is the classic error. Assistance animals are not pets, certification may not be required, no pet charge may be imposed, verification may not seek diagnosis or records, and delay functions as denial.

Fair lending reaches credit, insurance, marketing reach, and algorithmic decisions, and exclusion compounds because housing wealth is inherited — which does not license corrective steering.

Supply an appraiser information, never a target, and escalate a suspected biased appraisal through the broker and the lender's reconsideration process.

Structure beats effort. Written criteria, uniform scripts, consumer-driven search, objective sources, uniform protocols, documented evaluations, slowing under pressure, and outcome review — because patterns reveal what individual decisions never do.

Have the declining scripts and the unlawful-instruction response written in advance.

On a complaint: acknowledge, notify the broker, preserve everything, respond factually, commit nothing, notify the carrier — and never delete an unfavorable record.

The record is the defense, and the criteria are what make a list of showings defensible.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Identify every protected characteristic implicated and under which framework, classify each instruction or practice as permissible or prohibited and name the form it takes, rewrite any advertising copy that sorts people and state what the rewrite adds, classify any disability-related request as an accommodation or a modification and state the standard and what may be asked in verification, draft in your own words the response you would give to any unlawful instruction, state what you would escalate and to whom, and list what the file must contain for the decisions to be defensible. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Principles - Lesson Assignment 15

Leasing, property management, license law, and integrated transaction

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Identify the elements of a lease and the tenant's and landlord's principal rights and obligations.
  • Explain what a management agreement must establish and why unsigned authority is a structural defect.
  • Apply the trust fund rules and explain why an internal ledger does not cure commingling.
  • Describe California licensing, the conduct that produces discipline, and the recovery account.
  • Apply the material fact standard across the transaction and identify where disclosure obligations arise.
  • Audit an integrated California transaction, rank its defects, and distinguish a documented gap from a falsified record.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: leases and management agreements55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: trust funds and California license law50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: material fact disclosure and the integrated audit45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Lease formation and tenant interests

1.1 What a lease conveys

A lease conveys a leasehold estate — possession for a period — while the owner retains the reversion. Lesson 03 classified the four leasehold estates by how each terminates; this section addresses the agreement that creates one.

The parties: the lessor (landlord) conveys possession; the lessee (tenant) receives it.

1.2 The elements

A lease should identify the parties, describe the premises, state the term, state the rent and when it is payable, and reflect the parties' agreement on the operative terms.

Writing. Under the Statute of Frauds, a lease exceeding one year must generally be in writing. A shorter lease may be enforceable orally — and the professional practice is to write every lease, because the boundary is not obvious in the moment and because an oral lease proves nothing about its terms.

1.3 The operative terms

TermWhat it governs
Term and renewalDuration, and whether and how it extends
RentAmount, due date, place and method, late charges
Security depositAmount, purpose, and the accounting on termination
UseWhat the premises may be used for
Maintenance and repairWhich party is responsible for what
UtilitiesWho pays
EntryThe landlord's right, purpose, notice, and timing
AlterationsWhat the tenant may change, and restoration
Assignment and sublettingWhether permitted, and on what conditions
Default and remediesWhat happens on breach
Subordination and attornmentThe lease's position relative to financing, and to a successor owner

1.4 Assignment and subletting

Students merge these, and the difference has consequences.

AssignmentSublease
What transfersThe entire remaining interestLess than the entire remaining interest
Relationship createdThe assignee deals with the landlordThe subtenant deals with the original tenant
Original tenant's liabilityGenerally remains liable unless releasedRemains liable in full

In both cases the original tenant generally remains liable, which is the point tenants miss. A tenant who "assigned the lease and walked away" has usually not walked away from anything.

1.5 Habitability

A residential landlord must maintain the property in a habitable condition. The obligation covers the elements making a dwelling fit to live in — weather protection, plumbing, hot and cold water, heating, electrical, sanitary facilities, and structural integrity, among others.

Two features shape the response:

  1. It is not waivable by agreement. A lease clause purporting to relieve the landlord does not.
  2. It runs continuously, not only at move-in.

Where the obligation goes unmet, a tenant may have remedies. The exposure grows with delay, and a documented prompt response is the defense.

1.6 Quiet enjoyment and entry

The tenant holds a possessory right, and entry requires:

  1. A permitted purpose — necessary repairs, agreed services, showing the unit, permitted inspection, or emergency.
  2. Proper notice, in the manner and within the time the law requires.
  3. A reasonable time of day.
  4. Documentation that notice was given.

What does not substitute for notice: the tenant being home, the owner's authorization, the manager holding a key, or the convenience of a contractor's schedule.

1.7 Termination and self-help

Termination and enforcement procedures are formal and unforgiving. A good reason delivered by a defective notice fails, and the landlord starts over, having lost weeks and improved the tenant's position.

The elements that must be right: the type of notice, its content, the period it gives, who is named, how it is served, and the proof of service. Impeccable documentation of the tenant's conduct does not cure a defect in any of them.

Self-help is prohibited. Changing locks, removing doors, shutting off utilities, or removing belongings to force a tenant out is unlawful, with no exception for a tenant who is badly behaved, not paying, or apparently gone — and where a unit appears abandoned there is a legal process for that too.

Retaliation is separately prohibited. Enforcement action close in time to a tenant's complaint or assertion of a right can look retaliatory, and the protection is a documented, independent basis predating or unconnected to the protected activity — which is another reason the maintenance log matters.

1.8 A lease survives a sale

The buyer of a leased property takes subject to the lease on its terms. "The tenants will be out by closing" is a representation to verify against the leases, not to accept — along with the deposits held and their amounts, notices already given, and any written agreement to vacate.

1.9 Checkpoint

You should be able to distinguish assignment from subletting, state the entry requirements, and explain why a defective notice defeats a good reason.

2. Property management agreements

2.1 Management is licensed activity

Managing real property for another for compensation is licensed activity. It is performed for and under the employing broker, the funds handled are trust funds, and the records are the brokerage's records.

Management also differs from sales in a way that shapes the risk: it is continuous. A sale ends. A tenancy runs for years, generating funds monthly, decisions weekly, and exposure continuously. A defect in a sales file is one transaction; a defect in a management practice repeats every month across every unit.

2.2 What the agreement establishes

TermWhat it governs
PartiesThe owner and the brokerage
PropertyExactly which units are managed
Term and terminationDuration, notice to terminate, what happens on termination
Scope of authorityWhat the manager may do without asking, and what requires owner approval
Spending authorityThe approval threshold, and emergency authority
CompensationManagement, leasing, renewal, and any other fees
Trust fundsWhere funds are held and how they are accounted for
ReportingWhat the owner receives and how often
Owner obligationsFunding reserves, maintaining insurance, providing disclosures
Insurance and indemnityCoverage each party maintains
Fair housingThe parties' obligations, and the manager's authority to refuse unlawful instructions

2.3 Unsigned authority is a structural defect

A manager acting without a signed agreement is acting without established authority. That is not a paperwork gap:

  • The scope of authority is undefined, so every decision is arguable.
  • The compensation is unenforceable.
  • The handling of funds is not authorized in writing.
  • The owner can dispute any action taken.

An audit finding two owners with no signed agreement has found a defect of the same order as commingling, not a filing oversight.

2.4 Spending authority and emergencies

The most practically important term is the approval threshold, paired with emergency authority.

Emergencies do not wait for approval. A burst pipe, a failed heater in cold weather, a security failure — these require action, and the agreement should say so, define what counts, and require prompt notice afterward.

A manager without written emergency authority faces a genuine dilemma: act and be second-guessed, or wait while damage compounds and a habitability obligation runs. Settling it in the agreement removes the dilemma.

2.5 Tenant selection

The defensible practice, and the only one that survives review:

  1. Written criteria, established before applications are taken.
  2. Objective standards — income relative to rent, credit history, rental history, verifiable identity, lawful occupancy standards.
  3. Applied identically to every applicant.
  4. Documented — how each application was evaluated against each criterion.
  5. Disclosed to applicants before they apply, along with any application fee.

Uniformity is not merely fair; it is what makes a decision explainable afterward in neutral terms.

The failures from Lesson 14 apply in full: impression-based selection, compatibility judgments, referral preference, differential strictness, and discouragement — telling an applicant they probably would not qualify instead of taking the application, which produces a denial with no record of one.

2.6 Maintenance triage

Not every request is equal, and a manager who treats them uniformly responds too slowly to the ones that matter.

CategoryExamplesResponse
EmergencyNo water, no heat in cold weather, sewage backup, gas odor, electrical hazard, security failure, floodingImmediate; emergency authority applies
HabitabilityFailed plumbing, non-functioning heat, pest infestation, mold, roof leakPrompt, documented
OrdinaryAppliance malfunction, cosmetic damage, minor fixturesScheduled within a reasonable period
Cosmetic or improvementRepainting by preference, upgradesOwner decision

A loss of water is not routine maintenance. Requiring a written request or routing through the owner may be part of the process; neither justifies delaying the response.

2.7 Owner reporting

The manager holds and handles the owner's money. Reporting is how the accounting duty is discharged, which is why it must be regular and itemized rather than summary. An accounting that cannot be verified is not an accounting, and owner preference for less detail does not change the obligation.

Monthly: rent collected by unit with dates; delinquencies and status; itemized disbursements with supporting documentation; fees charged; reserve balance and trust funds held; security deposits held by tenant; maintenance requests and responses; and vacancy and leasing activity. Annually: a summary and tax reporting information.

An owner unreported to for four months is not an administrative lapse. The owner has been unable to verify the handling of their own funds for a third of a year, the trust records have gone that long without external scrutiny, and if there is an error nobody has been positioned to find it.

2.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to state what a management agreement must establish, explain why unsigned authority is structural, and triage a set of maintenance requests.

3. Trust funds and records

3.1 What a trust fund is

Money received in the course of licensed activity that belongs to someone else is a trust fund.

In practice that includes: purchase deposits, rents collected for owners, security deposits, owner-provided reserves, and application fees held pending processing.

3.2 The three obligations

  1. Separation. Trust funds go into a trust account or neutral depository, kept separate from the brokerage's own funds.
  2. Records. A complete accounting of every receipt and disbursement, identifying whose funds they are.
  3. Timeliness. Funds are placed as required, without being held personally in the interim.

A deposit check held in a desk drawer over a weekend is a trust fund handling problem, whatever the intention — and the requirement runs to the broker.

3.3 Commingling and conversion

Commingling is mixing trust funds with the brokerage's own funds — including depositing them into the wrong account.

Conversion is using trust funds for a purpose other than the one for which they are held. It is the more serious offense and is treated as such.

Commingling is complete when the funds are deposited in the wrong account, regardless of how carefully the total is tracked and regardless of whether anyone loses money.

3.4 Why an internal ledger does not cure commingling

The most common defense a licensee offers is that funds in the operating account are tracked separately in the accounting system. It does not work, for a structural reason worth teaching:

Funds in a brokerage operating account are exposed to the brokerage's creditors, to its operating losses, and to ordinary business risk. A ledger entry does not protect the money. Separation is what protects it, and the record is what proves the separation was maintained.

3.5 Shortages and overages

FindingWhat it meansCorrect response
ShortageSomeone else's money is missingCure immediately from the broker's own funds; document the cause; reconcile
OverageThe records do not accurately reflect whose funds are heldInvestigate and document the source; the broker may not simply keep it

Neither is a rounding matter. There is no tolerance threshold, and neither may be deferred to the next reconciliation.

A manager discovers he deposited a tenant's rent into the operating account by mistake. The correct response is to correct it immediately, document the error and the correction, and notify the broker so the trust records reconcile and the cause is addressed. Correcting it quietly without a record is its own problem: trust accounting is judged by the record as much as by the balance.

3.6 Reconciliation

The trust account is reconciled on a regular cycle, comparing three balances: the bank balance, the book balance, and the total of the individual beneficiary ledgers. All three must agree.

A reconciliation that balances bank against book but never against the beneficiary ledgers will not detect the error that matters most — money in the account belonging to a different beneficiary than the records say. The total is right the whole time, which is exactly why it goes unnoticed.

3.7 Security deposits

Deposits are the tenant's funds held for defined purposes, and California regulates their handling and their return.

  • Hold them as trust funds, separate from the brokerage's funds.
  • Document condition at move-in, thoroughly, with dated photographs. A deposit dispute is decided on the difference between move-in and move-out condition, and a manager without move-in documentation has no case.
  • Distinguish damage from ordinary wear and tear. Wear and tear is not deductible.
  • Account in writing on termination, itemizing deductions with supporting documentation, within the required time.
  • Return the balance within the required period.

Deposit accounting is the single most common source of tenant complaints, and nearly all of it is prevented by move-in documentation and an itemized written accounting.

3.8 Records generally

Transaction and management records belong to the brokerage, and the responsible broker answers for them whoever created them. They are retained for the required period, and cancelled and failed transactions are retained too — disproportionately the ones that later produce disputes.

A licensee leaving a brokerage may not unilaterally take files, redacted or not. What may be taken is governed by brokerage policy and by client direction, and that question should be settled in writing in the office policy long before anyone departs.

3.9 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain why a ledger does not cure commingling, name the three balances a reconciliation must agree, and state what wins a deposit dispute.

4. California licensing and discipline

4.1 The license structure

A broker may conduct licensed activity independently and may employ salespersons. A salesperson may conduct licensed activity only for and under a responsible broker.

The brokerage is the party to listings, representation agreements, leases, and management agreements. The salesperson performs the work.

What follows, restated because it recurs in every audit: cancellation of a listing is a broker-level decision; the file belongs to the brokerage; a second salesperson on the other side does not cure dual agency; and a salesperson may not contract in her own name for licensed activity — a common and serious property management finding.

4.2 Supervision

The responsible broker must exercise reasonable supervision, discharged by a system:

  1. Written policies.
  2. File review, close enough in time to catch problems before they mature.
  3. Advertising review before publication.
  4. Trust fund controls, including reconciliation.
  5. Training, particularly for new licensees.
  6. An escalation path a salesperson can actually use.

A policy nobody follows is not supervision, and it is not a defense for anyone. Independent contractor status does not reduce it — that characterization addresses the tax and compensation relationship and answers a different question.

4.3 Unlicensed activity

Licensed activity is defined by what is done and whether compensation is expected, not by a job title. Unlicensed assistants may perform administrative work; they may not solicit, negotiate, show property, discuss terms, or be compensated for referrals.

A licensee may accept compensation for licensed activity only from their responsible broker, and may not act for more than one responsible broker at a time.

4.4 What produces discipline

CategoryTypical conduct
MisrepresentationStating as fact something unverified or untrue, or concealing a material fact
Trust fund violationsCommingling, conversion, shortages, inadequate records
Failure to superviseA broker who did not review files, enforce policy, or notice a pattern
Unlicensed activityPerforming licensed acts, or compensating an unlicensed person for them
DiscriminationConduct violating fair housing obligations
Secret profit or undisclosed conflictTaking a benefit the principal did not know about
Dishonest dealingConduct demonstrating unfitness, including outside a transaction
FalsificationBackdating, altering, or creating records

Most of these do not require anyone to have set out to defraud. Careless misrepresentation and inadequate trust fund records are the ordinary cases.

4.5 The disciplinary process, in outline

Investigation, an accusation, an opportunity for hearing, and a decision that may revoke, suspend, restrict, or condition a license, or impose other discipline. Related conduct may also carry civil and criminal exposure.

A licensee contacted by the Department should notify the broker immediately, preserve records, and not respond independently or informally. Cooperation is expected; improvised cooperation without review is where licensees make statements they cannot support.

4.6 The Real Estate Recovery Account

Funded by license fees, the account may pay a consumer who obtains a judgment against a licensee for fraud, misrepresentation, deceit, or conversion of trust funds where the judgment cannot be collected from the licensee.

Two features: it is a last resort, available after the consumer has pursued the licensee; and payment from it affects the licensee's license until the account is reimbursed.

4.7 Errors and omissions coverage

E&O responds to claims from professional services within its terms and limits, and is commonly conditioned on prompt notice of a potential claim.

Three consequences: late reporting can forfeit coverage; it does not transfer responsibility for the underlying conduct; and intentional acts are generally excluded — coverage protects against mistakes, not against decisions to deceive.

4.8 What to report to the broker, promptly

Any error affecting a client's decision or a transaction's terms; any client dissatisfaction that could become a claim; any allegation of misrepresentation, discrimination, or mishandling; any contact from another party's counsel; any regulatory contact; any suspected fraud; any wire fraud incident or near-miss; any request to alter or backdate a record; and any unlawful instruction from a client.

The bias should be toward reporting. A report that turns out unnecessary costs a conversation. An unreported matter that becomes a claim costs the coverage and the credibility.

4.9 Checkpoint

You should be able to name the discipline categories, state what E&O does and does not do, and describe what the recovery account is and is not.

5. Material fact disclosure

5.1 The standard

A fact is material if it would affect a reasonable buyer's decision to buy or the price they would pay.

Measured against a reasonable buyer — not the seller's view of importance, not how often the condition appears, and not whether the buyer could have seen it.

5.2 The two independent duties

The seller discloses known material facts affecting the value or desirability of the property, principally through the statutory transfer disclosure statement supplemented by what the property, its age, and its location require.

The licensee conducts a reasonably competent and diligent visual inspection of the reasonably and normally accessible areas and discloses material facts it reveals.

Neither discharges the other, and a professional inspection discharges neither.

5.3 Completing and handling the seller's disclosure

Four rules, each preventing a specific failure:

  1. The seller completes the seller's disclosure. A licensee who fills it in misattributes the statement.
  2. Every item is answered. A blank is ambiguous; "unknown" is an honest, available, and complete answer.
  3. Uncertain items are not answered "no." Marking "no" asserts knowledge the seller does not have.
  4. Amend when new information arrives.

And where the licensee knows something the disclosure omits, they raise it with the seller and the broker. A licensee who delivers a disclosure they know omits a material condition has adopted the omission — the line separating a compliant licensee from a complicit one.

5.4 Observation versus inference

Record what was observed, not what it means:

  • Observation: "Brown staining approximately three feet across on the ceiling of the northwest bedroom; paint in that area appears newer than the surrounding ceiling."
  • Not a conclusion: "There was a roof leak that has been repaired."

The first is what the licensee observed and is qualified to state. The second is an inference the licensee is not qualified to make, and it may be wrong in a way the buyer relies on.

5.5 Where else material facts arise

SourceExamples
Physical conditionDefects, repairs, systems, hazards
Legal conditionLiens, boundary disputes, litigation, permit status, unpermitted work
Use constraintsZoning, association restrictions, easements
LocationMapped hazard zones, environmental conditions, proximity
Transaction factsPending government action, an assessment, association litigation
The licensee's own knowledgeFrom any source, including a prior transaction

5.6 What the law limits

California imposes specific limits on the disclosure of a prior occupant's death and on disclosure relating to a prior occupant's illness. The limits exist for a reason worth teaching: without them, disclosure practice would operate as a channel for discrimination and would attach a permanent penalty to properties for reasons unrelated to their condition.

Do not volunteer. Do not misrepresent. Escalate. The precise boundary is a legal question with real consequences on both sides, and it is not one to resolve at a showing.

5.7 What must never be represented as fact

  • A prediction — that values will rise, that a permit will issue, that a variance will be granted.
  • A legal conclusion — that a use is permitted, that an encroachment is unimportant, that a party may cancel.
  • A specialist's assessment — that a crack is cosmetic, that a repair was adequate, that mold is harmless.
  • Anything unverified that cannot be attributed.

The professional treatment of unverified information is to attribute it and flag it: "the seller reports the roof was replaced in approximately 2019; this has not been verified" — never to launder it into apparent fact by repeating it, and never to copy a figure from a prior listing as though it had been verified.

5.8 Delivery

Content is half the obligation. Delivery, and evidence of when it occurred, is the other half, because rights run from delivery.

For each disclosure the file shows what was delivered, to whom, when, by what authorized method, and the evidence.

Late delivery is not cured by delivery. Where a disclosure arrives after the buyer removed contingencies, rights running from delivery are not extinguished because he acted earlier without the information — and neither receipt before closing nor a closing extension addresses his right to respond. The obligation is to raise it immediately.

5.9 The three-part test

When unsure what to do about something:

  1. Do I know it? If yes, the licensee's own obligation is engaged, whatever the seller does.
  2. Would a reasonable buyer consider it important? If yes, it is material.
  3. Am I qualified to evaluate it? If no, disclose the observation, recommend the specialist, and do not characterize.

"Disclose everything" is not the rule. The rule is: disclose what is material and known, attribute what is unverified, decline what the law limits, and evaluate nothing.

5.10 Checkpoint

You should be able to apply the three-part test, distinguish observation from inference, and explain why late delivery is not cured by delivery before closing.

6. Integrated California transaction audit

6.1 The chronology

A transaction is a sequence in which each step supplies the information the next step requires. The test when something seems out of order:

Does this decision have, in front of it, the information it was meant to be made with?

#StepSupplies
1Agency disclosureThe consumer's understanding of the relationship
2Listing or representation agreementAuthority to act, and its terms
3Pricing analysis and adviceThe basis for the client's price decision
4Seller disclosure preparationThe material facts, before pressure exists
5MarketingExposure, on compliant copy
6Offer and negotiationThe terms
7AcceptanceThe contract, and the deadline clock
8Disclosure deliveryWhat the buyer needs before committing
9InvestigationWhat the buyer needs before removing contingencies
10Contingency removalThe buyer's commitment, informed
11Loan and appraisalThe financing
12Final verificationCondition confirmed while leverage exists
13Signing, funding, recording, disbursementThe transfer
14PossessionControl
15Audit and retentionThe record

Every common sequencing error is a decision made before its information arrived.

6.2 The audit standard

Reconstructability: could a person who was not there determine what happened, when, and on what basis?

6.3 The audit sequence

  1. Completeness — every required document present, including for the parts that did not go smoothly.
  2. Dates — everything dated; delivery evidenced where timing matters.
  3. Consistency — names, dates, amounts, and property descriptions reconcile.
  4. Chronology — the order makes sense against the contract and the disclose-before-decide principle.
  5. Nothing improper present — no unexecuted draft mistaken for final, no superseded figure presented as current.
  6. Retention — filed where it will be found, for the required period, including cancelled and failed transactions.

Keep superseded versions. The sequence of versions is frequently what shows when a term changed and who agreed — the question a dispute usually asks.

6.4 A worked audit, ranked

A closed file contains:

  • (a) An agency disclosure signed and dated three days after the listing agreement.
