  • (b) A confirmation naming the brokerage as buyer's agent, while the listing file shows seller's agent, with no dual agency consent anywhere.
  • (c) An email agreeing to move the closing date, with no signed amendment.
  • (d) A contingency removal, signed but undated.
  • (e) A transfer disclosure with two items left blank.
  • (f) A referral fee disclosure dated the day of closing, for a warranty the buyer bought six weeks earlier.
  • (g) Marketing copy reading "great family neighborhood."

Ranked by seriousness:

1. (b) The undisclosed dual agency. Different in kind. It describes the brokerage acting on both sides without either party's informed written consent — a breach of duties actually owed, not a documentation failure.

2. (g) The fair housing copy. Also different in kind: it sorts people rather than describing the property, and intent is not a defense.

3. (d) The undated removal. Rights ran from it and the file cannot show when. The defect a dispute is most likely to turn on.

4. (a) Disclosure after the agreement. The seller committed before being informed. The document exists; the protection does not.

5. (f) Late conflict disclosure. Delivered after the buyer acted — the one thing a conflict disclosure must not be.

6. (c) Unsigned date change. The operative contract still says the original date.

7. (e) Blanks in the disclosure. Ambiguous about whether the items were addressed.

The response: record each finding, escalate to the broker, obtain what legitimately exists, and address the processes that produced them.

6.5 The line

What must not happen: dating the removal now, completing the blanks now, producing an amendment now with a matching date, or creating the consent that was never obtained.

A documented gap is a compliance problem a brokerage can survive.

A manufactured document is falsification. It converts a defensible situation into a question about integrity, and it ends careers.

Corrections are made visibly, with the original preserved and the change attributable. A record that can be silently altered is not a record.

6.6 The course in four questions

When you meet a situation this course did not cover, four questions will usually produce the right answer:

  1. Whose decision is this? Price, terms, and whether to proceed belong to the client. The analysis and the advice belong to you. Legal effect belongs to counsel.
  2. Whose duty is engaged? Fiduciary duties run to the principal; honesty, fair dealing, and disclosure of material facts run to everyone.
  3. Does this decision have the information it requires? If not, something is out of order.
  4. What will the file show? If the answer is "nothing," create the record now, while it is contemporaneous and true.

6.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to take an unfamiliar problem, apply the four questions, and produce both the action and the record it requires.

Key terms

TermDefinition

Practice problems

Problem 1

A tenant on a two-year lease transfers her entire remaining interest to a friend and moves out, telling the landlord she is "done." Another tenant lets a roommate take one bedroom for six months. The landlord asks whether either original tenant still owes rent.

Classify each and answer.

Solution. The first is an assignment — transfer of the entire remaining interest, with the assignee dealing directly with the landlord. The second is a sublease — less than the entire interest, with the subtenant dealing with the original tenant.

In both cases the original tenant generally remains liable unless released, which is the point tenants miss. A tenant who "assigned the lease and walked away" has usually not walked away from anything.

Whether either transfer was permitted at all depends on the lease's assignment and subletting provision, and whether the landlord's consent was required or given.

Note also that this two-year lease had to be in writing — the Statute of Frauds reaches leases exceeding one year — which is what makes the terms provable at all.

Problem 2

A tenant reports Friday morning that the unit has had no water since Thursday night. The manager tells him to submit a written request and that the plumber's next routine visit is Tuesday. On Tuesday he lets the plumber in without notice because the tenant is home. Eleven days after the tenant complained to the city about the delay, the manager serves a termination notice naming one of two tenants on the lease, left under the door.

Assess each act.

Solution. A loss of water is an emergency, at the top of the triage list with no heat in cold weather, sewage backup, gas odor, electrical hazard, security failure, and flooding. Emergency authority exists precisely so this does not wait for approval. Habitability is not waivable and runs continuously; requiring a written request may be part of the process but does not justify delaying the response — and the exposure grows with the delay.

The entry is unlawful. It requires a permitted purpose, proper notice in the required manner and time, a reasonable hour, and documentation. The tenant being home does not substitute for notice — nor does the owner's authorization, holding a key, or the contractor's schedule.

The notice is defective on two elements — correct parties named, and correct service with proof. A good reason delivered by a defective notice fails, and the landlord starts over having lost weeks and improved the tenant's position.

And the timing creates a retaliation appearance. The protection is a documented, independent basis predating or unconnected to the protected activity — which here does not exist, because the only documented conduct is the manager's own delay.

Problem 3

An audit finds two owners with no signed management agreement, one owner unreported to for four months, security deposits in the brokerage operating account, and a salesperson signing leases in her own name. The broker says nothing has gone wrong and no one has complained.

Assess each and the broker's defense.

Solution. Four independent defects, and none depends on harm having occurred.

Unsigned agreements are structural, not clerical: scope undefined so every decision is arguable, compensation unenforceable, fund handling unauthorized in writing, and every action disputable. This sits alongside commingling, not alongside filing.

The unreported owner has been unable to verify the handling of her own money for a third of a year, the trust records have gone that long without external scrutiny, and if there is an error nobody has been positioned to find it. Reporting is how the accounting duty is discharged — regular and itemized, and an accounting that cannot be verified is not an accounting.

The deposits are commingled, complete on deposit.

The salesperson may not contract in her own name for licensed activity. The lease is the brokerage's, executed for the broker.

The absence of complaints is not evidence of compliance. It usually means the problems have not surfaced yet — and in a management practice, which repeats monthly across every unit, they will.

Problem 4

A manager deposits a month's rents and three security deposits into the operating account, pointing out that his software tracks each beneficiary to the cent and the total is correct. Two months later a reconciliation that matched bank to book to the penny turns out to have concealed a deposit credited to the wrong tenant's ledger. A $340 overage also appears, which the broker proposes to keep as a fee.

Address all three.

Solution. Commingling was complete on deposit, regardless of the tracking and regardless of whether anyone lost money. The structural reason the ledger fails: funds in an operating account are exposed to the brokerage's creditors, operating losses, and ordinary business risk. A ledger entry does not protect the money — separation does, and the record proves the separation was maintained.

The reconciliation missed the third balance. It must compare bank, book, and the total of the individual beneficiary ledgers — all three agreeing. Bank-to-book alone cannot detect money held for a different beneficiary than the records say, because the total was right the whole time. That is exactly why it went unnoticed for two months.

The overage may not be kept. An overage means the records do not accurately reflect whose funds are held; it is investigated and documented, and the broker may not simply keep it. A shortage is cured immediately from the broker's own funds with the cause documented. Neither is a rounding matter, neither has a tolerance threshold, and neither waits for the next reconciliation.

And when the original error was found, the correct response was to correct it immediately, document the error and the correction, and notify the broker — because trust accounting is judged by the record as much as by the balance.

Problem 5

A tenancy ends. The manager withholds the entire $2,400 deposit for carpet replacement and interior repainting, sending a one-line note five weeks later. There is no move-in condition record. The departing licensee who managed the property also takes copies of all her management files, with tenant personal information redacted.

Assess both.

Solution. The deposit handling fails on every element. A deposit dispute is decided on the difference between move-in and move-out condition, and a manager without move-in documentation has no case — which is the entire reason move-in documentation exists. Ordinary wear and tear is not deductible, and carpet wear and repainting after a tenancy are the standard examples. The accounting must be itemized in writing with supporting documentation, delivered within the required time, with the balance returned within the required period. A one-line note five weeks later is none of that.

Nearly all deposit complaints — the most common source of tenant complaints — are prevented by two practices: dated photographic documentation at move-in and move-out, and an itemized written accounting.

The file removal is not hers to make. Records belong to the brokerage, and the responsible broker answers for them whoever created them. Redaction addresses a different risk and does not change who owns the record. What may be taken is governed by brokerage policy and client direction — a question that should be settled in writing in the office policy long before anyone departs.

Problem 6

Previewing a listing, an agent notices ceiling staining with newer paint. The seller says a pipe leaked two years ago, was repaired, and there is "nothing to disclose because it's fixed." The disclosure comes back with two blanks and one uncertain item marked "no." The agent completes the blanks from what the seller told him verbally, writes in his own disclosure that "there was a roof leak that has been repaired," and copies "roof replaced 2019" from the prior listing into the marketing sheet.

Identify every error.

Solution. Six, and two duties that exist independently.

The leak must be disclosed by the seller. A repaired condition is still material because the adequacy of the repair is exactly what a buyer evaluates — and here there are no records.

The blanks must be answered by the seller, with "unknown" where that is the truth. A blank is ambiguous; "unknown" is honest and complete.

The item marked "no" asserts knowledge the seller does not have.

The agent completing the form misattributes the statement — the seller completes the seller's disclosure.

"There was a roof leak that has been repaired" is an inference the agent is not qualified to make and that the buyer may rely on. The correct entry is the observation: "brown staining approximately three feet across on the northwest bedroom ceiling; paint in that area appears newer than the surrounding ceiling."

"Roof replaced 2019" launders an unverified figure from a prior listing into apparent fact. The professional treatment attributes and flags it.

And the agent's own visual inspection duty is independent of the seller's disclosure and of any professional inspection. A licensee who delivers a disclosure knowing it omits a material condition has adopted the omission.

Problem 7

A buyer asks a licensee whether the prior owner died in the house. Separately, the licensee is asked by a client whether a crack in the foundation is "cosmetic," whether the city will approve a variance, and whether values in the area will rise. A disclosure is delivered nine days after the buyer removed contingencies, and the listing agent proposes a one-week closing extension if he objects.

Handle each.

Solution. The death question is legally constrained. California limits disclosure of a prior occupant's death and of a prior occupant's illness — limits existing so disclosure practice does not become a channel for discrimination or attach permanent penalties unrelated to condition. Do not volunteer. Do not misrepresent. Escalate. The precise boundary is a legal question with real consequences on both sides, and it is not one to resolve at a showing.

The other three are each things that must never be represented as fact: a specialist's assessment (the crack), a prediction about a discretionary decision (the variance), and a prediction (values). Disclose the observation, recommend the specialist, point to the planning counter, and decline the forecast.

On the late disclosure: raise it immediately. Rights that run from delivery are not extinguished because the buyer acted earlier without the information — late delivery may revive or extend his ability to respond. Neither receipt before closing nor a closing extension addresses that right, and the proposal to say nothing unless he objects is itself the violation.

The rule covering all of it: disclose what is material and known, attribute what is unverified, decline what the law limits, and evaluate nothing.

Problem 8

Rank the defects in this closed file and state the response: (a) an agency disclosure dated three days after the listing agreement; (b) a confirmation naming the brokerage as buyer's agent while the listing file shows seller's agent, with no dual agency consent; (c) an email agreeing to move closing, no signed amendment; (d) a contingency removal signed but undated; (e) a transfer disclosure with two blanks; (f) a referral fee disclosure dated the day of closing for a warranty bought six weeks earlier; (g) marketing copy reading "great family neighborhood." A colleague offers to "clean it up" before the broker sees it.

Rank, respond, and address the colleague.

Solution. 1. (b) The undisclosed dual agency — different in kind, describing the brokerage acting on both sides without either party's informed written consent. A breach of duties actually owed.

2. (g) The fair housing copy — also different in kind. It sorts people rather than describing the property, and intent is not a defense.

3. (d) The undated removal — rights ran from it and the file cannot show when. The defect a dispute is most likely to turn on.

4. (a) Disclosure after the agreement — the seller committed before being informed. The document exists; the protection does not.

5. (f) Late conflict disclosure — delivered after the buyer acted, the one thing it must not be.

6. (c) Unsigned date change — the operative contract still says the original date.

7. (e) The blanks — ambiguous about whether the items were addressed.

The response: record each finding, escalate to the broker, obtain what legitimately exists, and address the processes that produced them rather than only the instances.

The colleague is proposing falsification, and it is the one thing here that is not survivable. Dating the removal now, completing the blanks now, producing an amendment with a matching date, or creating the consent that was never obtained converts a compliance problem into a question about integrity. A documented gap a brokerage survives; a manufactured document ends careers. Corrections are made visibly, with the original preserved and the change attributable — a record that can be silently altered is not a record.

Chapter summary

A lease conveys possession while the owner keeps the reversion, and one exceeding a year must generally be written. Assignment transfers the whole remaining interest and subletting less — and in both the original tenant generally remains liable.

Habitability is not waivable and runs continuously. Entry requires purpose, notice, a reasonable hour, and documentation, and nothing substitutes for notice.

A defective notice defeats a good reason, self-help is prohibited without exception, and retaliation is answered only by a documented independent basis.

A lease survives a sale. Verify rather than accept assurances.

Management is licensed activity, continuous, and performed for the broker. Unsigned authority is structural; emergency authority belongs in the agreement; selection runs on uniform written criteria; requests are triaged; and reporting is regular, itemized, and verifiable.

Trust funds require separation, records, and timeliness. Commingling is complete on deposit, a ledger does not cure it because a ledger does not shield money from the brokerage's creditors, shortages are cured immediately and overages are never kept, and reconciliation compares three balances.

Move-in documentation wins deposit disputes, wear and tear is not deductible, and records belong to the brokerage — including failed transactions.

The brokerage is the party; supervision is a system; licensed activity is defined by the act, not the title. Most discipline categories require no intent to defraud, the recovery account is a last resort with license consequences, and E&O responds only on prompt notice and never to intentional acts.

Materiality is measured against a reasonable buyer, and the seller's disclosure and the licensee's visual inspection are independent duties. Record observations, never inferences; answer every item; never adopt an omission.

Attribute what is unverified, decline what the law limits, evaluate nothing — and remember that delivery, with its date, is half the obligation.

A transaction is a chronology, and every sequencing error is a decision made before its information arrived.

Audit for reconstructability, rank defects by kind rather than count, keep superseded versions, obtain what exists, record what does not, and fix the process.

And when the situation is unfamiliar, four questions: whose decision, whose duty, does this decision have its information, and what will the file show.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a full transaction and practice audit memorandum for the file presented with this assignment. Sequence the compliance events and identify any that occurred out of order; identify each defect and rank the defects by seriousness with your reasoning; classify every fund handled and state the correct treatment; identify each disclosure obligation engaged, whose it was, and whether delivery was evidenced; state for each defect what may legitimately be obtained now and what may not be created; identify the process failure behind each rather than only the instance; and state what you would escalate to the broker and refer to counsel. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric, including the ranking rationale.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.
